<meta>This is the title page of the third volume of Michael Praetorius's encyclopedic work on music. It establishes the scope of the volume, focusing on contemporary musical forms and performance practice.</meta>

# MUSICAL TREATISE
## BY MICHAEL PRAETORIUS C. <note>The 'C.' stands for Creutzbergensis, indicating his birthplace, Creutzburg.</note>
### VOLUME THREE.

->In which is contained:<-

->1. The meaning, as well as the classification and<-
->description of almost all names of Italian,<-
->French, English, and currently common German<-
->songs: such as Concertos, Motets,<-
->Madrigals, Canzonas,<-
->etc.<-

->2. What must be observed in singing regarding the notes and the beat <note>original: "Tactu"</note>, the modes<-
->and transposition, the parts or voices <note>original: "Partibus seu Vocibus"</note>, and the<-
->various choirs; also regarding unisons<-
->and octaves:<-

<margin>folio 144.</margin>
->3. How the Italian and other musical terms <note>original: "Termini Musici"</note>,<-
->such as: *Ripieno* <note>the full ensemble</note>; *Ritornello* <note>a recurring instrumental passage</note>, *forte* <note>loud</note>, *piano* <note>soft</note>: *presto* <note>fast</note>, *lento* <note>slow</note>: *Capella* <note>the choir or chapel ensemble</note>;<-
->*Palchetto* <note>a small stage or platform</note>, and many others are to be understood and<-
->used: How to distinguish, classify, and<-
->suitably name the musical instruments: How to<-
->use the General-Bass <note>also known as basso continuo or thorough-bass</note>: How to<-
->easily arrange a concerto with instruments and<-
->human voices for various choirs: And how<-
->young boys in schools are to be<-
->accustomed to the current Italian<-
->style and manner of singing.<-

->Together with an attached, detailed<-
->Index.<-

---

->Printed at Wolfenbüttel by Elias Holwein, Princely Brunswick Printer and Type-cutter<-
->there. Published by the Author. In the Year 1619.<-

<summary>The title page of the third volume of Michael Praetorius's Syntagma Musicum, outlining a comprehensive guide to musical genres, performance techniques, and the burgeoning Italian style of the early 17th century.</summary>

# To the Noble, Honorable, Prudent, and
## Highly Wise Lords, the Burgomaster <note>The chief magistrate or mayor of the city.</note> and Council of the
## Holy Empire’s Highly Renowned
## City of

# Nuremberg:

->To my highly favorable Lords, as my unique patrons and protectors <note>original: "Moecenatibus ac Patronis singularibus"</note> of the art of **MUSIC**,<-
->and as benefactors <note>original: "Evergetis"</note> of musicians.<-

Noble, Honorable, Prudent, and Highly Wise, especially highly favorable Lords: Since I, through **God’s** granted grace—and not without singular effort and labor—have brought together a *Musical Treatise* <note>original: "Syntagma Musicum"</note>, which is distributed into *four volumes* <note>original: "Quatuor Tomos"</note> and encompasses all kinds of musical matters, or indeed most things that pertain *to music* <note>original: "ad Musicam"</note>, as can be understood more extensively from the *General Title* and *Index*; [I have done this] for the benefit of all upright, fervent lovers of noble music arising in the present time and among dear *posterity* <note>future generations</note>—

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Michael Praetorius's "Syntagma Musicum" to the city council of Nuremberg, praising them as patrons of the musical arts. The author introduces his four-volume work as a comprehensive resource for both contemporary and future musicians.</summary>
<keywords>Nuremberg, dedication, patronage, Syntagma Musicum, music theory, music history, city council</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the author's introduction to his multi-volume work, following the catchword 'ein an-' from the previous page.</meta>

gen: Therefore, I have dealt with the most prominent and proven *writers* in the *First Volume*, concerning *the sacred or ecclesiastical music of the ancients, adapted for religious practice; as well as the political music of the ancients, in noble and liberal use and play outside the limits of the Church* <note>original Latin: "de Musica Veterum sacra vel Ecclesiastica... simul & de Musica veterum Politica"</note>.

And because in the *Second Volume* I have described the names, classification, pitch, sketches, and *likenesses* of all musical instruments (as are in use in our current time in *Europe*, as well in other places as in these parts), and what more is necessary to know about them: Then also concerning the old and new organs of our time, their description, *disposition* <note>The specific arrangement of stops and pipes in an organ</note>, characteristics, and whatever else is *connected* and belongs to the same: being necessary to know and pleasant to read for *organists, instrumentalists*, organ and instrument makers, and others who have a desire and love for *music*:

So I have now, in this *Third* and the following *Fourth Volume*, included and composed the most important things that will be necessary for a *Chapel Master* <term>Capellmeister: the director of a musical ensemble or choir</term>, *vocal teacher* <term>Phonasco: from the Greek 'phonaskos', a trainer of the voice</term>, and *practical musician* to know—especially in these current times, when *music* has risen so high that it is almost unbelievable that it could ever reach a higher point.

But because now, especially in *Italy*, such an exceeding number of musical *compositions* and songs are so very oft

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introduction to his musical treatise, specifically addressing the need for clarity regarding new foreign musical terminology.</meta>

<sig>):( 3</sig>

...set down in a different style, manner, and way than in former times, and have come to light and been prepared for printing—with more yet to come—along with their <term>applications</term> <note>practical uses or settings</note>. Within these works are contained and handled so many various, unknown Italian <term>vocabulary words</term>, <term>technical terms</term>, and <term>styles</term> <note>original: "Vocabula, Termini vnd modi"</note>, that not every <term>musician</term> can easily find his way or adapt to them. Since, according to Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a famous Italian scholar</note>, the eye of philosophers, in *Book 13 of The Causes of the Latin Language, final chapter*: "A single word has only one proper and principal meaning; others are either common, necessary, or even false." Since names are the marks of things (as *Cicero says in the Topics and On Ends*; and *Aristotle in Book 4 of the Topics*); the notions of things (*the same Scaliger in Book 7 of Poetics, chapter 1*); the imitations of things (*Aristotle in Book 3 of Rhetoric*); and the manifestations and signs (*Plato in the Sophist*). 

Therefore, in this <term>Third Volume</term> <note>original: "Tertium Tomum"</note>, I have provided:

1. First, the names of all Italian, French, English, and currently common German songs, including their <term>meaning</term>, <term>classification</term>, and <term>description</term>.

2. Secondly, a correct and intelligible explanation of several other distinct matters, which are useful to know not only for common musicians but also for distinguished <term>musical theorists</term> and <term>practitioners</term>.

3. And then thirdly, how to understand the Italian and other <term>musical terms</term> and <term>vocabulary</term>, and how to name and categorize musical instruments in the Italian language. Also, the <term>General-Bass</term> <note>also known as Basso Continuo; a system of shorthand notation for keyboard players</note> (which is quite a new Italian invention, exceedingly magnificent and useful...

<summary>The author introduces the third volume of his musical treatise, justifying the need to define new foreign musical terms and styles—particularly Italian ones—to help musicians navigate modern compositions. He cites classical philosophers to emphasize that clear definitions are essential for understanding the "essence" of musical forms.</summary>

<keywords>musical terminology, Italian style, music theory, General-Bass, Basso Continuo, Scaliger, musical instruments, classification of songs</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>Continuity established with the previous page's discussion on musical theory; the catchword "auch" (also) connects to the following page.</meta>

This work is intended for <term>Chapel Masters</term> <note>original: Capellmeister; the director of music for a court or large church</note>, <term>Directors</term>, <term>Cantors</term> <note>the lead singer and educator in a church school</note>, <term>Organists</term>, and <term>Lutenists</term>. It addresses how to handle and correctly use the new style which is only just beginning to emerge and come into use among us in <term>Germany</term>. Likewise, it explains how one can conveniently arrange and organize a <term>Concert</term> or a German or Latin <term>Motet</term> <note>a polyphonic choral composition based on a sacred text</note> set for many different <term>Choirs</term>, along with various other matters contained herein. All of this is for the most part accommodated and directed toward the current new style of <term>Music</term>. I have composed, written, and brought this together partly from the prefaces of several <term>Italian Musicians</term>; partly from the oral reports of several <term>Italians</term> and those who have traveled in <term>Italy</term>; and partly also from my own thoughts and modest <term>Invention</term> <note>original: Invention; refers to the creative process of finding and developing musical ideas</note>.

Since your Excellencies and Lordships—as well as the noble city of <term>Nuremberg</term>, which is highly renowned throughout the Holy Roman Empire and all of Europe—are not only a <term>Refuge</term> <note>original: Receptaculum</note> for many <term>Italian</term> and <term>Venetian</term> businesses, but also particularly regarding <term>Music</term>, you have at all times venerated and held the same and its <term>Cultivators</term> <note>original: Cultores</note> in high esteem. This can be seen from the fact that you [honored] not only the exceedingly excellent <term>Musician</term> <term>Orlando di Lasso</term> <note>one of the most famous and influential composers of the late Renaissance</note>—born in Ghent in Flanders and at that time <term>Chapel Master</term> to his Serene Highness in Bavaria (as he himself highly praises in a preface)—but also...

<summary>The author introduces a treatise on the "new Italian style" of music, intended for professional musicians and dedicated to the city of Nuremberg. He cites Italian influences and his own creative work as sources for these instructions on arranging large-scale choral works.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, Italian style, Nuremberg, Orlando di Lasso, chapel master, motet, musical arrangement, 17th-century music</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 3: The author is discussing the high esteem for music in specific cities, likely leading into a formal dedication of this volume.</meta>

...not only have they subsequently loved and honored other excellent musicians, but excellent musicians have also arisen within that same city at all times. Among these is the far-famed musician, composer, and organist Johann Leo Hassler, who also laid his foundations in Italy, at Venice, under the highly renowned and excellent composer and organist Andrea Gabrieli <note>original: "Andreâ Gabrielo"</note>. Likewise, his brother Caspar Hassler, Johann Gadt, Christoph Buell, and others have attained honor and glory in this laudable kind of exercise <note>original: "in hoc laudabili exercitii genere"; referring to the practice of musical composition</note>. To this is also added the fact that not only do the common people, but also the distinguished merchants and traders there, possess a singular and great passion for music. Furthermore, they practice it quite diligently themselves, in such a way and fashion that their musical works <note>original: "Opera Musicalia"</note> are promoted for printing and published, and they have thereby acquired and brought about an immortal name for themselves, etc.

Since it is therefore clearly visible and sufficiently apparent from this how Your Honors and Excellencies <note>original: "E. E. vnd Herrligk:" - abbreviations for "Eure Edelheiten und Herrlichkeiten"</note>, as well as your citizens and relatives, respect and esteem music, both vocal and instrumental <note>original: "tam Vocalem quàm Instrumentalem"</note>, as well as its practitioners; and since you are yourselves knowledgeable in it and use it both publicly and privately <note>original: "publicè & privatim"</note>, you are therefore all the better able to judge and pass sentence upon it:

Therefore, to the same, as lovers of music <note>original: "Philomusis"</note> and highly honored friends of the Muses <note>original: "Musophilis"</note>, I have [dedicated] this third volume of my musical works <note>original: "Tertium hunc Tomum Operum meorum Musicalium"</note>, which, as previously mentioned, concerns the current new style and Italian manner in music, for which...

<summary>The author praises the musical culture of a specific city (likely Nuremberg), citing famous figures like Johann Leo Hassler and the musical literacy of the merchant class as justification for dedicating this third volume of his works to the city's leadership.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Leo Hassler, Andrea Gabrieli, Venice, music education, musical patronage, Italian style, music publishing, Nuremberg</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of the treatise, likely following a list of the dedicatees' titles and virtues. The catchword "IN" from the previous page suggests the beginning of a sentence regarding the author's intent.</meta>

To Your Honors and Excellencies: I have desired to submissively dedicate and offer this work, which treats and deals with subjects in which you take particularly great pleasure and delight. I do so with a most diligent prayer that you will not take such a dedication in a negative light, but rather, <note>original: "pro innatâ sua humanitate & benevolentia"</note> out of your innate humanity and benevolence, graciously be pleased and satisfied with this aforementioned, albeit modest, little treatise <term>Tractätlein</term><note>A diminutive form of "treatise," often used by authors as a gesture of humility regarding their own work.</note>, and allow me and mine to be <note>original: "de meliori notâ commendiret"</note> recommended to you in the best possible regard. I place my firm trust in Your Honors and Excellencies. I acknowledge myself at all times indebted and highly diligent in constantly performing all pleasing, submissive, and ready services for you according to my modest ability.

I hereby faithfully commend the same to the merciful protection of the Almighty for all happy prosperity and peaceful governance. Dated at Wolfenbüttel, on the 14th day of the month of May, in the year of Christ, 1619.

->Your Honors and Excellencies'<-
->Servant and ready-to-serve,<-
->Michael Praetorius, C.<- <note>The "C." likely stands for "Capellmeister" (Chapel Master), his official title at the court of Wolfenbüttel.</note>

<summary>In this formal dedication dated May 14, 1619, Michael Praetorius humbly presents his musical treatise to his patrons, requesting their continued favor and protection. He concludes with a prayer for their prosperous governance and a signature reflecting his position as a court servant.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page contains a celebratory Latin poem dedicated to the renowned composer Michael Praetorius, written by the jurist and poet Georg Remus. It serves as a laudatory preface to one of Praetorius's musical publications.</meta>

# On the MOST ILLUSTRIOUS MAN
## Master MICHAEL PRAETORIUS.
### His harmonic compositions, which are truly divine.
### An Epode by GEORG REMUS.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial T featuring floral and vine motifs.</image-desc>
AMPHION, the founder of the city of Thebes,
(If faith is to be had in the old stories)
Drew stones along by the sound of his Lute; and the Thracian ORPHEUS
Tamed the wild Lions with his learned lyre.
LET US NOT MARVEL at these deeds. But let us marvel at this:
That while our bodies remain here below,
PRAETORIUS, by the sweetness of his song,
Lifts our souls UP TO THE STARS.
For who among mortals is so dull, so foolish, so slow-witted and senseless,
Who is so leaden of heart,
That he would not think himself to be among the blessed inhabitants of heaven;
And feel himself added to the Choirs of Angels,
As soon as Polyhymnia <note>The Muse of sacred poetry and hymn-singing.</note> begins to sing in the temple,
And soothes the depths of his breast?
You shall be called the SAXON ORPHEUS; nay, to me, PRAETORIUS,
You are the GERMAN ORPHEUS:
Indeed, I believe you are a second Son of Jesse <note>original: "JESSAEuS"; a reference to King David, the biblical psalmist and harpist.</note> given to our shores
From the high Pole of heaven.
Live on, a guest-friend to the earth: but after death, in heaven,
You shall compete with the Angels upon your Lyre <note>original: "Chely"; a term for a lyre or lute made from a tortoise shell.</note>.

->Written on the spur of the moment<- <note>original: "ἐκ τοῦ παραχρῆμα"</note>

->Composed at Nuremberg on the day before the Kalends of May,<- <note>April 30th.</note>
->1619.<-

<sig>):( s</sig>
<meta>Translator's note: The signature mark indicates the position in the book's gathering.</meta>

<summary>A laudatory poem by Georg Remus praising Michael Praetorius's divine musical talent, comparing him to the mythical Orpheus and the biblical King David. The author asserts that Praetorius's music has the power to transport the human soul to heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Michael Praetorius, Georg Remus, Orpheus, King David, sacred music, Nuremberg, 17th-century poetry, musical praise</keywords>

<meta>The catchword "And" suggests a continuation from a previous introductory section or title page. This page serves as the formal dedication and preface to the third volume of the Syntagma Musicum.</meta>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage, architectural elements, and two decorative masks or faces on either side of a central motif.</image-desc>

# To all distinguished Musicians, Chapel Masters,
### and Vocal Teachers <note>original: "Phonaséis"</note> of the German Nation, the Author wishes,
### alongside proper greetings and due respect for their station,
### his service, God’s blessing, and all
### prosperity:

**S**ince the most prominent matters in this Third Volume <note>original: "Tomo"</note> of the *Musical Treatise* <note>original: "Syntagmatis Musici"</note> are for the most part the Author’s own thoughts and inventions—which he particularly observed and diligently noted while organizing various Electoral and Princely concert music in different places, and through further reflection and thought, transferred into many other concerts for practical use, and finally set down upon paper: Except only that he has incorporated and placed into this volume certain information from the Italians <note>original: "Italorum Information"</note>. This he found partly in several prefaces of their printed concerts (just as his own draft and work were nearly finished) and partly received through oral reports from good people who have traveled in Italy. And yet, being taught by experience <note>original: "experientiâ edocto"</note>, he is not at all unaware that, especially during the publication <note>original: "evulgation"</note> of new books, detractors are always found. Out of an innate envious malice, these people strive to the utmost to criticize well-intentioned work and to make it appear trivial or even hateful to everyone. Therefore, he asks all those experienced in this art that they might interpret this work of his in the best possible light <note>original: "in optimam partem interpretiren"</note>, judge it candidly, and whatever is to be improved therein, either communicate it to him and all music-lovers for their particular benefit and profit, or release it publicly through the press: Since many eyes always see more than one <note>original: "Siquidem oculi semper plus vident, quàm oculus"</note>.

<summary>In this preface, Michael Praetorius dedicates the third volume of his musical treatise to German musicians, explaining that while the work is largely based on his own professional experience, it also incorporates contemporary Italian techniques. He concludes by asking for constructive criticism rather than envious judgment from his peers.</summary>

<keywords>Michael Praetorius, Syntagma Musicum, music theory, Italian influence, German music, chapel masters, musical composition, 17th-century music</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory appeal, transitioning from a defense against critics to his motivation for publishing this work: a desire to see Italian musical standards established in Germany, even if he must be the "simpleton" who breaks the ice for greater masters to follow.</meta>

And it was for many years his heartfelt wish and sole desire that someone—who from youth had been raised and fundamentally informed and directed in the schools of the most excellent musicians <note>original: *præstantiſſimorum Musicorum Scholis*</note> (which, without disparaging other noble nations, have always been found in Italy and are still to be found there today)—might take this effort upon himself and, according to his superior understanding, treat more accurately <note>original: *accuratiùs*</note> and more extensively those matters which are here only briefly and sparingly touched upon: so that the deficiency in musical matters might be increasingly satisfied, a completely perfect work might come to light, and be consecrated and left behind for posterity.

But because this, his longing wish and desire, has not yet been granted to him, and yet many distinguished and learned people who have read through and approved this writing of his—as well as cantors <note>original: *Cantores*</note> and vocal teachers <note>original: *Phonaſci*; musicians specializing in training the voice</note> who are eager for such concert music and carry a fervent desire for it—have unceasingly pressed him for its publication both previously and even now: He therefore could not and should not further refrain from serving everyone for their instruction and guidance with this modest talent graciously bestowed upon him by **GOD**, so that in our common fatherland of Germany <note>original: *Germania*</note>, the noble art of music might flourish more and more and be brought to a full state and advancement; and perhaps he has been the first to break the ice and pave the way.

But just as the world-renowned and most excellent instrumentalists <note>original: *Inſtrumentiſten*</note>, organists <note>original: *Organiſten*</note>, and lutenists <note>original: *Lauteniſten*</note> can hardly be induced to let themselves be heard by others, unless some simpleton has first offended against the rules of the school and, with his untimely and awkward fumbling and scraping, as well as unpleasant "fifthing" <note>original: *Quintiſiren*; the technically incorrect practice of using parallel fifths in a composition</note>, produced a boorish, unpleasing melody and so plagued the ears of the others with such pain that, out of displeasure, they themselves immediately reach for the lute or instrument and, after the presented toccatas <note>original: *Toccaten*</note> and preludes <note>original: *præambulas*</note>, [perform] a completely lovely and pleasing fantasia <note>original: *Phantaſiam*</note> and fugue <note>original: *Fugam*</note> with artistic and elegant diminutions <note>original: *Diminutionen*; the musical technique of breaking long notes into many shorter, decorative notes</note>,

<summary>The author explains his decision to publish the work despite his humble self-assessment, hoping to inspire better-trained musicians to improve German music by "breaking the ice." He uses an analogy of a master musician being provoked into playing by the terrible performance of an amateur.</summary>
<keywords>music education, Italian influence, Germany, cantors, vocal teachers, instrumentalists, musical theory, diminutions, parallel fifths</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's justification for publishing his musical treatise. He moves from discussing the technical preparation of musicians to the specific goals of his work—specifically the upcoming fourth volume on composition—and his desire to encourage other German scholars to share their knowledge, citing the common habit of musicians to either be too shy or too excessive in their performances.</meta>

...perform passages, tremolos, and tirades <note>Rapid scale-like musical runs</note> for the public, yet later lose their own place and do not know when they should stop. Long ago, Horace—who undoubtedly was not always flattered by musicians—wrote quite aptly about this:

> All singers have this vice: among friends, they can never bring themselves to sing when asked, but once they begin unbidden, they never stop. <note>original: "Omnibus hoc vitium Cantoribus, inter amicos, / Ut nunquam inducant animum cantare rogati; / Injussi nunquam desistant."</note>

In the same way, the author hopes to provide guidance and incentive to others who have pursued this noble and lovely study. Many have spent years in *Italy* and other places (which he himself could not reach because of his health, his obligations, and many other inconveniences <note>original: "propter valetudinem, occupationes, & multa alia incommoda"</note>) and have enjoyed the conversation, knowledge, and friendship of many excellent musicians <note>original: "Musicorum conversation"</note>, having been instructed by them in the best possible way. He hopes they will further reflect on these matters and not bury their talents in the earth, but willingly communicate them to others: something he earnestly expects from every honest and sincere musician <note>original: "candido & sincero Musico"</note>. 

And because we are all bound by nature to serve our common homeland, he will publish his **Fourth Volume of the Musical System** <note>Syntagma Musicum</note> or <term>Melopoieia</term> <note>The art of musical composition; from the Greek 'melos' (song) and 'poiein' (to make)</note>—not out of ambition or to hunt for a great name and fame, but for the good of the German Nation and the benefit of music lovers. This work has been gathered from the magnificent writings of Gioseffo Zarlino, Giovanni Maria Artusi, Pietro Ponzio, Orazio Tigrini, and many other excellent authors, and by God's grace, he will soon bring it to light through public printing. It is to be hoped that many pious and good hearts will be found who will not only understand and acknowledge his labor with a thankful and pleasing spirit, but will also take the cause and opportunity to urge others who are professionally devoted <note>original: "ex professo"</note> to this art to bring forth their own writings, which they still keep in their notebooks <note>original: "Adversariis"</note>. 

The author of this *System* <note>Syntagma</note> especially hopes this of Henricus Baryphonus (the cantor and singular musician of the school at Quedlinburg). He hopes Baryphonus will not hesitate to release the works he promised in his *Pleiades Musicae*, as well as others—particularly the *Harmonic Exercises* <note>original: "Exercitationes Harmonicas"</note>, in which everything necessary to know regarding theory <note>original: "ad Theoriam"</note> and practice <note>original: "Praxin"</note> is contained—so as not to delay others who are eager for this art any longer. To this end, the author of this *Syntagma*...

<summary>The author explains his motivation for publishing a fourth volume on composition (Melopoieia), emphasizing his desire to serve the German nation rather than seek personal fame. He encourages other scholars, specifically Henricus Baryphonus, to publish their musical drafts and theoretical notebooks for the common good.</summary>
<keywords>performance practice, Horace, Italy, music theory, Melopoieia, composition, German nation, Gioseffo Zarlino, Henricus Baryphonus, Syntagma Musicum</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface and introduces the table of contents for the third volume. The author expresses his willingness to fund the publication himself to ensure that German music reaches the same heights of cultivation and prestige as Italian music, ultimately serving the glory of God and the joy of the faithful.</meta>

...willingly offered to spend the costs and expenses <note>original: "sumptus vnd vnkosten"</note> for the printing for the benefit of general studies—provided other good people are not found for it—so that, following the example of the Italians <note>original: "Exempel der Italorum"</note>, music may also be not only cultivated <note>original: "excoliret"</note> but also propagated <note>original: "propagiret"</note> in our German fatherland <note>original: "Germana nostra patria"</note> just like other sciences and disciplines <note>original: "Scientiæ vnd Disciplinæ"</note>, and may be spread far and wide for the sole praise and glory of God, as well as for the blessed recreation <note>original: "Recreation"</note> and delight of God-fearing hearts.

With this, I recommend the true-hearted reader to Divine protection; may they recognize this good and well-meaning work in the best possible light and use it for their own good and benefit; and may they remain devoted to the author with such good and well-meaning affection <note>original: "affection"</note>, true heart, and mind, as he is disposed and affected <note>original: "affectioniret"</note> toward them and all dear musicians <note>original: "Musicis"</note> from the bottom of his innermost heart.

->In You, Jesus, I hope; I shall not be confounded forever. <note>original: "IesV In te spero,non ConfVnDar In æternVM." This is a chronogram, a common Baroque word-puzzle where the capitalized Roman numerals (I, V, I, V, C, V, D, I, V, M) add up to a specific year, in this case 1622.</note><-

---

# In this Third Volume three
## parts are contained.
### In the First Part
->the following is treated:<-

Regarding the meaning, categorization, and description of almost all the names of *Italian, French, English*, and currently common *German* songs:
->Namely:<-

1. Concertos. pages 4, 5.

# VOLUME THREE
## FIRST PART.

->A Treatise on Songs.<- <note>original Greek: "Asmatologia," referring to the systematic study or science of songs.</note>

->Or,<-

A Miscellany concerning the etymology <term>Etymology: the study of the origins and history of words.</term> and description of songs <note>original Latin: "cantionum"</note> used not only among the Italians, French, and English, but also within living memory among the Germans, and dedicated as much to ecclesiastical <note>church-related</note> use as to ethical, political, and domestic <note>social or household</note> life.

## First Part /
->In which<-

**The meaning of the names, as well as the description of almost all Italian, French, English, and currently common German songs—such as Madrigals, Canzoni, Villanelle, etc.—is explained.**

And the same shall be treated in the following 12 Chapters.

I. Concerning the table and classification of Italian, French, English, and now common German songs.
II. Concerning those songs which have sacred and serious secular texts: such as Concerti, Motets, and Falso Bordone <note>a style of four-part vocal harmony, often used for psalms, characterized by its "false bass" technique.</note>.
III. Concerning those songs which have secular, humorous <note>original German: "possirliche," meaning droll, comical, or entertaining.</note> texts in certain

# Concerning the Table and Classification of Italian, French, English, and Now Common German Songs.

Since many simple folk would often like to know what the various names of the Italian and French songs might mean; I have therefore wished to indicate such things here for their instruction, as I have gathered them from the writings of many noble authors, kindly requesting that this be received in the best spirit.

Chapter 2.

# Of those Songs Which have Sacred and grave secular Texts: Namely Concertos, Motets, and Fauxbourdon.

1. **SONG, HARMONY**, *or Symphony, is the modulation of diverse voices. By the Italians it is called a "Concetto" or "Concerto," which in Latin is a "Concertatio" <note>literally a competition or debate</note>, whereby various voices or musical instruments are brought together to create a "concert" [a joining together or competing]: for sweetness consists not so much in craftsmanship as in the variation itself: in German, a Concert.*

*This term "Concert" is used in a first, general sense for every harmonic song:* As a distinguished musician in Italy, **Ludovico Viadana**, directed his *Songs* according to the very charming and useful new style invented by him, entitled them with the name *Concert*, and in the preceding preface indicated among other things this: That he took great care so that not too many rests <term>Pausen: intervals of silence in a musical part</term> would be inserted, but especially that these *Concertos* would have more loveliness, cadences <term>Cadentien: harmonic conclusions or resolutions</term>, and passages <term>Passaggien: decorative, running musical ornaments</term> within them. Also, that every single word and its syllables might correspond exactly <note>original: "exactè seinen Noten respondiren"</note> to its notes, so that the listeners could more easily take in and understand all the words and phrases <note>original: "Sententias"</note>. He was particularly moved to do this because he saw that often a motet <note>original: "Mutet"</note> of 5, 6, or more voices was played on the organ, but the singers or cantors—especially in the monasteries—were rarely more than two or three. Thus, due to the lack of the other voices, much of the loveliness and ornament was taken away from the symphony, especially because the omitted voices are filled with fugues, cadences <note>original: "Clausulis"</note>, etc. (to which the other voices, provided by musicians, usually correspond with long rests). And then the protracted waiting and the many rests mutilate and mangle the text, bringing much frustration to the listeners and <unclear>troublesome</unclear> work to the singers. He therefore set pen to paper and composed several motets in a special concerted style <note>original: "Concertatweise"</note> for one, two, three, and four voices, and specially adapted <note>original: "accommodiret"</note> them for the organ. These gave others so much pleasure and delight that they were not only sung publicly in great numbers <note>original: "frequenz"</note> in the main churches in Rome, but also incited and stimulated many noble minds <note>original: "ingenia"</note> toward imitation.

Just as it is evident today that in Italy at this time, almost all—or indeed most—composers care very little for madrigals, but for the most part focus on this and similar 

<meta>catchword: similar</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Concerto" as a musical form characterized by the interaction of diverse voices and instruments, focusing on Ludovico Viadana’s innovations. Viadana's "concerted" style aimed to make sacred music more practical for small ensembles by reducing long rests and ensuring the text remained clear and unmangled.</summary>

<keywords>Concerto, Symphony, Ludovico Viadana, Motet, musical terminology, Roman church music, early Baroque style, text clarity, musical rests</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Concerto" and "Motet" genres, specifically how terminology was used by Italian composers like Ludovico Viadana and Stefano Nasimbeni. It transitions from a general definition of these terms to a more technical description of the "concerted" style involving vocal and instrumental competition, concluding with the English concept of the "Consort."</meta>

of the same kind, very magnificent things, which they have published in print with one, two, three, and four voices **with General Bass for the Organ** <note>original: *cum Basso generali pro Organo*</note> (of which more shall be said later in the third part), and they name and entitle them interchangeably <note>original: *indifferenter*</note> as **Concertos, harmonies** <note>original: *concentus*</note>, etc., and **Motets**.

And although they mostly entitle the Latin songs <note>original: *Cantiones*</note> or Motets set for more than 4—such as 5, 6, 7, or 8 voices—as **Sacred Songs, sacred Harmonies, and Motets**: yet it is found that these words **Concerto, Song, Harmony, Motet** are understood one like the other to mean sacred Latin songs and melodies. Just as **Stefano Nasimbeni** entitled not only his Masses and Psalms for 3 choirs with 12 voices, but also the others with 9, 5, and fewer voices, as **Ecclesiastical Concertos** <note>original: *Concertos Ecclesiasticos*</note>.

2. **Specifically from "Concerting"** <note>original: *In specie à Concertando*</note>: When, from a whole group of musicians, several—and especially the best and most prominent fellows—are selected, so that they alternate choir-style one after the other, both with the **human voice** <note>original: *voce humana*</note> and with all kinds of instruments, such as **cornetts** <term>Zincken: curved wooden trumpets with finger holes</term>, **trombones**, **recorders and transverse flutes**, **krummhorns** <term>Krumbhörner: "crooked horns" with a distinctive buzzy reed sound</term>, **bassoons or dulcians**, **ranketts** <term>Racketen: small, cylindrical double-reed instruments with a deep sound</term>, **viola da gambas**, large and small **violins**, **lutes**, **harpsichords** <term>Clavicymbeln</term>, **regals** <term>Regal: a small, portable reed organ</term>, **portatives**, or **organs**, etc., and whatever names they have or may yet be invented (of which more shall be said in the third part of this volume); and they contend against each other, as it were, so that one always wishes to surpass the other and be heard to better advantage.

For this reason, the word **Concert** appears as if it had its origin in the Latin verb *Concertare*, which means to skirmish with one another. However, primarily and properly, this type of song is to be called a **Concert** when perhaps a low or high choir are heard against each other and together. Although this style is also used in songs for six voices, it can be arranged nowhere better than in those set with many voices for 2, 3, 4, 5, or more choirs.

The English call it, quite appropriately from the word for "partnership" <note>original: *à consortio*</note>, a **Consort**, when several persons with all kinds of instruments—such as the harpsichord or large spinet, great lyre, double harp, lutes, theorboes, bandoras, penorcons, citterns, viola da gamba, a small descant violin, a transverse flute or recorder, and sometimes even a quiet trombone or ranket—accord together in a group and company quite quietly, softly, and lovelily, and tune together in a pleasant **Symphony** <note>original: *Symphonia*</note>.

<summary>This page defines the "Concerto" through two lenses: first as a general label for sacred Latin songs (Motets), and second as a specific style of musical competition between choirs and instruments. It also provides an early German perspective on the English "Consort" as a harmonious ensemble of diverse instruments.</summary>

<keywords>Concerto, Motet, Stefano Nasimbeni, musical competition, instrumentation, Cornett, Rankett, Viola da gamba, Consort, Symphony</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical terminology, specifically focusing on the etymology and various spellings of the word "Motet." It follows the previous page's introduction of how Italian composers used the terms "Concerto" and "Motet" interchangeably for sacred Latin songs.</meta>

## 2. MOTETS.

# The word "Motet" is pronounced and used in various ways by different authors.

->They vary in this way:<-

<note>This list illustrates how different famous composers and theorists spelled the word "Motet" and which grammatical gender they assigned to it in Latin.</note>

1. Jacob de Kerle: **Moteta** — in the neuter plural.
2. Lechner and Utendal / Philippe de Monte — **Motetta** — in the feminine singular / in the neuter plural.
3. Ivo de Vento: **Motteta** — in the neuter plural.
4. Lechner / Utendal and Riccius — **Motecta** — in the feminine singular / in the neuter plural.
5. Utendal and Ivo de Vento: **Muteta** — in the feminine singular.
They have so established. <note>original: *Posuerunt*</note>

Count Alphonse de Monte Dolio considers *Moteta*, *Motecta*, *Modeta*, or *Muteta* to be Italian words.

Furthermore, there are various opinions regarding the origin from which the name **Motet** <note>original: *Motectæ*</note> is derived.

Some, whom Philippe de Monte <note>A highly influential Flemish composer of the Renaissance</note> seems to support, wish for the name **Muteta** to be derived from "mutating" or "changing" <note>original: *à mutando*</note>, because the harmonies and fugues <term>Fugue: a musical technique where a short melody is introduced by one part and then taken up by others, overlapping in a "chase"</term> are, in a sense, exchanged for one another. For if a "verse" <note>original: *versus*</note> is so called from "turning" <note>original: *à vertendo*</note>—because words must be turned and inverted many times before a verse is made—then it is not unfitting for a *Muteta* or a "good song" <note>original: *bona cantilena*</note> to take its name from "changing." Indeed, a good song is rarely produced unless it is corrected perhaps a hundred times by changing the fugues, cadences <note>original: *Clausulis*</note>, intervals, and musical progressions.

Johannes Magirus <note>A German physician and music theorist</note> thinks the **Motet** <note>original: *Motecta*</note> is named as if "covered by a mode" <note>original: *quasi modo tectam*</note>, because the mode <term>Mode: the specific scale or "key" that gives a piece its character</term> or tone lies hidden and, as it were, "covered" within them. For a Motet is a song of a specific mode or genre.

Johannes Lippius, however, derives the word **Motet** <note>original: *Moteta*</note> from "moving" <note>original: *à motando*</note>, because through its gravity and natural artistry, it moves the listener most deeply.

And Johann Petreius, once a printer in Nuremberg, says in a certain preface:

> Because of the elegance of the melody <note>original: *Modulationis*</note>, the common people of Italy call these selected songs by the most excellent craftsmen **Modetas**.

But so that the kind musician <note>original: *Musicus benevolus*</note> may know how and in what manner several Italian musical authors have used these names—*Concertos*, *Motets*, *Harmonies* <note>original: *Concerti, Motetti, Concentus*</note>, etc.—without distinction, I have wished to demonstrate and indicate this somewhat here.

<meta>catchword: Motet-</meta>

<keywords>Motet, etymology, Philippe de Monte, Johannes Magirus, Johannes Lippius, music theory, Renaissance terminology, music notation</keywords>

<column-break/>

Among these are several authors who use both terms (**Concertos** and **Motets**), such as **Antonius Faber** and **Simone Molinaro**. **Tomaso Cecchino** titles his **two-part songs** <note>original: *Bicinia*</note> as **Concerted Motets**. Others use titles such as **Harmonies, Sacred Songs, Praises, Pearls, Praises of God, Divine Praises, Sacred Melodies, Spirituals, The Heavenly Drum**, etc. <note>The author lists various Latin titles used for publications: *Concentus, Sacra Cantica, Sacras Cantiones, Laudes, Harmonias, Margaritas, Dei Laudes, divinas Laudes, Melodias sacras, Spirituales, Tympanum cœleste*.</note>

Even though these **Songs** <note>original: *Cantiones*</note> set for 2, 3, 4, or 5 voices can very appropriately be called **Concertos** for certain reasons—namely, because in some of them two, three, or four voices follow one another in **Harmony**, and in others they imitate **passages** or **divisions** <note>original: *diminutiones*; fast, decorative melodic runs</note> and mimic what was sung before, then suddenly all fall in together and thus, so to speak, **concert** <note>original: *concertiren*; from the Latin *concertare*, meaning to contend, dispute, or work together</note> with one another to see who can perform it best. My own works in the **Polyhymnia, Parts 3 & 5** (discussed in the third volume), as well as those in the 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 5th styles, are arranged in this way.

Yet most composers have titled this same type of **Song** and **Harmony** with the name **Motet**. Very few have maintained the distinction that Motets should be composed in the true **style of Orlando** <note>Referring to Orlando di Lasso, the master of traditional Renaissance polyphony</note>, while **Concertos** should be composed in the **Madrigal style** <note>A more modern, expressive, and rhythmically varied style</note>.

Some also wish to make this distinction: that **Concertos** are arranged for several different **choirs** and are mostly quite simple, without special **variations** or strict **fugal observations**; Motets, however, are composed **with greater diligence and skill** <note>original: *majori industria & artificio*</note>.

<summary>This page concludes a list of contemporary composers and explains the overlapping definitions of "Motet" and "Concerto" in early 17th-century music. Praetorius notes that while "Concerto" implies a competitive, imitative interplay between voices, the terms were often used interchangeably by publishers.</summary>

<keywords>music theory, Michael Praetorius, Motet, Concerto, Orlando di Lasso, Madrigal style, musical terminology, polyphony, imitation, divisions, sacred music</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s classification of musical forms, specifically refining the definitions of "Motet," "Concerto," and "Symphony" by citing the practices of contemporary Italian and Franco-Flemish masters. It transitions from the previous list of composers into a technical discussion of terminology.</meta>

...and not set for more than 8 voices. Which, however, is not the case for all of them; since in **Giovanni Gabrieli’s** <note>original: *Ioan Gabrielis*</note> first book of *Sacred Symphonies* <note>original: *Symphoniarum sacrarum*</note>, the **Songs** <note>original: *Cantiones*</note> are set for 6, 7, up to 16 voices, arranged for 2, 3, and 4 choirs. It is right that these should be called not only **Motets**—since they are set in the true Motet style, of which **Orlando di Lasso** <note>original: *Orlandus*; Praetorius adds: "whom I consider to be the preeminent master in this genre in our memory"</note> made use—but they must also be called **Concertos**, because they are arranged for different choirs, and the vocal parts therein, so to speak, "concert" <note>from *concertiren*: to compete or strive together in a musical sense</note> with one another.

And although the **Ecclesiastical Songs** and **Church Concertos** of **Giovanni Gabrieli**, **Lambert de Saive**, and other distinguished musicians are inscribed with the title *Sacred Symphonies or Motets* <note>original: *Symphoniæ sacræ sive Motettæ*</note>, it is understood through this that such **Songs** are to be arranged with **Concerted Voices** <note>original: *ConcertatStimmen*</note> alongside all kinds of instruments. This is then rightly and properly called a **Symphony** <note>original: *Symphonia*</note>, which is to say a lovely **blending of sounds** <note>original: *Concentus*</note>, a playing together, and a pleasant harmony.

Yet it is found in the more recently published work of the aforementioned **Giovanni Gabrieli** that he wishes the word **Sinfonia** (otherwise **Symphonia**) to be understood also in this way: when something is to be performed without vocal parts, using only instruments, be they **viols**, **trombones**, and the like.

Just as **Ludovico Viadana** also titled his canzonas—which he set very finely and skillfully for 8 voices to be used on all kinds of instruments—with the name *Musical Symphonies* <note>original: *Sinfoniæ Musicali*</note>. For this reason, I have also had cause to use the word **Sinfonia** in this same sense. Likewise, before our time (though this has now fallen out of use), the word **Symphonia** or **Symphoney** was used when one summoned the **Household Musician** or the **Town Piper** with his entire **Symphoney** <note>meaning his full ensemble</note>, that is, with all kinds of instruments, such as: **cornetts** <note>original: *Zincken*</note>, **trombones**, **trumpets**, **violins**, **flutes**, **crumhorns**, **dulcians** <note>early bassoons</note>, etc.

---

## FALSO BORDONE.

1. First, the psalms at the beginning of Vespers, which are set in a row one after another in unison <note>meaning in simple, block chords</note> as **Note against Note** <note>original: *Nota contra Notam*</note>, are called **Psalms in Falso Bordone** <note>original: *Psalmi Falsi Bordoni*</note>. However, in these the **Bass** is now always found a **Fifth** below the **Tenor**, which makes the harmony good and complete.

2. Among the **Italians**, however, it is **FALSO Bordone** (which the **French** call **FAVLX BQVRDON**), when a song is sung with nothing but consecutive sixths...

<summary>Michael Praetorius defines the evolving terminology of "Symphonia" and "Concerto," noting how they transitioned from vocal styles to include instrumental ensembles. He also introduces the "Falso Bordone" style of simple, chordal psalm singing used in church services.</summary>

<keywords>Giovanni Gabrieli, Orlando di Lasso, Ludovico Viadana, Symphony, Motet, Concerto, Falso Bordone, instrumentation, Stadtpfeiffer, early music theory</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical terminology and composition. Following the previous page's definition of "Symphony" and "Motet," Praetorius now focuses on the "Falso Bordone" (Fauxbourdon), explaining its structure and the acoustic principles of vocal registration and interval placement.</meta>

...so that the **Alto** is a Fourth lower than the **Soprano** <note>original: *Discant*</note>, and the **Tenor** is a Third lower than the **Alto**; thus there is a Fourth above and a Third below relative to the **middle voice**.

It was accepted by the ancients that the most pleasant harmonic excursions should sometimes be established in this manner. But since they do not have a true **Bass**, and because the Italians use the word **Bourdon** <note>original: *Bordone*</note> to signify the string that closely follows the **lowest string** <note>original Greek: *ὑπάτην* (hypate); in ancient music theory, this was the lowest-sounding note</note> or the largest string on the **Lute** <note>original: *Testudine*</note>, it is called **Fauxbourdon** <note>literally "False Drone"; original: *Falso Bordone*</note>. For the **Third** does not have its natural seat in **low and deep sounds** <note>original: *sonis gravibus & inferioribus*</note>, but rather in **high and superior sounds** <note>original: *sonis acutis & superioribus*</note>.

3. Third, just as the word **Bourdon** is interpreted as a large **bumblebee** <note>original: *Hummel*</note>—which rustles, hums, and drones—so this style provides not a lovely harmony, but a rustling, humming, and droning one. This is due to the following reasons. First, because the **Third** has its **natural place** <note>original: *locum naturalem*</note> in high sounds, as can be seen from the fundamental and proportional harmonic numbers 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, and the following diagram.

<column-break/>

<image-desc>A vertical musical diagram. On the left is a five-line musical staff with diamond-shaped notes. To the right of the staff, brackets and horizontal lines define intervals according to a numerical series from 1 at the bottom to 6 at the top. Brackets are labeled "Octave" (between 1 and 2), "Fifth" (between 2 and 3), and "Fourth" (between 3 and 4).</image-desc>

For 1 and 2 establish an **Octave** in **lower sounds**. But just as there is no other whole number between 1 and 2, so there is neither a **Fifth** nor a **Third** between G and g. 2 and 3, however, establish a **Fifth**; just as 3 and 4 establish a **Fourth** in the **middle sounds** <note>original: *sonis intermediis*</note>. In the same way, the Fifth cannot be divided into a **Major** and **Minor Third** (because there is no whole number between 2 and 3). Finally, 4 and 5 establish a **Major Third**, and 5 and 6 a **Minor Third** in **high and superior sounds**.

From this one sees that the **Third** has its **natural place** not in low sounds, but in high ones. And since harmony is best when the **consonances** <note>intervals that sound pleasing together</note> are placed in their proper and natural locations, and the Third creates a lovely harmony in high sounds, it follows that in low sounds it produces a sad, unlovely, and rustling **blending of sounds** <note>original: *concentum*</note>. Likewise, an **Octave** in **high and superior sounds** causes a **gaping harmony** <note>original: *hiulcam harmoniam*; a disjointed or "hollow" sound</note>, as can be seen from this diagram, in which the natural seat and series of the **consonances** is inverted.

<image-desc>A vertical musical diagram positioned to the right of the text. It depicts a staff with diamond notes and brackets on the right labeling intervals in a descending numerical order from 1 at the top to 6 at the bottom. The labels are: "Octave", "Fifth", "Fourth", "Minor Third", and "Major Third."</image-desc>

<summary>Michael Praetorius explains the acoustic theory behind "Fauxbourdon," arguing that musical intervals like thirds sound best in high registers, while placing them in the bass creates a "droning" or "buzzing" effect. He uses the mathematical harmonic series to prove that octaves belong in the bass and thirds belong in the soprano.</summary>

<keywords>Fauxbourdon, Falso Bordone, harmonic series, music theory, intervals, octave, fifth, major third, minor third, acoustics, vocal registration, Michael Praetorius</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical critique of the Falso Bordone (Fauxbourdon) style, specifically addressing its theoretical irregularities and the etymological origins of musical terms like "Tenor" and "Bordon." It bridges the discussion from liturgical music to secular forms like the Madrigal.</meta>

Secondly, because the **Fourth** is considered by some to be a **perfect consonance** <note>original Latin: *consonantiæ perfectæ*</note>, and since two of these—let alone more—cannot follow one another without error <note>original Latin: *absque vitio*; standard counterpoint rules generally forbade parallel perfect intervals</note> (indeed, not even two **imperfect consonances** may do so), it would follow that such a **composition** <note>original Latin: *Concentus*</note> should not be approved.

4. Fourthly, in **oblique relationship** <note>original Greek: *σχέσει* (schesei); referring to the relationship between voices moving at different intervals</note>, **non-harmonic relations** occur, which **theorists** are reluctant to permit because they are **faulty progressions**. For when the **Third**, which is placed in the **low sounds**, is raised by an **octave** into the **high sounds**, one finds as many **Fifths** between the upper and middle voice as there were **Fourths** previously.

5. Thus, the **final cadences** <note>original Latin: *Clausulæ finales*</note> of any mode are also called **false bordons**. For **Bordoni** properly refers to the hems and borders on clothing, serving as the end or a specific limit of a thing: as can be seen in the **Antiphons** (where the **final cadences**, along with the final cadences of the modes and the **Basses**, appear in part to be uncertain and false). In this regard, it should be remembered that some believe the **Tenor** also takes its name from **Bordon**, which is **Tenor** in Latin, and in German a **SUPPORT** <note>original German: *STENDER*</note>; like a prop placed under a tree branch that hangs heavy with growth, upon which the entire tree rests. Or like a **Jacob’s staff** or **pilgrim’s staff** (**Bordone**, the staff that the pilgrim carries on a journey) which a traveler holds in his hand and must lean upon; likewise, a beam reinforced with iron used to support a house so the entire load rests upon it. Carpenters call this a **Bordonale**, a support beam. Thus, the **Tenor** is said to have received this name among the Latins, just like a **Bordon**, because it is intended to support the entire song. However, the opinion of **Aristides Quintilianus** <note>A 3rd-century music theorist</note> is well known: who testifies in Book 1, Chapter 5, that the name of the **Tenor** is derived from *Tonos*, that is, **accent**. For just as we measure the nature of a word by its **accent**, so we consider the nature of harmony primarily in the **Tenor**.

->But more on this in Volume IV, God willing.<-
->The names and origins of other Church Songs were introduced in detail in the first part of Volume One.<-

### Chapter III.
## Concerning songs which have secular, whimsical texts in certain verses: such as Madrigals, Stanza, Sestini, and Sonnets.

### 1. MADRIGALS.

**Madrigals**, as well as those following—such as **Dialogues**, **Stanza**, **Sestini**, **Sonnets**, **Canzoni**, and **Canzonette**—do not take their name from the melody of the song, but from the **text and the verses**.

<summary>Praetorius explains the theoretical objections to Fauxbourdon and provides an extensive etymological study of the 'Tenor' as a structural support, before transitioning to define secular vocal genres like the Madrigal.</summary>
<keywords>Fauxbourdon, Tenor etymology, Aristides Quintilianus, counterpoint theory, Madrigals, musical terminology, support beam metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the technical discussion of the "false bordon" and liturgical music theory into the origins and definitions of the Madrigal. It focuses on how poetic forms dictate musical structure and explores various etymological theories for the term "Madrigal."</meta>

Furthermore, **Madrigal** is the **name of a poem** <note>original Latin: *Nomen Poematis*</note>, and not the name of a **song** <note>original Latin: *Cantionis*</note>; the texts of these are mostly taken from **Francesco Petrarch**, **Boccaccio**, **Pietro Bembo**, and **Dante**.

It may be observed, however, that the word **Madrigal** could be derived in several ways:

1. **As if "mother of finery"** <note>original Italian: *madre della gala*</note> or **"mother of the thought"** <note>original Latin: *mater de sententia*</note>: Since these poets capture an entire meaning in exactly 8, 9, or 10 verses or lines—neither more nor less. A composer sometimes takes this same text as a single part, and sometimes divides it into two parts, as can be clearly seen in the **compositions** <note>original Latin: *Composition*</note> of **Luca Marenzio** and others. However, when sacred themes are written in this many rhymes or lines and set to harmony by the composer, they are called **Spiritual Madrigals** <note>original Latin: *Madrigalia Spiritualia*</note>.

2. **As if "mother of joy"** <note>original Italian: *Madre della gaia*</note>, or in French **gay**, which means **gladness** or **joyful** <note>original Latin: *lætitia, lætus*</note>. Or even **"elegant mother"** <note>original Italian: *Madre galante*</note>, that is: **charming, witty, beautiful, elegant, as if a mother of alacrity or joy**. This is because they are cheerful secular songs that are somewhat more pleasant, fresh, and merry than **motets** or **lute songs**.

3. **As if "song of the herd"** <note>original Latin/Italian: *Mandri-gale*</note>, that is, a **pastoral song** <note>original Latin: *Carmen pastorale*</note>, named after the **stall** <note>original Latin: *Mandra*</note>, specifically from the flocks of sheep, because such rustic songs were sung while the animals were grazing. In the very same way, even today, **herdsmen** and **shepherds** are accustomed to pipe to their little sheep with **bagpipes** <note>original German: *Sackpfeiffen*</note>. For a **shepherd** or guardian of sheep is called a **Mandriale** or **Mandrian**.

->Example from F. Petrarch<-
->Madrigal 2<-

> Because she bore the sign of Love upon her face,
> a pilgrim lady moved my hollow heart,
> so that every other seemed less worthy of honor to me.
> And following her thus through the green grass,
> I heard from afar a high voice saying:
> "Ah, how many steps you lose in the forest!"
> Then I drew close to the shade of a beautiful beech tree,
> all pensive, and looking around,
> I saw my journey to be quite perilous,
> and turned back almost at midday.
<note>This is a translation of the Italian text provided in the source, which is Petrarch's Madrigal 52, "Perch'al viso d'Amor portava insegna."</note>

->2. STANZA.<-

<meta>catchword: Stanza,</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Madrigal" as a poetic form primarily derived from great Italian authors and explores three etymological origins for the term: as a "mother of thought," a "joyful song," or a "pastoral song of shepherds." It concludes with a poetic example from Francesco Petrarch, highlighting the interplay between verse and musical structure.</summary>
<keywords>Madrigal, Petrarch, Boccaccio, Pietro Bembo, Dante, Luca Marenzio, Spiritual Madrigals, etymology, pastoral song, bagpipes, shepherds</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the forms and etymologies related to the Madrigal, moving from the definition of the term itself to the structure of its poetic components, specifically the "Stanza" and the "Sestina."</meta>

**Stanza**, that is, a **dwelling** or **habitation** <note>original Latin: *Domicilium seu habitaculum*</note>, like a chamber in which something is locked away, and where everyone has their own special **cupboard** or **compartment** <note>original German: *Schapp oder Fach*</note>. Poets create exactly 4, 5, or 6 **strophes** <note>original German: *Gesetze*, literally "laws," here meaning poetic sections</note>—no more and no less—and these are called the **First Stanza**, **Second Stanza**, etc. This is much like the **first strophe** <note>original Latin: *primus strophus*</note> or **first part**—the first "cupboard," "strophe," or part, then the second "cupboard" or "strophe." **Composers** <note>original German: *Componisten*</note> also create musical sections based on these, and they use them as follows:

First }
Second } **stanza**, that is, **part**. <note>original Latin: *id est, pars*</note>
Third } The first, second, or third part.

### 3. SESTINI.

**Sestini** derive their name from the **number of verses** <note>original Latin: *à numero Versuum*</note>, as they consist of six and a half **strophes** <note>original German: *Gesetze*</note>, and in every strophe there are six **lines** <note>original German: *reyen*</note>. The last words of the first strophe are repeated in all the following six strophes, yet in such a way that they are shuffled and never placed back into the same lines where they stood before.

---

#### Example from Petrarch.
#### Sestina 4.

->I.<-

He who is determined to lead his life <note>original Italian: *Chi e fermato di menar sua vita*</note>
Upon the treacherous waves and through the reefs,
Safe from death with a small wooden vessel:
Cannot be very far from his end;
Therefore it would be wise to withdraw to port,
While the sail still yields to the steering.

->II.<-

The gentle breeze; to which steering and sail
I committed upon entering this life of love
And hoping to reach a better port?
Then it led me into more than a thousand reefs
And the cause of my sorrowful end.
No longer was it around me, but inside the vessel.

<summary>This page defines the "Stanza" as a structural unit in both poetry and music, likening it to a partitioned room or cupboard, and introduces the "Sestina" form using Petrarch's poetry as an example.</summary>
<keywords>Stanza, Sestini, Petrarch, poetic structure, musical parts, strophe, verse repetition</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the transcription and translation of Petrarch's Sestina 80, following the introduction to the form and the first two stanzas provided on the previous page. The text continues to use nautical metaphors—ships, sails, and reefs—to describe the poet's spiritual journey.</meta>

### III.

Closed for a long time in this blind vessel <note>original Italian: *cieco legno*, literally "blind wood," a common poetic term for a ship</note>
I wandered without lifting an eye to the sail,
Which, before my time, was carrying me toward my end;
Then it pleased Him, who brought me into life,
To call me back so far from the reefs
That at least from afar the harbor appeared to me.

### IV.

As a light at night in any harbor
Was never seen from the high sea by ship or vessel, <note>original Italian: *nave ne legno*</note>
Unless the storm or the reefs took it away;
So from above the swelling sail
I saw the signs of that other life
And then I sighed toward my end.

### V.

Not because I am yet certain of my end,
For wishing to reach the harbor by daybreak
Is a great journey in such a short life;
Then I fear, seeing myself in a fragile vessel,
And more than I would like, the sail is full
Of the wind that pushed me onto these reefs.

### VI.

If I emerge alive from these treacherous reefs <note>original Italian: *dubbiossi scogli*</note>
And bring my exile to a beautiful end,
How I would long to turn the sail
And cast the anchor in some harbor:
Were it not that I burn, like wood on fire <note>original Italian: *acceso legno*; here the metaphor for "vessel" shifts back to the material "wood" to describe the heat of passion or trial</note>

<sig>4.</sig>
<meta>catchword: Si</meta>

<summary>This page contains stanzas III through VI of Petrarch's Sestina 80, utilizing a complex nautical allegory where the ship, sails, and harbor represent the poet's life, will, and spiritual salvation.</summary>
<keywords>Petrarch, Sestina, nautical metaphor, allegory, salvation, Italian literature, Canzoniere</keywords>

> *Since it is hard for me to leave my accustomed life,* <note>original Italian: *Si m’ è duro a lassar l’ usata vita*</note>
> *Lord of my end, and of my life,*
> *Before I break the ship among the reefs*
> *Steer the weary sail to a good harbor.*

### 4. SONNETS.

It is a type of poem <note>original Latin: *genus Carminis*</note> of 14 verses / which have their own special character in their rhymes <note>original German: *Reymen*</note>; as can be seen from the following example from *Petrarch*.

---

## SONNET.

I would sing of love in so novel a manner,
That from the highest heaven, a thousand sighs a day
I would draw from my breast, and a thousand other desires
I would rekindle in my frozen mind;
I would see my spirit often changing
Its vain affections, and through pious paths
Expanding its virtues without suffering,
As one who repents of his error.
No more red roses amidst the snow
Would I gather here, nor silver, gold, and ivory,
But the Good, which always mirrors and looks at itself in heaven,
If the high Creator into my brief heart
Should come, and I could say: I glory,
Lord, that your grace is no longer delayed.

<keywords>Sonnet, Petrarch, poetic form, rhyme, spiritual repentance, Italian poetry, nautical allegory</keywords>
