<header>Chapter IV.</header>

# On those songs which have secular, playful texts, yet are not in fixed verses: such as Dialogues, Canzoni, Canzonette, and Arias.

## 1. Dialogues.

What Dialogues <note>original Latin: *Dialogi*</note> are is known to everyone: for a Dialogue <note>original Latin: *Dialogus*</note> means a conversation, as when one person answers another following a question, and they take turns one after another as if in choirs. In this same manner, Echoes <note>A musical effect where a phrase is repeated more softly to simulate an echo.</note> can also be categorized here.

## CANZONI: Or Canzones in the Neapolitan style.

There are two types of Canzones: For 1. some, like the preceding Sonnets (in Latin *Cantilena* or *Cantio*, which are more general terms; in French *Chanson*), are truly secular songs or love-ditties <note>original German: *Buhlenliedlein*</note> that people sing. And these vary among the poets, but they do not always have the same number of stanzas and rhymes, nor do they always follow the same style, much like the Pindaric Hymns <note>Referring to the complex, irregular odes of the Greek poet Pindar.</note> or Horatian Odes <note>Referring to the polished, various meters used by the Roman poet Horace.</note>.

->As here, for example, from Petrarch.<-

## CANZON. 17.

> From thought to thought, from mountain to mountain
> Love leads me, for I find every trodden path
> Contrary to a peaceful life.
> If on a lonely shore, by a stream or spring,
> Or if between two hills a shaded valley <note>original Italian: *amhrosa vallei*, a typo for *ombrosa valle*</note> sits,
> There the dismayed soul grows quiet;
> And as Love invites it,
> Now it laughs, now it weeps, now it fears, now it feels secure.

<meta>catchword: And the</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "Dialogue" and "Canzone" as musical and poetic forms, noting their secular nature and flexible structure compared to sonnets, using a stanza from Petrarch's Canzoniere as a primary example.</summary>
<keywords>Dialogue, Canzone, Neapolitan style, secular music, Petrarch, Pindaric hymns, Horatian odes, poetic meter</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Canzone, specifically concluding the verse from Petrarch's Canzone 17. It then moves on to define smaller vocal forms like Canzonettes and Arias, before beginning a new chapter on "musical mixtures" like the Quodlibet.</meta>

->*And the face, which follows the soul wherever it leads,*<-
->*Is troubled, and then clears up,*<-
->*And remains in one state for only a short time;*<-
->*So that upon seeing him, a man experienced in such a life*<-
->*Would say: "This one burns, and is uncertain of his state."*<-

2. There are also some [Canzones] composed without text, consisting of short fugues and pleasant "fantasies" <note>An instrumental composition that follows the composer's imagination rather than a fixed form.</note> for 4, 5, 6, 8, or more voices. In these, the first fugue from the beginning is usually repeated at the very end to conclude the piece. These are also called *Canzoni*. Many beautiful Canzones of this kind have been published in Italy, especially those by Giovanni Gabrieli <note>original: *Iohan Gabrielis*, an influential Venetian composer known for his brass and polychoral works</note> for both few and many voices.

->3. **CANZONETTE.**<-

This is the diminutive form <note>meaning a "little Canzone"</note>, and these are short little songs or master-songs <note>original German: *Meistergesänge*, referring to the structured tradition of German singing guilds</note>, yet they always have secular texts. The Canzones, however, sometimes also have sacred texts, and in those cases they are called *Canzoni Spirituali* <note>Spiritual Canzones</note>. In these Canzonettes, the first and last lines are usually repeated, but the middle one is not.

->4. **ARIA** or **AIR.**<-

This is a pretty tune or melody which a person sings as if from their own head <note>implying a more natural, soloistic, or perhaps improvised style</note>. Among us [Germans], these are also German secular songs with beautiful, elegant texts. Such beautiful Arias and similar pieces are now called *Scherzi* <note>original: *Schertzi*, Italian for "jokes" or "playful trifles"</note> by the Italians.

---

### Chapter V.
# On those Songs which are Composed of Various
## Pieces: Such as the *Messanza*
### and *Quodlibet*.

**M**essanza or *Mistichanza* <note>original Latin/Italian: *Messanza seu Mistichanza*</note>: This is a *Quodlibet* <note>Latin for "whatever you please"</note> or a mixture of all sorts of herbs—a "mixed salad" <note>original Italian: *una salata de Mistichanza*</note>. It is otherwise generally called a *Quodlibet*. Here, specifically, from many and various motets, madrigals, and other German secular and even comical <note>original German: *possirlichen*</note> songs, a half or whole line of text is taken along with the melodies and notes set to them, and from many little pieces and patches, a whole "fur coat" is stitched and patched together, as it were.

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: 1. Etli</meta>

<summary>This page defines secular musical forms including the instrumental Canzone, the light Canzonette, and the Aria (or Scherzo). It also introduces the Quodlibet (Messanza), describing it as a humorous musical "patchwork" or "salad" made of fragments from other songs.</summary>
<keywords>Canzonette, Aria, Air, Canzoni Spirituali, Messanza, Mistichanza, Quodlibet, Motet, Madrigal, Giovanni Gabrieli, Scherzi, musical forms, Renaissance music</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical forms, transitioning from the definition of the Quodlibet to descriptions of street music, serenades, and dance songs.</meta>

There are, however, three types of these **Quodlibets** <term>Quodlibet: literally "what you please," a musical composition that playfully combines several well-known melodies or texts simultaneously</term>.
->three types.<-

1. Some have a distinct and complete text in every voice part. For example, one that I like very much is found where one voice sings *Lord, keep us* <note>original: "Erhalt vns HERR"</note>, the second sings *O God, from heaven look down* <note>original: "Ach Gott vom Himmel"</note>, the third sings *Our Father in Heaven* <note>original: "Vater vnser im Him"</note>, the fourth sings *We all believe* <note>original: "Wir gleuben"</note>, and the fifth sings *Through Adam's Fall* <note>original: "Durch Adams Fall"</note>, all carried through to the end. *By the author Johann Göldel.*
2. Some indeed have a distinct text in every voice, but it is quite mangled and fragmented, as in the Quodlibet by Nicolaus Zangius.
3. Some have the same text in all voices, but it is also incomplete and **abrupted** <note>original: *abrumpirt*, from the Latin for "broken off"</note>, and soon another text is snatched up to follow it. This can be seen in the Quodlibets of Melchior Franck, and in the two **Messanze** <note>a "mixture" or medley</note>, *Mirani* for 5 voices and *Nascela pena* for 6 voices.

---

# Chapter VI.
## Concerning Songs Used in Street Serenades and Masquerades: Such as the Giustiniani, Serenata, and Balletti.

### 1. GIUSTINIANI.

These are certain amorous songs (called "rough and light music" by some <note>original Latin: *rudis & levis Musica à quodam appellata*</note>) mostly for three voices, in the **Bergamasque** <note>the dialect of Bergamo, Italy</note> tongue, named after a noble courtesan from the city of Bergamo.

### 2. SERENATA.

This is an evening song for three or more voices, used when strolling through the streets in the evening to perform a **serenade** <term>Gassaten: from the German *Gasse* (alley), referring to street music</term>. At universities, this is called giving the young ladies a "serenade" or "courtly honor" <note>original: *Ständchen* or *Hoferecht*</note>. **Ritornellos** <note>short instrumental interludes</note> are played between the vocal sections. More will be said on this in Chapter 1 of the third part.

### 3. BALLI, or Balletti.

->These are of two types:<-

1. First, they are special songs which are sung during **round dances** <note>original: *Reyen*</note> and for dancing,

<summary>This page categorizes the three styles of the Quodlibet—a musical "medley"—and begins a new chapter on secular social music, including street serenades and dance-based vocal works.</summary>
<keywords>Quodlibet, Johann Göldel, Nicolaus Zangius, Melchior Franck, Giustiniani, Serenata, street music, Balletti, dance songs</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of dance-based musical forms, specifically focusing on the Balletto. It follows the previous page's discussion of the Giustiniani and the Serenata.</meta>

(for *Ballare* means *Saltare* <note>original Latin: "to leap"</note>, that is, to dance), of which kind some very lovely and charming **Ballets** <note>original: *Balleten*; refers here to the Balletto, a light, rhythmic vocal dance song</note> by **Giacomo Gastoldi** and **Thomas Morley** have been found published.

2. Of the other kind are **Bassi** <term>Basse danse: a stately court dance "low" to the ground</term> or **Ballets** which have no text. And when these same pieces are played for dancing with shawms or pipes, they are called **stampita** <term>Estampie: a medieval and Renaissance instrumental dance form</term>. In French, one calls it a *Ball*, which includes all kinds of dances in general, such as **Branles, Courantes, Voltas, Galliards**, etc. **Ballet**, however, refers to special dances made for masquerades and processions, which are played for the **Masquerade** <note>original: *Mascarada*</note>. These are arranged according to their specific **Inventions** <note>original: *Inventiones*; here meaning the creative concept or choreography of the performance</note>, and every **Ballet** usually has three parts:

1. The **Intrada** <note>Entrance</note>, when the persons in the masquerade appear for the entrance.
2. The **Figures**, which the masked persons form while standing, stepping, or exchanging places, and otherwise forming the shapes of letters in a ring, a wreath, a **Triangle**, square, hexagon, or other shapes, winding through one another; upon this the whole **Invention** and essence of the **Ballet** consists and is directed.
3. The **Retrajecte**, that is the retreat or exit, by which the **Invention** and the whole **Ballet** is ended and concluded. These are not used afterwards, but end together with the **Masquerade**. Yet they are not useless to use in music as before with instruments, like any other lovely song. Examples of these, alongside all kinds of other French dances and songs such as **Branles, Courantes**, and the like, are found sufficiently in my **Terpsichore** <note>Published by Michael Praetorius in 1612, this was a massive collection of over 500 dance tunes</note>, where more is said about this at length.

->*Example from Petrarch.*<-

### Balletto 6.

*From time to time, she becomes less harsh to me,* <note>original Italian: "Di tempo in tempo mi si fa men dura"</note>
*The angelic figure, and the sweet smile,* <note>original: "L’angelica figura, e’l dolce riso"</note>
*And the air of that beautiful face,* <note>original: "E l’aria del bel viso"</note>
*And of those graceful eyes, less obscure.* <note>original: "E de gli occhi leggiadri meno oscura"</note>
*What are these sighs doing with me now,* <note>original: "Che fanno meco homai questi sospiri"</note>
*Which were born of sorrow;* <note>original: "Che nascean di dolore"</note>
*And showed outwardly* <note>original: "E monstrauan di fore"</note>

### Chapter VII.

# Concerning those Songs / Which are sung by laborers and peasants: Namely:

->*Vinette, Giardiniero, and Villanelle.*<-

### 1. VINETTE.

Vinette or Vinate is a little song of a vinedresser or vineyard worker, such as those who work in the vineyards; for *Vinetto* means a vinedresser or vineyard worker. *Vinette*, however, refers to a common, low-quality table wine <note>original Latin: *vinum cibarium ignobile*</note>. Vinates are drinking songs, or if I should title them correctly, "swilling songs," which are neither rare nor uncommon among us in Germany. And I hold it to be true that there is no vanity or triviality in the world upon which some music has not been directed or found.

### 2. GIARDINIERO.

Gardiniero is a gardener’s song, such as those they sing during their work in the garden; for *Giardiniero* means a gardener, and *Giardino* a garden or orchard.

### 3. VILLANELLEN, Villages.

Villanelles take their name from *Villa*, which is a village, and *Villano*, a peasant: Likewise *Villanello*, the diminutive form <note>original: *diminutivum*</note>, which means as much as a "rustic fellow" <note>original Latin: *subrusticus*</note>;

<vocab>Vinette, Vinate, Vinedresser, Vineyard worker, Drinking songs, Gardener, Garden, Villanelles, Peasant, Rustic</vocab>

<summary>This page defines three types of rustic vocal music: the Vinette (vinedresser or drinking songs), the Giardiniero (gardener's songs), and the Villanelle (peasant songs), highlighting their origins in daily labor and country life.</summary>
<keywords>Vinette, Giardiniero, Villanelle, folk music, work songs, drinking songs, musical terminology, vineyard, garden, peasantry</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of vocal forms from the previous page, specifically focusing on the transition from rustic, texted songs of the common people to instrumental forms that lack text altogether. It introduces Chapter VIII, which begins a technical classification of independent instrumental music.</meta>

*Thence the Villanella*, a little peasant song, which the peasants and common craftsmen sing. Therefore, composers also often diligently place four or five **fifths** <note>Consecutive fifths were strictly forbidden in formal "learned" music, but used here to mimic the raw, unpolished sound of folk singing.</note> one after another, contrary to the **rules of musicians**. Just as the peasants do not sing according to the rules of art, but rather as it occurs to them: and it is a rustic *music* for a rustic subject. Some such songs are also called *Villotta* or *Vilatella*, by which a small village is otherwise understood. Otherwise, in France, the peasant dances—which are *invented* by the peasants themselves and played with shawms and also fiddles, where often two, three, or more persons are used for a single voice—are called by the name *Villages*. And thus far concerning the songs that have texts: now follow those without texts.

---

### Chapter VIII.
## Concerning Preludes in their own right: such as Phantasias,
## Fugues, Symphonies, and Sonatas.

### 1. *Fantasia, or more correctly Phantasia: Capriccio.*

Capriccio or a sudden Phantasia: When someone takes it upon himself to *treat* a *fugue* according to his own *pleasure* and liking, but does not *linger* long within it, but soon falls into another *fugue* as it comes to his mind. For because—just as in proper fugues—no text needs to be placed beneath it, one is also not bound to the words; whether one makes much or little, one may *digress*, *add*, *subtract*, and turn and twist it as they wish. And in such Phantasias and Capriccios, one can just as well demonstrate their art and *skill*: since he may use everything that is *permissible* in music, including the binding of *dissonances*, *proportions*, etc., without any hesitation. Provided, however, that he does not exceed the *mode* and the *melody* <note>original: *Ariam*</note> too greatly, but remains within the *boundaries*: of which more shall be said in another place, God willing.

### 2. *Fugue: Ricercar.*

"Fugues are nothing other than," as Abbot Lord Johannes Nucius <note>Johannes Nucius (c. 1556–1620) was a German composer and music theorist whose work *Musica Poetica* heavily influenced Praetorius.</note> says, "the frequent echoing of the same theme through distinct places [voice parts], succeeding each other with the intervention of rests." <note>original Latin: *Fugæ nihil aliud sunt... quàm ejusdem thematis per distinctos locos crebræ resultationes Pausarum interventu sibi succedentes.*</note> They are named, however, from "chasing" <note>original Latin: *à fugando*</note>, because one voice chases the other, bringing forth the same melody. By the Italians they are called *Ricercari*: for *Ricercare* is the same as to investigate, to seek, to search out, to explore with diligence and...

<sig>C iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: search (nach-)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the description of rustic vocal forms like the Villanella and begins a new chapter on instrumental music, defining the Phantasia, Capriccio, and Fugue. It highlights the freedom of instrumental composition compared to the strict rules of vocal settings.</summary>
<keywords>Villanella, Phantasia, Capriccio, Fugue, Ricercar, Johannes Nucius, consecutive fifths, instrumental music, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical classification of instrumental music from Chapter VIII, specifically finishing the section on Fugues and moving into definitions for Sinfonias and Sonatas, before beginning Chapter IX on the Intrada.</meta>

...search; because in the treatment of a good **fugue**, one must search every corner with special diligence and reflection to see how it might be joined, woven, and doubled in many various ways—whether through **direct and indirect** <note>original: "per Directum & indirectum," referring to melodic motion in the same direction or in mirror/contrary motion.</note> or **contrary motion**—and how it can be brought together in an orderly, artistic, and graceful manner and led through to the very end. For a musician’s genius is to be judged most of all by this form: whether they know how to extract suitable fugues according to the specific nature of the **Musical Modes** <note>The precursor to modern keys (Major/Minor); each Mode was thought to have a specific emotional character.</note>, and how to rightly join those extracted themes with a good and laudable coherence.

### 3. Sinfonia: or more correctly, Symphonia.

**Sinfonia**, as indicated above, is understood by the Italians to mean a fine, complete **harmony** <note>original: "Concentus"</note> composed in the manner of a **Toccata**, **Pavan**, **Galliard**, or other similar harmony for 4, 5, 6, or more voices, to be used on instruments alone without any vocal parts. This type of music is sometimes used by them at the beginning—just like a **prelude** <note>original: "Præambulum"</note> on the organ—and often also in the middle of concerted songs performed by choirs. More on this, as well as what is to be understood by the terms **Ripieni** <note>The full ensemble or "filling" parts.</note>, **Ritornello** <note>A recurring instrumental interlude.</note>, etc., will be found in the 8th Chapter of the third part of this **Third Volume**.

### 4. SONATA, Sonada.

**Sonata**, from *sonando* <note>original: "à sonando," meaning "by sounding" or "playing an instrument."</note>, is so named because it is not performed with human voices but with instruments alone, like the **Canzonas**. Very beautiful examples of this type are to be found in the **Canzonas** and **Symphonies** of **Giovanni Gabrieli** <note>The influential Venetian composer known for his grand instrumental works.</note> and other authors. In my opinion, however, the difference is this: that **Sonatas** are composed in a very grave and magnificent style, in the manner of **Motets**; whereas **Canzonas** pass through with many "black notes" <note>In the notation of the time, "black notes" (minims and semiminims) indicated faster tempos and shorter durations.</note> in a fresh, joyful, and quick manner. 

Furthermore, that the word **Sonata** or **Sonada** is also used for the trumpet calls played for dining and dancing will be discussed further in the 8th Chapter of the third part of this **Third Volume**.

---

## Concerning Preludes for the Dance, such as Intradas.
->I N T R A D A.<-

**Intrata** (commonly **Intrada**) or **Entrata**, that is, an entrance or an approach: from entering <note>The page ends mid-sentence as it begins the etymology of "Intrada" from the Latin *intrare*.</note>...

## The X. Chapter.
# Concerning Preludes to Motets or Madrigals:
## namely, the Toccatas.
### TOCCATA.

A **Toccata** is like a preamble or prelude which an organist—when first touching the organ or **harpsichord** <note>original: "Clavicymbalum"</note> before beginning a motet or a fugue—previously improvises from their own head, with simple chords and **colorations** <term>colorations: rapid melodic ornaments, scales, and runs used to "color" or embellish a musical line</term>, etc. One person has this style, another has that; it is unnecessary to treat the matter extensively here, and I also consider myself too humble to prescribe anything to one or the other in this regard.

And although I have collected many magnificent Toccatas by the most prominent Italian and Netherlandish organists, and have even composed several myself according to my own simple and modest abilities with the intention of publishing them in print: yet for certain reasons, I have not wished to put this into effect at this time.

In my opinion, however, they are called by the name **Toccata** by the Italians because *toccare* means to touch or to strike, and *toccato* means "touched." Thus the Italians also say: *Toccate un poco* <note>original: "Toccate un poco," meaning "touch [it] a little."</note>; that is, strike the instrument or touch the keys a little. Therefore, a Toccata can very well be called a "running-through" or "grasping" of the keyboard.

---

## The XI. Chapter.
# Concerning Dances, which are moved to certain paces and steps:
## Namely: Paduana, Passamezzo,
## and Galliard.
### I. PADUANA.

The **Paduana** <note>commonly known in English as the Pavane, a slow, processional dance</note>, in Italian *Padoana*, is said to take its name from the city of Padua in Italy, where, as some believe, this type of music was first invented:

<meta>catchword: Galli</meta>

<keywords>Toccata, keyboard music, improvisation, organ, harpsichord, colorations, dance forms, Paduana, Pavane, Padua</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of instrumental forms, specifically moving into the distinct styles of courtly dances. The previous page concluded the definition of the Toccata; this page begins the descriptions of the Pavan, the Passamezzo, and the Galliard.</meta>

The French <note>original: "Galli"</note> and the English <note>original: "Angli"</note> call it the **Pavan** <note>original: "Pavana"</note>. The **Pavan** is a type of steady and solemn music. Indeed, when Pavans are performed in a **consort** <term>consort: a small ensemble of instruments, often of the same family (like viols) or a "broken consort" of mixed instruments</term> of all kinds of lovely instruments, they produce an extraordinary, graceful, and also magnificent harmony. Otherwise, it is primarily arranged and intended for solemn dances; in England, it is used for dancing at all times. It usually consists of three repetitions, each of which must have 8, 12, or 16 **measures** <note>original: "tact"</note>, but no fewer, because of the four steps or paces that must be observed therein. These pieces contain nothing special in the way of **fugues** <note>original: "Fugen," referring here to imitative counterpoint</note>; occasionally they may begin a fugal theme slightly, but they soon leave it and come to a close. The dance itself, which is called *La pavane* or also the "Spanish Pavan" <note>original: "La pavam de Espaigné"</note>, originally comes from Spain; for one sees that it must be formed with particularly slow, elegant steps and Spanish solemnity.

### 2. Passamezzo.

**Passamezzo** <note>original: "Passamezo"</note> comes from "passing" or "crossing over" <note>original: "á passando, transeundo"</note>, because one steps very softly and gradually when dancing to it. For to the Italians, *passare* <note>The OCR text "passaz" is likely a typo for the Latin or Italian verb for "to pass."</note> means to pass, to go through, or to depart; and a "passing" <note>original: "passamento"</note> is the same as a transition. 
And just as a **Galliard** has five steps and is therefore called a **cinquepace** <term>cinquepace: from the French "cinq pas," meaning five steps; a core movement of the Galliard dance</term>, a **Passamezzo** has barely half as many steps as a Galliard, as if you were to say "half a pace" <note>original: "mezo passo"</note>.

### 3. GALLIARDA.

**Galliard** <note>original: "Galliarda"</note>; in Italian, *Gagliarda*. It signifies nimbleness, strength, and vigor <note>original Latin: "strenuitas, fortitudo, vigor"</note>. In the French language, it is *Gaillard* or *Gaillardise*, and it means a certain direct speed. Thus, if I wish to describe a well-proportioned, upright man, I say of him: "he is a very robust man" <note>original French: "c'est un homme bien gaillard"</note>. 
Therefore, because the **Galliard** must be performed with more uprightness and good physical disposition than other dances, it has undoubtedly received its name from this.
The Galliard is measured in an **unequal and trochaic meter** <term>trochaic meter: a rhythmic pattern consisting of a long or stressed syllable followed by a short or unstressed one, giving the dance its triple-meter feel</term>. Like the Pavan, it has three repetitions, each of which must have 4, 8, or 12 measures, and should have neither more nor less. The Italians commonly call them **Saltarelli** <note>Italian: "little hops"</note>, and sometimes amorous texts are set to them, which they sing themselves during masquerades and dance to at the same time, even if no instruments are present.

The <note>The catchword "Das" at the bottom of the page indicates the first word of the following page.</note>

<vocab>Pavana, Passamezo, Galliarda, Music, Tanz, Engelland, Hispanien, Harmoniam, Consort, Cincquebaß, Gaillardise, Saltarelli, Mascaraden</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the characteristics and origins of three major Renaissance dance forms: the solemn Pavan, the transition-based Passamezzo, and the vigorous Galliard. It highlights their structural requirements, such as measure counts and step patterns, and notes regional variations from England, Spain, Italy, and France.</summary>
<keywords>Pavan, Passamezzo, Galliard, dance forms, Renaissance music, music theory, Saltarelli, consort music, choreography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of dance forms from the late Renaissance and early Baroque periods. Having previously discussed the Pavan and the Passamezzo, the author now transitions into the "Bransle," "Courante," "Volte," and "Allemande," focusing on their etymologies and physical characteristics.</meta>

# Chapter 12.

# Concerning those dances which are not bound to specific steps and paces: such as the Bransle, Courante, Volte, Allemande, and Mascharada.

## 1. BRANSLE.

The **Bransle** <term>Bransle: a type of French choral dance where participants move sideways in a circle or line</term> is a French dance. The name perhaps comes from the French word *bransler* <note>original: "Bransler"</note>, which means to shake, to stir, to move, or to shift. However, the movement and agitation in these dances is not as intense as it is in the **Galliards** and **Courantes**; rather, it is quite gentle, performed only with the knees and without jumps. As for the distinctions observed between the various Bransles and other French dances, a more detailed account has been given in the preface to my work, *Terpsichore Musarum Aoniarum* <note>original: "Terpsichore Musarum Aoniarum," a 1612 collection of over 300 dances by Michael Praetorius</note>.

## 2. COURANTES.

**Courantes** <note>original: "Couranten"</note> take their name from "running" <note>original: "à Currendo or Cursitante"</note>, because they are mostly used in dancing with certain measured jumps up and down, much like running.

## 3. VOLTE.

The **Volte** <note>original: "Volte"</note> comes from the word for "turning" <note>original: "à Vertendo"</note>. For the Italian word *Volta* <note>original: "Volta"</note> is equivalent to the Italian word for a "turn" <note>original: "versura"</note>, such as that made by an orator; and in French, it means to turn around <note>original: "vmbkehren"</note>. It refers to turning or rotating <note>original: "Voltare; Vertere, versare"</note>, because in this dance, the partners swing and turn one another from one side to the other. The **Volte** must have only half as many measures in its repetition as the **Courante**.

## 4. ALLEMANDE.

**Allemande** <note>original: "Alemande"</note> means as much as a "German little song" or "little dance," for *Alemagna* means Germany, and an *Allemand* <note>original: "un Alemand"</note> is a German. However, this dance is not so nimble or quick, but somewhat more serious and slower <note>original: "schwermüthiger vnd langsamer"</note> than the **Galliard**, especially since no extraordinary movements <note>original: "extraordinariæ motiones"</note> are used in it. They sometimes have two and sometimes three repetitions, each of which is mostly set to four **measures** <term>tact: the basic unit of time or beat in music of this period</term>. And although in the **Pavans** there must also normally be only four measures or paces, they are nevertheless in **double proportion** <note>original: "dupla proportione," a 2-to-1 rhythmic ratio</note> compared to the Allemande. For instance, if a Pavan has 16 measures or **whole notes** <note>original: "semibreves"</note> in a repetition, the Allemande has only half as many—namely, 8 measures in **half notes** <note>original: "notis Minimis"</note>.

D
<meta>catchword: 5. MA- (referring to Mascharada)</meta>

<summary>Michael Praetorius defines the characteristics, etymologies, and rhythmic structures of the Bransle, Courante, Volte, and Allemande. He distinguishes these dances based on their physical movements—such as the gentle knee-bending of the Bransle versus the vigorous turning of the Volte—and their relative musical tempos.</summary>
<keywords>Bransle, Courante, Volte, Allemande, dance history, Michael Praetorius, musical terminology, Terpsichore, Renaissance dance, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 12 of the treatise on dance forms. Following the previous discussions on the Bransle, Courante, Volte, and Allemande, the author defines the Masquerade (Mascherada) and concludes this section of the work with a reference to his extensive dance collection, Terpsichore.</meta>

### 5. MASQUERADE.

**Mascharato** <note>original: "Mascharato"</note>, which the Italians call a masked person <note>original: "Personata"</note>; **Maschara** <note>original: "Maschara"</note>, that is, a persona or character; and **Maschera** <note>original: "Maschera"</note>, or in Latin *Larva* <note>original: "Larva"</note>, meaning a masked or disguised face—this is known in German as a **Masquerade** <note>original: "Mummerey"</note>. It occurs when several individuals disguise themselves with masks and costumes and thus appear at banquets <note>original: "Pancketen"</note> and the formal refreshments <term>Collationibus: light meals or social gatherings for the nobility</term> of noble persons, accompanied by music. Consequently, the specific songs and music performed for this are also called a **Mascherada** or **Maschara**. Although these songs have their own unique melodies and are specific dances, they are always performed in masquerade clothing and in "masks" <note>original: "Maschen"</note> or, as they are called, *Larvae* <note>original: "Larven"</note>. This form belongs to the **Ballets**, which were discussed above.

In addition to these five types of dances, many other different names are assigned and given to them, a more detailed account of which can be found in my work *Terpsichore of the Aonian Muses* <note>original: "Musarum Aoniarum Terpsichore," a collection of over 300 dances published by Praetorius in 1612</note>.

# On LIGATURE, or the binding together of notes.
## Chapter I.
### Concerning certain ligatures.

<image-desc>Ornate decorative initial V containing a figure in a garden landscape.</image-desc>The rule of the ancients states: *the first note lacking a tail is long, and if the second hangs, it is long* <note>original: "prima carens caudâ, longa est, pendente secunda"</note>. I do not see why this must be observed, but I judge that in a <term>Ligature</term> <note>a grouping of musical notes written as a single connected shape, common in early notation</note>, both descending and ascending notes should always be regarded as <term>Breves</term> <note>a "short" note, though in modern terms it is equivalent to a double whole note</note> without distinction. This is especially true since that particular ligature <image-desc>musical notation: a descending ligature of two square notes</image-desc> has now almost gone out of style and is very rarely found in the printing shops.

I agree with the opinion of Lippius, Hasler, and others <note>Johannes Lippius and Hans Leo Hassler were influential music theorists and composers of the late 16th and early 17th centuries</note> that all intricate ligatures should be removed, except for this single one for <term>Semibreves</term> <note>the equivalent of a whole note</note> <image-desc>musical notation: a ligature of two diamond-shaped notes</image-desc>; and in their place, this little slur mark <image-desc>musical notation: a slur or tie mark</image-desc> should be used. Therefore, not only for <term>Minims</term> <note>half notes</note>, <term>Semiminims</term> <note>quarter notes</note>, and <term>Fusae</term> <note>eighth notes</note>, but also for Breves and Semibreves—which I see is already being done by our countrymen as well as the Italians—I have placed that slur mark in place of a ligature. This allows, among other things, the choral melody to be better observed. For while the ancients would have noted separate diamond-shaped notes <image-desc>musical notation: two separate diamond-shaped notes</image-desc> as tied using this sign <image-desc>musical notation: a ligature of two diamond-shaped notes</image-desc>, I think that in this current time, when we use a faster movement of notes, they are not inconveniently marked with a slur.

---

# How certain notes in Triple time and Sesquialtera are to be drawn for better and greater convenience in singing.
## Chapter II.
### Concerning the blackening and perfection of notes in Triple time and Sesquialtera.

I have decided it is worth the effort to blacken <note>the practice of "coloration," where a white note is filled in with ink to indicate a change in its rhythmic value</note> the <term>Breve</term> <image-desc>musical notation: a solid black square note (colored breve)</image-desc> in <term>Tripla</term> <note>triple meter</note>, and the <term>Semibreve</term> <image-desc>musical notation: a solid black diamond-shaped note (colored semibreve)</image-desc> in <term>Sesquialtera</term> <note>a rhythmic proportion where three notes are played in the time of two</note> whenever they are sung against the beat. This is so that the pulse can be more easily observed and distinguished in both Tripla and Sesquialtera.

Furthermore, I must also advise that "perfection" <note>a rule in older music where a note is considered "perfect" (three beats) rather than "imperfect" (two beats) based on its context</note> has been observed by many even in sesquialtera. Thus, for a Semibreve <image-desc>musical notation: a white diamond-shaped note (semibreve)</image-desc>, when it is "perfected" by a following Semibreve <image-desc>musical notation: a white diamond-shaped note (semibreve)</image-desc>, they have added no dot, but instead used blackness where necessity required—

<sig>D 3</sig>
<meta>The catchword "qui-" indicates the first syllable of the next page, likely "quidem" or "quid."</meta>

<summary>On this page, the author argues for simplifying musical notation by replacing old, complex ligatures with modern slur marks and explains the use of "blackening" notes to clarify rhythms in triple meters.</summary>
<keywords>ligature, music theory, notation, Breve, Semibreve, slur, coloration, Triple time, Sesquialtera, Lippius, Hasler</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on simplifying musical notation, moving from the use of slurs in place of ligatures to the debate over "perfection" in triple meter and the specific uses of accidentals (flats, naturals, and sharps).</meta>

...would require, they have removed perfection. But most modern writers, among whom Seth Calvisius <note>Sethus Calvisius (1556–1615) was a highly influential German music theorist, astronomer, and cantor at the Thomasschule in Leipzig.</note> also adds his support, do not observe this. Instead, they always add a dot to the semibreve <note>original: "semibrevem"</note> <image-desc>diamond-shaped semibreve note</image-desc>, even if it is "perfect" <note>In older "perfect" notation, a note could be worth three beats without a dot; modern notation requires a dot to make a note worth three beats.</note> in *Sesquialtera* <note>a musical proportion where three notes are played in the time of two</note>, and to the breve <image-desc>square-shaped breve note</image-desc> in *tripla* <note>triple time</note>. By this reasoning, they abolish "perfection" as something not strictly necessary. Indeed, it is done in vain through many steps what can be accomplished through fewer.

Therefore, although I observed this "perfection" in the earlier parts of my *German Muses* <note>likely referring to his *Musae Sioniae*, a massive collection of Lutheran chorale arrangements</note>, I have nevertheless abolished it in my later Latin and German works by correcting it with the dot.

---

### Chapter VIII.
### *On the true use of the round b, the square b, and the latticed sharp.*
## How and where the ♭, ♮, and ♯ are to be correctly used.

<margin>♮</margin>
The square <note>original: "Quadratum," referring to the natural sign (♮), which was originally a "square" b</note> has its own proper place in a song, either on the B-key in a transposed system, or on the F-key in a regular one: namely, when a fifth <note>original: "quinta"</note> is placed below it in the harmony.

This is done to soften the "mi" and "fa" in the *Diapente* <note>the interval of a perfect fifth</note>; that is, when a diminished fifth is changed into a true fifth by adding a minor semitone <note>the smallest interval in the scale</note> which was previously lacking.

When, however, the latticed sharp <note>original: "♯ cancellatum"</note> is marked, it is not necessary for the sound to always be raised or lowered by a full minor semitone. For it is only added to a minor third or even a minor sixth so that they may be turned into major ones. Either of these intervals, while passing into a major third or sixth by gradually raising the pitch, sounds consonant everywhere, whether it has fully reached its destination or not. This does not happen if the square natural <note>♮</note> is used. For when that is assigned, it is necessary that the note be raised by a full minor semitone. In this way, the entire song is changed into the *Chromatic genus* <note>a style of music using sharps and flats to create more expressive, "colored" harmonies</note>, and a transposed system becomes a regular one, or vice-versa, a regular system becomes "fictitious" <note>original: "fictum," referring to *musica ficta*, where notes are altered outside the standard scale</note>.

<meta>catchword: Certain</meta>

<summary>Michael Praetorius explains his shift toward using dots to indicate triple meter (rather than the old system of 'perfection') and distinguishes between the structural use of the natural sign and the expressive use of the sharp.</summary>
<keywords>notation, perfection, Sethus Calvisius, semibreve, breve, sesquialtera, accidentals, natural sign, sharp sign, chromaticism, musica ficta</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on musical notation, moving from the use of dots and perfections to the specific application of accidentals—sharps, flats, and naturals—and the debate over whether they should be explicitly written in the score or left to the performer's knowledge of musical rules.</meta>

Certain composers <note>original: "Melopœi," referring to those who compose melodies or music</note>, while they do not understand this distinction, have nevertheless dared to do something regarding it: they think that in the B-key <note>original: "Clave b"</note> that "square natural" <note>original: "♮ quadratum"; the ancestor of the modern natural sign ♮</note> should only and always be written if a chromatic sign is to be placed there, regardless of which harmony is set against it. In this matter, they seem to me to be childishly hallucinating. *Read Seth Calvisius, Exercise 3, page 139.* <note>Sethus Calvisius (1556–1615) was a leading German music theorist of the era.</note>

In addition to this, it must also be diligently observed here that, although these signs—the sharp <note>original: "♯ cancellatum," meaning "latticed sharp"</note> and the flat <note>original: "b rotundum," meaning "round b"</note>—were not marked at all (or only very rarely) by the most prominent and excellent old composers in places where the song otherwise required and demanded one of these signs by its own nature <note>original: "per se"</note>; and therefore even now in our time some composers persist in the same manner and believe it is not at all necessary. They assume that every cantor and musician knows for himself that when a tritone <note>original: "Tritonus"; an augmented fourth, often called "the devil in music"</note> or a diminished fifth <note>original: "Semidiapente"</note> occurs, he must sing and use a proper perfect fourth <note>original: "Diatessaron"</note> or perfect fifth <note>original: "Diapente"</note>, and use the semitone <note>original: "Semitonium"</note> at a formal cadence <note>original: "Clausula formali"</note>. *Likewise, when a single note ascends above 'la', one must always sing 'fa', etc.* <note>original: "unicâ notulâ ascendente super la, semper canendum esse fa"; this was a standard rule of "musica ficta" where a flat was added to avoid an awkward interval when a melody went one step above the highest note of a hexachord.</note>

For this reason, *Philippe de Monte* <note>Philippe de Monte (1521–1603) was a famous Flemish composer of the Renaissance.</note> and other highly distinguished musicians and composers would not allow their students to mark the flat sign in such cases. I, however, am entirely of the opinion that it is not only very useful and convenient, but also highly necessary—not just for the singers, so that they are not interrupted <note>original: "interturbiret"</note> in their singing, but also for the simple town instrumentalists and organists who do not understand music theory <note>original: "Musicam"</note>, much less how to sing correctly, and therefore (as I have often seen and experienced myself) do not know how to make any distinction in this matter. Not to mention that the compositions of certain composers are designed such that these two chromatic signs are used in some places, but in others they may not be observed. Therefore, the best precaution <note>original: "Caution"</note> would be if composers wrote them clearly in all places where it is necessary; then there would be no need for second-guessing or doubt.

<sig>Thus</sig>

<summary>The author argues that accidentals (sharps, flats, and naturals) should be explicitly written in musical scores to assist less-educated performers, criticizing the "old school" approach that relied on unwritten rules of harmony.</summary>
<keywords>accidentals, sharps, flats, naturals, music theory, music education, town musicians, organists, Philippe de Monte, Seth Calvisius, musica ficta</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on musical notation, specifically how singers and instrumentalists interpret intervals that are not explicitly marked with sharps or flats, focusing on the correction of the "forbidden" tritone and diminished fifth.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

Although in all these examples every cantor <note>original: "Cantor"; the lead singer or choirmaster responsible for the music in a school or church</note>, and even a schoolboy, must know that when such and similar intervals <note>original: "Intervalla"</note> occur in a song, he must necessarily take the perfect fourth <note>original: "Quart"</note> in place of the tritone <note>original: "Tritono"</note>, and the proper perfect fifth <note>original: "Quint"</note> in place of the diminished fifth <note>original: "Semidiapente"</note>, if he wishes to achieve <note>original: "assequirn"</note> it with the voice and perform it correctly—since no tritone or diminished fifth can be sung naturally—nevertheless, some organists <note>original: "Organisten"</note> and other instrumental musicians <note>original: "Instrumentales Musici"</note>, who often cannot correctly solmizate <note>term: "solmifiren" (solmization); the practice of singing musical scales using syllables like ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la to identify intervals</note> or sing, do not know how to distinguish one from the other. This has also been pointed out in my *Uranio Chorodia* <note>A significant collection of polyphonic hymns published by Michael Praetorius in 1613</note>.

Because schoolboys also mostly sing the third note in the preceding example under number 5 as a major third <note>original: "Ditonum oder tertiam majorem"</note> when descending—just as if a flat <note>original: "b"; the sign for a flat</note> were marked there—rather than singing a minor third <note>original: "minorem oder den Semiditonum"</note>; even though the composer did not write it that way.

| <note>original: "Semidiapente"; a dissonant, "narrow" fifth</note> | <note>original: "Tritonus"; an augmented fourth, known as the "devil in music"</note> | <note>original: "Semidiapente"</note> |
| **3. Fifth** | **2. Fourth** | **3. Fifth** |

->Formal Cadence<-
<note>original: "clausula formalis"; a standard melodic formula used to conclude a musical phrase</note>

[Musical notation of a cadence]

<summary>Praetorius explains that while trained singers naturally correct dissonant intervals like tritones, instrumentalists often fail to do so, necessitating clearer notation. He also notes common errors where students add unwritten flats to create major thirds where minor ones were intended.</summary>
<keywords>tritone, diminished fifth, solmization, Uranio Chorodia, major third, minor third, musical education, musica ficta</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on musical notation and performance practice, specifically concerning the use of accidentals like flats and sharps, and begins a new chapter on how to properly mark rests in complex polyphonic music to prevent singer error.</meta>

<page-num>33.</page-num>

...or intended, then one must necessarily mark the *b rotundum* <note>original: "b rotundum"; the "round b" or flat sign (♭), used to indicate the note B-flat</note> to distinguish it and provide certain instruction. But where the flat sign is not present, it should not be sung that way; instead, the minor third <note>original: "tertia minor"</note> should be maintained. And it is unnecessary to draw the sharp <note>original: "Diesin ♯"; the sharp sign</note> there.

However, the rule <note>original: "Regula"</note> that "a sharp requires the following note to ascend" <note>original: "quod Diesis sequentem Notulam ascendentem requirat"</note> cannot always be observed in concerts with many voices and choirs. Nevertheless, it does no harm if a composer takes care that in those parts sung by the human voice <note>original: "humana voce"</note>, he uses the following note after the semitone <note>original: "Semitonio"</note> in a descending manner very little and rarely. This is because it sounds somewhat strange and is more difficult to intone while singing. Although such things can now be found written with special diligence in the works of Sigismondo d'India, the Prince of Venosa <note>Carlo Gesualdo (1566–1613), a composer famous for his expressive, chromatic music</note>, and other madrigals <note>original: "Madrigalien"; a secular vocal music composition popular in the Renaissance and early Baroque</note> of our current time, as well as in the works of Luca Marenzio and Giovanni Gabrieli. In those parts intended for instruments <note>original: "Instrumentis"</note>, however, there are no concerns at all, and it can be used very well and appropriately, even in songs <note>original: "Cantionibus"</note> with few voices.

---

### Chapter IV.

->*Concerning the numbers that are found placed beneath rests.*<-

## Regarding the numbers and digits that are found marked next to or under the rests.

Agostino Agazzari <note>An influential Italian composer and music theorist (1578–1640)</note> mentions in a preface <note>original: "Præfation"</note> that the primary vice <note>original: "vitium"</note> of all cantors and singers is that they do not pronounce the text well or clearly, and they are reluctant to count the rests <note>original: "Pausen"</note>.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'D' featuring floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
Accordingly, because in concerts for multiple choirs <note>original: "Concerten per Choros"</note> there are often very many rests for certain choirs—so many that a musician cannot pay sufficiently close attention and sometimes forgets or loses his place because of the frequency and variation of such rests, and also because he listens diligently to the other voices and enjoys himself <note>original: "recreiret"; literally 'recreates' or refreshes himself by listening to the other performers</note> thereby—I have devised this remedy and found it highly necessary to place the count of the rests above or below the notes. This is especially useful when the measurements of sounds are equal <note>original: "Mensurationes sonorum æquales"</note>, <term>𝄴</term> <term>𝄼</term> <note>These symbols represent different forms of common time or cut time</note>, and unequal...

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>The catchword "als" (as) at the bottom indicates the first word of the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the practical application of sharps and flats in vocal music versus instrumental music and introduces a system for numbering long rests to help singers stay in time during multi-choir performances.</summary>
<keywords>accidentals, vocal performance, Sigismondo d'India, Carlo Gesualdo, musical rests, Agostino Agazzari, mensuration, choir management</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on musical notation, specifically addressing the confusion that arises when different meters and proportions are used interchangeably, and introduces the use of tactile markers—precursors to modern bar lines—to help performers stay in time.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

...as 3 and 3/2 <note>proportional signs indicating different speeds of triple meter</note> often mix and alternate with one another; where one and the same rest <note>original: "una & eadem pausa"</note> counts for four beats in equal measure <note>original: "Aequali mensura"</note>, but in unequal triple measure <note>original: "Inaequali Tripla"</note> counts for only 2 beats. And therefore—as anyone can find for themselves, and as I have learned through experience <note>original: "experientiâ edoctus"</note>, not without some frustration—confusions can easily be caused. This disaster can, however, be prevented to some extent by the following method.

---

### The Fifth Chapter.

*Concerning the little lines <note>original: "De Virgulis"</note> necessary for singing, and for discerning the beat <note>original: "Tactum"</note> in quavers <note>original: "Fusis"</note> and triple time <note>original: "Tripla"</note>.*

## Of the little lines and strokes <note>original: "Virgulis vnd Strichlin"</note> placed below and in the middle between the notes.

1. Since in many songs, but especially in instrumental pieces <note>original: "Symphonien"</note> without text, many quavers <note>original: "Fusen"; eighth notes</note> are placed one after another, and thus (especially in proportional rhythms) errors regarding the beat <note>the "tactus," or the steady pulse used by musicians to keep time</note> can easily occur at first glance <note>original: "primo intuitu"</note>: I judge it necessary that small strokes or little lines <note>original: "Virgulæ"</note> (such as those found in my *Terpsichore* <note>a famous collection of dance music published by Praetorius in 1612</note>) be placed between each beat. This allows one to better follow the beat in the heat of performance, and if one loses their place, they can more easily find the beat again. It is especially highly necessary in triple time and the "three-against-two" proportion <note>original: "Tripla vnd Sesquialtera"</note> that one distinguishes at least the first and second beats at the very beginning with a little stroke. This is because, up until now, most musicians have used the signs for triple time and sesquialtera quite indiscriminately <note>original: "indiscretè"</note> (as discussed in the previous third chapter), so that one cannot distinguish one from the other. Thus, one should immediately be able to see and grasp at the start whether it is a Greater Unequal Beat <note>original: "tactus Inaequalis Major"</note> of whole notes <note>original: "Semibrevium"</note>, or a Lesser <note>original: "Minor"</note> beat of half notes <note>original: "Minimarum"</note>.

And although I have since found in some Italian authors that they use dots between the notes to distinguish the beats, I cannot yet decide for myself which of these two is more convenient to use. For dots are often mistaken for actual musical dots belonging to the notes <note>i.e., dots of prolongation which increase a note's value</note>; likewise, little strokes placed down by the text might cause no small amount of hindrance.

->As may be seen here.<-

<summary>Michael Praetorius discusses the practical difficulties of maintaining a steady beat in complex rhythms and proposes the use of "virgules" or small strokes (early bar lines) to mark individual beats, especially in triple meters and instrumental music.</summary>
<keywords>tactus, bar lines, triple time, sesquialtera, Terpsichore, music theory, rhythmic notation, proportions, Michael Praetorius</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical notation, moving from the use of small "tactus" marks for rhythm to larger strokes used for structural navigation and flexibility during church performances. It concludes the fifth chapter and begins the sixth, which focuses on identifying musical modes.</meta>

<page-num>35.</page-num>

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation in white mensural style. The notes are diamond-shaped (rhomboids). The first system begins with a time signature of '3' and contains vertical bar lines of varying lengths interspersed throughout the notes. The second system begins with a 'C' (common time) signature and similar vertical strokes.</image-desc>

Because I have finally found that the little strokes cause less confusion than the dots: I have followed the use of little strokes in all my subsequent works, just as I previously chose to use them in my *Terpsichore* <note>A famous 1612 collection of dance music by Praetorius</note>.

2. I have also placed long strokes in the middle of concerted songs in several places, particularly where a verse or stanza of a Psalm or hymn comes to an end. This is not done so that the <term>final</term> <note>the concluding note of a musical section</note> must always be held there; but rather: 1. If that song is performed in church before or after the sermon and might drag on too long (as a musician can easily overstep in this regard), one can stop in a hurry where and when one wishes, and thus conclude with the <term>final</term>.

2. So that one can also leave out, <term>at will</term> <note>original: "pro libitu"</note>, anything contained between two strokes, and in its place have those same verses sung <term>in the manner of a chorale</term> <note>original: "Choraliter"; meaning a simple, congregational hymn style</note> with the people in the church, or handle and arrange it however one wishes.

3. If perhaps <term>confusions</term> <note>original: "Confusiones"; instances where the ensemble loses their place or rhythm</note> should occur—as can easily happen even in the best and very well-appointed ensembles <note>original: "Capellen"</note> among the most reliable and best musicians—one might then recover at such a stroke, hold still there, thus stop the <term>confusion</term>, and everyone may know how to start again correctly with their choir after the following stroke.

---

## The VI. Chapter.
->*Concerning the synoptic knowledge of the Modes.* <note>original: "De synopticâ Modorum cognitione"; "synoptic" refers to a comprehensive overview or table</note><-

# How one can very easily recognize, as if in a table,
### what Mode or Tone each song is in.

I would have added a few things about the Modes—which perhaps are not found in many people's books—but because an opportunity to speak of these will present itself in the Fourth Volume, God willing, and since many most illustrious men in the Italian and Latin languages, among whom is also Sethus Calvisius <note>A significant German music theorist and astronomer (1556–1615)</note>, have most clearly handed down the Doctrine of the Modes <note>original: "Doctrinam Modorum"</note> and why they begin from the pitch C, I refer the musical reader there.

For the simple ones <note>The "unlearned" or less-experienced musicians</note>, however, I want to place a <term>table</term> <note>original: "Tabellam"</note> here, from which they can very easily recognize and know in the <term>Bass</term> and <term>Soprano</term> <note>original: "Discant"</note>, and thereafter also in the <term>Alto</term> and <term>Tenor</term> (since the Alto is in every way identical to the Bass, and the Tenor to the Soprano, except that they stand an <term>octave</term> apart), <term>to which mode or tone</term> <note>original: "ad quem Modum vel Tonum"</note> every song—whether <term>hard</term> and <term>regular</term> <note>original: "durus" and "regularis"; referring to the "Cantus durus" system which uses B-natural and starts on standard pitches</note>, or <term>soft</term> and <term>transposed</term> <note>original: "mollis" and "transpositus"; referring to the "Cantus mollis" system which uses a B-flat and moves the scale to a different starting pitch</note>—should be <term>assigned</term> <note>original: "referiren"</note>.

# For an organist who is accustomed to German Tabu-
# lature, and who perhaps might not be able to manage
## musical notes at all: it does not seem improper to me
## to distinguish the <term>modes<note>The precursors to modern musical keys; these were scale patterns that determined the character and structure of a piece of music.</note></term> in this manner.

### <term>Authentic<note>The "authentic" modes are the primary versions of the scales where the melodic range extends from the home note (the final) to the octave above it.</note></term> or Regular Modes in
### <term>Hard Song<note>original: "Cantu duro" — a musical system or "hexachord" that uses B-natural. In this context, it refers to music written without a flat in the key signature.</note></term>.

A detailed musical diagram enclosed in a rectangular frame, divided into twelve cells arranged in two rows of six. Each cell is numbered at the top: the top row contains cells 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, and 11; the bottom row contains cells 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, and 12.

Inside each cell is a vertical arrangement of musical pitches written in German organ tablature (letters with horizontal bars above them to indicate octaves). The pitches are grouped by curly braces and labeled on the left and right sides of each cell to indicate voice parts:
- 'C' for Cantus <note>The highest voice, or Soprano.</note> and 'T' for Tenor on the left side.
- 'A' for Altus <note>The Alto voice.</note> and 'B' for Bassus <note>The Bass voice.</note> on the right side.

For example, in cell 1 (Dorian mode), the Cantus (C) indicates high d, a, and middle d. The Tenor (T) indicates middle a. The Altus (A) brace encompasses the top three pitches, and the Bassus (B) brace encompasses the lower pitches a and d.

## Soft Song <note>original: "Cantu bmolli" — a musical system or "hexachord" characterized by the use of a B-flat. In the 16th and 17th centuries, this usually indicated a key signature with one flat.</note>.

The abbreviations in the table refer to the four standard voice parts:
**C**: <term>Cantus</term> (Soprano)
**A**: <term>Altus</term> (Alto)
**T**: <term>Tenor</term>
**B**: <term>Bassus</term> (Bass)

The letters inside the braces (such as g̿, ḡ, ƀ) represent specific musical pitches in **German organ tablature**. The horizontal bars above the letters indicate the octave: two bars (̿) denote a very high octave, one bar (̄) denotes the middle-high octave, and lowercase or Fraktur letters (𝔊, 𝔄) denote lower octaves. The symbol **ƀ** specifically refers to B-flat.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the twelve musical modes, specifically illustrating their ranges and how they appear in notation. It then transitions into a new chapter regarding the "Tactus" (the beat) and the various time signatures used in the late Renaissance and early Baroque periods. The previous page detailed the pitch ranges for the "Soft Song" (B-flat) system.</meta>

And these are therefore the ranges <note>original: "Ambitus"</note> of each mode: wherein the breve <note>a square note representing a standard unit of time</note> <image-desc>mensural white breve note symbol</image-desc> indicates the final <note>the "home" note or tonic</note> of that same mode; while the black notes indicate the repercussion <note>the "reciting tone," a frequently repeated note that characterizes the melodic identity of a mode</note>. However, the range of any mode consists generally within an octave <note>original: "Diapason"</note>; but by poetic license, sometimes a whole tone or a semitone is added at both the lower and upper ends. The Dorian and Hypodorian modes allow for a minor third <note>original: "Semiditonum"</note> above the octave, and the Hypophrygian adds several intervals at the upper end, while neglecting the lower ones.

# C H A P T E R  V I I.
## Concerning the TACTUS, or the Measure of Notes (called *Battuta* by the Italians); and the Signs.
What distinctions are to be maintained regarding the beat, the signs, and the characters; also how the *Sextupla* <note>a compound meter, often 6/4 or 6/2</note> is to be understood.

Since in the books of certain musicians, various characters and signs are found prefixed to songs, I have decided to briefly touch upon the power and reasoning of the most important ones and to illustrate them with their proper forms. I do this not to bring back those "monstrous signs" to the musical stage—which, as Glarean <note>Heinrich Glarean (1488–1563), a famous music theorist</note> testifies in Book 3, Chapter 12, involve infinite labor but no actual utility—but rather to serve those who, though well-trained in the musical school, are either not easily able to navigate or are offended by these obstacles when they encounter such signs. For it is still in the memory of some that the most sweet musician Jacobus Händelius <note>Jacob Handl, also known as Jacobus Gallus (1550–1591)</note>, because of obsolete signs no longer in daily use which he had prefixed to a song of singular artifice (titled *The Mockers, God shall mock them* <note>original: "Subsannatores, Subsannabit Deus"</note>, which I add at the end of this chapter), incurred the grave offense of certain people who were less familiar with the meaning of the signs.

->*The measurement of notes is considered in relation to the signs.*<-

Signs are either Common, which are used in Equal Beat <note>duple meter</note>; or Proportional, which are used partly in Equal Beat and partly in Unequal Beat. For the <term>Tactus</term> <note>the physical motion of the hand marking time</note> is divided according to its motion.

The Equal or "Spondaic" <note>a steady, two-part beat like a poetic foot of two long syllables</note> beat is either slower or faster, depending on the variation of the signs.

The sign for the slower beat is **C**, with which Madrigals are marked; the sign for the faster beat is <image-desc>mensural sign for tempus imperfectum diminutum (a "C" with a vertical stroke through it)</image-desc>, with which Motets are marked.

<summary>This page explains the melodic ranges of musical modes and introduces the "Tactus" (beat), distinguishing between slow and fast duple time as indicated by specific musical signs.</summary>
<keywords>Ambitus, musical modes, Tactus, time signatures, Jacobus Gallus, Glarean, Madrigal, Motet, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Tactus (the beat) and musical signs. The previous page introduced the concept of the beat as a measure of time and began to dismiss overly complex "monstrous" signs of earlier eras in favor of practical application.</meta>

A **Sign** is either:
*   **Common**, and that in **Equal Beat** <note>original: "Tactu æquali"</note>:
    *   **Slower**, **C**, by which **Madrigals** <note>a type of secular vocal music, often poetic and expressive</note> are marked.
    *   **Faster**, **𝇋**, by which **Motets** <note>sacred choral compositions, usually more rhythmically driving than madrigals</note> are marked.
*   **Proportional**, and that in **Beat**:
    *   **Unequal**, such as:
        *   **Tripla** 3/1 and **Sesquialtera** 3/2 <note>ratios indicating triple-time meters where three notes are played in the time of two or one</note>.
    *   **Equal**: such as:
        *   **Dupla**; **Subdupla**; **Quadrupla**, **Subquadrupla**. **Sextupla**, not used by the ancient musicians, but by certain modern ones.

### Concerning the Common Signs in Equal Beat: C & 𝇋.

The old musicians called the **C** *Tempus perfectum minus* <note>original: "Tempus perfectum minus," or Minor Perfect Time</note>, or the **Sign of the Lesser Beat**. In this time, they reckoned one **semibreve** <note>a diamond-shaped note that serves as the whole note of this period</note> <image-desc>diamond-shaped semibreve note</image-desc>, or two **minims** <note>half-length notes with stems</note> <image-desc>two minim notes with stems</image-desc> to one beat; in Italian, they called this *alla Semibreve* <note>meaning "by the semibreve"</note>.

The **𝇋**, however, they called *Tempus perfectum majus* <note>original: "Tempus perfectum majus," or Major Perfect Time</note>, or the **Sign of the Greater or Total Beat**. In those songs marked with this sign, **𝇋**, they measured two semibreves—and thus two "lesser beats"—into a single, albeit very slow, beat. The Italians called this *alla Breve*. It was measured such that one semibreve <image-desc>diamond-shaped semibreve note</image-desc> or two minims <image-desc>two minim notes with stems</image-desc> were sung on the **downbeat** <note>original: "depressione"</note>, and the other semibreve <image-desc>diamond-shaped semibreve note</image-desc> or both minims were sung on the **upbeat** <note>original: "elevatione"</note>.

This was customary in the time of **Orlando di Lasso** <note>original: "Orlandi," the influential Flemish composer (c. 1532–1594)</note> and is still in use today in several noble chapels and schools. We see an example in Orlando’s song:

<image-desc>
A musical example in two staves comparing the 𝇋 (cut-C) and C time signatures using the same melody. The notes are written in white mensural notation (diamond-shaped heads). Below the notes is the text: "Bene- dicam Do-minum in omni tempore" (I will bless the Lord at all times).
</image-desc>

G <note>signature/clef marker</note>

<summary>This page explains the categorization of musical signs, distinguishing between common signs like C and 𝇋 and proportional signs. It details the historical transition from "alla Semibreve" to "alla Breve" beats, citing the works of Orlando di Lasso as a standard.</summary>
<keywords>Tactus, Time Signatures, Alla Breve, Semibreve, Orlando di Lasso, Madrigals, Motets, Equal Beat, Proportional Beat</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Tactus (the beat) and the specific application of the time signatures C and 𝇋. It follows the explanation of how these signs determine the measurement of semibreves and minims.</meta>

Therefore, at that time, it had to be observed with the greatest care that those songs marked with this sign 𝇋 <note>original: "$\mathbb{C}$", the *alla breve* or "cut-C" sign</note> should always end *cum tempore* <note>meaning "with the time," finishing a full unit of two semibreves</note> (according to the way songs are divided); otherwise, the song would have ended on the upbeat <note>original: "elevatione Tactus"</note>. For because two **semibreves** (which complete one *tempus* <note>the basic unit of musical measurement</note>) here make no more than one beat, and if the song ended immediately after the first semibreve—which here counts as only half a beat—then the final note would occur on the upbeat. Yet the end and cadence <note>original: "clauſula"</note> of songs should be finished on the downbeat <note>original: "depreſſione"</note>; for the downbeat of the **Greater Beat** <note>original: "Majoris Tactus"</note> is considered the beginning of the *tempus*.

In this sign, **C**, however, it is not of great importance whether it ends *in tempore* <note>on the beat</note> or *cum tempore* <note>at the completion of the full time-unit</note>. This can be proven from many madrigals, such as in **Luca Marenzio's** *Spiritual Madrigals*, where he often uses the **𝇋** sign and always ends *cum tempore*. But where he places **C**, he mostly lets it end *in tempore*: as in numbers 11, 12, 22, 23, 26, etc. Although some maintain—and I also find this for the most part (though not always) in the compositions of **Giovanni Gabrieli** and **Claudio Monteverdi**—that the final cadence, especially before triple-meter sections, should be closed and ended *cum tempore*.

->A song ended *in tempore*. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *Cum Tempore*.<-

<image-desc>Two musical notation snippets side-by-side. The left snippet is labeled 'In Tempore finita Cantio.' and shows a 'C' time signature with several notes on a five-line staff, ending with a final note before a double bar. The right snippet is labeled 'Cum Tempore.' and shows a 'cut-C' (alla breve) time signature with similar notation, demonstrating a different rhythmic placement of the final cadence relative to the tactus or measure.</image-desc>

---

In the present time, however, these two signs are mostly observed in such a way that **C** is primarily used in **madrigals**, while **𝇋** is used in **motets**. Because madrigals and other songs under the sign **C**, which abound in **semiminims** <note>quarter notes</note> and **fusae** <note>eighth notes</note>, proceed with a faster motion; whereas motets, which abound in **breves** and **semibreves** under the sign **𝇋**, proceed more slowly. Therefore, a faster beat <note>tactus</note> is needed here [for the motets], and a slower beat there [for the madrigals], so that a middle path is kept between the two extremes. This prevents a sluggish progression from causing boredom in the listeners' ears, or a too-rapid pace from leading to a precipice—just as the horses of the Sun carried away **Phaethon** <note>a figure from Greek mythology who lost control of the sun-chariot</note>, where the chariot obeyed no reins.

Therefore, it seems to me not poorly done if one marks motets and other sacred songs—which are composed with many "black notes" <note>shorter, filled-in note values like quarter and eighth notes</note>—with this sign **C**, to indicate that the beat must then be kept somewhat slower and more solemnly <note>original: "graviteriſcher"</note>. As **Orlando di Lasso** <note>original: "Orlandus"</note> did in his *Magnificats for 4 Voices*, and **Marenzio** in the aforementioned *Spiritual Madrigals* and other madrigals...

<meta>catchword: such [ſolche]</meta>

<summary>This page explains the practical difference between the time signatures C and 𝇋, focusing on how they dictate the speed of the beat (tactus) and the proper placement of the final cadence. The author argues that C should be used for slower, more solemn beats when many short notes are present, while 𝇋 is suited for faster beats in music with longer notes.</summary>
<keywords>tactus, downbeat, upbeat, alla breve, madrigal, motet, Luca Marenzio, Giovanni Gabrieli, Claudio Monteverdi, Orlando di Lasso, musical notation, cadences</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the Tactus (the beat) and how composers use different time signatures to indicate the speed and style of a performance.</meta>

...[this has been] observed. However, anyone can reflect on these matters for themselves and observe, **from a consideration of the text and the harmony** <note>original: "ex consideratione Textus & Harmoniæ"</note>, where a slow or fast <term>Tactus</term> <note>the conducted beat or pulse of the music</note> must be maintained.

For this much is certain and highly necessary: that in **concertos for multiple choirs** <note>original: "Concerten per Choros"</note> a very slow, dignified <term>Tactus</term> must be held. But because in such **concertos**, sometimes the **madrigalian style** and sometimes the **motet style** are found mixed and alternating with one another, one must also direct oneself accordingly in the conducting. Therefore, it is a very necessary **invention** <note>original: "inventum"</note> that sometimes (as described below in the first chapter of the third part) the **Italian words** <note>original: "Vocabula von den Wälschen"</note> *adagio* (slowly), *presto* (quickly)—that is, *tardè* (slowly) and *velociter* (swiftly)—are noted and marked in the musical parts. Otherwise, having to change so often between the two **signs** **C** and **𝄵** might cause and stir up more **confusion** and hindrance.

And when I look at the **compositions of the Italians** <note>original: "Italorum Compositiones"</note> in these current times—which in just a few years have been directed toward an entirely different and special new style—I find very great **discrepancies** and **varieties** in the **prefixing of the signs for equal and unequal meter** <note>original: "præfixione Signorum Tactus æqualis & Inæqualis"</note>.

For **Giovanni Gabrieli** <note>original: "Iohann Gabriel"</note> has marked all his **concertos, symphonies, canzonas**, and **sonatas** (both with and without text) with the **𝄵** throughout; so much so that even until now, I have never found the **sign** **C** in all his **works** <note>original: "Operibus"</note>. 

Some others, however—and indeed the majority—retain the **C** throughout on its own.

**Claudio Monteverdi** <note>original: "Claudius de Monte Verde"</note> **prefixes** <note>original: "præponirt"</note> the **𝄵** in those works that he has composed in the **motet** style and which can be performed to an **alla breve beat** <note>original: "ad Tactum alla Breve," a quicker beat where the pulse falls on the breve rather than the semibreve</note>. In all others, however, in which there are more black notes than white notes, he prefixes the **C**.

**Ludovico Viadana** <note>original: "Lud. Viadana"</note> uses the **𝄵** in all his pieces **with text** <note>original: "cum Textu"</note>. In the **symphonies** **without text** <note>original: "sine Textu"</note>, however, he has retained the **C**.

Some mix them together—sometimes using **𝄵** in one, and **C** in another—even though no difference can be recognized in the notes or the song as a whole.

I, in my simple opinion <note>original: "Einfalt"</note>, am best pleased by this method: that in **motets** composed in the style of **Orlando di Lasso** (**the most famous and sweetest musician of that time, and who, in the application of the text and the correct observation of musical rules, left and exhibited to us the highest industry and dexterity above all others** <note>original Latin: "Musici tum temporis præclarissimi suaviissimiq;, & qui in applicatione Textus, justaq; observatione Regularum Musicalium præ cæteris, summam industriam & dexterity nobis reliquit & exhibuit"</note>) and which can be sung to an **alla breve beat** if necessary, one uses the **𝄵**. But in all others, especially in the **concertos**,
