# On the Proportionate Signs in Unequal Meter.
## Unequal or Trochaic Meter is twofold:
->Major and Minor.<-

Major is commonly called **Triple proportion**; Minor is called **Sesquialtera**. **Triple** is when three **semibreves** <note>diamond-shaped notes, roughly equivalent to modern whole notes</note> <image-desc>three diamond-shaped semibreve notes</image-desc> or their equivalents are measured by one **beat** <note>original: "Tactu"</note>. Signs are used for this by Orlando [di Lasso], [Luca] Marenzio, Felice Anerio, and others: 3. $\frac{3}{1}$. 𝄵 3. ◯ 3. $\frac{2}{1}$. 𝄶 $\frac{3}{2}$. **Sesquialtera** is when three **minims** <note>notes with stems, roughly equivalent to modern half notes</note> <image-desc>three diamond-shaped minim notes with stems</image-desc> or their equivalents are measured by one beat. Just as "Sesquialtera" is used in arithmetic when the larger term contains the smaller once plus its half [i.e., a 3:2 ratio]: so in music, the **semibreve** <image-desc>semibreve note</image-desc> and its smaller part, the **minim**, are required to establish Unequal Meter in sesquialtera. Moreover, it is appropriately noted by this sign: $\frac{3}{2}$. For just as in **Triple proportion** $\frac{3}{1}$ indicates that three semibreves <image-desc>three semibreve notes</image-desc> are to be related to one beat, so in **Sesquialtera** $\frac{3}{2}$ denotes that three semibreves are to be related to two beats <note>This explains the mathematical ratio of the notation: three notes now occupy the time previously taken by two</note>. Other signs are also found, such as C 3. ◯ 3. ◯ 3. ◯ $\frac{3}{1}$. C $\frac{3}{2}$. ◯ $\frac{3}{2}$. ʘ. C. C. 3. C. $\frac{3}{2}$. Here, however, it must be noted that by the sign of **Greater Prolation** <term>Greater Prolation</term> <note>original: "Majoris prolationis"; an older system of time measurement where the relationship between note values was determined by specific symbols like a circle with a dot</note> ʘ or C, when it is applied to all voices simultaneously, **Sesquialtera** is indicated; but if it is found in only one voice, it indicates **augmentation** <note>stretching out the note values</note> or a **sub-double** [slowing down], as can be seen below in the following example by the noble **Benedetto Pallavicino**.

->Modern Italians maintain:<-

In pro- / portion / to / one / beat.
{
three [three semibreve symbols] against two [two semibreve symbols]
three [three minim symbols] in place of two [two minim symbols]
}
in / perfect / time
{
major [barred circle with dot] $\frac{3}{2}$
minor C $\frac{3}{2}$
}
ought / to / be / sung.

For just as in **Equal Meter** under "Major Perfect Time" 𝄵, two semibreves <image-desc>two semibreve notes</image-desc>; and under "Minor" C, two minims <image-desc>two minim notes</image-desc> are related to one beat: so under "Major Time," there arises <image-desc>two minim notes</image-desc> the proportion—

<meta>catchword: portio- (proportion)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical and symbolic foundations of triple meter (Unequal Tactus), distinguishing between Triple proportion (3:1) and Sesquialtera (3:2) and how they are indicated in contemporary Italian musical notation.</summary>
<keywords>Unequal Meter, Trochaic Meter, Triple Proportion, Sesquialtera, Semibreve, Minim, Greater Prolation, Orlando di Lasso, Luca Marenzio, Felice Anerio, Benedetto Pallavicino</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical proportions, specifically clarifying how the sign 3/2 relates to the number of notes per beat, and proposing a simplified system of notation for different musical genres.</meta>

...portion of three **semibreves** <image-desc>three semibreve notes</image-desc>, and under the Minor [proportion], of three **minims** <image-desc>three minim notes</image-desc>, which are to be referred to one **beat** <note>original: "Tactum"</note>. Both are denoted under the sign 3/2—indicating that three notes in this proportion are worth only as much as two are worth in Equal Meter <note>original: "tres Notas in proportione tantùm valere, quantum in Tactu Aequali, duæ valent"</note>—by adding it to the perfect time or to the minor **C** [common time], or the major <image-desc>cut C mensuration sign</image-desc>.

However, I see that many do not observe their own rules and use one sign for the other without distinction. Therefore, so that the minds of students and singers are not delayed by superfluities, I think—in my humble opinion—that all those signs shown at the beginning should be completely abolished and removed from musical scores. These signs, which have been used by even the most distinguished musicians up to this hour, show nothing but unnecessary, useless, and even intricate difficulties. Instead, in **Triple proportion** <image-desc>three semibreve notes</image-desc>, only this single sign 3/1 or 3 should be used; while in **Sesquialtera** <image-desc>three blackened minim notes and two white minim notes</image-desc>, the appropriate sign should be employed.

And although some are of the opinion that even **Sesquialtera** and the blackened notes in <term>Hemiola</term> <note>A rhythmic device where three notes are played in the time of two, often indicated by coloring the note heads black</note> should be abolished—since these and any others of this kind could be expressed through a single **Triple proportion**—nonetheless, I would not say this is done conveniently. For the sake of a more helpful distinction for the singers, they should be left to certain types of songs. Specifically, **Triple proportion** should be kept for <term>Motets</term> <note>Sacred vocal compositions</note> and <term>Concertos</term>; while **Sesquialtera** should be retained for <term>Madrigals</term>, and especially for <term>Galliards</term>, <term>Courantes</term>, <term>Voltes</term>, and other songs of that kind, in which a faster <term>beat</term> <note>original: "Tactu"</note> is necessarily required. This is because most of these songs require such a fast <term>beat</term> that, due to the novelty of the matter, I had to think of new names that have not been used in that way before; thus, I have tried to express this faster pace with the term <term>Sextupla</term> <note>A six-fold proportion</note> or a "diminished <term>Trochaic beat</term>" <note>A beat following a long-short rhythmic pattern</note>.

---

| **Triple Proportion.** | <image-desc>Staff with 3/1 signature, three semibreves, and a long note</image-desc> |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Sesquialtera.** | <image-desc>Staff with 3/2 signature, three blackened minims, two white minims, and a long note</image-desc> |

Minor <term>Hemiola</term> <note>original: "Hemiolam minorem"; a rhythmic relation where three notes occupy the time of two</note> <image-desc>mensural sign circle with vertical stroke and three black diamond-shaped notes</image-desc> is rare except in Sextuple proportion: but I think Major <term>Hemiola</term> <image-desc>mensural sign circle with number 3 and three black square notes</image-desc> can be used here when the <image-desc>three black square notes</image-desc> sense of the words requires it, and when the frequent insertion of various signs seems to disturb and confuse the song.

->*On Proportional Signs in Equal Meter.*<-
<note>original: "De Signis Proportionatis in Tactu Æquali"</note>

Although it is almost unnecessary to mention anything here regarding the signs of the ancients <note>original: "Signis veterum"</note>, since the variety of them not only has no special use, but is also nothing other than a wandering and confusion, through which not only the youth in the schools but also often experienced vocal and instrumental musicians in chapels are perturbed, hindered, and even fully confused: yet I have decided to touch upon these following signs very briefly here (which are still found today in the compositions of several distinguished modern musicians <note>original: "Musicorum Neotericorum"</note>) with good intentions, and especially for the benefit of those who do not know them. I wish to include the corresponding examples at the same time: not with the intention that one *should* use them, but so that, if such and similar signs happen to appear in songs <note>original: "Cantionibus"</note>, a singer <note>original: "Cantor"</note> or an organist, when arranging music <note>original: "Absetzen"; refers to intabulation or transcribing scores</note> or otherwise, can find their way through them.

<columns>2</columns>

**1. Dupla, where half the value is taken away from the notes:** its signs are $^2/_1$ $^4/_2$ $^6/_3$ $^8/_4$ $^{10}/_5$. As if you were to say, two notes <image-desc>two black diamond-shaped notes</image-desc> are worth only as much as one <image-desc>one black diamond-shaped note</image-desc>. &c.

**2. Quadrupla, where three-quarters of the value is taken away under the signs** $^4/_1$ $^8/_2$ $^{12}/_3$. <note>In this proportion, four notes are played in the time of one, making each note four times faster.</note>

<column-break/>

<insert>
<image-desc>The word "Opposites" is written vertically between two large curly brackets grouping the left and right text sections.</image-desc>
**Opposites.**
<note>original: "Contraria"</note>
</insert>

**Subdupla, which increases the notes twofold, and has the signs** $^1/_2$ $^2/_4$ $^3/_6$ $^4/_8$; as if you were to say, one note <image-desc>one hollow diamond-shaped note</image-desc> is worth as much as two others <image-desc>two hollow diamond-shaped notes</image-desc>.

**Subquadrupla, where the value of the notes is increased fourfold, under the signs** $^1/_4$ $^2/_8$ $^3/_{12}$ **&c.**

1. The Voice, which is the

->*On Proportional Signs in Equal Meter.*<-
<note>original: "De Signis Proportionatis in Tactu Æquali"; refers to musical signs that change the speed of notes relative to a steady, even beat (tactus)</note>

Although it is almost unnecessary to mention anything here regarding the signs of the ancients <note>original: "Signis veterum"</note>, since the variety of them not only has no special use, but is also nothing other than a wandering and confusion, through which not only the youth in the schools but also often experienced vocal and instrumental musicians in chapels are perturbed, hindered, and even fully confused: yet I have decided to touch upon these following signs very briefly here (which are still found today in the compositions of several distinguished modern musicians <note>original: "Musicorum Neotericorum"</note>) with good intentions, and especially for the benefit of those who do not know them. I wish to include the corresponding examples at the same time: not with the intention that one *should* use them, but so that, if such and similar signs happen to appear in songs <note>original: "Cantionibus"</note>, a singer <note>the text continues on the next page, likely explaining that the singer may recognize and perform them correctly</note>

<summary>This page provides a visual and theoretical guide to musical proportions (Double, Quadruple, and their inverses) and critiques the overly complex notation systems used by older composers. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion on proportional signs, transitioning from the general table of proportions on the previous page to the practical application of these signs in specific musical genres like Madrigals and Motets. It focuses on how the starting time signature determines the relative speed of subsequent rhythmic changes.</meta>

->But if these common and proportional signs vary, one must observe<-
->which sign was prescribed at the beginning.<-

1. If the <term>𝄴</term> sign for Madrigals <note>original: "Signum Madrigalium"; a specific time signature used in secular Italian vocal music</note> is found prescribed at the beginning, and in the middle of the song signs of Double proportion are added, or signs of diminution <term>𝄴</term>, <term>C 2.</term>, or <term>𝄵</term>: then a Long <term>𝅜</term> is worth 2 beats / a Breve <term>𝅝</term> one beat / a Minim <term>𝅗𝅥</term> a half / a Crotchet <term>𝅘𝅥</term> a quarter / and so forth, so that each note has only half its natural value <note>original: "Valorem"</note>. But if signs of Quadruple proportion, or <term>𝄴 2.</term> and <term>𝄵</term>—which are called "diminished diminution"—are found, then a Long <term>𝅜</term> is worth only 1 beat / a Breve <term>𝅝</term> a half / and so on / each note being worth a fourth part less than otherwise: as can be seen below in the examples of Sessa d'Aranda and Benedetto Pallavicino.

2. But if the <term>𝄴</term> sign for Motets <note>original: "Signum Motectarum"; the standard signature for sacred choral works</note> was prescribed at the beginning, then <term>𝄴 2.</term> and <term>𝄵</term> take away only a half part; for <term>C 2.</term>, <term>𝄵</term>, and <term>𝄴</term> diminish the C signature; and <term>𝄴 2.</term> and <term>𝄵</term> in turn diminish the <term>𝄴</term> signature. The C signature, however, when it is found in this context, is a sign of augmentation <note>original: "Signum augmentationis"; a sign that slows the tempo down</note>, and increases the length by double; so that one Long <term>𝅜</term> is worth 4 beats, one Breve <term>𝅝</term> worth 2 beats / and so forth: concerning which see the example of Jacob Handl <note>original: "Iacobi Händelii"; a prominent late Renaissance composer known for his motets</note>.

Besides this, it must also be remembered that in my first German works, as well as in the Hymns, I have set the chant in the soprano part <note>original: "Choral in Cantu"</note> in a few places with ligatures <term>ligaturis</term> <note>a series of notes joined together in a single symbol, common in older notation</note> so that the application of the text might be better observed. However, I have marked these with the sign of diminution <term>𝄴 2.</term> to indicate <note>original: "ad notandum"</note> that all following notes lose half of their right and usual value <term>Valore</term>, which can then also be easily gathered from the other voices added to them.

<meta>catchword: Jacob</meta>

<term>detrahentes</term>: In the Latin Vulgate Bible (Psalm 70:13), this refers to "adversaries" or "detractors"—those who speak maliciously against someone to diminish their reputation or life.

<term>animae meae</term>: Literally "of my soul" or "against my life." In Renaissance sacred music, the "soul" often represents the entirety of the person's being before God.

<term>mensural notation</term>: The standard system for writing music in the 16th century. Unlike modern notation, it does not use bar lines; the duration of notes is determined by their shapes (such as the diamond-shaped semiminims seen here) and the context of the surrounding notes.

<term>polyphony</term>: The texture of this music, where the four voices (Cantus, Altus, Tenor, Bassus) sing independent melodic lines that overlap and intertwine to create harmony.

<term>Mensural notation</term>: A historical system of music notation used from the late 13th century until about 1600. Unlike modern notes, which are round, mensural notes are often diamond-shaped (rhomboid). The "white" notes seen here indicate that they are hollowed out, a style that became standard in the mid-15th century.

<term>C-clef</term>: A musical symbol that identifies where the note "Middle C" is located on the staff. In this period, the clef would move up or down the lines to suit the range of the singer.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical proportions and time signatures, moving from the specific examples of the previous page into a general discussion of "Sextupla" (six-fold) rhythm. The author, Michael Praetorius, is categorizing how various European nations (Italians, British, French) denote these rhythms and introduces his own practical solutions for complex choral works.</meta>

73.

### ->On SEXTUPLA, or the Diminished Trochaic Beat.<-

**S**extupla, as the ancients <note>original: *veteres*</note> used it, has now entirely fallen out of use; but in these times, it is called Sextupla when six <term>semiminims</term> <note>equivalent to modern quarter notes</note> are referred to one <term>tactus</term> <note>the basic beat or hand motion used by a conductor</note>; for which reason some people write the number 3 either below or above three <image-desc>musical notation: three black diamond-shaped notes with stems</image-desc> or six semiminims. In this manner: 3. <image-desc>musical notation: two black diamond-shaped notes with stems</image-desc>

I find, however, besides this, three other types used by the Italians and the British, regarding how and in what form such Sextuplas could be designated and marked. Namely:

1. In the 1st Type, the notes are all set in black, just like in <term>Hemiola Minore</term> <note>a rhythmic relationship where three notes are played in the time of two</note>; which—as mentioned earlier regarding the <term>Tempore perfecto majore</term> <note>Great Perfect Time, a meter involving triple groupings</note> <image-desc>musical symbol: alla breve (C with a vertical line)</image-desc>—is directed toward the double beat <term>alla Breve</term> <note>a fast duple meter</note>; such that three <image-desc>musical notation: three black diamond-shaped notes with stems</image-desc> blackened <term>minims</term> <note>half notes</note> or a blackened <term>semibreve</term> <note>whole note</note> together with a blackened minim <image-desc>musical notation: one black diamond-shaped note without stem followed by one with a stem</image-desc> must be sung and "slung" <note>likely referring to a smooth, connected style of singing</note> on the downbeat <note>original: *depressione Tactus*</note>, and the following three on the upbeat <note>original: *elevatione*</note>. The sign is 6/2, to signify that six semiminims, or blackened minims, are required for one whole beat.

2. In the second type (which is mostly observed by the Italians and French in their <term>Corantos</term>, <term>Sarabandes</term>, and other such pieces), minims and semiminims are used in the same way as the blackened semibreves and minims were used in the first type. The sign is 6/4, to indicate that 6 semiminims must be worth as much as four would otherwise be in number.

Although the French <note>original: *Galli*</note> use different beats for this, according to the style of each dance. Concerning this, I have treated more extensively in my *Terpsichore of the Aonian Muses* <note>original: *Terpsichore Musarum Aoniarum*, a famous collection of dance music published by Praetorius in 1612</note>.

Examples of Sextupla are found in English Pavanes and other songs of the British and French; likewise, in the 5th part of the *German Muses of Zion* <note>original: *Musar. Soniar. Germanic.*, referring to his *Musae Sioniae*</note> Numbers 156, 158, 157, etc. In the *Hymnodia* Numbers 134, 135. In the *Megalynodia* Number 14, and very many are found in the *Terpsichore*.

3. The 3rd Type, I have had to invent myself out of necessity in my humble way. For since I saw and noticed that this seemed quite difficult for some (as in my *Polyhymnia II*, the "Omnes gentes"); I therefore worried that it might not go off without confusion; and accordingly in my *Te Deum laudamus* for 22 and 26 voices, in the third part, "Thou, Christ, art the King of Glory" <note>original: *Tu Rex gloriae Christe*</note>, I also directed it toward the equal beat of the Sextupla (where I have entirely...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 73 regarding the "Sextupla" rhythm. The author is explaining his notation choices intended to help school-aged singers and less experienced musicians navigate the shift from a triple (unequal) beat to a faster duple (equal) beat without becoming confused or looking ridiculous while conducting.</meta>

...directed) should be measured: And since for some, especially in schools, it might be very difficult and almost impossible to be brought to such a beat, I had to think of another means of how such a **Sextupla** should be signed and notated, so that both the **equal beat** <note>original: *Tactus æqualis*; a beat where the downstroke and upstroke are of equal length</note> and **unequal beat** <note>original: *Tactus Inæqualis*; a beat used for triple time where the downstroke is twice as long as the upstroke</note> could be observed and taken into account according to everyone's convenience and opportunity. And therefore, under the proportional sign of the **Sesquialtera** <note>a 3:2 rhythmic ratio</note> **3/2**, I have written the **semibreves** and **minims** <image-desc>white semibreve and two white minims</image-desc>, so that for those who are not accustomed to the other beat, the correct **Unequal Trochaic Sesquialtera Proportional Beat** (though kept quite fast) would be maintained and confusion avoided.

But so that the correct **Equal Sextupla Beat** might also be conveniently observed at times—especially by those who have knowledge of these things and are already fairly accustomed to them—I have placed the sign of **Double Diminution** <note>indicating a very fast tempo</note>, namely the **3** over an **alla breve signature** over a **2** <image-desc>musical symbol: 3 over a cut-C signature over a 2</image-desc>, before the sign of the **Sesquialtera 3/2**. This is to indicate that in such a proportion one can use the **alla Breve** beat <note>a quick duple meter</note> just as well as under the **Great Perfect Time** <image-desc>musical symbol: cut-C signature</image-desc>; thus, one **Sesquialtera** beat is performed on the downbeat <note>original: *depressione*</note> and the other on the upbeat <note>original: *elevatione*</note>. Therefore, for better instruction, I have encompassed and included both unequal beats together within a small line drawn below the notes.

It could indeed be that the **unequal beat** is always observed here and (as the French dance masters use in their **Courantes**, **Sarabands**, and other such dances) is kept very fast, which would also result in fewer errors and be more correct; as can be found noted in my ***Megalynodia*** No. 14 in the *Sicut erat*. But I like the **equal beat** in such a manner—where two unequal beats are brought into one equal one—far better, <note>original: *ne creberrimâ manus & brachii commotione spectatoribus risum & auditoribus tædium excitemus, ansamq; vulgo cavillandi & convitiandi exhibeamus*</note> lest we provoke laughter in the spectators and boredom in the listeners by the too frequent movement of the hand and arm, and give the common people an opportunity for mocking and reviling.

And therefore the **Sextupla** could very well be called the **Diminished Trochaic Beat**: <note>original: *Quia quemadmodum in Trochaico Simplici Sesquialtero, tres tantummodò Minimæ vel Semiminimæ uno Tactu mensurantur; Ita hîc, sex Minimæ vel Semiminimæ ad unum Tactum referuntur; quarum tres depressioni, tres verò elevationi respondent.*</note> Because just as in the **Simple Trochaic Sesquialtera**, only three **minims** or **semiminims** are measured in one beat; so here, six minims or semiminims are referred to one beat, of which three correspond to the downbeat and three to the upbeat. Whence the beat becomes equal, in this manner:

He references French dance styles and his own Megalynodia as practical examples of these rhythmic proportions.</summary>

Having previously discussed three ways to adapt triple-time signatures for steady "equal beat" conducting, the author now introduces a fourth method he discovered in newly arrived Italian sheet music by Giovanni Fergusio.</meta>

4. Just now—as this work was already finished at the printer’s to this extent—the motets of **Giovanni Fergusio** from **Italy** came into my hands. In these, I find that in several places he uses the numbers <note>Proportion sign 6 over 2</note> in such a way that 6 **Minims** <term>Minims</term> <note>original: *Minimæ*; diamond-shaped notes with a stem, which served as the basic unit of the musical pulse in this period</note> are worth no more than 2 <note>image shows two minim notes</note>. Consequently, 3 **Minims** <note>image shows three minim notes</note> are counted against a single <note>image shows one minim note</note> pulse, and all 6 **Minims** must be sung to complete *one equal beat* <term>Equal Beat</term> <note>original: *ad unum Tactum æqualem*; a conducting method where the hand goes down and up in equal duration, ensuring a steady tempo</note> <note>image shows one minim note</note>.

This is therefore the fourth type, and for further and better instruction, I have chosen to include an excerpt here from that same Motet.

## Chapter VIII.
### On the variation of the Beat or Measure in the progression and conclusion of songs.
### In what manner various variations and changes can be established in the lowering and raising <note>original: *in depressione & elevatione*</note> of the Voice and the Beat.

Singing must not be rushed: for there arises a confusion of the whole ensemble <term>Symphony</term> <note>In this period, *symphonia* referred generally to a harmonious vocal or instrumental ensemble rather than the modern orchestral form.</note>, even the most pleasant one. However, with a more drawn-out Beat, the harmony becomes more graceful and is better perceived.

Equality of measure must also be maintained, lest the harmony be deformed or disturbed. For to sing without law and measure is to offend God himself, who "arranged all things by number, weight, and measure," as Plato says <note>The author attributes this famous biblical quote (Wisdom 11:20) to Plato, reflecting the Renaissance tendency to blend Neoplatonism with Christian theology.</note>. Yet, according to the requirements of the text, to use sometimes a slower Beat and sometimes a faster one in turns possesses a singular majesty and grace, and wonderfully adorns the song.

Nor does a variation of human and instrumental voices provide less charm <note>original: *Venerem*, literally "Venus," meaning beauty or attractiveness</note> to the harmony and the songs, if at times the songs are performed with a more lively voice, and at others with a more relaxed one. (From the theses of Quitschreiber <note>Georg Quitschreiber (1569–1638), a German composer and theorist known for his writings on performance practice.</note>.)

<summary>This page provides a visual taxonomy of musical beats (Tactus) and begins Chapter VIII, which argues that while a steady measure is divine, performers should vary their tempo and dynamics to suit the emotion of the text.</summary>
<keywords>Tactus, musical measure, tempo variation, performance practice, Plato, Georg Quitschreiber, ensemble singing, musical proportions</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of performance practice, moving from the basic definitions of the beat (Tactus) to stylistic nuances in composition and the specific etiquette of endings. It then transitions into the technical requirements of transposing music based on clef arrangements.</meta>

Some do not want to admit that one should mix the Motet and Madrigal styles together in the composition of a song. However, I do not allow myself to be pleased by this opinion; since it provides and wins a special sweetness and charm for Motets and Concertos if, at the beginning, several measures <note>original: *Tempora*</note> are set very affectively <note>original: *pathetisch*</note> and slowly, and afterwards several fast passages <note>original: *Clausulen*</note> follow. Then again, slow and stately, and then again mixing in faster variations, so that it does not always proceed in one tone and sound <note>original: *Tono vnd Sono*</note>. Rather, such changes—with a slow and fast Beat <term>Tact</term> <note>The "Tact" or Tactus was the fundamental pulse of the music, which the author suggests should be flexible for expressive purposes.</note>, as well as with the raising of the voices and sometimes with a very quiet sound—should be observed with all diligence, as was indicated shortly before.

Furthermore, since it is not very pleasing or welcome when town singers, organists, and other instrumental musicians, following their usual custom, rush immediately from the penultimate note of any song to the final note without any slight pause, I believe those who have not yet observed this in princely courts and other well-ordered musical choirs should be warned here: they should linger somewhat longer on the penultimate note—no matter its written value—singing and sustaining it for four, five, or even six beats <note>original: *Tactum*</note>, and only then finally end on the last note. Once the song is brought to a close, all other voices should fall silent together at the will and nod of the Director or Choirmaster. Nor should the Tenor players prolong their voice at length on the fifth above the Bass or foundation (where the Tenor's end is most often established) while the Bass is silent; but if the Bass is sustained somewhat longer for two or four beats, no one can deny that ornament and grace are won for the song <note>original: *Cantilenæ*</note>.
->G. Q.<-

### Chapter IX.
->*On the transposition of songs.*<-

# How and in what manner certain songs must be transposed when being arranged.

<margin>
<image-desc>A vertical column containing four small musical staff fragments, each showing a different clef position (C-clefs and F-clefs) on a five-line staff.</image-desc>
</margin>

Although every song which is "high-cleffed" <term>hoch Claviret</term> <note>This refers to the "Chiavette" system, where a specific set of high clefs was used to signal to the performer that the piece should be transposed down a fourth or fifth to fit a standard vocal range.</note>—that is, where in the Bass the F-clef <image-desc>F-clef on the second line from the top of the staff</image-desc> is on the second or third line counting from the top, or the C-clef <image-desc>C-clef on the third line of the staff</image-desc> is on the third line...

<summary>The author advocates for expressive variety by mixing musical styles and fluctuating tempo/dynamics, while also providing instructions for dignified endings and the technical rules for transposing music written in high clefs.</summary>
<keywords>performance practice, Motet, Madrigal, Tactus, endings, transposition, Chiavette, clefs, musical expression</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical transposition and the practical challenges organists face when adapting vocal scores to their instruments, specifically focusing on the limitations of keyboard tuning and the "false" sounds created by certain keys.</meta>

...is also found [image-desc] <note>A small musical diagram consisting of two short stave segments. The first segment contains a flat (♭) symbol on the third line of a five-line stave. The second segment contains a bass clef followed by a flat (♭) symbol.</note>; If it is in B-flat <note>original: "b mol"</note>, it must naturally be brought and transposed into the Tablature or Score <term>Partitur</term> <note>A "partitur" is a full musical score showing all parts, as opposed to separate part-books.</note> by organists, lutenists, and all others who use fundamental instruments <note>Instruments capable of playing harmony, like the organ or lute, which provide the "foundation" of a piece.</note>, by a lower fourth into the "hard" key <note>original: "in durum." In the hexachord system of the time, "durum" (hard) referred to scales using B-natural, while "mollem" (soft) referred to those using B-flat.</note>. If it is in F major, however, it must be transposed by a lower fifth into the "soft" key <note>original: "in mollem"</note>.

Yet it is found that in several modes <term>Modi</term>—such as the Mixolydian, Aeolian, and Hypoionian—if they are transposed by a fifth, a **more listless and sluggish** <note>original: "languidior & pigrior"</note> harmony is generated because of the lower sounds. Therefore, it is simultaneously better, and the singing will also be much fresher and more pleasant to hear, if these modes are transposed by a fourth from "hard" into "hard" <note>original: "ex duro in durum"</note>.

However, since this is not only difficult and more inconvenient for organists to play, but also produces an unpleasant harmony in some places; namely, when the B-natural <note>represented by the ♮ symbol</note> must be played with the F-sharp <note>original: "fis"</note>, and in the middle the major third <term>Tertia major</term>, the D-sharp <note>original: "Dis"</note> (which is somewhat too "young" <note>In the context of historical tuning, "young" implies a note that sounds too sharp or tight.</note> and too high, and thus false in comparison) must be struck. 

Therefore, an organist must not only look through and calculate such things with diligence but also take good care to either leave out the third entirely, or touch the minor third, the D, or else compensate for it with sharp **mordants** <term>mordanten</term> <note>A type of musical ornament consisting of a rapid alternation between the main note and the note below it, used here to "hide" a poorly tuned interval.</note> so that the dissonance is not actually observed or heard. For this reason, it is very good and highly necessary that in those organs and harpsichords <term>Clavicymbeln</term> used for musical concerts, the black semi-tone <term>Semitonium</term> D-sharp—and where possible, the G-sharp—should be doubled <note>This refers to "split keys" on an organ keyboard, which provided two separate keys for D-sharp and E-flat so that both could be tuned purely.</note>, as I mentioned in Volume Two, Part Two, Chapter 38, regarding the **Universal Harpsichord** <note>original: "VniversalClavicymbel"</note>. Although it is not yet as necessary in harpsichords and spinets as it is in organs: since in that case, the string on the same key, D-sharp, can very easily be let down a little lower and tuned to the correct major third between the B and F-sharp.

Indeed, Calvisius <note>Sethus Calvisius (1556–1615), a renowned German music theorist and composer.</note> once wrote to me (and I was already of the same opinion, and for this reason had intended to have two separate stops in the Court Chapel here set half a tone lower) that he had often thought—since some organists would be unaccustomed to the new keys <note>original: "Clavium"</note> on the organs—whether it would not be better if one had placed in the organs, instead of the coarse and other fifths, one or two other stopped <term>Gedackt</term> <note>A "Gedackt" is an organ stop made of "stopped" or capped pipes, producing a sweet, flute-like tone.</note> or open, lovely sounding stops voiced at 8-foot pitch, which were tuned a whole tone or a minor third <note>original: "Semiditonum"</note>...

<sig>k</sig>
<meta>catchword: lower</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the technical difficulties of transposing music for keyboard instruments, highlighting how certain transpositions lead to dissonant major thirds (like D-sharp) due to the tuning systems of the time. The author suggests using split keys on organs or adding specifically tuned stops to accommodate different keys more harmoniously.</summary>
<keywords>transposition, organ tuning, temperament, major third, D-sharp, split keys, Sethus Calvisius, Gedackt, musical modes, Syntagma Musicum</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical transposition and the practical challenges organists face when adapting vocal scores to their instruments, specifically focusing on the limitations of keyboard tuning and the "false" sounds created by certain keys.</meta>

...tuned lower than the entire organ so that it could be used for music-making. Although it is true that many **fifths** <note>original: "Quinten"</note>—for which some organ builders have a particular, though misguided <note>original: "sed malè"</note>, passion—are of very little use in the organ, especially the coarse ones of 6-foot length, one could certainly manage with a **fifth** of 3 feet in the **Upper-Work** <term>Oberwerk</term> <note>The chest of pipes located physically above the main manual of the organ.</note> and one of one-and-a-half feet in the **Chair Organ** <term>Rückpositiv</term> <note>The division of the organ located behind the organist’s back.</note>. Yet, in small organs where one cannot easily do without the other stops, this is not tolerable. One can, however, do this: tune the **Regals** <note>A small pipe organ or an organ stop that uses reeds to create a buzzing sound.</note> and other **reed stops** <term>Schnarrwerk</term> a **semitone** <note>original: "Semitonium"</note> higher or lower than the organ itself. To go further, up to a whole tone, is difficult to achieve, as anyone familiar with the nature of reed stops will easily realize and experience for themselves when trying.

In those places where, for the sake of the singers' voices <note>original: "propter voces Cantorum"</note>, it is customary to sing low (especially in church), one can transpose such **modes** <term>Modi</term> <note>Musical scales or systems used before the modern major/minor system.</note> by a **fifth** <note>original: "quintam"</note>. However, in some large Catholic chapels (as mentioned in Volume 2, Chapter 2 of the second part), the **transposed or "soft" Hypoionian mode** <note>original: "Hypojonicus transpositus seu mollis"</note> is moved by a whole **seventh** from D, and the **Hypodorian** by a **third** from E. This, however, is very low and unpleasant for the **sopranos** <term>Discantisten</term> <note>Boys or men singing the highest vocal part.</note> to sing, unless **castrati** <note>original: "Eunuchi"</note> and **falsettists** perform the best parts.

One also often transposes the **"soft" Hypodorian** <note>original: "Hypodorium mollem"</note> by a tone—that is, a **second** lower—starting from F. In that case, the B-flat <note>original: "♭"</note> must necessarily have two keys <note>original: "zweene Claves"</note> in all octaves if one wishes to have and use the **minor third** <note>original: "tertiam minorem"</note> between F and A. Sometimes other **modes** are also sung a tone lower, such as the **Dorian** from C (with B-flat); the **Hypomixolydian** from F; the **Hypoaeolian** from G (with B-flat); and the **Ionian** from B-flat. Every organist must rightly adapt to this.

As for those **songs** <note>original: "Cantiones"</note> which span three octaves, where the **soprano** is high and the **bass** is low—such as from F to high A, or G to high B, etc.—one can transpose neither by a **fourth** nor a **fifth**. Instead, they must be left as the composer set them, or, for the sake of the **sopranos**, transposed into a **neighboring key by a lower tone**.

This can be handled quite well on some new organs where the **B-natural key** <note>original: "Clavis ♮"</note> is **doubled** <note>This refers to "split keys" or subsemitones, which allowed organists to play different tunings for the same note (like D-sharp and E-flat) to avoid harsh intervals.</note>.

It is also necessary to mention here that the **Ionian mode**, when set in the **natural and regular system**, should be played a tone higher. However, when it is found in the **transposed system**, it is very convenient to play it a **lower third** from D (as a "fictional" key) <note>original: "auẞm d fictè"</note>; for in the **regular** system it is too low and sleepy, but in **transposition** it is too high...

<summary>Michael Praetorius discusses the technical challenges of transposing music to accommodate singers, suggesting adjustments to organ tuning and the use of split keys. ...is high and uncomfortable for the singers to sing, it can be performed as seen in the following examples:

->*The regular Ionian mode.* <note>original: *Ionicus regularis*; this refers to the C major scale in the authentic church mode system.</note><- ->*Raised by a minor tone.* <note>original: *Per Tonum minorem elevatus*</note><-

<image-desc>Two musical staves printed side-by-side. The left stave displays a scale in C major using white mensural notation, titled "The regular Ionian mode." The right stave displays a scale in D major with two sharps indicated, titled "Raised by a minor tone."</image-desc>

->*The transposed Ionian mode.* <note>original: *Ionicus transpositus*</note><- ->*Lowered by a third.* <note>original: *Per tertiam depressus*</note><-

<image-desc>Two musical staves printed side-by-side. The left stave displays a scale in F major with a B-flat clef signature, titled "The transposed Ionian mode." The right stave displays a scale in A major with three sharps indicated, titled "Lowered by a third."</image-desc>

However, it is not always necessary to transpose every song into a **fifth** <note>original: *quintam*</note> or **fourth** <note>original: *quartam*</note> in the musical **score** <term>Partitur</term> or in the **figured bass** <term>General Baß</term>. Instead, one should simply set and write it down as it is found in the notes themselves. This is because **transposition** <term>Transposition</term>, especially by a **fifth** <note>original: *per quintam*</note>, is much easier to observe and understand in standard notation than in the German letter **tablature** <term>Tabulatur</term> <note>A system of musical notation using letters of the alphabet rather than notes on a staff, common for keyboard and lute music in this era.</note>. This is due to the fact that one can easily imagine a different **clef** <term>Clavem signatam</term> <note>literally "marked key," referring to the clef sign at the beginning of a musical staff.</note> at the beginning and follow it accordingly. 

If someone is unaccustomed to this and cannot find their way quickly at first, they can write the correct **clef** <term>Clavem signatam</term> on a small piece of paper and stick it onto the lines with wax; in this way, they have it before them exactly as they desire. In the same manner, in my *Terpsichore* <note>A famous 1612 collection of over 300 instrumental dances by Praetorius.</note>, I have marked two different **clefs** <term>Claves signatas</term> at the beginning of several **Courantes** <term>Courranten</term> <note>A lively French dance in triple meter.</note> for the purpose of **transposition** <note>original: *propter transpositionum*</note> on the instruments. I consider it not unhelpful to include something about this here for the benefit of the less experienced, to provide better instruction.

->*The regular Ionian mode.* <note>original: *Ionicus regularis*</note><- ->*Transposed down a fifth.* <note>original: *Per quintam inferiorem transpositos*</note><-

If, however, one intends to transpose these **modes** <term>Modi</term> down a fourth <note>original: *in quartam inferiorem*</note>, it cannot be done in that manner. Instead, I must imagine a different **clef** <note>original: *Clavem*</note> and play a fifth <note>original: *Quint*</note> higher, in the following way:

<image-desc>Musical notation using diamond-shaped mensural notes on four staves. It features various clefs, including C and F clefs, and accidentals such as sharps and flats. The staves are separated by double bar lines.</image-desc>

But then the **octave** <note>original: *Octava*</note> below must be played on the **keys** <note>original: *Clavirn*</note>. And thus a double **transposition** <term>transpositio</term> occurs here: one by a **fifth higher** <note>original: *per Quintam Superiorem*</note> when fingering the keys, the other by an **octave lower** <note>original: *per octavam inferiorem*</note> when **striking** <note>original: *Schlagen*; a period term for the physical act of playing a keyboard instrument</note> the notes.

### Chapter 10.
->*Regarding the labeling of musical parts or voices.*<- <note>original: *De Cantilena Partium ſeu vocum Inſcriptione*</note>

# How the **parts** <term>Partes</term>, part-books, or voices—which are otherwise called **Soprano** <term>Cantus</term>, **Alto** <term>Altus</term>, **Tenor**, **Bass** <term>Bassus</term>, **Fifth Voice** <term>Quintus</term>, etc.—should be appropriately marked with numbers and distinguished from one another.

Since I have found that in various **parts** <term>Partes</term> or voices (as they are usually called), it has seldom been observed which voices should be placed in proper order for each **choir** <term>Chori</term>; as a result, arranging several **concertos** <term>Concert</term> <note>In this period, a 'concerto' refers to a sacred vocal work with instrumental accompaniment, rather than the modern purely instrumental form.</note> in quick succession can be very laborious and tedious. For it is often found that in one song, a part belongs to the first choir, but in the following song, that same part belongs to the second or third choir, etc. This greatly **perturbs and confuses** <note>original: *perturbiret vnd confundiret*</note> anyone who wishes to organize the music **spontaneously** <note>original: *ex improviſo*</note>. This is especially problematic when the **choirs** <term>Chori</term> are positioned far away from one another, requiring one to take a part-book from one person and give it to another, and thus run from one place to another carrying the parts. Not to mention other inconveniences that everyone experiences for themselves and which are unnecessary to recount here. Therefore, I have wanted to devise this method whereby one [marks] the **parts** <term>Partes</term> (Par-

He proposes a new system of numbering parts to prevent confusion when choirs are physically separated in a church or hall.</summary>
<keywords>Aeolian mode, part-books, music organization, choir management, performance practice, terminology, multi-choir music</keywords>

## In the III, IV, V, and VI Styles.

The voices and parts that are sung by the human voice <note>original: *humana voce*</note>—which were called <term>Concertato Parts</term> <note>original: *ConcertatStimmen*. These are soloistic vocal parts meant to stand out from the ensemble.</note> in the first chapter of the third part—are always placed at the beginning and designated with the first numbers, according to how they are arranged within their choirs. Only after these do the <term>Instrumental Choirs and Ensembles</term> <note>original: *Chori vnd Capellæ Instrumentales*</note> follow. This is because, in this style, the Concertato parts are the primary work; if instrumentalists are unavailable, these parts can even be sung and performed alone, accompanied only by an organ, a <term>positive organ</term> <note>A small, portable pipe organ.</note>, or a <term>regal</term> <note>A small portable reed organ with beating reeds.</note>.

Even if five parts are occasionally set in some choirs—which might seem to cause a bit of disorder in the previously established numbering—one can find one's way through it much more easily and better than would otherwise be possible.

Since we must eventually reach the point where, after the Soprano, Alto, Tenor, and Bass <note>original: *Cantu, Alto, Tenore vnd Basso*</note>, we begin counting as the fifth voice, sixth voice, and so on <note>original: *Quintus, Sextus, &c.*</note> up to the twelfth and fourteenth voices <note>original: *usque ad Duodecimum & Quartumdecimum*</note>—as can be seen clearly enough in the <term>Symphonies</term> <note>original: *Symphoniis*</note> and <term>Canzonas</term> <note>original: *Canzonen*</note> by the most excellent and highly famous musician, Giovanni Gabrieli, published this year in Venice, as well as in other works <note>original: *operibus*</note>—I have little doubt that my intention will be well-received and not misinterpreted.

However, since in those concertos where more than 14 parts are present, the remaining parts must be distributed among the others, the internal numbers cannot always match the external number printed on the title page for every part and in every location. Therefore, in each *Polyhymnia* <note>This refers to the author's published collections of sacred music, likely the *Polyhymnia Caduceatrix & Panegyrica*.</note>, I have placed a special table next to the <term>clefs</term> <note>original: *Clavibus signatis*</note> in the 15th part, near the <term>thorough-bass</term> <note>original: *Basso continuo*</note> at the end, so that one can quickly find what is needed in a hurry and act accordingly.

### N B.

I also wish to mention here: that in the <term>General Basses</term> <note>original: *General Bässen*; also known as basso continuo.</note>, I have always noted at the end of each piece how many <term>measures</term> <note>original: *Tempora* - units of musical time or beats.</note> each song, or each section of a song <note>original: *pars Cantionis*</note>, contains. For I found it necessary to observe how many measures <note>original: *tempora*</note> can be performed in a quarter of an hour when maintaining a proper, moderate beat <note>original: *Tact*</note>. Namely:

80.

80 <term>measures</term> <note>original: *tempora*. In this context, these are units of musical time or beats used to measure duration.</note> in { half } a quarter hour.
160 } a whole { quarter hour.
320 } { half } an hour.
640 } { a whole hour.

In this way, one can better plan and determine how long a particular song or <term>concerted work</term> <note>original: *Concert*. A multi-part musical composition, often involving a mix of voices and instruments.</note> might last, so that the sermon is not delayed <note>original: *remorirt*</note>, but can be started at the proper time, and so that the other church ceremonies can be performed alongside it.

### The XI. Chapter.
#### *Distinction and Naming of Various Choirs.* <note>original: *Distinctio & Denominatio Variorum Chororum*</note>
## How the different CHOIRS are to be distinguished and identified with numbers.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'A' featuring floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

At this point, I must also not overlook the fact that various methods and opinions, styles and views are found regarding how to distinguish between choirs.

1. Some begin counting from the bottom and take the lowest choir as the **first choir** <note>original: *primum Chorum*</note>, the next following as the **second**, and so on. In a work set for two choirs, the highest is the **second**; with three choirs, the **third**; with four, the **fourth**, and so forth. They do this for the reason that the lowest choir, as the true foundation, must be present first and foremost, and therefore should rightly have precedence: *For unless someone has faithfully laid the foundation, whatever he builds upon it will collapse.* <note>original: *Nam nisi quis fundamentum fideliter jecerit, quicquid superstruxerit corruet.*</note>

2. Some call the choir that begins first the **first**, as if it were the **beginning of the song** <note>original: *primordium cantionis*</note>. The one that follows it is the **second**, and so on. In my opinion, however, this method must necessarily only be used in those compositions that have equal choirs <note>Choirs with the same vocal ranges, such as two four-part SATB choirs.</note>.

3. Some begin from the top and call the **upper choir** <note>original: *superiorem Chorum*</note> the **first**; the next following, which is set lower, the **second**, and so on. In this system, the lowest choir is the very last—the **second** in a two-choir work, or the **third** in a three-choir work.

<summary>Praetorius provides a mathematical table for estimating musical performance time to ensure services stay on schedule, then outlines different historical methods for numbering and prioritizing choirs in a multi-choir setup.</summary>
<keywords>time management, musical measures, church liturgy, choir organization, musical foundation, polychoral music, performance duration</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how to number and organize multiple choirs in large-scale musical works. The previous page established that some prefer to count from the lowest choir upward because the bass provides the "foundation."</meta>

In a work for four choirs, the next would be called the *Fourth Choir* <note>original: *Quartus Chorus*</note>, and so on. I have observed this order in all my own modest *compositions* <note>original: *Compositionibus*</note>. However, the very famous and excellent *musicians* <note>original: *Musici*</note>, such as *Andrea* and *Giovanni Gabrieli*, almost always *observed* <note>original: *observiret*</note> a second method of numbering choirs in their early *compositions*, whether they were for equal or unequal *choirs*. They named the *choir* that begins the piece—whether it be the lowest or the highest—the *First* <note>original: *Primum*</note>, and then named the others *Second* and *Third* <note>original: *Secundum vnnd Tertium*</note> according to the order in which they follow one another in singing. Occasionally, they also use a third method: numbering the highest *choir* as the *first* <note>original: *primum*</note>, etc. This third method is most commonly found in the later *works* <note>original: *I. G. Operibus*; referring to Giovanni Gabrieli</note>.

*Lambert de Saive* has *observed* the first method in his recently published and very famous *concertos* <note>original: *Concerten*</note>; *Paulus Sartorius* uses the second; while the excellent *contrapuntist* <note>original: *Contrapunctista*</note> and *musician*, *Johann Stadelmayr* (the Archducal Chapel Master <note>original: *Capellmeister*</note> at *Graz*), *Alexius Alexander*, and most *Italian* and *German musicians* keep to the third style and method, as I can *observe* from their publicly printed works. Indeed, they follow this third method so strictly that even among equal *choirs*—which share the same *clef signatures* <note>original: *Claves signatas*</note>—they always *label* <note>original: *inscribiren*</note> the *choir* that goes deepest into the bass range as the *second choir*, even if it begins first without any *rests* <note>original: *Pausen*</note> and could very well be called the *first choir* according to the second method.

Now, although it does not matter all that much whether one begins counting from the top or the bottom, it nonetheless causes great confusion, and one who is not used to a particular system cannot find their way through it quickly—unless they change the *numbering* <note>original: *Numerum*</note> to the style they are accustomed to.

In the works of some *authors*, it is found that they maintain no *distinction* <note>original: *vnderscheyd*</note> in their *works*, but *discern* and *name* the *choirs* sometimes according to the second method and sometimes according to the third. In my opinion, it would be better if they remained consistent with one single certain method. In works for two or three choirs, one could also call the highest *choir* the *Higher* <note>original: *Superiorem*</note>, the next the *Middle* <note>original: *Medium*</note>, and the lowest the *Lowest* or *Lower Choir* <note>original: *Infimum vel Inferiorem Chorum*</note>. But because in works for four, five, or more choirs one cannot do without the words *First, 2nd, 3rd, Fourth Choir* <note>original: *Primus, 2. 3. Quartus Chorus*</note>, etc., it is better to stay with the same names to avoid further confusion.

It is also found that some place the second *soprano* <note>original: *Discant*</note> in the *fifth* or *fifth voice* <note>original: *Quintum oder Quintam Vocem*</note> and the second *tenor* in the *sixth* <note>original: *Sextum*</note>; while others place the second *tenor* in the *fifth* and the second *soprano* <note>original: *Cant*</note> in the *sixth*. I, however, abide by the first opinion.

<sig>M</sig>
<meta>catchword: Some</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes three different historical systems for numbering multiple choirs in polychoral music, citing specific practices of famous composers like the Gabrielis and Stadelmayr. The author argues for consistency in numbering to prevent confusion during performance and rehearsal.</summary>
<keywords>polychoral music, choir numbering, Andrea Gabrieli, Giovanni Gabrieli, Johann Stadelmayr, Lambert de Saive, music theory, clef signatures, voice parts</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the arrangement and numbering of choirs in polychoral music, specifically addressing how to name additional voices and how to layout parts for the convenience of the performers and the director.</meta>

Some people, when composing a *concerted song* <note>A work for multiple voices and instruments, often divided into groups</note> for several choirs where one choir contains five or six voices, name the fifth and sixth voices the *Second Soprano, Second Tenor, or Second Bass of the first or other choir* <note>original: *Secundum Cantum, Secundum Tenorem, Secundum Bassum primi vel alterius Chori*</note>. However, it seems better to me that one should use and keep the terms *Fifth, Sixth, and Seventh* <note>original: *Quintus, Sextus, Septimus*</note> for these voices.

But as for the fact that some—even the most prominent *musicians* <note>original: *Musici*</note>—place the *bass-part of the first choir* <note>original: *Basset primi Chori*; this refers to the lowest voice of a high-pitched choir, which often functions as a tenor</note> (otherwise called the *Bass or lowest voice of the Upper Choir* <note>original: *Bassus vel Infima vox Superioris Chori*</note>) into the *Tenor* partbook, and the *lowest voice of the Lower Choir* <note>original: *Infimam vocem Inferioris Chori*</note> into the *Bass* partbook: this pleases me very much. It is correct and has its *valid reasons* <note>original: *Iustas rationes*</note>. However, when a *Music Director* <note>original: *Directori Musices*</note> is arranging these *concertos*, or when an organist wants to transcribe and set them into a *musical score* <note>original: *Noten-Partitur*</note> or letter *tablature* <note>A system of notation using letters or numbers instead of notes on a staff</note>, it creates a great deal of confusion and inconvenience. Therefore, I leave it to each individual to think further on the matter, whether my opinion set forth in the previous 9th Chapter is not more correct: that the voices of each choir should follow one another quickly according to their *numbers* <note>original: *Numeris*</note>, and thus be placed into the partbooks according to that same order.

Furthermore, when two voices must be printed one above the other, I have always brought the *Soprano* <note>original: *Cant*</note> and *Tenor* together, and the *Alto* and *Bass* together. Thus, the *numbers* <note>original: *Numeris*</note> are divided there so that 1 and 3, and 2 and 4, etc., are found together. The reason is that I have not only seen this most often among *musicians*, but it is also better for the sake of the *harmony* and *concordance* <note>The pleasing sound of notes played together</note> itself. When singers must stand so close together and perform from a single part, they should do so with lovely *sixths*, as the *Soprano* and *Tenor* harmonize; or with *fifths* and *octaves*, as the *Alto* and *Bass* *concord* together. They should not have to listen to the jarring *fourths*, such as those between the *Soprano* and *Alto*, or *Tenor* and *Alto*, which usually move together. Such intervals fill the ears of one singer with the other's part and make the whole song unpleasant. It even makes one weary of singing, especially if the other two parts are not positioned very close by to complete the *harmony* and bring it to *perfection*.

However, in this matter, as in all others, I do not wish to prescribe anything to anyone, but only to indicate my simple opinion and what I have found to be good from my own experience. For every man will surely know his own *reasons* <note>original: *Rationes*</note> and causes, and will act accordingly.

---

## Chapter XII.

<summary>The author argues for a standardized numbering of voices (Fifth, Sixth, Seventh) and suggests pairing voices that harmonize well, such as Soprano/Tenor and Alto/Bass, to assist singers and directors.</summary>
<keywords>voice naming, partbook organization, musical intervals, sixths, octaves, choral arrangement, music theory, Michael Praetorius</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page follows a discussion on the naming conventions for voices in large ensembles and the practicalities of arranging scores for directors and organists. It transitions here into a technical defense of specific harmonic choices in polychoral composition.</meta>

# Concerning the progression of unisons and octaves, and their limited use in songs for distinct multiple-voice choirs.
M 2
<meta>catchword: Tenor</meta>

### On the ways in which unisons and octaves can be used, passed, and permitted.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring intricate scrollwork and foliage within a circular frame.</image-desc>
In my *UranoChorodia* <note>A collection of hymn settings published by Michael Praetorius in 1613</note>, I initially introduced several reasons <note>original: *rationes*</note>—based on highly significant causes and important arguments <note>original: *rationibus*</note>—for why I set the Sopranos <note>original: *Discant*</note>, and especially the Basses <note>original: *Bässe*</note>, in unison <note>original: *In Vnisono*</note> in choral music <note>original: *im Choral*</note> when the choirs come together. Therefore, it is unnecessary to repeat much of that here. Nevertheless, because this was not so much in use in previous times, and many who have not seen this style before might take occasion to quibble <note>original: *cavilliren*</note> over one thing or another, I have deemed it necessary to explain the aforementioned reasons a bit more extensively and bring them to light. This is so that they may be sufficiently convinced by logic <note>original: *rationibus*</note> and authority <note>original: *autoritate*</note> and might agree with me in this matter.

### Why, namely, not only the Soprano and Bass, but also the middle voices, can be set together in unison and also sometimes in octaves in a full choir quite well and without accusation of faultiness <note>original: *Vitiositet*; in musical theory, this refers to "vices" or errors in composition like forbidden parallel intervals</note>:

->Namely:<-

### *SOPRANO.* <note>original: *CANTUS*</note>

So that the Soprano <note>original: *Discant*</note>—because it can otherwise easily be drowned out by the other singers, such as the Altos <note>original: *Altisten*</note> and Tenors <note>original: *Tenoristen*</note>, due to the gentle and lovely voices of the young boys—may be heard more clearly everywhere in all choirs and in various places and ends, and so that the text may be better understood.

### *MIDDLE VOICES,* the inner voices, such as the Alto and

<summary>Praetorius defends his use of unisons and octaves between different choirs in a large-scale work, arguing that doubling the soprano melody is necessary for the text to be heard over the lower voices.</summary>
<keywords>unison, octaves, part-writing, soprano, vocal doubling, polychoral music, UranoChorodia, Michael Praetorius</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical justification for using unisons in polychoral music, specifically addressing the middle voices and the critical role of the Bass as the harmonic foundation.</meta>

**Tenors** can likewise proceed in unison across each and every choir. For in such cases, it sounds no different than when, among the singers <note>original: *Cantoribus*</note> in a large group, eight, nine, or ten boys—or sometimes an instrumentalist on a **trombone** <note>original: *Posaun*</note>, **cornett** <note>original: *Zincken*</note>, or **violin** <note>original: *Geigen*</note>—are placed together at a single location to play or sing a single voice part. 

When one arranges distinct choirs by separating them from one another—placing one here, another there, a third even further away, and so on—it is far better (and the **harmony** <note>original: *Harmonia*</note> will sound much more complete throughout the entire church) if the middle voices in each choir proceed in their full integrity. This is preferable to hacking and butchering them with all sorts of rests and breaths <note>original: *suspiriis*</note>, as well as many awkward **syncopations** <note>original: *Syncopationibus*</note>, simply to avoid unisons. Such hacking makes the notes difficult to sing and the text very hard to apply, so that it often sounds nothing like a song at all.

->B A S S<-

Finally, because the **Bass** is the foundation <note>original: *Fundament*</note> of all voices in every place and part, it must be heard more distinctly and properly than the other voices—especially when the choirs are positioned at a great distance from one another in the church. 

For if one Bass is at the **fifth** <note>original: *Quint*</note> above the true **foundation bass** <note>original: *FundamentBaß*</note>—which must necessarily happen in such compositions if one wishes to follow the rules of music—the foundation bass might not be heard with equal strength in every spot because it is at another distant location. This produces a very unpleasant harmony, because the **Tenor** or **Soprano** <note>original: *Cantus*</note>, which would otherwise stand an **octave** <note>original: *Octaven*</note> apart from the true foundation bass, becomes poorly disposed or even produces a discordant sound when placed a **fourth** <note>original: *Quart*</note> or **eleventh** <note>original: *Vndecima*</note> above this "middle" bass. 

This can only be avoided if one has a **harmonic body** <note>original: *FundamentCorpus*</note>, such as a **regal** <note>a small portable reed organ</note> or **positive organ** <note>a small, non-portable organ without pedals</note>, nearby. Such instruments are rarely available in town churches, though they are found in princely chapels <note>original: *Fürstlichen Capellen*</note>. In such a setup, the **organist** must always keep the lowest bass as the foundation when the choirs play together. Otherwise, it is certain and true—and experience and hearing bear witness—that it creates more **dissonance** <note>original: *Dissonantz*</note> than **consonance** <note>original: *Consonantz*</note>. 

Therefore, this [practice of unisons] is especially necessary in schools and town churches, where one cannot have an organist, regal, or positive organ for every choir, as is possible in princely and other chapels. For this reason, the excellent German musician **Jacob Regnart** <note>original: *Iacobus Regnardus*; a prolific Flemish-born composer who worked in the Habsburg courts</note>, already more than ten years ago, wrote several **masses** <note>original: *Missen*</note> for eight voices in which both basses are always in unison when the choirs come together with...

<meta>catchword: with each [einan-]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that middle voices and basses should play in unison in large churches to ensure a full, stable sound. It explains that "hacking" music with rests to avoid unisons ruins the song, and that a strong, unified bass foundation is essential for harmony when choirs are physically separated.</summary>
<keywords>polychoral music, musical foundation, bass, unison, church acoustics, Jacob Regnart, organ, regal, musical theory</keywords>

...proceeding with one another / allowing them to be put into print and published: Also at this current time, they are used everywhere by the Italians without distinction / so that the voices proceed together not only in **unisons** <note>original: *Vnisonis*</note> but also in **octaves**. For this reason, I have chosen to include here, word for word, the 16th chapter of the 2nd book, part 2, of the excellent **Music Theorist** <note>original: *Musici Theorici*</note>, **Giovanni Maria Artusi of Bologna** <note>original: *Ioan Mariae Artusij Benoniensis*</note>, where he treats the **art of counterpoint** <note>original: *artem de Contrapuncto*</note> very clearly and likewise provides detailed **reasons** <note>original: *rationes*</note> and causes for this / translated **from Italian into our German language** <note>original: *ex Italico in nostrum idioma Germanicum*</note>.

->*Opinion Regarding Musical Concertos.*<-

# What is to be generally observed and noted in all musical concertos.

,, THE **obscurities** <note>original: *Obscuritates*</note> or defects / which one often hears in concertos <note>The term "Concerten" here refers to "concerted" music, where different groups of voices and instruments play together.</note> / are of three types:
,, 1. First / that the **intellects** <note>original: *Ingenia*</note> are not always sharp enough to explore and fathom the art of a composed concerto.
2. Second / That the **instruments** <note>original: *Instrumenta*</note> are not **arranged** <note>original: *accommodiret*</note> according to the merit or nature of the concerto; Or also that the same **instruments** <note>original: *Instrumenta*</note> **clash** <note>original: *discordiren*</note> with the human voices / and do not harmonize together.
3. The third and foremost cause / about which I now wish to speak more extensively / is that many are found (without disparaging any noble composers) / who do not take enough care / that in the preparation of a concerto they place the deep Bass in or from one choir alone: While in the other choir (meaning // when all the choirs sing together) they use the Bass at a fifth, third, or octave / and thus bring with it a kind of sadness / which offends the ear more than it delights it. But this happens because these voices / over which the others / namely the upper and middle voices, are made and confirmed / and which first held the Bass and the foundation for their choir / these now become
,, **Middle Voices** <note>original: *MittelStimmen*</note> / and so with a single Bass that thing is performed /
,, which otherwise should properly happen in each individual choir: Therefore the single

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the translated excerpt from Giovanni Maria Artusi regarding the proper arrangement of voices and instruments in concerted music, focusing specifically on the necessity of a strong bass foundation across multiple choirs.</meta>

...the **bass** is so weak that one can often hardly hear it; and thus the **Principal** <note>The "Principal" here refers to the leading or most prominent melody parts.</note> and most important voices, which should be heard before all others and, as it were, open up the hearing, now bring about a sadness and a bad **disposition** <note>original: *affectum*; referring to the emotional effect or mood of the music.</note>, as a knowledgeable person can sufficiently see and perceive in such **concertos** <note>original: *Concerten*; "concerted" music where groups of voices and instruments play together.</note>.

,, To prevent the aforementioned disorder, it is highly praised that in the joining of the **choirs** <note>original: *Choren*</note>, those voices which are the lowest in each **choir** <note>original: *Chor*</note>—namely the **basses**—should all modulate and harmonize together in **unison** <note>original: *Vnisono*</note>, so that the entire **composition** in all **choirs** receives a truly **sufficient foundation** <note>original: *sufficiens Fundamentum*</note>. Furthermore, no **minor sixth** <note>original: *Sexta Minor*</note> or **minor third** <note>original: *Tertia Minor*</note> should be used or placed in the middle **parts** <note>original: *Parteyen*</note> against the bass, because they offend the hearing more than they delight it.

And in this way, the **choirs** <note>original: *Chori*</note> which are found far from one another—that is, those placed in separate locations—are much more **nourished** <note>original: *nutriret*</note> and maintained through such **unisons** <note>original: *Vnisonos*</note>: than if their basses and **foundation** voices were placed among the middle parts, or used as a middle **part** <note>original: *Partey*</note>. For then one must hear and say that these **choirs** are without bass and **foundation**.

What kind of good **effect** <note>original: *effectum*</note> could be achieved if the work were performed in one place and the **foundation** were laid in another place?

Likewise, what kind of lovely **harmony** <note>original: *Harmoniam*</note> it could bring when one hears three or four voices of a song without a bass, or else separated so far from the single **foundation**-bass that one can hardly hear it; every person can weigh and consider this for themselves.

Therefore, it is much better, and the song will have much more **harmony** <note>original: *Harmoniæ*</note>, if—when the **choirs** come together or proceed with one another—the **basses** are then all set in **unison** <note>original: *Vnisonum*</note>. Since they are, in such a way, those voices which, as it were, maintain, nourish, and increase the **harmony**, according to the verse of **Mantuanus** <note>Baptista Mantuanus (1447–1516), a famous Neo-Latin poet whose works were often cited by musicians.</note>:

->*The bass nourishes the voices, strengthens, founds, and increases them.* <note>original: *Bassus alit voces, ingrassat (Confortat) fundat & auget.*</note><-

->**And thus far Giovanni Maria Artusi.** <note>original: *Ioh. Maria Artusius*</note><-

And it occurs to me that I read in one place before this: **In motets** <note>original: *Mutetis*</note> **of eight, 10, 12, 16, or many voices, when the choirs are separated at a greater distance, the true basis or lowest voice in each choir, if all are mixed together, should have—** <note>original: *In octo, 10. 12. 16. aut plurimum vocum Mutetis, quando Chori longiùs disiunguntur, vera Basis vel vox infima, in quolibet Choro, si omnes simul miscentur, habeat...*</note>

The author then begins to cite another source supporting the same rule for large-scale motets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the arrangement of musical parts, specifically addressing the use of unisons and octaves between vocal and instrumental groups to ensure a solid harmonic foundation.</meta>

...be heard; and especially at the end, it should be heard perfectly above the others; otherwise **cacophony** <note>original: *κακοφωνία* (kakophonia); a harsh, discordant sound.</note> exists where a foundation is not laid underneath, as the compositional table and experience testify.

From this, it is now sufficiently and clearly to be seen and understood that **unisons** <note>original: *Vniſoni*; when two or more parts play or sing the same pitch.</note> can be applied and used throughout, both in voices and instruments, in high, low, and middle registers, very well and without any further hesitation.

However, as regarding **octaves**, one must deal with them somewhat more sensibly and neatly.

->*Octaves can be tolerated in all voices: when one voice sings, the other sounds.*<-

For in the arrangement of **concertos** <note>In the 17th-century sense, this refers to "concerted" music where groups of voices and instruments are contrasted and combined.</note> (as will soon follow in Part 3), it is quite common that for a low-pitched **choir**—where the **soprano** <note>original: *Cantus*; the highest melodic part.</note> part must be sung by an **alto** singer accompanied by three **trombones** <note>original: *Poſaunen*</note> or three **bassoons** <note>original: *Fagotten*</note>—one assigns a **treble violin** <note>original: *DiſcantGeigen*</note> to the alto singer. Then the instrumentalist on the violin must play whatever the alto sings an **octave higher** or (as some say) "play it up":

Thus, in a **full choir** <note>original: *pleno Choro*</note>, or otherwise when only a few choirs perform together, one can easily have the **alto** part (which is in the vocal choir) written an **octave higher** <note>original: *in die Octavam ſuperiorem*</note> in the instrumental choir; so that from the alto, instead of the

The **soprano** <note>original: *Cantus*</note>, however, remains in the <image-desc>A five-line musical staff showing an alto clef (C-clef on the third line) positioned lower on the page.</image-desc> and is titled with the word **Alto** <note>original: *Altus*</note>. And then it often happens that where previously the alto and soprano were at an interval of a **fourth** <note>original: *Quarten*</note>, they now become **fifths** <note>original: *Quinten*</note> up above. Some believe that this can be allowed in such a manner; a view which I cannot yet agree with at this time.
