<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the use of octaves and unisons when combining voices and instruments, specifically how to maintain harmonic balance when substituting one for the other.</meta>

->Soprano, Alto, Tenor, Bass<-

<image-desc>A printed musical staff containing four different clefs. From left to right: a Soprano (C) clef on the first line, an Alto clef on the third line, a Tenor clef on the fourth line, and a Bass (F) clef on the fourth line. The letters C., A., T., and B. are printed above their respective clefs.</image-desc>

If one now wishes, in the absence of instruments, to staff such a choir with **cantors** and **vocalists** <note>original: *Cantoribus vnd Vocalisten*</note>, one should have the **alto** part sung by a **soprano** <note>original: *Discantisten*</note> and the **soprano** part sung by an **alto** an **octave lower**; in this way, it returns to its proper original state.

And **for this reason** <note>original: *hac ratione*</note>, one can easily allow this to happen across all voices; it produces no **unpleasant sound to the ears** <note>original: *ingratum sonum auribus*</note> when that which the **lead singer** <note>original: *Concentor*</note> sings with the human voice is performed by an instrumentalist on **cornetts, violins, flutes, trombones, or bassoons** <note>original: *Zincken / Geygen / Flöitten / Posaun oder Fagotten*</note> an octave higher or lower. For some **simple instruments** <note>original: *Instrumenta simplicia*</note>, especially **flutes**—as can be seen with more detail in Volume II, Chapter IV—are always to be reckoned one or even two octaves higher according to their **foot-pitch** <note>original: *Fuβthon*; a term referring to the length of organ pipes that determines their octave register</note> than the vocal part itself is written.

In such a case, it is no different than when many various stops in an **organ**, which agree with one another in **unisons, octaves, super-octaves**, and even **sub-octaves** in the large **sub-bass** <note>original: *Vntersatz*; the deepest, foundational pipes of an organ</note> and (as some call them) **contrabasses**, are pulled together. Therefore, it also yields a magnificent harmony in a **full choir** <note>original: *pleno Choro*</note> when, for a bass part where many instrumentalists are present, one arranges a common or **quarto-trombone**, a **chorist-bassoon**, or a **large bombard** <note>original: *QuartPosaun / ChoristFagott / PommerBombard*; these are specific sizes of Renaissance wind instruments</note> which play the bass at the correct pitch; and alongside them an **octave-trombone, double bassoon**, or **large double bombard**, and **large double bass** <note>original: *OctavPosaun / doppel Fagott / groß doppel Bombard / groß Baßgeyg*</note>, which—just like the **sub-basses** or **sub-foundations** <note>original: *SubBäße oder Vntersätze*</note> in organs—sound an **octave lower**. This is very common in those **Italian concertos** and is quite justifiable.

Therefore, in the **Capella Fidicinia** <note>original: *Capella Fidicinia*; a term for a string-based ensemble used to fill out the harmony</note> (of which a report will follow later), I have arranged it without hesitation so that when two, three, or four **sopranos**, or two **tenors**, sing together, the parts mixed into the **String Ensemble** with violins or other instruments **to complete the harmony** <note>original: *ad complendam Harmoniam*</note> sometimes also proceed in octaves with the **human voices** <note>original: *Vocibus humanis*</note>. Any sensible musician who thinks further on these matters will certainly approve and agree with me in this regard.

Although in my **Uranichordodia** <note>original: *VranoChorodiâ*; one of Praetorius’s published collections of sacred music</note> I have in several places the cho-

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the use of octaves between different vocal parts, specifically focusing on when it is permissible for the bass parts of separate choirs to double each other at the octave.</meta>

# 97
## N
<meta>catchword: Octaves</meta>

generally in the **Soprano** and **Alto** <note>original: *Discant vnd Alt*</note>—which must be sung **with the live voice** <note>original: *viva voce*</note>—set in **octaves**; yet I have introduced these **reasons** <note>original: *rationes*</note> there among others: because the entire congregation in the church, small and large, high and low, is accustomed to singing along with the **chorale** <note>original: *Choral*; refers to the plainchant or congregational hymn tune</note> at the same time, etc. Otherwise, outside of the chorale, I cannot allow this to pass at all.

This, however, is now quite common: that in **Italian-style concertos** <note>original: *Italorum Concerten*</note>, where high and low choir **bassettos** <term>Basset: the lowest voice part of a high-register choir, which functions as a bass but is written in a higher clef</term> are present, the **bassetto of the upper choir** <note>original: *Basset Superioris Chori*</note> mostly proceeds in octaves with the **bass of the lower or other choir** <note>original: *Baß inferioris vel alterius Chori*</note> when the choirs come together.

Although this could occasionally be changed in **compositions** <note>original: *Cantionibus*</note> for two or three choirs—specifically by letting one bass ascend and the other descend, **and vice-versa** <note>original: *& vice versâ*</note>—it can nevertheless be excused and justified for the following reasons:

1. Because usually, for such a **bassetto** in the high choir, one assigns a **tenorist** to sing **with the live voice** <note>original: *viva voce*</note>; but in the deep choir, one assigns a **bass trombone** <note>original: *Quart Posaun*</note>, a **large shawm** <note>original: *Pommern Bombard*</note>, or a **double bassoon** <note>original: *doppelte Fagott*</note> to the bass.

2. Even if in those **concertos** performed with 3, 4, or more choirs, and where the basses are set two or three octaves apart, the **bassetto** must proceed in octaves together with the lowest or other basses in different choirs—and even if two or more of those basses are sung **with the live human voice** <note>original: *viva & humana voce*</note>—I cannot simply reject or condemn this. Instead, I must necessarily make use of it and point it out to anyone who takes it in hand: If he arranges such a **concerto** in a church or a large hall with only two choirs, one high and one low, placed far apart at both ends facing each other, and stands by the **upper choir** <note>original: *Choro superiori*</note>, he will find that when both choirs sound together in **full choir** <note>original: *pleno Choro*</note>, he cannot easily hear the deepest choir. Consequently, he will find there is no **foundation** <term>Fundament: the harmonic "ground" or bass line that supports the upper harmonies</term> at all for the higher choir. Instead, due to the lack of the lower fifths which the **foundation bass** <note>original: *Fundament Baß*</note> provides for the **bassetto** or tenor of the high choir, mostly dissonances and fourths will be heard, especially if no **foundation instrument** <note>original: *Fundament Instrument*</note> such as a **positive organ** <note>original: *Positiff*</note> or **regal** <note>original: *Regal*; a small portable organ with reed pipes</note> is present.

> "I have also recently been written to from Venice that the most prominent
> " **musicians in Italy** <note>original: *Musici in Italia*</note> in the **full sections** <note>original: *Ripieni*</note> (that is, in the **full choir** <note>original: *pleno Choro*</note>) use
> " **unisons** and **octaves** <note>original: *Vnisonos vnd Octaven*</note> with all diligence, from their own experience and
> " practice, because such styles in such large churches, where the choirs are far apart,
> " give much better power when they [sing] together with the other choirs in unisons or

<summary>Praetorius discusses the practical and acoustic reasons for allowing bass parts in different choirs to double each other in octaves, particularly in large spaces where choirs are physically separated. He notes that this practice is common among Italian musicians to ensure that high-register choirs maintain harmonic stability and "power" when they cannot hear the main bass of the lower choir.</summary>

<meta>continuity: This page continues the argument for using octaves and unisons between different choirs in polychoral music to ensure a full and resonant sound, addressing potential criticisms from traditional music theorists.</meta>

# 98

,, proceed in **octaves** (in the manner that will soon be indicated); as if
,, one had **studiously** <note>original: *studiosamente*</note> labored to avoid **unisons** and **octaves**
,, with great diligence, such a **perfect harmony** <note>original: *perfecta Harmonia*</note> and **resonance**
,, could then never be perceived or heard: For what one gives to
,, one choir is, in turn, taken away from the other, etc.

And I could certainly name several very distinguished old **musical theorists** and **practitioners** <note>original: *Musicos Theoricos und Practicos*</note> who, in the beginning, did not want to let such a thing pass; later, however, when they took the matter into their own hands and reflected further upon it for themselves, they had to **approve** <note>original: *approbiren*</note> it, admit I was right, and confess that previously they had regarded it as nothing less than a **mortal sin** <note>original: *TodtSünde*</note>. Now, however, they find for themselves that the **unisons**, as well as the **octaves** in the basses, cannot be avoided if a **perfect harmony** is to exist and remain in all the choirs.

Yet one could still employ this remedy: if in a **concerto-style composition** <note>original: *Concert Gesang*</note> such a **basset** <term>Basset: the lowest voice of a choir that is composed of high-range voices</term> set according to **musical rules** <note>original: *Regulis Musicalibus*</note> were found—as **Giovanni Gabrieli** <note>original: *Iohann Gabriel*</note> observed in the **first book** of his **Sacred Symphonies** <note>original: *Symphoniarum sacrarum*</note>, though in the **second** and **third** he did not observe this at all—then one must provide and arrange for that same **upper choir** <note>original: *Choro Superiori*</note> either a **regal**, **positive organ**, or **organ**, or at the very least a **fundamental bass**. This bass, when the choirs fall in together, should be able to carry the **fundamental** <note>original: *Fundament*</note> along at the same time, **either by human voice or by instrument** <note>original: *vel voce humana vel Instrumento*</note>, whether it be with a **trombone**, **bassoon**, or **bass viol** <note>original: *Bassgeigen*</note>, so that the song may not be heard and judged as **imperfect** and incomplete.

And thus, from the **reasons** <note>original: *Rationibus*</note> and causes just indicated, it is easy to conclude:

1. That **UNISONS** are to be used throughout in all choirs and voices without any hesitation.
2. Similarly, that **OCTAVES** in an **instrumental choir** <note>original: *Choro Instrumentali*</note>—when either all sorts of instruments are alone, or instruments and human voices come together—can pass no less than unisons.
3. Furthermore, the basses and **fundamental** voices are to be used in **octaves** as well as in **unisons**.
4. Only in a **vocal choir** <note>original: *Choro Vocali*</note>—where a **soprano** <note>original: *Discantißt*</note> would sing in octaves with a **tenor**, or an **altist** with a **bassist**, etc., simultaneously with one another—can I not yet hold this to be good or let it pass; regardless of the fact that **Stefano Nasimbeni**, **Chapel Master of the Ducal Church in Mantua** <note>original: *Steffanus Nasimbenus Ducalis Ecclesiæ in Mantua Capellæ Magister*</note>, **Valerio Bona in Brescia (and others)** set **octaves** in the **soprano**, middle, and **bass** voices without

<summary>Praetorius argues that octaves and unisons between choirs are necessary for "perfect harmony" and resonance, citing Giovanni Gabrieli's evolving style as evidence. He concludes that while doubling in instrumental and bass parts is acceptable, he still discourages vocal parts like sopranos and tenors doubling each other in octaves.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Gabrieli, resonance, unisons, octaves, fundamental bass, basso continuo, polychoral music, Stefano Nasimbeni, Valerio Bona, orchestration</keywords>

# 99

to use this distinction without any hesitation: Examples of this can also be found in other distinguished **authors** <note>original: *Autoribus*</note> (whom I will not name, as anyone can find them), where they place three kinds of **octaves** <note>original: *Octaven*</note> above one another in the middle voices—two of which are marked **human voice** <note>original: *voce humana*</note>—for several **measures** <note>original: *Tact*</note>.

> Just as **Lodovico Viadana** <note>original: *L. Viadana*</note> also wishes to defend the **use of octaves and unisons** <note>original: *usum Octavarum & Unisonorum*</note> in his **Preface to the Psalms to be sung in four Choirs** <note>original: *Præfation supra Psalmos quatuor Choris concinendos*</note> with the following words; where he says:
> 
> It can happen quite well without any danger and without any **confusion** <note>original: *Confusion*</note> that one may, in **concertos for choirs** <note>original: *Concerten per Choros*</note>, bring in different **ensembles** <note>original: *Capellen*</note> according to one’s pleasure. And it does no harm that the **choirs** create **octaves** and **unisons** <note>original: *Unisonos*</note> with one another, because one cannot particularly perceive or distinguish such things, given that the **choirs** are **separated** <note>original: *separiret*</note> and placed in distinct locations far from each other. However, each **choir** must **observe** <note>original: *observire*</note> and maintain the correct **consonances** <note>original: *Consonantien*</note>, namely according to the **foundation** <note>original: *Fundament*</note> and the designated **concerting** voices <note>original: *Concertat Stimmen*</note> upon which the entire song rests (which shall be discussed much more extensively in the third part); for it pleases me much better to see it done this way, since the **music** and **harmony** <note>original: *Concentus*</note> of the song is **developed** <note>original: *deducirt*; Italian: *riuscirt*</note> and carried out much more magnificently and perfectly than if one wanted to compose and set such **full instrumental parts** <term>Ripieni: supplementary parts used to fill out the texture in a large ensemble</term> and **full choirs** <note>original: *plenos Choros*</note> **exactly** <note>original: *exactè*; Italian: *observatamente*</note> according to the musical rules. For then one must necessarily set so many whole, half, and quarter rests, **sighs** <note>original: *Suspiria*</note>, dots, **illegitimate intervals** <note>original: *illegitima intervalla*</note>, **syncopations** <note>original: *Syncopationes*</note>, and entanglements, such that it is like listening to (**strained, rustic, and stubborn music** <note>original: *Musica stiracchiata, rustica ed ostinata*</note>) a truly mutilated, peasant-like, stiff, and broken music, whose neck has been, as it were, torn off: Because one must always sing in a mutilated and hacked-up manner (**at a breakneck pace** <note>original: *à rompi-collo*</note>), which then occurs with very little **grace** <note>original: *gratia*</note> and no particular delight for the **singer** <note>original: *Cantoris*</note> or the **listener** <note>original: *auditoris*</note>.

Nevertheless, I know well that some who possess a particularly **subtle temperament** <note>original: *subtilen humoren*</note> and wish to make a special **claim** <note>original: *profession*</note> of having a pure, **delicate** <note>original: *delicaten*</note>, and clean ear, may be found: To these people, this **novelty** <note>original: *novitas*</note> and new style does not please at all; rather, they will have a **scruple** <note>original: *Scrupel*</note> and a fault here or there, and will criticize now this, now that. Regardless, many others before me (says **Viadana**) have composed in such a manner and fashion, as among others

> **Pallavicino** <note>original: *Palavicinus*</note> in the *Jubilate* and *Laudate* for 16 voices, in which the **soprano** <note>original: *Cant*</note> and **tenor** are found to be set in **octaves** for 15 or more **measures** <note>original: *Tact*</note> one after the other. But to conclude, I have set such things according to my judgment in this way: If it now

others will also do the same / so such a time will be at hand / when he / who then makes it very badly and sloppily / will be regarded / as if he had made it very excellently and for the best. God be with you. *And thus far Lodovico Viadana.* <note>original: "Et hactenus L. Viadana." Viadana was an influential Italian composer who pioneered the use of basso continuo.</note> If I now, according to my humble ability <note>original: *tenuitet*</note>, should offer my simple opinion / I would wish / that octaves be avoided as much as possible / and only **1.** be used <note>original: *adhibiret*</note> in the little-basses <term>Bassetten: smaller or higher-pitched bass instruments</term>, basses, and lower parts / where it cannot be tolerated otherwise; And then **2.** also in the upper and middle voices / but principally in the Alto: Yet in such a way / that one part is performed vocally <note>original: *Vocaliter*</note>, and the other instrumentally <note>original: *Instrumentaliter*</note>, but not both vocally: Although octaves would finally be more justifiable in an instrumental choir <note>original: *Choro Instrumentali*</note> than in a vocal one for the reasons indicated above: Yet the opinion of one or the other in this matter is not to be disapproved or disregarded.

And therefore it is to be diligently observed / that in those concertos where several different vocal and instrumental choirs or ensembles <note>original: *Chori vel Capellæ vocales & Instrumentales*</note> are present / not only the vocal choirs / or the concerting and vocal parts in themselves / but also the parts in the instrumental choirs and ensembles should likewise be set against each other quite purely / cleanly / and without errors <note>original: *vitiis*</note> according to the rules of music <note>original: *Regulis Musicalibus*</note>: But because it cannot be kept so perfectly straight everywhere in so many parts / unless one would gladly hear such a gaping music <note>original: *hiulcam Musicam*</note> (of which Lodovico Viadana has just reported and indicated) / which is completely mangled and ruined through many pauses, short rests <note>original: *Suspiria*</note>, unfriendly intervals, and insulting leaps <note>original: *insultos saltus*</note>; So one must allow unisons <note>original: *Vnisonos*</note> and other minor forbidden types <note>original: *Species*</note>, which are otherwise permitted in songs of many voices (but fifths not at all): although I see that imperfect fifths <note>original: *Quintæ Imperfectæ*</note> are quite common among the Italians: and also the diminutions <term>Diminutiones: the breaking up of long notes into many fast, short notes</term>, which are sometimes mixed in, help very much to excuse and conceal them. But as far as the octaves are concerned / I will let the matter rest entirely with the aforementioned reminder.

If now the vocal choirs and the instrumental choirs are each set so purely in their own parts / it is considered unnecessary by some that these two kinds of choirs should be revised, weighed <note>original: *ponderiret*</note>, and examined against each other / since one should allow all forbidden types <note>original: *Species*</note> in voice and instrument simultaneously: Which then finally can be modestly allowed in unisons and octaves / for the reasons cited before / but in fifths not at all.

### Chapter I.

->**Exposition** <note>original Greek: *ekthesis*</note>: Explanation of terms for the Instrument and the Instrumentalist; Concerted parts; Vocal Choir, Instrumental Choir; Choir of Viols; etc. The Refrain [Ritornello], Interlude [Intermedio], Full Ensemble [Ripieno]; Loud [Forte], Soft [Piano]; Slow [Lento], Leisurely [Adagio], Broad [Largo], Fast [Presto]; The little bass [Bassetto].<-

In the first chapter of the second part of Volume Two, it was pointed out that by the word or name **Instrumentalists**, we do not mean the **Organists**—who are able to play on harpsichords <note>original: *Clavicymbeln*</note> and virginals <note>original: *Symphonien*</note> as well as on organs—but rather those who make music on all kinds of individual instruments, such as cornetts, trombones, flutes, bassoons, violins, viols, and the like.

And it is therefore better to say "striking" or "playing" on the harpsichord or virginal, rather than on "the instrument"; for the word *Instrumentum* <note>Latin: tool or instrument</note> must be referred and

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: drawn</meta>

<summary>This page provides a structural overview of the manual's contents and begins Chapter 1 by defining technical musical terminology. Praetorius specifically clarifies that "instrumentalist" refers to players of wind and string instruments, distinguishing them from keyboard-playing organists.</summary>
<keywords>terminology, instrumentalists, organists, harpsichord, cornett, Basso continuo, musical arrangement, Michael Praetorius, Cheiragogia</keywords>

<meta>continuity: This page continues the detailed explanation of musical terminology categorized in the previous diagram, specifically moving into the second section of Chapter 1, which focuses on "Concerted Parts" and the role of the vocal ensemble in modern Italian-style compositions.</meta>

...be drawn; otherwise there are confusions and errors if one were not rightly distinguished from the other in such a way.

### 2. Concerted Parts or Voices:
## Concerted Voices.

*Parti* <note>Italian: parts</note>—these are "parties," voices, or as one says in the schools, *Partes* <note>Latin: parts</note>; as when one says, "you take this part," that is, this voice <note>original Latin: *vocem*</note>, this part <note>original Latin: *partem*</note>. Thus Hieronymus Jacobi <note>Girolamo Giacobbi (1567–1629), a prominent composer at the Basilica of San Petronio</note> (Choirmaster at St. Petronius in Bologna, in his preface) calls them the **Concerted Parts**, **concerted voices**, or **Concertat-voices**. These are the voices which, in a concerto, are specifically directed and composed for singing and not for instruments. For this reason, I have placed them in the first and foremost parts, and they are not unjustly preferred over the instrumental choirs <note>original Latin: *instrumentalibus choris*</note>, because they can perform their part without the aid of instruments <note>original Latin: *absque adminiculo instrumentorum*</note>. I call them **concerted voices** <note>original Latin: *voces concertatas*</note>, or rather, **contending voices** <note>original Latin: *concertantes*</note>; they respond to one another, as it were, and "concertize" or struggle among themselves to see who can perform best. Therefore, for such parts, one must select, appoint, and arrange the best cantors and singers, who are not only perfect and steady, but also have a good disposition for singing in the current new manner and style, so that the words are pronounced correctly and clearly, and are recited audibly just like an oration; for which reason the Italians also sometimes call it the **recitative choir** <note>original Latin: *chorum recitativum*</note>.

> And [Lodovico] Viadana <note>Lodovico Viadana (c. 1560–1627), an influential Italian composer who helped popularize the use of the basso continuo</note> desires that for this **vocal recitative choir**, or concerted voices, the choirmaster or choir director should stand and constantly have the **general or continuous bass** <note>original Latin: *bassum generalem seu continuum*, also known as Basso Continuo</note> in his hand before him—or else diligently observe the one the organist has before him—noting how the music proceeds in sequence. He must indicate when one voice alone, when two, three, four, or more should begin to sing; which must always be marked accordingly in the general bass. But when the **ripieni** <note>Italian: "fillings," referring to the full ensemble that reinforces the main parts</note> and the **full choir** <note>original Latin: *plenus chorus*</note> begin, he should turn his face toward all the choirs and raise both hands high to indicate that they should all join in together and continue making music.

And in this way, the concertos in the works <note>original Latin: *operibus*</note> of Lodovico Viadana and all other authors of this new style can very fittingly be called **concerted parts**. However, I very much like the words of the aforementioned musician Hieronymus Jacobi, which I have not seen before in any other author, nor [reported] by anyone...

<meta>catchword: reported</meta>

<summary>Praetorius defines 'Concerted Voices' as specialized vocal parts meant for skilled singers to perform in a clear, oratory-like 'recitative' style. He also cites Viadana’s instructions for how a conductor should lead these ensembles and coordinate with the Basso Continuo and full choir.</summary>
<keywords>Concerted Voices, Hieronymus Jacobi, Lodovico Viadana, Basso Continuo, Recitative Choir, Ripieni, Conducting, Italian Style, Vocal Performance</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>continuity: This page follows the author’s discussion on how to categorize and name the various vocal and instrumental parts in modern musical compositions, particularly focusing on how to distinguish the "concerted" voices meant for skilled soloists from the larger ensemble groups.</meta>

...can be reported/how and with what names such voices are to be titled; until I finally thought about the matter myself/and in my own—admittedly modest, and in the least not to be compared with these excellent ones—concertos, I had to use these words *Chorus Vocalis* <note>Latin: Vocal Choir</note> and *Chorus Instrumentalis* <note>Latin: Instrumental Choir</note>. 

1. **Chorus Vocalis:** is that which must be made with human voices alone/without the help of instruments: and is the same/as if I were to put/Choir of Concertat-Voices <note>The soloistic, "contending" voices described on the previous page</note>. 

2. **Chorus Instrumentalis** is that which is performed with instruments alone, whether they be trombones/cornetts/bassoons/flutes or violins, and is joined to the *Choro Vocali* <note>Latin: Vocal Choir</note>, that is/the Concertat: or Vocal-voices/for the sake of greater weight and fuller harmony <note>original Latin: *majoris gravitatis & plenioris harmoniæ gratia*</note>. 

But after I had used both terms <note>original Latin: *terminos*</note> in this way/only this year did a musical work <note>original Latin: *Opus Musicum*</note> by Josephus Gallus of Milan <note>Giuseppe Gallo, a Milanese composer</note> come into my hands/in which he also uses a special invention almost identical to my own; in that he calls it a "new invention of double-concertos" <note>original Latin: *Duplicium Concentuum novam Inventionem*</note>/and distinguishes between *Chorus vocum* <note>Latin: choir of voices</note> and *Chorus Instrumentorum* <note>Latin: choir of instruments</note>.

->When a choir is established / and arranged for:<-

### Changed Choir. <note>original Latin: *Choro mutato*</note>

I found this term in the work of Hieronymus Jacobi <note>Girolamo Giacobbi (1567–1629), choirmaster at San Petronio in Bologna</note> / but at this time I cannot yet properly determine / how he wishes it to be understood. In my modest opinion, however, it must be understood in this way / because *Mutato* <note>changed</note> is the participle of the verb *mutare* <note>Latin: to change</note> / so that a "changed choir" is the same as / when one choir alternates with another / and remains silent through pauses: Or that this choir (because he...

the lower choir is) having no Soprano <note>original: *Discantum*</note> / but instead another Tenor, and the Alto takes the melody <note>original: *Cantu*</note>, and thus has a different kind and manner / than the upper choir / which is for four human voices alone <note>original Latin: *Solis quatuor vocibus humanis*</note>, while this one is played with three trombones or other instruments, and only the Alto is sung within it. Therefore, it might well be / that he intended it to be understood / that "Changed" <note>original: *Mutato*</note> here is the same as "Mute" <note>original: *mutus*</note> / silent / still / Muted voices <note>original Latin: *Voci mutatæ*</note>, Changed choir <note>original Latin: *Choro mutato*</note>, when the voices and the choir are still and secret: just as Full voices <note>original Latin: *Voci pienæ*</note>, Full choir <note>original Latin: *Choro pieno* or *Chorus plenus*</note> are used when they are to be sung and performed loudly and strongly / as the choirs then come together and create a complete harmony. And thus, through this "Changed choir" <note>original: *Choro mutato*</note>, or "Mute choir" <note>original: *Chorus q: mutus*</note>, a quiet choir would be understood / which is not staffed with instruments or voices / but only perhaps with three trombones and a single human voice: in the same way that deep, low-pitched choirs are commonly arranged.

## R I T O R N E L L O.
### Interlude: or alternating Muse <note>original Latin: *Intermedio: seu Gamœna alterna*</note>.

To return <note>original: *Ritornare*</note> is the same as to go back: (A return horse <note>original Italian: *Un Cavallo di ritorno*</note>, a horse / that one sends back again / as is the custom with post-horses.) Here, however, the word *Ritornello* is interpreted and understood by the Italians in this way; when one goes for a stroll in the streets in the evening / or as it is called at universities / "street-walking" <note>A term for student serenading</note>: where first a *Serenata* or evening song (of which mention was made in the first part) is sung with two / three or more voices / whereupon immediately something is performed and played in between on a quintern <note>A small guitar-like instrument</note>, lute / chitarrone, theorbo <note>Large, long-necked lutes</note> or other instruments; then again a stanza or little verse of the *Serenata* is sung / followed again by a response on the quintern or theorbo / and thus one after the other they alternate between singing and playing. That which is performed on the theorbo, quintern or other instruments between the singing is called a *Ritornello*. Therefore, in my opinion, "to return" <note>original: *Ritornare*</note> is to be understood as "to reiterate" <note>original Latin: *reiterare*</note>, and "Ritornello" as "reiteration" <note>original Latin: *reiteratio*</note> / because the first and same harmony is always repeated and echoed. And precisely to this end / as I can conclude no other way / is the hymn, *Ave maris stella* <note>Hail, Star of the Sea</note> for 8 voices in Claudio Monteverdi's *Vesper Psalms* directed: where the first little verse for 8 voices is sung by both choirs; the second verse in the first choir with four vocal or "concertat" voices; then follows the *Ritornello* in the first choir / and the melody of the second choir with 5 voices / in a triple time <note>original: *Tripla*</note> of 20 beats <note>original: *Tacten*</note>, without text, and is performed by instruments alone: the 3rd verse in the second choir with a single vocal voice:

<note>original vocabulary: Changed choir, Muted voices, Full choir, Ritornello, To return, Claudio Monteverdi, Hail star of the sea, Serenade, Theorbo, Quintern, Trombones</note>

<summary>This page explains the "Choro mutato" as a quiet, instrument-heavy ensemble and defines the "Ritornello" as an instrumental interlude that repeats a theme between vocal verses, citing Monteverdi as a primary example.</summary>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>continuity: This page continues the author’s explanation of musical terminology, specifically moving into a detailed analysis of the "Ritornello" as both a structural device and a specific style of instrumental interlude, following a discussion on choral arrangements.</meta>

Voices: Immediately afterward, the instrumental interlude <note>original: *Ritornello*</note> is repeated again with the instruments. In the 4th verse, the Soprano <note>original: *Cantus*</note> of the 1st Choir sings alone; in the 5th verse, the Soprano of the 2nd Choir sings alone; likewise, in the 6th verse, the Tenor of the 1st Choir sings alone to the General Bass <term>Basso continuo: an accompanying part that provides the harmonic structure</term> with the human voice <note>original Latin: *humana voce*</note>. However, between each verse, the instrumental interlude is repeated and performed, until in the 7th verse, both choirs—including instruments and vocal parts—join together in unison. It is also important to know this: there are only three types of melodies or arias present in this entire Psalm. This is because the 1st and the last verses are identical to one another. The 2nd, 3rd, 4th, 5th, and 6th verses all share a single, identical bass line, though the voices singing over it are not always the same in number or type. The instrumental interlude <note>original: *Ritornello*</note> is also just one kind, which is repeated every time. I have composed the Mass <note>original Latin: *Missam*</note> for 3 or 5 choirs based on the plainchant *Lord, Source of All Goodness* <note>original Latin: *Kyrie fons bonitatis*</note> in almost this same manner. In that work, the instrumental choirs <note>original Latin: *Chori Instrumentales*</note> do not only join in several times in the middle, but they also begin the piece and finally conclude it at the end <note>original Latin: *in fine*</note> together with all the other choirs. However, many other variations with instruments and vocal parts are mixed in there as well.

The previously mentioned Claudio Monteverdi <note>Cl. de Monteverde, 1567–1643</note> arranged the Psalm *The Lord said to my Lord* <note>original Latin: *Dixit Dominus Domino meo* (Psalm 110)</note> for 6 voices in the same work (where six voices and six instruments are to be found, as in Chapter 8, Type 9). In certain places, he did not provide text, but placed only the word "Ritornello" under the notes to indicate that the instruments should be used alone without the vocal parts. In my opinion, this is the correct understanding of the term Ritornello.

**Note Well:** Just as I was working on this volume, Claudio Monteverdi’s *Musical Jests for three voices* <note>original: *Scherzi Musicali à tre voci*</note> came into my hands. These are playful three-part songs <note>original Latin: *Tricinia jocosa*</note> in which he set two Sopranos and a Bass with several texts for singing. Immediately after these, he composed a Ritornello without text for instruments, such as two violins and a bass viol <note>original: *Bass-Geigen*</note> or bassoon <note>original: *Fagott*</note> (provided no harpsichord <note>original: *Clavicymbel*</note> or large lute <note>original: *Chitaron*, also known as a *Theorba*</note> is available). This instrumental interlude is repeated at the beginning, at the end, and between every stanza of the text.

Although I find that some authors do not properly distinguish between the words "Symphonia" and "Ritornello," I can finally conclude this: a "Symphonia" is like a lovely Pavane or a grave, solemn Sonata. A "Ritornello," however, is not unlike the dance-like pieces set for 3, 4, or 5 parts for violins, cornetts <note>original: *Zincken*</note>, trombones <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, lutes, or other instruments—such as Galliards, Saltarellos, Courantes, Voltas, or even Canzonas "peppered" with fast notes <note>original Latin: *semi minimis und Fusen*</note>. These are usually 12, 13, or 20 measures long, but very rarely longer. And just...

<summary>The author defines the "Ritornello" as an instrumental interlude that repeats between vocal sections, using Claudio Monteverdi’s works as primary examples. He distinguishes the lively, dance-like Ritornello from the more solemn and grave Symphonia.</summary>
<keywords>Ritornello, Claudio Monteverdi, musical structure, orchestration, instrumentation, Symphonia, dance forms, basso continuo, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s practical advice on musical performance, transitioning from a discussion on how to structure a sacred Psalm to a broader application of these techniques in social and theatrical settings. It focuses on the use of the "Ritornello" or "Intermedio" as a functional tool for both aesthetic variety and the physical recovery of performers.</meta>

Just as in stage plays <note>original: *comœdien*</note>, where a fine, lovely instrumental music <note>original Latin: *Musica Instrumentalis*</note>—alternating with cornetts, viols, or other such instruments, and occasionally arranged with vocal parts—is placed between every act <note>original Latin: *Actu*</note> and is called an interlude <note>original Italian: *INTERMEDIO*</note> by the Italians; this is done so that the masked actors <note>original Latin: *personatæ personæ*</note> may change their costumes and prepare <note>original: *præpariren*</note> for the following act, as well as to allow them to breathe <note>original: *respiriren*</note> and recover a bit. In the same manner and fashion, one can also organize a good musical performance for a great lord’s table or other joyful gatherings <note>original Latin: *Conventibus*</note>. For example, after one has had two or more boys, or other alto, tenor, and bass vocal parts (which I call concerted voices <note>original Latin: *Voces Concertatæ*</note>), sing to a harpsichord <note>original: *Clavicymbel*</note>, a regal <term>Regal: a small, portable pipe organ with a reed-like sound</term>, or a similar foundation instrument, one should immediately begin to make music without vocal parts, using only instruments—such as lutes, bandoras, fiddles, cornetts, trombones <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, and the like. Afterward, one should alternate again with vocal parts, thus switching back and forth between instruments and voices. 

In the same way, after a concert or a grand motet <note>original: *Mutet*</note>, one should immediately bring forth a lively canzone, galliard, courante, or similar piece played solely on instruments. An organist or lutenist can also observe this practice on his own: when he has played a motet or madrigal in a fine, slow, and solemn <note>original: *Gravitätisch*</note> manner during banquets <note>original Latin: *Conviviis*</note>, he should immediately follow it with a joyful allemande, intrada, branle, or galliard. Afterward, he may take up another motet, madrigal, pavane, or a skillfully crafted fugue <note>original Latin: *Fugam*</note>. Such alternation can very fittingly be called by the names refrain <note>original: *Ritornel*</note> and interlude <note>original: *Intermedio*</note>. 

This is currently very common among those arriving from Italy: at the beginning, they play such a refrain <note>original: *Ritornello*</note> or a lovely, short melody alone on the theorbo or chitarrone <term>Theorbo/Chitarrone: large, bass-range lutes with extended necks used for accompaniment</term>. Following this, they sing the first stanza or verse of an Italian or German secular song quite gracefully to the theorbo. Then, they immediately repeat <note>original: *reiteriren*</note> the first refrain; then the second stanza of the song is sung to the theorbo; and the refrain is either played again on the theorbo alone or performed by other instrumentalists on lutes, citterns, bandoras, fiddles, and the like. This allows the singers to pause, breathe <note>original: *respiriren*</note>, catch their breath, and thus recover. Therefore, such alternation is not only very charming for the sake of delightful variety <note>original Latin: *propter varietatem delectantem*</note>, but also very necessary to observe for the sake of breathing <note>original Latin: *propter respirationem*</note>.

->RIPIENO:<-
<note>Ripieno: Italian for "full," referring to the sections of a piece where the full ensemble plays, as opposed to the soloists.</note>

<summary>The author explains the use of the 'Ritornello' or 'Intermedio' as an instrumental bridge used in plays, banquets, and solo performances to provide variety and allow singers to rest. He highlights the Italian style of alternating vocal stanzas with instrumental refrains on instruments like the theorbo.</summary>

<keywords>interlude, ritornello, intermedio, instrumental music, performance practice, banquet music, breathing, variety, theorbo, chitarrone, vocal accompaniment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's technical guide to musical terminology and performance practice, specifically focusing on the structure and orchestration of large-scale vocal and instrumental works.</meta>

# RIPIENO : Acclamation <note>original Greek: *Epiphōnēsis*</note> : Shouting Together <note>original Latin: *Conclamatio*</note>, Harmony <note>original Latin: *Concentus*</note>, Full Chorus <note>original Latin: *Plenus Chorus*</note>.

The Italians use this word when they want to indicate that all voices <note>original Latin: *Voces*</note> and instruments <note>original Latin: *Instrumenta*</note> in all choirs should enter together at once: in German, this is a full-voiced music in all choirs. For *Pieno* means the same as full <note>original Latin: *plenum*</note>; *Ripieno* means replenished or filled <note>original Latin: *repletum*</note>. And it is exactly the same as when I have noted and marked the words Everyone <note>original Italian: *Tutti*</note>, All <note>original Latin: *Omnes*</note>, Full Chorus <note>original Latin: *Plenus chorus*</note>, or Full Chapel <note>original Latin: *Capella plena*</note>; at which point a small recorder <note>original: *Klein Flöttlin*</note> can be quite fittingly added and used for ornamentation. Thus, if a large company of musicians <note>original: *Musicis*</note> is available, one can have such *Ripieni* copied out two or three times and distribute and divide them into different choirs separated far from one another: which I have then mostly called a "Choir for the Chapel" <note>original Latin: *Chorum pro Capella*</note>. 

And it is not unpleasant if the organist, when the full-voiced music enters, uses the full works of the organ; or, if that should be a bit too strong against the entire ensemble, uses the Principal stop alone with the Superoctave and the Sifflöte or the Zimbel stop. For the soloistic voices <term>Concertat-Stimmen: voices or instruments that perform in a soloistic, "competing" style, often requiring more agility</term>, however, he should use a soft, gentle, and lovely *Gedackt* <term>Gedackt: a stopped organ pipe with a sweet, mellow tone</term> or another quiet flute stop in the choir organ <note>original: *rückpositiff*; the division of the organ located behind the player's back</note>. For the soloistic voices require a soft, moderate, and lovely singing and sounding, whereas the full-voiced choir requires a grave resonance and ringing. 

And thus the *Ripieni* are nothing other than certain musical phrases or small parts <note>original Latin: *Clausulæ* or *particulæ*</note> from a concerto <note>original: *concert*</note> which are sung and played together with the main choirs in order to create a full-voiced music in the other choirs at certain times. Therefore, *RIPIENO* actually does not mean "Full Chorus" <note>original Latin: *Chorus plenus*</note>, but rather "Repeated Fullnesses" <note>original Latin: *Reiteratæ plenitudines*</note>—the phrases taken from the main parts of the concerto and set in other distinct, separate choirs to create a complete and full-voiced concerto.

Hieronymus Jacobi, in his "Note to the Reader" <note>original Latin: *Nota ad lectorem*</note>, wants the letter **R.** to be marked by the notes to indicate the beginning of the *Ripieno*. However, this easily leads to confusion, especially when many *Ripieni* follow one after another. I have preferred to clearly add such words between the notes at the places where they occur. And in my opinion, among the words *Symphonia*, *Ritornello*, *Ripieno*, and *Intermedio*, which are employed <note>original Latin: *adhibirt*</note> and used at the beginning of a concerted song, this is the distinction:

*Ritornello*, where it is without text / and thus for instruments
alone <note>original Latin: *Instrumentis solis*</note>
*Ripieno*, with text / and thus for voices and instruments <note>original Latin: *Vocibus & Instrumentis*</note>
<image-desc>A large curly brace on the right groups the descriptions of Ritornello and Ripieno together, pointing to a shared concluding characteristic.</image-desc>
are the same; it is always reiterated and repeated with a fast beat <note>original: *Tact*</note>.

<summary>This page defines the term "Ripieno" as a full-ensemble reinforcement in musical compositions and provides practical advice for organ registration and the distribution of choirs. It also distinguishes "Ripieno" from related terms like "Ritornello" based on the presence of text and instrumentation.</summary>
<keywords>Ripieno, Tutti, orchestration, organ stops, Concertat-Stimmen, Chorum pro Capella, Ritornello, Hieronymus Jacobi, musical terminology</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s explanation of Italian musical terms, transitioning from the definition of "Ripieno" to "Intermedio," "Sinfonia," and dynamic markings, concluding with a definition of the "Bassetto" voice.</meta>

Therefore, I am of the opinion that **Ripieno** is a **compound word** <note>original Latin: *vocabulum Compositum*</note>, as if one were to say **Full Ritornello**, a **Ritornello** or **Repetition** <note>original Latin: *Reiteratio*</note>, which harmonizes together with the full choir, and where the **instrumental** parts are **joined** <note>original Latin: *adjungiret*</note> with the **vocal** parts at the same time. **Intermedio** <note>Interlude</note>, where either with or without texts, with **instrumental** or **vocal** parts, or with both together, other **songs** <note>original Latin: *Cantiones*</note> and chants are **interposed** <note>inserted</note> between every **act** in **comedies**, or in Masses, **Magnificats**, and **motets**; as can be found in my **Megalynodia** <note>A collection of large-scale Magnificats published by Praetorius in 1611</note> and will appear more frequently, God willing, in my **Polyhymnia VIII**. **Sinfonias** are like **Pavanes** and **Galliards** <note>popular dance forms of the period</note>, which one can use in a musical work at the beginning before the first part, and after it, as well as before and after the second or even the third part, if one is present: instead of the organist always playing a **prelude** <note>original Latin: *Præambulum*</note> on the organ before and in between. In this way, **Sinfonias** can also quite appropriately be called **Intermezzos**. **Loud, Soft; Fast; Slow.** <note>original: *Fortè, Pian: Præsto; Adagio Lento*</note> These words are sometimes used by the **Italians**, and in **concertos** <term>Concertē: at this time, "concerto" referred to any ensemble work where voices and instruments "competed" or worked together</term> they are marked in many different places or underneath the music to indicate the alternation of both voices and choirs, which I personally find quite pleasing. Although some believe that such things are not good to use, especially in churches, it seems to me that such **variation** and alternation—if it is undertaken and carried out **moderately** <note>original Latin: *moderatè*</note> and with a fine **grace** to **express** the **emotions** <note>original Latin: *affectus*</note> and **move** the listeners—is not only not unpleasant or wrong, but rather it **affects the ears and minds of the listeners** <note>original Latin: *aures & animos auditorum afficire*</note> and **wins a special character and grace for the concerto** <note>original Latin: *gratiam conciliire*</note>. Indeed, the **composition** itself, as well as the text and understanding of the words, often demands this: that one should occasionally—but not too often or too much—lead the **beat** <term>Tact: the physical marking of time, which Praetorius suggests should be flexible for expressive purposes</term> now quickly, now slowly again; and also let the choir **resonate** now quietly and softly, now strongly and fresh. Though in such alternations, a greater **moderation** is required in church than at the dinner table. Now, everyone knows for themselves what such words mean: **Loud** <note>original: *Fortè*</note>, **exalted, clear, that is, with the highest or most intense voice** <note>original Latin: *id est, sumâ seu intentâ voce*</note>, when the **instrumentalists** and **vocalists** are to play strongly together. **Soft** <note>original: *Pian*</note>, **submissively** <note>original Latin: *submissè*</note>, when they should **moderate** the voice and at the same time **tune** and **make music** very quietly. Otherwise, **Soft** <note>original: *Pian*</note> is the same as **peacefully, step-by-step, at a slow pace** <note>original Latin: *placidè, pedetentim, lento gradu*</note>: meaning that one should not only moderate the voices, but also sing more slowly.

## LITTLE BASS <note>original: *BASSETTO*</note>:

Basset is the **diminutive** of the **Bass**, and signifies the lowest voice that carries the **foundation** in high-voiced choirs, and behaves like a Bass in its **intervals**. However, it is mostly cleffed like a **Tenor**, and sometimes like an **Alto**.

<summary>Praetorius defines structural terms like Ripieno, Intermedio, and Sinfonia, while defending the use of dynamic markings (loud/soft) and tempo changes to move the listener's emotions. He concludes with a technical definition of the Bassetto voice.</summary>
<keywords>Ripieno, Intermedio, Sinfonia, dynamics, Forte, Piano, tempo variation, Bassetto, liturgical music, Michael Praetorius, musical expression</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of vocal and instrumental ranges, transitioning from the definition of "Ripieno" and "Bassetto" into a detailed explanation of "Capella" and the organization of large-scale musical ensembles.</meta>

<page-num>133</page-num>

Therefore, in the case of **bombards** <note>original: *Pombarden*; a family of powerful double-reed instruments</note>, **flutes**, and other instruments tuned to the same pitch and key, they are also called **basset bombards**, **basset flutes**, etc., as was indicated in Volume 2 <note>Referring to Praetorius’s *De Organographia* (1619), which details instruments</note>.

Strictly speaking, however, in both concerts and motets, the voice that is the lowest—whether it be the soprano <note>original: *Cantu*</note>, alto, or tenor, as is especially common in fugues—is called the **Bassetto**.

->**B A R Y T O N U S**<-
<note>Baritone</note>

By this word, the Italians understand the **tenor** or the **fifth voice** <note>original: *Quintum*</note> in "low choirs" <note>ensembles composed of lower-pitched voices</note>, specifically when the **F-clef symbol** is found marked on the third line.

What is understood by the words **Accent**, **Trill**, **Group**, **Tremolo** <note>original: *Accentus, Trillo, Gruppo, Tremoletti*</note>, etc., shall be explained in detail at the end of this third volume.

## Chapter 11.

->**Chapel**; **Choir for the Chapel**;<-
->**Little Stage**<-
<note>original: *Capella: Chorus pro Capella: Palchetto*</note>

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut initial 'D' with intricate vine and leaf patterns in a square border.</image-desc>The word **Capella** <term>Capella: literally "Chapel," but here referring to the ensemble of musicians or a specific reinforcing choir</term> is found to be used in three different ways.

Firstly, in the beginning—in my opinion—it was understood by the Italians alone to mean that when various separate choirs with all kinds of instruments and human voices are arranged in the large **Imperial**, **Austrian**, and other **Catholic** chapels or musical establishments, a special choir is then drawn out from among them all. This is called the **Chorus pro Capella** <note>Choir for the Chapel</note>. This is because the entire vocal choir, or the whole "Chapel," performs that part in the choir loft, completely separated from the other choirs. Like the **full organ** <note>original: *volles Werck*</note> on an organ, they join in together. This provides a magnificent ornament, splendor, and display in such music, since this choir usually enters at the same time as all the other choirs come together.

And such harmony is fulfilled even more and expanded with greater splendor if one also assigns a **large bass bombard**, a **double bassoon** <note>original: *doppelt Fagott*</note>, or **large bass viols** (called *Violone* by the Italians), as well as other instruments if they are available, to the middle and upper voices. Such "Chapels," however, can be drawn out differently—one, two, or three from each concerto—a...

<meta>catchword: each</meta>

<meta>This page continues Chapter 11's discussion of the "Capella." Praetorius is explaining how this reinforcing choir functions in large-scale musical works and how it differs from the main soloistic parts.</meta>

...each specifically staffed with only four or more people—if they can be had—and placed in separate locations in the church. Also, if there is a lack of people, depending on the circumstances, they may be entirely omitted. This is because this **Capella** <term>Capella: here meaning a reinforcing ensemble</term> acts almost like the **Ripieni** <term>Ripieni: filling or supplementary parts that double the main parts to add volume</term>, serving only to fill out and strengthen the music. It is an **extra choir** <note>original: *Chorus ascititius*</note> extracted and drawn out from the other choirs. In these parts, **unisons** and **octaves** are placed without distinction, for the **reasons** <note>original: *Rationibus*</note> cited in the 12th Chapter of the second part.

I have seen many different examples of such *Capellas* in several transcribed **concertos** <note>original: *Concerten*; at this time, the term referred to vocal-instrumental works rather than purely orchestral ones</note> by **Giovanni Gabrieli** <note>A famous Venetian composer (c. 1554–1612) known for his pioneering use of multiple choirs and specified instrumentation</note>; however, these are not present in those **published** <note>original: *publicirten*</note> in print just this past year.

2. In those same works, as well as in his first **Sacred Songs** <note>original: *Cantiones sacrae*</note> published in the year 1597, one finds that the word **Capella** means the same to him as if I were to say **Vocal Choir** <note>original: *Chorus Vocalis, Chorus Vocum*</note>; that is, the choir which must be staffed with **singers** <note>original: *Cantoribus*</note> and human voices. For example, if in a concerto one choir is set with **cornets**, another with **violins**, a third with **trombones**, **bassoons**, **flutes**, and similar instruments—provided that in each choir at least one **soloistic** <term>Concertat-Stimme: a prominent solo voice part intended to "compete" or harmonize with the instruments</term> human voice is arranged alongside them—there is usually also a choir present where all four parts are staffed with singers. **Giovanni Gabrieli** calls this the *Capella*. And such a choir or *Capella*, because it belongs among the **Principal Choirs** <term>PrincipalChor: the core ensemble essential to the piece's structure</term>, can by no means be left out. It can be immediately recognized by the **clef signs** <note>original: *Clavibus Signatis*</note> and can sometimes also be performed with **viola da gambas** or **violas da braccio** <note>String instruments held either between the legs (gamba) or on the arm (braccio)</note>. A more detailed report on this will follow hereafter in the 8th Chapter.

In my own concertos, especially the **Latin** ones as well as several **German** ones, where I did not wish to include a **Choir for the Capella** <note>original: *Chorum pro Capella*</note>, I have mostly marked the words **All** and **Alone** <note>original: *Omnes* and *Solus*</note>, or **Voice**, **Instrument**, **Trombone**, etc. Anyone can easily understand this and conduct themselves accordingly. For wherever **Voice & Trombone** or **Voice & Violin** <note>original: *Voce & Violono*</note>, etc., is written, one must place a **vocalist** and an **instrumentalist** with a trombone or violin there. Where **Voice** <note>original: *Voce*</note> is found alone, the vocalist sings alone; where **Trombone**, the trombone plays alone; where **All** <note>original: *omnes*</note>, they both make music together. The same procedure is followed when other types of instruments are found noted there. Thus, not only from such divided works but also from all other concertos, anyone—provided they are supplied with a sufficient company of singers and instrumentalists—can, according to their pleasure, extract one or two *Capellas* of four parts and that which precedes the word

<summary>Praetorius defines the "Capella" as a reinforcing vocal ensemble used to add volume and texture, drawing on the practices of Giovanni Gabrieli. He also explains his own notation system for directing soloists, instrumentalists, and full ensembles using terms like "Omnes" and "Solus."</summary>
<keywords>Capella, Ripieni, Giovanni Gabrieli, instrumentation, vocal choir, soloists, orchestration, Baroque performance practice</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues Michael Praetorius's detailed technical instructions for the "Capella" (reinforcing choir). He transitions from defining the term to explaining the physical arrangement of musicians in the church and the practicalities of notation.</meta>

Wherever the mark **all** <note>original: *omnes*</note> or **Chorus** is written, or where all the choirs are meant to join in together, or where it otherwise seems appropriate, these parts should be written out on separate sheets (following the method shown in the 12th Chapter of the second part). However, as soon as the word **Solo** <note>original: *Solus*</note> or **Voice [with] Instrument** <note>original: *Voce Instrumento*</note>, etc., follows, an equal number of pauses must be set in its place. At that point, that same **reinforcing choir** <note>original: *Chorus pro Capella*</note> must be arranged and positioned in a separate location for the performance.

Several years ago, I already began to use the words **all** <note>original: *omnes*</note> and **alone** <note>original: *solus*</note> in my **Sacred Songs** <note>original: *Cantionibus*</note>. I find, however, that the Italians now use the word **Ripieni** <term>Ripieni: from the Italian for "full" or "filled," referring to the supplementary parts that fill out the sound of a large ensemble</term> in their concertos.

3. Now, however, some also call it a **Capella** when an **instrumental choir** <note>original: *Chorum Instrumentalem*</note> is composed and set alongside a **vocal choir** <note>original: *Choro Vocali*</note>. In this case, the instrumental choir—which acts as **less principal** <note>original: *tanquam minus Principalis*</note> and could easily be left out if instrumentalists are lacking—is separated from the vocal choir. This vocal choir is the **principal** <note>original: *Principalis*</note> part and can satisfy the requirements of the piece on its own without the help of instrumentalists, provided an organist with a **positive organ** <term>Positive organ: a small, portable pipe organ</term> or **regal** <term>Regal: a small portable organ with reed pipes, known for its buzzy, sharp sound</term> is present. It is arranged perhaps opposite, or in a higher or lower location. This is also called a **little stage** <note>original: *Palchetto*</note> in Italy, where they sometimes place more than one **reinforcing choir** <note>original: *Chorus pro Capella*</note>, stacking one above the other. This is perhaps similar to how the musicians in the Temple during David’s time were arranged and divided into various higher and lower choirs. For this reason, certain special Psalms—from 120 to 134—are called "Songs in the higher choir" <note>These are traditionally known as the Psalms of Ascent or Songs of Degrees; Praetorius links the biblical term to his own multi-level choir arrangements</note>, as can be seen more extensively in **Volume One, Part One, Section 1, Chapter 2** <note>original: *Tomo primo partis primæ Membr. 1. Cap. 2.*</note>.

The word **little stage** <note>original: *Palchetto*</note> can be even better understood from the following short report. In several churches, and especially in Princely Chapels, it is customary to build a specific stand like a **theater** <note>original: *Theatro*</note> out of beams and boards, either on the ground or in another convenient spot where the musicians can remain undisturbed by the listeners. Sometimes boards are laid over chairs where suitable and then decorated and furnished with linens and tapestries. One can also, if desired, build a special elevated place, similar to a small **gallery** <term>Poerkirchen: a raised balcony or gallery in a church, common in Lutheran architecture</term>, where different choirs can be placed and set far apart from the others. Indeed, such suitable places are often found in old churches, particularly in the rear of the choir stalls, which can be used for the purpose just described and thus called a **little stage** <note>original: *Palchetto*</note>.

### Chapter 3.
## THE STRING CHOIR <note>original: *CAPELLA FIDICINUM, vel FIDICINA*</note>.

<note>original: P ij</note>
<meta>The catchword "Wie" (How) indicates the start of the next page's first sentence.</meta>

<summary>Praetorius explains how to notationally mark and physically position reinforcing choirs (Capellas) within a church, comparing his methods to Italian "Ripieni" and biblical musical arrangements. He also describes the construction of "Palchetto" or temporary wooden galleries to accommodate these diverse ensembles.</summary>
<keywords>choir arrangement, acoustics, church architecture, Ripieni, Palchetto, positive organ, regal, Psalms of Ascent, performance practice</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s instructions on the "Capella" (a reinforcing ensemble), focusing on the practical reasons for using it and the specific instruments that best complement the "new Italian style."</meta>

# How and in what manner the same [Capella] should be arranged and organized
->to be.<-

I have personally observed that this <term>Capella</term> <note>a reinforcing ensemble used to fill out the sound</note> is not unnecessary, but is in fact quite useful. This is because some among us Germans are not yet accustomed to the new Italian invention—where one occasionally lets only a single <term>concertato voice</term> <note>a soloistic, decorative vocal part</note> or perhaps two or three sing to an <term>organ</term> or <term>regal</term> <note>a small, portable organ with reed pipes that has a buzzing sound</note>. To some, this style does not quite appeal, as they feel the singing sounds too "bare" and lacks any particular distinction or <term>grace</term> <note>original: *gratiam*</note> to those who do not understand music. Therefore, I had to consider this remedy: adding a <term>choir</term> or <term>Capella</term> of four voices to the piece, which could always join in simultaneously with either <term>trombones</term> or <term>violins</term>.

And because such a <term>harmony</term>, when arranged in the church in this manner, fills the ears somewhat more, I immediately achieved <term>popular approval</term> <note>original: *applausum popularem*</note> through it.

Furthermore, in my opinion, it also <term>resounds</term> <note>original: *resoniret*</note> quite well if a <term>regal</term> or a <term>reed stop</term> <note>original: *Schnarr-Werck*</note> in the <term>organ</term> is used for such <term>concertos</term> with one or two <term>concertato voices</term> (especially when <term>lively and more cheerful voices</term> <note>original: *Voces vivaces & alacriocres*</note> are used). For the <term>organist</term>—as will be shown in the 6th chapter regarding <term>basso continuo</term> <note>original: *General-Baß*</note>—must play for such <term>concertato voices</term> quite <term>simply</term>, with fine <term>concordances</term> <note>chords</note> and <term>syncopations</term>, without any <term>diminutions</term> <note>fast, divided notes</note> or <term>coloraturas</term> <note>ornamental flourishes</note>. If played this way on the <term>flute stops</term> <note>original: *Flöit-Werck*</note>, it sounds far too plain and unpleasant; however, on the <term>regal</term> or other <term>reed stops</term>, which <term>resound</term> almost like <term>trombones</term>, the <term>harmony</term> is much more pleasant, provided one treats the song gracefully, <term>stately</term>, and slowly, without any <term>diminutions</term>.

It should also be well noted: 1. That I have called this <term>Capella</term> a <term>string ensemble</term> <note>original: *Fidicinium*, from the Latin *fides* for stringed instrument</note> because it is better to provide for this <term>Capella</term> with <term>stringed instruments</term>—such as <term>violins</term>, <term>lutes</term>, <term>harps</term>, and all others, and especially with <term>violas da gamba</term> <note>leg viols</note> where they can be had. In their absence, however, one may use <term>violas da braccio</term> <note>arm viols or violins</note>. For the <term>sound</term> and <term>harmony</term> of the <term>viols</term> and <term>violins</term> is <term>sustained</term> continuously one after another with singular sweetness, without any <term>breathing</term> <note>original: *respiration*</note>, which one cannot avoid on <term>trombones</term> and other wind instruments.

<meta>catchword: However</meta>

<summary>Praetorius explains that adding a reinforcing "Capella" of voices and instruments helps the "new" Italian solo style appeal to German audiences by making the sound fuller. He recommends using reed stops on the organ and stringed instruments for their continuous, "breathless" sustaining power.</summary>
<keywords>Capella, concertato style, organ registration, reed stops, stringed instruments, viola da gamba, Michael Praetorius, basso continuo, performance practice</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s practical guide for conductors and composers on how to arrange the "Capella Fidicinina" (an instrumental ensemble) to support voices. He provides specific examples of instrument combinations and advice on spatial arrangement within a church.</meta>

However, for the sake of variety <note>original: *pro variatione*</note>, one can occasionally use 4 <term>trombones</term> <note>original: *Posaunen* (sackbuts)</note>. In this case, it is not an error if the <term>Cantus</term> <note>the highest vocal part</note> is played an octave lower <note>original: *in Octava inferiore*</note> on the trombone. Or, one might use 3 trombones and a <term>tenor flute</term> <note>likely a tenor recorder</note>, or a <term>cornett</term> for the <term>discant</term> <note>the soprano or highest part</note>; alternatively, one could use a <term>bassoon</term> and 3 flutes for variety.

2. For these reasons, I have occasionally placed <term>virgulas</term> <note>small marks or dividing lines</note> and little strokes in the middle of the music. This is so that one might use <term>viols</term> for one <term>versicle</term> <note>a short verse</note>, trombones for the second, and flutes and bassoons for the third. Or, where <term>lutenists</term> are available, one can alternate with violins, and then occasionally let the lutes and violins play together. From this, a musician can very easily create and transcribe two or more <term>choirs</term>, arranging them according to his own good pleasure.

It is also very pleasant to hear when one arranges this <term>stringed ensemble</term> <note>original: *Capellam Fidicininiam*</note> in the English fashion with a full <term>consort</term>. This is done so that a loud <term>harpsichord</term> <note>original: *Clavicymbel*</note>, two or three lutes, a <term>theorbo</term> <note>a large, long-necked lute</note>, a <term>bandora</term> <note>a wire-stringed instrument with a scalloped body</note>, a <term>cittern</term> <note>a small, wire-stringed instrument similar to a mandolin</note>, a <term>bass viol</term> <note>original: *Baßgeig*</note>, a <term>recorder</term> or <term>transverse flute</term>, a <term>mute trombone</term> <note>a softer version of the sackbut</note>, a <term>viola bastarda</term> <note>a specialized viol used for virtuosic improvisation</note>, and a small treble violin are all tuned together purely and lovably. They proceed together while the <term>concertato voices</term> bring their parts into the mix with a pleasing and decent harmony <note>original: *cum grata & decenti harmonia*</note>.

3. I have also found through observation <note>original: *ex observatione*</note> that it is better to place and arrange this same <term>Capella</term> or string choir somewhat to the side, separate from the organ and those who lead the <term>concertato voices</term>. This is done so that the vocalists are not drowned out or overshadowed by the instruments, but rather that each may be heard and perceived distinctly from the other. This shall be reminded again in Chapter 8 in the instructions regarding the third style.

4. However, it is left to each person’s pleasure whether they wish to arrange and use this <term>Capella</term> or leave it out; for, as mentioned at the beginning, I only chose to compose it this way because of the approval of certain listeners <note>original: *ex auditorum quorundam approbatione*</note>, as I otherwise would not have given it much weight.

5. And if one wanted to compose and set up such a <term>stringed ensemble</term> for all <term>concerti</term> of this kind—such as those that one might find most pleasing in the works of authors like <term>Lodovico Viadana</term>, <term>Agostino Agazzari</term>, <term>Antonio Cifra</term>, and similar creators—it would entice the listeners here in our Germany. Many of them do not yet know how to adjust to this new style, and they would be, as it were, further "charmed" <note>original: *infasciniren*</note> by it, so that they would undoubtedly find great satisfaction and contentment in it.

6. Such a <term>Capella</term> is also useful to those organists who are unpracticed and inexperienced in composing, and therefore [cannot] find their way in the <term>thorough-bass</term> <note>original: *General Baß* or Basso Continuo</note>...

<summary>Michael Praetorius discusses flexible instrumentation for ensembles, including the "English Consort" style, and emphasizes the importance of spatial separation between singers and instruments. He suggests that adding these instrumental "Capellas" helps German audiences appreciate the modern Italian soloistic style of composers like Viadana.</summary>

<keywords>instrumentation, trombone, consort, lute, harpsichord, musical arrangement, acoustics, German music history, Italian style, basso continuo, Viadana, Agazzari, Cifra</keywords>

...could not find at the very beginning; since it would be much easier for them to transcribe all the middle voices or parts (which are otherwise not present in such <term>concertos</term> <note>In the 17th century, a "concerto" referred to a sacred vocal work with instrumental accompaniment</note>) into their <term>tablature</term> <note>original: *Tabulatur*; a shorthand notation system for keyboard or plucked instruments</note>, rather than having to think and speculate for a long time about whether they should play fourths and sixths, or fifths and thirds, etc. For this reason, I have also called this *Capella* in some places an *Ensemble for the Organ* <note>original: *Capellam pro Organo*</note>, as well as *for the Lute* <note>original: *pro Testudine*</note>, *Theorbo*, etc.

7. Furthermore, one should not be misled by the fact that in this *Capella* the four voices for the stringed or wind instruments occasionally proceed in unisons and octaves simultaneously with the other soloist or vocal parts. This has already been accounted for and demonstrated more fully in Chapter XII of the Second Part <note>referring to an earlier section of *Syntagma Musicum*</note>, explaining why unisons can be used throughout, and octaves can pass quite well when one part is sung and the other is performed by instruments such as <term>trombones</term> <note>original: *Posaunen* (sackbuts)</note>, <term>cornetts</term> <note>original: *Zincken*</note>, or violins. Moreover, no one who has gained experience in princely or other <term>chapels</term> <note>musical establishments</note> will find this offensive—nor will other musicians in the cities, when they consider and observe that even in their own choir, they place a town piper or professional musician with a cornett or trombone next to the students, who then intone and tune together in unison and octaves.

To ensure that a musician does not become lost or confused among the many different names, I have chosen to set them down here together, as far as they are known to me, so that at first glance <note>original: *primo intuitu*</note> everyone can see and understand which are synonyms and have the same meaning.

| Human Voices | Instrumental | Full/Ripieno |
|:---|:---|:---|
| Human voices <note>original: *Voces humanæ / MenschenStimmen*</note> | By instrument <note>original: *Instrumento*</note> | Filling parts <note>original: *Ripieni*</note> |
| Voice <note>original: *Voce*</note> | Instrumental choir <note>original: *Chorus Instrumentalis*</note> | All <note>original: *Tutti*</note> |
| Solo voices <note>original: *Voces solæ*</note> | Choir of instruments <note>original: *Chorus Instrumentorum*</note> | All <note>original: *Omnes*</note> |
| Recitative voices <note>original: *Voces recitativæ*</note> | Instrumental ensemble <note>original: *Capella Instrumentalis*</note> | All, with voices and instruments |
| Concertato voices <note>original: *Voces Concertatæ*</note> | Symphony choir <note>original: *Chorus Sinfoniæ*</note> | Choir |
| Soloist parts <note>original: *ConcertatStimmen*</note> | Symphony <note>original: *Symphonia*</note> | Full choir <note>original: *Plenus Chorus*</note> |
