#### Chapter IV.
### Division or Distinction of Instruments. <note>original: *διαίρεσις seu Distinctio Instrumentorum*</note>
# A Brief Categorization of all Instruments,
## As they are used for making music.

In the last chapter of the second part of Volume I, as well as in the first chapter of the first part of Volume II, I presented several distributions and classifications of all musical instruments in various propositions <note>original: *thesibus*</note> and tables. It is unnecessary to repeat those here. However, one thing must be pointed out regarding this work: that musical instruments are succinctly categorized into two orders: namely:

1. All-voiced or Total. <note>original: *Omnivoca seu Totalia*</note>
2. Single-voiced or Simple and Specific. <note>original: *Univoca seu Simplicia, & Specifica*</note>

Or, as the magnificent musicians Agostino Agazzari <note>An Italian composer (1578–1640) who wrote one of the earliest and most important treatises on playing basso continuo</note> and Giovanni Girolamo Kapsberger <note>original: *Ioannes Hieronymus Iacobi*; a famous German-Italian virtuoso of the lute and theorbo</note> call them, **Foundation and Ornament instruments**; which terms we shall also retain here.

#### I.

**All-voiced** <note>original: *Omnivoca*</note> or **Foundation instruments** are those that can carry and include all the voices or parts of any song, and thus support the entire body and the complete harmony of all parts—including the middle and lower voices—in both vocal and instrumental music. These include: organs, <term>positive organs</term> <note>original: *Positiff*; a small, playable chamber organ without pedals</note>, <term>regals</term> <note>original: *Regalwerck*; a small portable organ that uses vibrating brass reeds to make a buzzing sound</note>, and powerful double, triple, and quadruple <term>harpsichords</term> <note>original: *Clavicymbel*</note>.

Also included here are spinets, lutes, theorboes, double harps, large citterns, lyres, etc., when they are used as Foundation instruments—though they are mostly used for only one, two, or three voices in a quiet and intimate setting. Otherwise, in a loud, resonant music performed by many and various people, they are better used for decoration and as **Ornament instruments**.

<vocab>All-voiced, Total, Single-voiced, Simple, Specific, Foundation Instruments, Ornament Instruments, Agostino Agazzari, Giovanni Girolamo Kapsberger, Organs, Positive Organ, Regal, Harpsichord, Spinets, Lutes, Theorboes, Harps, Citterns, Lyres</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the classification of musical instruments into two categories: Foundation instruments (which can play all parts of a composition, like the organ) and Ornament instruments (used for decoration). Praetorius draws on the theories of Italian contemporaries like Agazzari to define these roles in an ensemble.</summary>
<keywords>instrument classification, foundation instruments, ornament instruments, basso continuo, Agostino Agazzari, organ, harpsichord, 17th-century orchestration</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s technical instructions on the use of instruments in ensemble music. The previous page introduced the two primary categories defined by Agostino Agazzari: Foundation instruments, which provide the harmonic structure, and Ornament instruments, which decorate the melody.</meta>

-> 2. <-

**Single-voiced or simple instruments** <note>original: *Univoca seu simplicia*</note>, or **Ornamental Instruments**, are those used in a piece of music as if to play or "joke" <note>original: *Schertzando*, as the Italians say</note> and to create counterpoint, making the harmony more lovely and pleasing to the ear. They are also employed to decorate and adorn the song. These include all simple instruments that can only produce and sustain a single voice or part at a time. They are divided into **Wind instruments** <note>original: *Inflatilia seu Tibicinia*</note> and **String instruments** <note>original: *Fidicinia*</note>; in Italian, *Instrumenti di fiato* and *Chorde*; in German, *Blasende*—such as cornetts, flutes, trombones, bassoons, etc.—and **Bowed instruments** <note>original: *Besaittere Instrument*</note>, such as violins and viols, etc. These are described at greater length in the fifth chapter of the second part of Volume II.

As previously mentioned, spinets, lutes, and theorboes, etc., are also referred to by Agostino Agazzari <note>An Italian composer (1578–1640) whose work *Del sonare sopra 'l basso* is one of the earliest guides to playing from a figured bass</note> as ornamental instruments when they are used solely for decoration and to fill in the middle parts, rather than as foundation instruments, as shown in the following table.

| Instruments are either: | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **I. Specific** <note>original: *Propria*</note> | **Foundational:** <br> such as: | Pipe Organ, Positive Organ, Regal, and Large Harpsichord. <note>original: *Organum pneumaticum, Positivum, Regale, Clavicymbalum majus*</note> |
| | **Ornamental:** <br> such as: | Violas, Violins; Cornetts, Flutes; Fifes, Trombones; Shawms, Bombards; Crumhorns, Krumhorns <note>original: *CornaMusa, storti*</note>; and Bassoons, etc. |
| **II. Common** <note>original: *Communia*</note> | Which relate to both categories equally, such that they can sometimes serve as the foundation and sometimes as the ornament: | Spinet; Lute <note>original: *Testudo*</note>; Theorbo; Double Cittern; Harp; Lyre; and Chitarrone. |

## Chapter V.
### The Naming of Things: <note>original: *ὀνοματοποίησις (Onomatopoiesis)*</note> The Proper Naming of Instruments.

<meta>Catchword: How</meta>

<summary>Michael Praetorius classifies musical instruments into 'Foundational' (those providing harmony, like organs) and 'Ornamental' (those providing melodic decoration, like cornetts and violins), citing Agostino Agazzari's theories.</summary>
<keywords>Agostino Agazzari, ornamental instruments, foundation instruments, wind instruments, string instruments, organology, musical classification, Renaissance music theory</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of Agostino Agazzari’s classification of instruments into "Foundation" and "Ornament" groups. # How Instruments are Most Conveniently Named 
## and Pronounced in the Italian Language.

Since the names of musical instruments cannot be designated and observed as accurately with Latin words as they can with Italian ones, I have chosen to use the Italian terms for the most part in my works, and wish to record their meanings here.

### All-voiced, or Foundation Instruments:
<note>original: *Omnivoca, vel Fundament Instrumenta*. These are instruments capable of playing full chords to support the harmony.</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical glossary and classification of musical instruments. # L
## Chapter 6

<term>augmentative</term>, <term>diminutive</term>, <term>contemptuous</term>, Trombone, Violone, Trombino, Violino, Trombaccio, Violaccio, Cavallaccio, Fife, Flute

It is also well worth noting here, for further instruction, that the Italians use specific two-syllable suffixes at the end of words in their language to better and more clearly distinguish large instruments from smaller ones.

### The Nouns ending in:

**-one**
> These are called augmentatives <note>original: *augmentativa*</note> because their meaning is enlarged. Therefore, by adding this little word, large bass instruments are indicated: **Such as:**
> **Trombone**: Quarter-Trombone. <note>A bass trombone pitched a fourth below the standard tenor.</note>
> **Violone**: Large Bass Violin. <note>A term often referring to the double bass or a large bass viola da gamba.</note>
> (Although the word *Trombone* is commonly used and intended for a standard tenor trombone; the Quarter-Trombone, however, is called *Trombone majore* <note>greater trombone</note>, and the Alto Trombone is called *Trombone picciolo* <note>small trombone</note>).

**-ino**
> These are diminutives <note>original: *Diminutiva*</note>, because their meaning is diminished: and by this, the small soprano <note>original: *Discant*</note> instruments are meant.
> **Trombino**: Alto Trombone.
> **Violino**, **Violini**: Soprano fiddles.
> (And from this comes the practice that a bass player is called a *Violonistam*, while a soprano player is called a *Violinistam*).

**-accio**
> These are called contemptuous terms <note>original: *Contemtiva*</note> because they are used for disparagement. Not only instruments but all other things that are worthless and held in contempt are named this way.
> **Trombaccio**: Trombone
> **Violaccio**: Fiddle
> **Cavallaccio**: Horse
> } ...which is not fit nor useful to be used: a bad, useless trombone, fiddle, or horse, etc.

---

In addition, it should be noted that the *o* and *e* designate the singular number, while the *i* designates the plural: **As in**

| | |
|----------|----------|
| Trombone, a trombone: | Fiffaro, a fife: <note>original: *Querflöt* (transverse flute)</note> |
| Tromboni, trombones: | Fiffari, fifes. |
| Flauto, a flute: <note>In this period, *Flauto* usually refers to the recorder.</note> | Flauti, flutes / etc. |

<summary>This page explains the Italian system of suffixes (-one, -ino, -accio) used to classify instrument sizes and qualities, providing a linguistic key for musicians to understand terminology.</summary>
<keywords>Italian terminology, suffixes, instrument classification, augmentative, diminutive, trombone, violin, pluralization, musical grammar</keywords>

<header>Chapter VI. On the General or Continuous Bass.</header>

# How the General Bass,
## Continuous Bass, or Bass for Organ is to be understood,
->how the same should also be handled and used, as well as what else is<-
->necessary to remember regarding it: namely, what is generally expected of<-
->an organist, lutenist, or harpist, and how each should handle<-
->and use their instrument according to<-
->its type and nature.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'D' featuring a central mask or face surrounded by scrolling foliate patterns.</image-desc>
The **General** or **Continuous Bass** <note>original: *Bassus generalis seu Continuus*</note> is so named because it **continues** from the beginning to the end and, as a **General Voice**, encompasses the entire motet or concerto within itself. This practice is very common in **Italy**, and has been brought to light and published especially now by the excellent musician **Ludovico Viadana**, the **primary pioneer of this new invention** <note>original: *novæ inventionis primario*. Viadana is often credited with publishing the first collection of church music with a written-out basso continuo in 1602.</note>, who discovered the method of singing with one, two, three, or four voices accompanied only by an organ, regal <note>a small, portable reed organ</note>, or other similar **foundation instrument** <note>original: *FundamentInstrument*</note>. In such cases, a **General** and **Continuous Bass** for the organist or lutenist, etc., must necessarily be present **as a foundation** <note>original: *tanquam fundamentum*</note>.

By some, the **Continuous Bass** is very **appropriately** called a **GUIDE**, **that is, a Leader**, <note>original: *accommodè GVIDA, hoc est, Dux*</note> a companion, or a signpost.

Now, the **General Bass** was not invented for the sake of certain negligent or lazy organists—who would otherwise be reluctant to transcribe music <note>original: *absetzen*; the process of converting vocal parts into a score or tablature for the keyboard</note>—but primarily so that an organist, even if he cannot immediately play along from it at first, can more easily transfer it into his score or **tablature** <note>original: *Tabulatur*; a specific system of notation using letters or numbers instead of notes on a staff</note>. He should then place the one, two, or three voices above it and, with particular diligence, pay attention to and observe how they stand against one another. From this, he can then more easily and conveniently accustom himself to creating his own parts from such a **General Bass**.

In my humble opinion, this is the most important and best use of the **General Bass**: that it is a fine **compendium** <note>a concise summary or useful shortcut</note>, especially for the benefit of a **Chapel Master** <note>original: *Capellmeister*</note> and other **directors of musical choirs** <note>original: *Musicorum Chororum Directoribus*</note>, when such a General Bass is copied out several times, primarily in **concertos for multiple choirs** <note>original: *Concerten per Choros*</note>;

<meta>Catchword: will be [wird];</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the General Bass (Basso Continuo), crediting Ludovico Viadana with its popularization as a structural foundation for small-scale vocal works. Praetorius argues that it is a tool for professional growth and practical direction rather than a shortcut for lazy musicians.</summary>
<keywords>General Bass, Basso Continuo, Ludovico Viadana, foundation instrument, organ tablature, Chapel Master, musical direction, accompaniment</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distribution and practical use of the General Bass (Basso Continuo) part. It explains how this shorthand notation saves time for musicians at court and allows the director to oversee the entire ensemble. It also introduces the specific requirements for an organist to perform this style effectively, drawing on the Italian treatises of Viadana and Agazzari.</meta>

...so that these parts may be immediately distributed among the organists and lutenists—who now, at the courts of great lords and mostly in their princely chapels, accustom themselves to this method to save the time they would otherwise have to spend on intabulation <note>The labor-intensive process of rewriting polyphonic vocal parts into instrumental notation</note>—for each choir (ensuring that the part each is to play is specifically marked or underlined with red ink). In this way, the Director may also keep one for himself, so that he may have the entire composition before him, not only for the sake of the beat when it changes into triple meter or otherwise, but also to assist one choir or another.

However, so that an organist may also know how to make use of such a **General Bass** <term>GeneralBaß</term> and use it well, I have extracted the most important and necessary points for the benefit of those who do not know them from the instructions and lessons provided in the works of the aforementioned **Ludovico Viadana** and also **Agostino Agazzari**. I have translated these from the Italian language <note>original: *ex Italico sermone*</note> into our German here, and I have also wished to include and point out my own observations. At this point, it is necessary to speak and explain something not only about the organist, lutenist, or other instrumentalists, and what qualities they should possess, but also about the cantor, director <note>original: *Concertori*</note>, or singer (concerning whom more is said in Chapter IX).

---

# On the Organist.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'D' surrounded by intertwining vines and flowers.</image-desc>Regarding the organist, there are two highly necessary points and matters to be observed with all diligence:
1. What qualities he must possess.
2. Secondly, how and in what manner he should play, handle, and lead each song or concerto.

---

### The First Part.

## What Qualities an Organist Should Possess.

An organist who wishes to play from a **General Bass** <term>GeneralBaß</term> must possess three kinds of qualities and requirements <note>original: *requisita*</note>:
1. He must understand **counterpoint** <term>Contrapunct</term> <note>The art of composing or improvising melodic lines that follow specific harmonic rules against the bass</note>, or at the very least, be able to sing perfectly...

<sig>Q iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: Also (translated as "Also" or "Moreover")</meta>

<summary>This page explains the practical benefits of the General Bass for court musicians and begins a detailed guide for organists on how to master this new Italian style, citing authorities like Viadana and Agazzari.</summary>
<keywords>General Bass, Basso Continuo, organist, Ludovico Viadana, Agostino Agazzari, counterpoint, musical direction, court chapels, intabulation</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of requirements for a keyboard player or lutenist performing from a General Bass (Basso Continuo) part. It emphasizes the need for technical fluency so that the performer can focus on the singers rather than their own hands, and discusses the transition from German letter-tablature to staff-based notation.</meta>

...to also correctly recognize the **proportions** <note>mathematical ratios defining rhythmic changes</note> and the **beat** or **measure**, and to know how to resolve the "bad" notes <note>dissonances</note> into "good" ones <note>consonances</note> through all keys; likewise, to correctly distinguish between major and minor thirds and sixths, and to take heed of other similar matters.

2. He must well understand the **score** or **note-tablature**, and have great practice upon the keys, keyboards, or the fingerings on the neck of his instrument—be it an organ, regal, lute, theorbo, or similar "foundation" instrument. This is so that he does not have to "beg together" the harmonies or first search and speculate upon the strokes or chords <note>original: "lebotte," likely from the Italian *le botte*, meaning strokes or beats</note> required for what is being sung. He must know that his eye should always be directed and turned toward the book—the motet, concerto, madrigal, or song he has before him—and therefore he can give very little attention to the keys or the fingerings of his organ, instrument, or lute.

<margin>M. P. C. <note>Michael Praetorius Creutzbergensis</note></margin>
But since most organists in Germany use the German **letter-tablature** <note>A system using letters for notes rather than staff notation, unique to German-speaking lands</note> (which in itself is correct, good, easy, and convenient, not only for playing but also for composing), and since it would be very difficult for them to suddenly get used to staff-based notation or scores: it is therefore best advised that, in the beginning, they transcribe the concertos and songs entirely into their usual letter-tablature. There, they can see as much as is necessary how everything agrees with the **General Bass** <term>General-Baß</term>, so that through diligent observation and practice they might eventually accustom themselves to such a General Bass.

<margin>AA. 3. <note>Agostino Agazzari, point 3</note></margin>
3. He must also have a good and subtle ear for hearing, so that in the harmony, as the song proceeds, he knows how to follow and yield to the <term>Concentori</term> <note>The soloist or leader of the concertato ensemble</note>. However, it is impossible to set down or give any certain rules as to how and in what manner the songs and "pieces" (as we Germans call them) are to be played, unless certain signs are marked beside or above the notes—which Agazzari must indeed admit, though he does not observe this in everything.

<margin>M. P. C.</margin>
And although **Lodovico Viadana** in his first preface is of the opinion that it is not necessary to apply these signs <note>the figures or numbers above a bass line</note>, it is now found otherwise, not only in the General Basses of several other excellent (and indeed most) Italian composers—who have published many very beautiful and lovely concertos for one, two, and more voices in print—but it is also highly necessary that they make use of **signatures** and **figures**. <margin>A. A.</margin> This is in consideration of the fact that one cannot always [know] the composer's...

<summary>This page outlines the technical demands on a continuo player, including the need to play without looking at one's hands and the necessity of using figured bass signs (numbers) to correctly interpret the composer's harmony.</summary>
<keywords>General Bass, Basso Continuo, organist, German letter-tablature, musical notation, proportions, Michael Praetorius, Agostino Agazzari, Lodovico Viadana, musical figures</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of figured bass (numbers above the bass line) for keyboard and lute players. Praetorius argues that because harmony is a matter of a composer's individual choice, a performer cannot guess the intended chords without specific notation.</meta>

...necessarily follow his intent, sense, and composition. Furthermore, the composer is free to place at his pleasure a fifth or sixth, a third or fourth—yes, even a seventh, second, etc., in the **syncopations** <note>suspensions, where a note is held over from a previous chord to create a temporary dissonance</note>. Likewise, he may use major or minor sixths and thirds (according as it seems more convenient and better to him, or as the words and the text require).

<margin>M. P. C. <note>Michael Praetorius Creutzbergensis</note></margin>
But it is impossible for even the best composer <note>here likely meaning "performer" or "accompanist"</note> to immediately know or guess which **species** <note>types</note> of **concordances** <note>consonances</note> or **discordances** <note>dissonances</note> the author or composer has used.

Therefore, it is of the highest necessity—not only for the unpracticed, but also for well-practiced and experienced organists and **foundation-instrument** <note>instruments like the organ, harpsichord, or lute that provide the harmonic base</note> players—to mark the **signs and numbers** <note>the figures in figured bass</note> above the notes.

And just now, as I am handing this work over to the book printer, there has come to hand from Italy—exactly as I wished—a preface by **Bernardo Strozzi** in his third book, titled *Affettuosi Concerti Ecclesiastici* (that is, *Charming Spiritual Concert-Songs*), in which he approves of this very opinion of mine among others; and I have deemed it not unuseful to include it here.

## His words, translated from the Italian into German, read as follows:

> "Since I frequently find that in several **basso continuos** or **general basses** of concerts and other songs, no **numbered signatures** <note>original: "signatura Numerorum"</note> are placed above to designate the fourths, sevenths, ninths, or similar **dissonances** <note>original: "dissonantias"</note>, or even the **consonances** <note>original: "Consonantias"</note> such as major and minor sixths and likewise major and minor thirds: I cannot refrain from clearly adding and proving that such numbers are entirely and highly necessary, let others say what they will. For no organist can know or guess the thoughts of the composer. For if the organist thinks the composer has placed a fifth in a certain spot, it may well be a sixth; and I say the same of other consonances and dissonances: if he thus plays a fifth according to his own thoughts, and the singer sings a sixth, I leave every pure and clean ear to consider what kind of 'sweetness' the song will then yield. For everyone is free to create and add something in his own way and manner, provided it gives the ear and hearing a charming melody, which is the **goal of music** <note>original: "finis Musicæ"</note>—the right purpose and aim of all music."

<meta>catchword: Nun (Now)</meta>

<summary>Praetorius argues that organists cannot guess a composer's intended harmonies and must therefore rely on figured bass notation, citing Italian composer Bernardo Strozzi to support his claim. Strozzi emphasizes that incorrect harmonies (like playing a fifth against a singer's sixth) ruin the music's intended "sweetness."</summary>
<keywords>figured bass, basso continuo, music theory, Bernardo Strozzi, Michael Praetorius, dissonance, consonance, musical intervals, organ accompaniment</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and polemical argument for the use of "figured bass" (numbers written above the bass line). Praetorius is refuting the common counter-argument that a skilled organist should be able to play by ear or by simply following the singer.</meta>

Now, some say that the ear or the sense of hearing should be delighted as it pleases, and that one should move the fingers according to what one hears. But to those I answer that such a thing is not a good **logical conclusion** <term>Consequentz: the logical result of an argument</term>. For when the **keyboard** <term>Clavier: any keyed instrument, here likely the organ</term> is struck, it immediately gives off a voice and sound; even if one wished to pull the finger away again quickly, the instrument has already performed its function, and the **dissonances** <note>clashing or "harsh" musical intervals</note> have already been heard.

,, Furthermore, some say that the organist, in this manner and fashion, should always 
,, pay attention to the singer with his ear or hearing.

**Response:** If he were deaf, or could not hear well, and had to live in constant fear that he might play a **fifth** instead of a **sixth**, or a **third** instead of a **fourth**, he would be able to pay very little attention to his **basso continuo** <note>the "continuous bass" line that provides the foundation for the harmony</note> in such a state of fear. Often, while he is searching for the **sixths and sevenths** which he hears, he will skip over the notes and lose his place and the beat—which would not happen if he saw the **concordances and discordances** <note>the harmonies and clashing notes</note> marked before him with numbers. He could then move his fingers back and forth in a more correct manner with much greater ease.

And since such great difficulty is found when one sings into the organ with only one voice, I leave it to others to consider how much more difficult it would be when one sings with two, three, four, or five voices. In that case, it would be necessary for organists to be like the **donkeys of the March** <note>a regional joke; "the March" refers to Brandenburg. These donkeys were humorously said to have three ears</note> who, as **Paulus Fiviranus** <note>likely a reference to a contemporary writer or anecdotal source</note> relates, have three ears, so that they could give one ear to each **cantor** <note>singer or song leader</note> to hear what kind of harmony he was making; and even that would not be enough.

For I have also heard from the most prominent organists to be found today—who also did not think much of these **signatures** <term>the numbers and signs used in figured bass</term>—that **while playing** <note>original: "Sonando"</note> they have made a thousand kinds of dissonances because they did not want to pay attention to them. Then, when they heard the error themselves, they quickly began to **diminish** and **colorize** <note>types of fast musical ornamentation used to "fill in" or hide a note</note> until the **frenzy** <note>original: "furia"; a difficult or fast passage</note> had passed, whereby they often disrupt the ornamentations and coloratura of the singer.

Others, however, when they have a suspicion of an unusual passage or hand-position, have played three or four **octaves** on the keyboard so that the mistake would not be so easily noticed. This is not only unpleasant to hear, but is also an error when one makes so many octaves even **in a leap** <note>original: "in saltu"; jumping between distant notes</note> on the organ—which is the behavior of inexperienced women.

,, And there is now no need at all for me to repeat that this **signature** and **marking** 
,, up...
<meta>catchword: marking (zeich)</meta>

<summary>Praetorius argues that relying solely on one's ears to accompany singers leads to errors and musical chaos. He insists that figured bass notation is essential for accuracy, mockingly comparing ear-only players to three-eared donkeys or "inexperienced women" who try to hide their mistakes with loud octaves and flashy ornaments.</summary>
<keywords>basso continuo, figured bass, musical ornamentation, coloratura, dissonances, organ performance technique, accompaniment, musical polemic</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius's defense of "figured bass"—the practice of writing numbers above a bass line to indicate harmonies. He is addressing critics who believe that a truly skilled organist should not need these "training wheels" and should instead rely on their ears or a full score (tablature).</meta>

„ that the marking of numbers and figures was intended as if one wished to 
„ hold the **organist** <term>Organist: the keyboard player responsible for the accompaniment</term> to be so unskilled and ignorant, as if he did not know 
what he ought to do. To this I answer: That without these numbers, one would much rather consider him a fool—one of whose characteristics, among others, is that they must expect a thousand kinds of hesitation and folly. And so, when the organist presumes to guess the **composer's** <term>Componist: the author of the music</term> mind, sense, and thoughts—and to know them in advance—he will, by a **cloud of smoke** <note>original: "Rauch (tuffo)" — likely referring to a state of confusion or "hot air"</note>, become unskilled, dazed, and a fine **pigeon** <note>original: "Pivion" — meaning a simpleton or an easy target</note>, that is, a **fool** <note>original: "Coglion" — a vulgar Italian term for a fool or "blockhead"</note>. For this reason, people quickly say, "the organist is foolish and does not know where his head is." This should rightly move one to compassion—that such a poor and helpless organist must strike the keys and play so blindly and according to mere guesswork.

The **tablature** <term>Tabulatur: a detailed musical notation showing every single note of every voice part, rather than just the bass</term> of all parts was indeed invented before this time so that one should play them correctly, exactly as the music was set down; this was very well done. Whoever understands it correctly and can play well from it **at sight** <note>original: "ex tempore"</note> should follow it as best he ever can. But because it is a very difficult thing, and also tedious, to play from it with true security—and because the people who invented and taught it have either died or are at least very old—it would be necessary, as that old guard passes away, to relieve oneself of the burden. However, so that one could immediately join in a **concert** <note>a large-scale musical performance</note> without such long-windedness and difficulty, the **basso continuo** <term>Bassus Continuus: "continuous bass," a shorthand where the organist plays from a single line and fills in the harmony</term> was invented, which then creates a beautiful **consonance** <note>pleasing, stable harmony</note> and harmony.

But since some noticed and recognized that many **dissonances** <note>clashing or "harsh" sounds</note> were heard when one played such a bass so plainly and simply—because everyone follows the rules of music according to his own manner, **whim** <note>original: "Capriccio"</note>, inclination, and judgment—it was highly necessary to invent a means by which one could play the same quite accurately, so that no errors would be heard, and directed as much as possible according to the composition of the **author** <note>original: "Autoris"</note>. This could not have happened in any other or easier way than through this medium of figures or numbers. Through these, even a young boy, if he has only made himself a little acquainted with them, will play and handle the song as correctly and well, without dissonances, as if he were playing from the full tablature.

As I have then heard from some, and also proven in effect, they [the numbers] allowed the **motets** <note>sacred vocal compositions</note> of **Palestrina** <note>Giovanni Pierluigi da Palestrina (c. 1525–1594), the most famous composer of the late Renaissance, known for his perfect mastery of harmony</note> (which, as everyone well knows, are excellently formed according to the rules, with **fugues** <note>musical themes that "chase" each other through different voices</note>, and in sum with beautiful **ligatures** <note>connected notes</note> and **syncopations** <note>original: "Syncopationi" — rhythmic displacements where notes begin on a weak beat and hold over to a strong beat</note>...

<summary>Praetorius argues that "figured bass" (using numbers) is essential for accuracy, preventing organists from making mistakes when they cannot see the full score. He explains that while traditional full scores are better, they are too difficult for modern performance needs, making numbered shorthand the best practical solution.</summary>
<keywords>Basso Continuo, Figured Bass, Tablature, Palestrina, Music Theory, Organ Performance, Musical Education, Dissonance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Praetorius's argument for "figured bass"—the use of numbers over a bass line. He concludes a quoted passage arguing that without these markings, an organist cannot possibly guess the exact harmonies the composer intended, leading to "clashes" with the singers. He then begins a technical explanation of how to write these figures.</meta>

[syncopa]tions <note>The text begins mid-word from the previous page, likely "Syncopationibus" or syncopations</note> were mixed and intricate), they have, with the help and addition of such a signature of numbers, handled and performed the music in such a way that it seemed no different to the listeners than if it had been set in a full **tablature** <term>Tabulatur: a comprehensive score showing every individual note, which ensures the player makes no mistakes</term>; for the listeners heard no dissonances in the performance.

And who can really know whether, in a **cadence** <term>Cadenz: the musical "punctuation" or resolution at the end of a phrase</term>, there should be a fourth or a third, or even just the third alone? Or perhaps the third, the fourth, and then the third again, since it is left to everyone’s own discretion to add and create these **as they please** <note>original Latin: "ad placitū"</note>? Let the organist try to play a fourth and a third when the singer has the third, fourth, and third before them—or even just the third alone—as can be seen in various places in the *Vespers* of **Vincenzo Ruffi** <note>An Italian composer of the 16th century known for his sacred music</note>. Then let him tell me afterward what kind of "charming melody" this will provide for the listeners! What I now say regarding cadences, I also say regarding all **ligatures** <term>Ligaturen: notes tied together or connected in a specific rhythmic grouping</term> and **syncopations** <term>Syncopationibus: rhythms that shift the emphasis to the "off-beat"</term>. However, I do not wish to be understood as saying that one should write signs over every single note, as can be seen in the work of **Giovanni Battista Trabacci** <note>A Neapolitan composer and organist whose complex notation Praetorius finds excessive</note>; for in that way, one would confuse the organist far too much. Especially certain unusual notes, which do not occur every time and which a person cannot guess or think of in a hurry, must necessarily be marked and indicated. **Thus far the author** <note>original Latin: "Hactenus ille"</note>.

However, how and in what manner such signing occurs, and how it must be observed, should not be forgotten to be remembered here.

1. Over the notes in the **bass** <term>Baß: the lowest musical part played by the organist's left hand</term>, the **consonances** <term>Consonantiæ: pleasant, stable intervals</term> and **dissonances** <term>Dissonantiæ: clashing or tense intervals</term> which the composer and author have applied to it are marked with numbers and figures. For example, if over the first part of a **semibreve** <term>𝅝: a long musical note, roughly equivalent to a "whole note"</term>, the first **minim** <term>𝅗𝅥: a note half the length of a semibreve, or a "half note"</term> is intended as a fifth above it, but over the second part of the same semibreve, the second minim is intended as a sixth; then the numbers **5. 6.** must be written and marked next to each other above that note.

In the same way, it is handled with the 7th, 4th, 3rd, and **second** <note>original: "Secundæ"</note> when the composer has used them, as can be seen in the example placed below.

->**Altus.**<-

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for organists and composers on the use of figured bass notation. Having previously argued that numbers are necessary for precision, Praetorius now discusses the practical choice between simple and compound numbers, and the rules for marking accidental harmonies.</meta>

But because it would be too extensive and only make the work more difficult, it would be best to stick with simple numbers. Then an organist must use a good ear and great diligence to pay attention to whether it is better to remain below in the **third, fourth, or fifth** <note>original: "Tertia, Quart, Quint"</note>, or to use the **octaves** <note>original: "Octaven"</note> above them, such as the 10th, 11th, 12th, etc.

Sometimes it also happens that three or various different **consonances** <note>original: "Concordanten"</note> are set to one note; then just as many **numbers** <note>original: "Numeri"</note> should be drawn above it, as the following example shows. This, however, is very rarely observed, since the printers also complain about having to place so many numbers on top of one another.

<image-desc>A musical notation example on a five-line staff. The left measure contains a sequence of notes in diamond-shaped notation with multiple chords. The right measure contains four whole notes (semibreves) on the same staff line, annotated with thoroughbass figures. The first note has "5 6 5 4" above it, the second has "3", and the final two notes share a notation consisting of a horizontal line with "5 6 5" above and "3 4 4 3" below.</image-desc>

**A. A. 2.** All **consonances** <note>original: "Consonantiæ"</note> are either from the nature of the same **mode** <term>Toni: refers to the musical key or mode of the piece</term> in which the song or concerto is set, and these are not marked; or they are mixed in **accidentally** <note>original: "per Accidens"</note> (that is, they are not of the same mode, but of another), and these must be marked above. 

For example: if the song is inherently in the **"hard" key** <term>♮ dur / Cantu duro: the hexachord or scale system using a "hard" B (B-natural), often used for major-sounding modes</term>, then the **major third** <note>original: "Tertia major"</note>—which must naturally be used with G in "hard" music anyway—does not need to be marked. But if the composer (as sometimes happens) has set a **minor third** <note>original: "Tertia minorem"</note> to the G, it must be marked with a **flat** <note>original: "b"</note>, considering it occurs accidentally and cannot naturally be used with G in a "hard" song. 

Conversely, in **"soft" music** <term>Cantu B.molli: the hexachord or scale system using a "soft" B (B-flat), often used for minor-sounding modes</term>, the minor third is naturally always present; but if the **major third** is to be employed, one must note it with a **sharp** <note>original: "Diesi"</note> or—as it is otherwise called—a **canceled B** <note>original: "B Cancellato" or ⁜; a symbol used to raise a note, acting as a sharp or natural</note> above it, because here it occurs accidentally. And the same must be held for the **sixths** <note>original: "Sexten"</note>, as is set out hereafter.

**Tablature.**

<image-desc>A segment of musical notation on a single bass staff. **Note.** When the sharp sign <note>original: "Diesis"</note> ♯ stands to the side of or below the note, it changes and designates that same note where it is found; but when it stands above the note, it signifies the **consonance** <note>original: "Consonantiam"</note>, namely the **major third** <note>original: "Tertiam"</note> or **major sixth** <note>original: "sextam majorem"</note>, which should be struck and played with that same note. M. P. C. <note>Michael Praetorius Creuzburgensis</note>. And since almost always (except in **fictive music** <term>Cantu ficto: music containing accidentals that fall outside the standard Guidonian hexachord system</term>) a **minor sixth** <note>original: "Sexta minor"</note> must be used as the **consonance** <note>original: "Consonantia"</note> for the note where the sharp ♯ is found marked on the side in the **thoroughbass** <note>original: "GeneralBaß"</note>, as well as for the note **Mi** in **b-flat music** <term>Cantu b molli: music written with a flat in the signature, using the 'soft' hexachord</term>; therefore, some believe it is not entirely necessary to place the **number 6** above it, since it would not otherwise be appropriate, as can be seen in the previous example at the sixth note and in the **example in B-flat** <note>original: "exemplo B mollari"</note>.

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the notation of figured bass (GeneralBaß) for organists, specifically focusing on when accidentals and intervals must be explicitly marked versus when they can be assumed by the performer. It concludes the section by citing Bernardo Strozzi to support the author's practical approach to notation.</meta>

And because I find this same opinion in the aforementioned preface by Bernardo Strozzi, I have wanted to include it here as well, and his words read as follows:

> "It is quite unnecessary to mark the **sixth** <note>original: Sextam</note> above the note **Mi** in **b-flat music** <term>B-moll: music with a flat signature or using the soft hexachord</term>, since this is understood without further instruction—as nature itself gives and requires it. For if one were to play a **fifth** <note>original: Quintam</note> there, a **false fifth** <note>a diminished fifth</note> would be heard. But if the composer has intentionally placed a **fifth** there, it is necessary that this **fifth** be marked with the **semitone** <note>a sharp or natural sign</note> above it, as follows:
>
> ->#<-
>
> **5** so that the organist—in playing the **sixth** in a natural and orderly way—does not create an unbearable **dissonance** <note>original: dissonantiam</note>."

And although one should also do the same in the marking and notation of **major and minor sixths**, as well as **thirds, fifths, and sevenths**, which sometimes occur on the same notes through a **suspension** <note>original: Ligatur</note> and **syncopation**, and thus place two numbers above one another: Nevertheless, because it is quite lengthy and also a burden for the printers, it is left to each person’s pleasure. One must have some patience here, so that the singer turns his ear toward the organ and regulates and conducts himself according to what he hears. Therefore, it is much better to include or set one more **flat** <note>original: Bmolle</note> or **sharp** <note>original: Diesin</note> ♯ than to fail to harmonize and accord because it was omitted.

It is enough that the organist knows, or at least should know, that when he moves from a **sixth** to an **octave** <note>original: Octaven</note>, it should normally be a **major sixth**. And when moving from a **third** <note>original: Tertia</note> to a **major sixth** or **octave**, it should be a **major third**; and likewise in many other cases. For although these are regular and certain rules, they must nevertheless undergo all sorts of exceptions according to the **humor and whim** <note>original: humor vnd capriccio</note> (the brain and fancies) of every composer. Therefore, one can do no better than to indicate it with numbers above; then it is all the more certain, and one need not fear any error.

And so, to close this discourse, I say that those who are of another mind and contrary opinion perhaps do not understand the **art of the organ** <note>original: Orgelkunst</note>, or else cannot play it, since they cannot recognize or grasp the difficulty and hardship that the organist finds when he must play blindly (and always stand in fear that he might fail).

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical execution of thorough-bass (General-Baß) for organists. While the previous page addressed the notation of sixths and the use of accidentals to avoid dissonance, this page focuses specifically on the treatment of thirds in cadences and the debate between using figured bass versus fully written-out notation.</meta>

3. Since all **cadences** <term>Cadentien: harmonic resolutions signaling the end of a musical phrase or section</term>, both in the middle of a song and at the end, require a **major third** <term>Tertia Majorem: a "bright" sounding interval of four semitones</term>, the **sharp** <term>Diesis: the ♯ symbol used to raise a note's pitch</term> is not always marked above the note by some. Although the aforementioned **Agostino Agazzari** <note>original: A. Agazzarius; an influential Italian music theorist</note> himself considers it advisable that—for the sake of greater certainty, especially in the middle of a piece—one should mark it above. I am entirely of this same opinion; because a **composer** <term>Componist</term> often sets a **minor third** <term>Tertiam minorem: a "somber" sounding interval of three semitones</term> due to a **fugue** <term>Fugen: a musical texture based on melodic imitation</term> or other preceding reasons, which an **organist** <term>Organist</term> cannot simply guess. 

However, since I have found that some who are still inexperienced do not know how to distinguish how to recognize the **cadences** in the bass and how to use the **major thirds** correctly: It is to be noted that **cadences** in the bass **descend** <term>descendiren</term> by a **fifth** and rise by a **fourth**. But when the bass rises by a **fifth** or falls by a **fourth**, it is not a **cadence**, and the **minor third**—not the **major**—must be used. This is unless the **composer** has specifically set it otherwise for particular reasons; otherwise, **naturally** <note>original: naturaliter</note>, the **major third** can have no place there. Because I have heard and seen that even some experienced **organists** do not yet pay particular attention to this distinction, I felt it necessary to provide this reminder.

<image-desc>Two musical notation examples on five-line staves using white mensural notation (diamond-shaped notes). The first example is labeled "Minor third" (original: Tertia minor.) and shows three measures in bass clef illustrating cadential movements (descending fifths/ascending fourths) with minor thirds in the upper part. The second example is labeled "Major third" (original: Tertia major.) and shows four measures with similar bass movements, but with a sharp symbol (♯) placed above the staff to indicate major thirds.</image-desc>

Some are of the **opinion** <note>original: opinion</note> that in **thorough-bass** <term>General-Baß: a shorthand system where numbers and symbols indicate the harmony to be played over a bass line</term>, it would be better to use ten or twelve staff lines and write out the **thirds**, **fifths**, **fourths**, **sixths**, **sevenths**, etc., with notes above the bass, rather than marking them with **figures** <term>Ziffern</term> or **signs** <note>original: signis</note>. This is because it initially seems very difficult to those who have never seen this system of figures, let alone practiced or applied themselves to it. For my part, I find this **opinion** quite agreeable, primarily so that one can know exactly whether the **cadences** or **formal conclusions** <term>Clausulæ formales: standard melodic or harmonic formulas used to end a musical phrase</term> should be used in the upper voices. And I would have used this method in my own **works** <note>original: operibus</note> if such musical type had always been available for typesetting and printing; as I will report more extensively in the **appendix** <note>original: Appendice</note> of the following second part.

<summary>The author explains how organists can identify cadences by bass movement to correctly apply major thirds and discusses the historical debate over whether to use figured bass or fully written-out parts.</summary>
<keywords>cadences, major third, minor third, thorough-bass, Agostino Agazzari, musical notation, organ performance, music theory</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for organists on playing thorough-bass. While the previous page discussed identifying cadences based on whether the bass moves by a fifth or a fourth, this page explains how to handle specific musical modes when they are transposed, particularly focusing on how key signatures dictate the use of major or minor thirds.</meta>

However, it must be particularly observed and noted here: that in those songs where the **Mixolydian**, **Aeolian**, and **Hypoionian modes** <term>Mixolydij, AEolij, Hypojonici Modi: ancient systems of scales used in church music</term> are transposed down a **fourth** <term>quartam inferiorem</term>—because playing in a **fifth** <term>Quint</term> might sound too "sleepy," as indicated above, while a **fourth** <term>Quart</term> sounds somewhat fresher and more pleasant, especially on instruments—a **sharp** <term>Diesis</term> ♯ is marked at the beginning by the **clef signature** <term>Clave Signata</term> 𝄢. 

In such cases, the entire **song** <term>Cantus</term> must be played strictly using the **semitone f-sharp** <term>Semitonium fi</term> (which organists call *fis*). Therefore, it is unnecessary to place a ♯ above the notes found on *d* to indicate a **major third** <term>Tertiæ Majoris</term>. Since the ♯ is already marked at the beginning on the **f and F lines** <term>Claves f vnd F</term>, it signifies that all notes throughout those same lines or spaces must be treated as sharpened, just as if a ♯ were written before every single one. Consequently, the **major third** <term>Tertia major</term> must be used **naturally** <note>original: naturaliter; meaning "as a matter of course" due to the key signature</note> with the note *d*. If a **minor third** <term>Tertia minor</term> is perhaps mixed in **accidentally by the composer** <note>original: Autore accidentaliter</note>, it must be specifically marked with a **flat** <term>b</term>.

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for organists on the performance of thorough-bass (basso continuo). While the previous page focused on the theoretical application of major and minor thirds based on key signatures, this page transitions into the practical aesthetics of accompaniment, contrasting the strict simplicity of the Italian style with a more responsive, imitative approach.</meta>

**L. V. 1.** <note>These initials likely refer to Lodovico Viadana, an Italian composer who published some of the earliest rules for basso continuo.</note> The organist should play from this General Bass or score quite **simply** <note>original: *simpliciter*</note> and plainly, yet as cleanly and accurately as is ever possible, following the notes as they proceed one after another. He should not make many runs or **coloraturas** <term>Coloraturas: elaborate, ornamental melodic flourishes</term>, especially in the left hand, which carries the **foundation** <term>Fundament: the structural bass line that supports the harmony</term>. If, however, he wishes to use some speed or movement with the right hand—specifically in lovely **cadences** <term>Cadentien: the concluding melodic or harmonic formulas of a musical phrase</term> or other pleasing **musical clauses** <term>Clausulen: standard melodic figures</term>—it must be done with particular moderation and restraint. This is so that the **singers** <term>Concentores: from the Latin *concentor*, referring to those singing together in harmony</term> are not **hindered** and **confused** in their intention, or their voices **muffled** and suppressed thereby.

**M. P. C.** <note>These initials refer to Michael Praetorius Creutzbergensis, the author of this work.</note> For I have been informed by knowledgeable and distinguished persons devoted to music that there are several excellent organists in **Italy** and elsewhere who, in such **concertos** <note>In this period, "concerto" referred to any piece where voices and instruments "contended" or worked together, not just the modern orchestral form.</note>, use neither **diminutions** <term>Diminutiones: the division of long notes into many shorter, faster notes</term> or **passages** <term>Passaggi: ornamental scales or runs</term>, nor any **groups** <term>Groppos: a type of trill or turn, usually at a cadence</term> in the cadences, or even **mordents** <term>Mordanten: a rapid alternation between the main note and the note below it</term>. Instead, they play quite simply and directly, exactly as found in the **General Bass**, striking one chord and grip after another so that the motion and movement of the hands is almost unnoticeable. 

I find this quite agreeable, especially since one should not **apply** <note>original: *adhibire*</note> any **eighth or sixteenth notes** <term>Chromata or Semichromata: rhythmic terms for fast-moving notes</term>. However, it seems to me not entirely improper that in certain concertos, the organist should **observe** with special care when the **singer** makes his diminutions and passages. At those times, the organist should proceed quite **simply** and plainly from one **note** <term>Clave: literally "key," referring to the specific pitch or finger-key</term> to another, as if stepping gradually from one stair to the next. But when the singer—after performing many different movements, beautiful diminutions, groups, **tremolos** <term>Tremoletten: a rapid repetition of a pitch or a pulsing effect</term>, and trills—becomes somewhat slow or tired, and due to shortness of breath must begin to sing the following notes plainly and simply, then the organist (using only the right hand) should introduce fine, tasteful diminutions, etc. He should strive to **imitate** the singer's previously used movements, diminutions, and variations, so that they create an **echo** together until the singer recovers and can let his skill and sweetness be heard once more. 

Furthermore, in my humble opinion, even if no diminutions or similar movements are used, one should not entirely forget mordents or tremolos. For these do not **disturb** <note>original: *interturbiret*</note> the singer's or vocalist's voice at all—or at least not as much as various coloraturas and diminutions might. However, from now on, no one should be prescribed anything to their **harm** <note>original: *præjudicio*</note>; rather, everyone is left free to act as they wish.

2. Thus, it will not be possible in all concertos to play so strictly **on the spot** <note>original: *ex tempore*</note>...

<sig>S</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...on the spot</meta>

<summary>This page outlines two philosophies of organ accompaniment: the strict Italian method of playing only the written bass notes to avoid distracting the singer, and Praetorius's preferred flexible method where the organist "echoes" the singer's ornaments when the singer needs to rest.</summary>
<keywords>basso continuo, organ performance, musical ornamentation, accompaniment ethics, Michael Praetorius, Lodovico Viadana, vocal-instrumental balance</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for organists on the performance of thorough-bass. While the previous section dealt with the harmonic intervals, this page focuses on the physical execution of the accompaniment—how to start a piece, how to balance the organ's volume against the singers, and the importance of matching the vocal register during musical conclusions.</meta>

...play in time from the **thorough-bass** <term>General-Bass: a system of musical notation where a bass line is provided with numbers to indicate harmonies</term>; therefore it is necessary—especially for an **inexperienced player**—to first diligently and thoroughly review the song which he is to play. In this way, once he has correctly understood and become familiar with the **style** <note>original: *Stylum*</note>, the manner, and the nature of this music, he can more perfectly and surely **adapt** and join his fingerings and strokes on the organ accordingly.

3. **L. V.** <note>These initials refer to Lodovico Viadana, an Italian composer famous for developing early basso continuo rules.</note> Thus, Lodovico Viadana desires that the **cadences** <term>Cadentien: the melodic or harmonic formulas that conclude a musical phrase</term> on the organ be made in the same place and in the same voice as they are sung by the **lead singer** <term>Concentori: from the Latin *concentor*, meaning those who sing together in harmony</term>. Thus, if a **bassist** sings alone with the organ, the organist must also make the cadences in the bass; if a **tenor** sings, he must make them in the tenor, and so on. For it would sound quite poorly if a **soprano** <term>Discantist: a singer of the highest vocal part</term> made his cadence in his proper place, and the organist wanted to make the same cadence in the tenor an octave below, **and vice-versa** <note>original: *& contrà*</note>. Although some are of a different opinion on this, as will be mentioned hereafter in the 6th point.

4. When the song begins with a **fugue** <term>Fugen: a musical form where a theme is introduced by one voice and then taken up by others</term> or a **chorale** <term>Choral: a plain, often monophonic melody</term>, the organist should likewise begin the fugue as it is written, with only one voice or fingering on a single **key or note** <note>original: *Clave oder Calculo*</note>. However, when the other voices subsequently join in, he is free to add more keys <note>meaning notes to form chords</note> as he sees fit.

5. In a song or **concerto** <note>In the early 17th century, "concerto" referred to any piece for voices and instruments together.</note> where several voices have previously sung alone with the organ, and then occasionally all voices join in together—which the Italians call **full ensemble sections** <term>Ripieni concerti: sections where the full choir or ensemble performs together, rather than just soloists</term> (as mentioned above in Chapter 1)—one should indeed play with both **hands and feet** <note>original: *manibus & pedibus*</note>, engaging the **manual and pedal** fully together. However, one should not pull any additional **stops** <term>Register: the sliders on an organ that control which sets of pipes sound</term> to add more voices. Otherwise, the delicate and gentle tone of the singers would be far too overwhelmed by the loud sound and clangor of the many drawn stops in the organ, causing the organ to be heard more and more loudly than the **singers** <note>original: *Cantores*</note>.

<margin>«</margin> Although some—such as **Agostino Agazzari** and **Sebastiano Millerocca**—
<margin>«</margin> are of the opinion that one should pull more stops on the organ when the 
<margin>«</margin> **full ensemble sections** or **full harmonies** <note>original: *Ripieni concerti, oder pleni concentus*</note> begin. This can be done even more conveniently where **two keyboards** <term>Clavier: the manuals or keyboards of the organ</term> are available: so that on one, a very gentle set of stops is ready, and on the other, something somewhat stronger. Thus, in such an exchange, one can move from one keyboard to the other, using the gentle register when there are **few** voices, but when more and many voices join in, one can use the stronger or doubled stops and play **full harmonies** <term>Concordantias: the sounding together of various notes in a chord</term>. In...

<meta>catchword: few</meta>

<summary>This page outlines practical rules for organ accompaniment, emphasizing that the organist must adapt their playing to the singer's register and the overall ensemble size. It highlights a debate between theorists like Viadana and Agazzari regarding whether to increase organ volume (registration) during full choral sections.</summary>
<keywords>basso continuo, organ registration, Lodovico Viadana, Agostino Agazzari, musical balance, cadences, ripieno, accompaniment style</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific practical advice for organists accompanying vocal music, transitioning from the general rules of Lodovico Viadana to those of Agostino Agazzari. It focuses on maintaining a proper balance between the organ and the singers, avoiding register clashes, and the basic principles of voice leading in basso continuo.</meta>

...few voices / but reduce the **concordances** <term>Concordantien: here referring to the number of notes in a chord or the use of multi-rank organ stops that add harmonic reinforcement</term>, and bring few of them into the playing, so that the single voice of two or three people is not drowned out by a multitude of **concordances** or loud-sounding pipes.

**L. V.** <note>Lodovico Viadana</note> **6.** Thus, it is not so necessary for the organist to pay attention in the **score** <note>original: *Partitur*</note> to two **fifths** or two **octaves** <note>The organist is encouraged to focus on the singers rather than strict academic rules against parallel intervals in the accompaniment</note>, but much more to that which is being sung by the human voice. Therefore, when a **piece for equal voices** <note>original: *Concert ad æquales* (*â voce pari*)</note> is set, or a **tenor** or bass voice is sung, the organist should never go up high into the **soprano** <term>Discant: the highest vocal part</term> register, but should always remain below. Conversely, when high **soprano** voices are sung, he should not remain in the depths, but rather stay and **dwell** <note>original: *commoriren*</note> in the upper register; however, in the **cadences**, he may well use the **octaves** below, for through this the **melody** will become more lovely and pleasant.

**A. A.** <note>Agostino Agazzari, an Italian composer and music theorist</note> However, **Agostino Agazzari** desires that one should entirely leave out and avoid the high and bright voices in **foundation instruments** <term>Fundament Instrumenten: instruments like the organ or harpsichord that provide the harmonic basis of a piece</term>, because they hinder and **interfere with** <note>original: *occupiren*</note> the singers, especially the **soprano** and **falsetto** voices. Just as he also desires that one should avoid the "grip"—that is, one should not touch the same notes that the **soprano** sings—so that a doubling is not created, and thereby the loveliness which the good singer adds with **scales** <term>Tiraten: rapid musical runs that "shoot" through an interval</term> and **little runs** <note>original: *Läufflin*</note> is not obscured. Therefore, nothing is better than that one should, as said before, **proceed** quite **strictly**, restrainedly, and **with gravity** in one's playing.

7. Furthermore, besides this, several **principles** and **terms** are indicated by **Agazzari** regarding how one should play from the bass: such as that one should step **from an imperfect to a perfect** consonance <note>original: *ab Imperfecta ad Perfectam*</note>, and to its very nearest **concordance**; **likewise**, one should **resolve** the dissonant **intervals** <note>original: *bösen Species*</note> with the neighboring "good" ones, such as the **seventh** with the **sixth**, the **fourth** with the **third**, when the part resolves in such a way that the **sixth** or **third** comes above it. But if it is placed below, then on the contrary the **opposite** must be used. But to speak of this belongs properly **to the art of composition** <note>original: *ad Melopœiam*</note> in the **fourth volume**; therefore we now only wish to say how the hand should be placed upon the organ.

## The Bass moves in four
## ways:

For first, it continues itself and moves one after another **by continuous steps** <note>original: *per gradus continuos*</note>,

<sig>S ij</sig>
<meta>catchword: thereafter)</meta>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific practical advice for organists regarding basso continuo accompaniment, specifically focusing on the motion of the right hand in response to different melodic patterns in the bass line. It emphasizes the principle of contrary motion to avoid prohibited parallel intervals and maintain an elegant performance style.</meta>

...then with leaps <note>original: *per saltus*</note>; then with sequential runs <note>original: *Tiratâ continuatâ*</note>: And finally with separated and leaping black notes <note>original: *notis disjunctis*; these are shorter note values like eighth or sixteenth notes</note>.

1. If the bass rises by **continuous steps** <note>original: *per gradus continuos*</note>, then one should move against it with the right hand from above downward, either in a continuous manner or with leaps.

2. Conversely, if the lower hand in the **bass** <term>Baß: the lowest musical part</term> leaps, or descends from **thirds**, **fourths**, or **fifths** <note>original: *per saltus, von der Tertien, Quart oder Quint*</note>, one should proceed **stepwise** <note>original: *per gradus*</note> with the upper right hand. For it is not good to leap or descend with both hands at the same time; this is not only unpleasant to hear, but also unsightly to see—unlovely and inelegant—as there is no variety in it, but only plain **octaves** and **fifths** to be heard.

3. If the **bass** rises upward with a **run** <term>Tirata: a rapid scale-like passage connecting two main notes</term> and sequential small steps, the upper hand must remain stationary.

4. But if the music moves with separated black notes, one must assign to each note its own special **accompaniment** <note>original: *accompagnaturam*</note>, partnership, and **concordance** <term>Concordanz: a harmonious combination of notes</term> with the upper hand, as can be seen in the following example.

This should also be observed: when one wishes to set out a **figured bass** <term>GeneralBaß: a system of musical notation where a keyboard player improvises an accompaniment based on a bass line and indicated chords</term> from the actual bass part, the **little runs** <note>original: *Läufflin*</note> written with black notes <note>shorter rhythmic values such as eighth or sixteenth notes</note> should not always be written exactly as they are found. Instead, one should write and set them simply and plainly with **whole notes** <note>original: *Semibrevibus*</note> and **half notes** <note>original: *Minimis*</note>, as appropriate for the musical context.

M.P.C. 8. In several **figured basses** <term>GeneralBaß: a system of musical notation where a keyboard player improvises an accompaniment based on a bass line and indicated chords</term>—as in the **Dialogic Concertos** <note>original: *Dialogicis Concentibus*, referring to works written in a conversational or antiphonal style</note> of **Agostino Agazzari** and others—I find that even when high alto or tenor voices carry the foundation, the **figured bass** is set to be played an octave lower on the organ. This is displeasing to some, but to me, it does not seem so very wrong or improper. This is because on organs, and especially on **positive organs** <note>small, portable pipe organs without pedals</note>, one does not always have stops of **eight-foot** pitch <note>the standard pitch length that sounds in the same octave as the written note</note> which are equal to the human voice. Instead, one must sometimes use small stops of **four-foot** pitch, which sound an octave higher. Occasionally, one even has a **Regal** <note>a small, portable reed organ with a buzzy tone</note> of **sixteen-foot** pitch, which naturally sounds an octave lower than the human voice. In such cases, the music always proceeds an octave lower than the human voice, even if the melody is played exactly at the pitch where it is written. In this matter, I find that **Adriano Banchieri** in his *Cartella* <note>referring to Banchieri's *Cartella Musicale*, an influential music theory handbook</note> is entirely of my opinion.

But this has been treated with more detail in the second part of this third volume <note>original: *Tomi*</note>. And although **fifths** and other **forbidden intervals** <note>original: *Speties*; referring to rules of counterpoint that usually forbid parallel fifths or octaves</note> are sometimes found against the **figured bass**, surely no one is so inexperienced as not to know that such things do not arise from the **composer’s ignorance or negligence** <note>original: *ex inscitia vel incuria Componistæ*</note>. For even though the voices—

He also defends composers against charges of technical errors, such as forbidden fifths, suggesting these are intentional or unavoidable in complex accompaniments.</summary>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the proper arrangement of musical parts. The catchword "Bassus" from the previous page indicates the ongoing focus on the foundational bass line and how it interacts with other voices in different types of notation.</meta>

...must be composed exactly according to the **Musical Rules** <term>Regulis Musicalibus: the formal laws governing harmony and counterpoint</term>. However, when all voices are set into **musical tablature** <term>Tabulatur: a system of notation where multiple voice parts are written on a single large grid of 10, 12, or 14 lines</term>, as is customary, it is inevitable that parallel fifths and octaves will frequently appear to the eye in such a tablature. This occurs because the voices are written mixed together, appearing now above and now below one another: the **Soprano** <note>original: *Cantus*</note> below the **Alto**, the **Alto** below the **Tenor**, or the **Bass** above the **Tenor**, depending on how each part is found. This confusion, however, will not be found in a proper **score** <term>Spartitur: a "partitioned" score where each individual voice is given its own separate staff</term>, where the voices are written distinctly one above the other.

# Appendix.

And since I have observed among **organists** (who are not yet accustomed to this style where only one or two voices are set against a **thorough-bass**) that they merely play the **thorough-bass** and the one or two voices exactly as they are written; and yet it would sound quite poor and thin if no middle voices were played by the **organist** on the organ or other **foundation instrument** <term>FundamentInstrument: any instrument capable of providing a harmonic accompaniment, such as an organ, harpsichord, or lute</term>: Therefore (since an untrained person might not grasp this from the previous report), I must **explain** <note>original: *expliciren*</note> more clearly here how a beginning **novice** <note>original: *Tyro* and *Incipient*</note> can apply himself to the **thorough-bass** and learn how to play from it.

1. When a **thorough-bass** is encountered, one should ensure that with the left hand at the bottom, for every **foundation note** <note>original: *FundamentNoten*</note> found in the **thorough-bass**, one plays either a bare **fifth** <note>original: *Quintam*</note> alone, or with the **third** <note>original: *Tertien*</note> added; or else a full **octave** alone, or together with the **fifth**. With the right hand, however, one should play the **octave** alone to the **foundation note**, or the **third** added to it (which is a **tenth** <note>original: *Decima*</note> against the foundation), or even this **tenth** and **twelfth** <note>original: *Duodecimam*</note> together; and so on throughout. However, the signs ♯, ♭ and numbers 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, etc., marked above regarding **major and minor thirds and sixths** <note>original: *Tertias & Sextas Majores & minores*</note> (which were mentioned in the third point of the first section of this chapter) must be very diligently observed. And so, it is not necessary for the **organist** to **follow** <note>original: *observire*</note> the **vocal parts** exactly as they are sung while playing, but rather he should simply play the **harmonies** <term>Concordantien: the agreeable combinations of notes that form a chord</term> to the foundation for himself. For the sake of **better understanding and clarification** <note>original: *melioris intellectus & declarationis gratia*</note>, I have chosen to include this example here from the second part of my **"We All Believe"** <note>original: *Wir gläübens*</note>, which can be found in **The Caduceus-bearing Polyhymnia, or Messenger of Peace** <note>original: *Polyhymnia Caduceatrice seu Pacis nuncia*</note>.

<meta>This page continues the practical instructions for playing from a thorough-bass, specifically focusing on the organist's technique and the ideal ensemble composition for the basso continuo. It follows the musical examples of the previous page.</meta>

2. Careful attention must be paid to where the **clef symbols** <term>Claves signatæ: musical symbols such as the C, F, or G clefs placed at the beginning of a staff</term> vary and change upon the lines at the beginning and in the middle; otherwise, errors can very easily occur.

3. When a **soprano** <term>Discant: the highest vocal part</term> sings alone, or when two sing together with the **thorough-bass** <note>original: *General-Baß*</note>, it is, in my opinion, better to stay for the most part in the upper, smaller registers and keys. However, when **tenors**, **altos**, or **basses** sing, one should dwell <note>original: *immorire*, from the Latin *immorari*</note> in the lower, heavier, and deeper keys.

4. When few voices are singing, one should likewise use few keys—for instance, <gloss>c g c’ / d f / c’ c’ / etc.</gloss>—so that the voices can be heard distinctly and clearly above the organ. But when many voices begin to sing, then one should strike more keys and play with a fuller sound.

->**N.B.**<-
<note>Latin abbreviation for *Nota Bene*, meaning "Note Well"</note>

5. This is also particularly to be noted: when only two or three voices are sung to the **thorough-bass**—which the **organist** or **lutenist** <note>original: *Lauttenist*</note> has before him and plays from—it is very good, and indeed almost necessary, to have a bass instrument join in. This might be a **bassoon** <note>original: *Fagott*</note>, a **dulcian** <term>Dolcian: an early Renaissance woodwind instrument, the predecessor to the modern bassoon</term>, or a **trombone** <note>original: *Posaun*</note>, or—which is best of all—a **bass viol** <note>original: *Baßgeigen*; here likely referring to a large stringed instrument like a violone or a cello</note>. Therefore, I have encouraged several **cantors** <term>Cantores: church music directors or lead singers</term> in this regard, and it would be very praiseworthy if many of them would take it up. They should practice playing the bass in the choir on a **bass viol** (which is quite an easy art to learn). Because one cannot always have good bass singers in every school, this greatly adorns and helps to strengthen the **foundation** <term>Fundament: the structural bass line that supports the harmony</term>.

Or, one can also have the **thorough-bass** part itself be sung; for that reason, I have applied the text beneath it as well as it would fit in those **songs** <note>original: *Cantionibus*</note> where the text is not already found in the **instrumental bass parts**.
