<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for continuo players, transitioning from the organist (discussed on the previous page) to those playing plucked instruments like the lute and harp. It establishes the dual role of these instruments as either providing the harmonic foundation or adding decorative flourishes.</meta>

# On the Lutenist, Harpist, etc. How
->namely, that everything which has been discussed regarding the **thorough-bass** <note>original: *GeneralBaß*</note> and directed solely toward the organist may likewise be observed and utilized on the lute, harp, theorbo, and the like.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring ornate vine and foliage scrollwork.</image-desc>
Everything previously stated must now also be carefully observed on the lute, harp, **chitarrone** <term>Chitarrone: a large, long-necked lute used for accompanying singers</term>, or **theorbo** <term>Theorba: a bass lute with additional bass strings</term> when they are used as **foundation instruments** <note>original: *Fundament Instrumenta*; these provide the essential harmonic structure</note> and one or more voices are sung to them. For they should always maintain a firm, steady, full-sounding, and continuous harmony that carries the **human voices** <note>original: *voces humanas*</note> as it were; and they should be struck now secretly and quietly, then again strongly and vigorously, according to the quality and number of voices, and also according to the nature of the place and the concert. However, while the voice is performing its beautiful runs and **coloraturas** <term>Coloratura: elaborate melodic ornamentations or flourishes</term>, or otherwise **representing an emotion** <note>original: *affectum repræsentirer*</note>, one must not pluck the strings too forcefully, so that the voice is not interrupted or obscured thereby. And let this be said regarding foundation instruments.

But when the lute, theorbo, harp, chitarrone, etc., are used as **ornament instruments** <note>original: *Ornament-Instrumenta*</note>, they must be heard in a different manner than as foundation instruments. These ornament instruments are used in various ways to vary and mix with the voices for no other purpose than to adorn, decorate, and, as it were, **season and spice** <note>original: *Condiren vnd Würzen*</note> them. For just as the foundation instruments keep the proper basis and the harmony firm and steady, these ornament instruments must now adorn and "dress up" the melody with a variety of changes and beautiful **counterpoint** <term>Contrapunct: the art of combining different melodic lines</term>, according to the quality of the instrument. But herein lies the difference: on these ornament instruments, it is necessary that the instrumentalist has good knowledge of counterpoint, because he must compose new **passages** <note>original: *Passaggien*</note>, counterpoint, and thus almost entirely new parts or voices over that same bass—which is not so greatly required of the foundation instruments.

Therefore, the lutenist should play his lute—since it is an elegant, lovely, and indeed **noble** <note>original: *Nobilitiert*</note> instrument—well and grandly, with many kinds of inventions and variations. He should not act like some who, because they
<meta>catchword: with</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the rules for thorough-bass apply to lutenists and harpists, distinguishing between their roles as "foundation" providers of harmony and "ornament" players who add decorative counterpoint.</summary>
<keywords>lute, harp, theorbo, chitarrone, thorough-bass, foundation instruments, ornament instruments, counterpoint, coloratura, musical accompaniment</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for ornamentation and the proper role of continuo players, moving from general advice for foundation instruments to the specific techniques required for the lute, theorbo, and double harp. It focuses on how these players should avoid cluttered playing and instead use variety and judgment to enhance the ensemble.</meta>

...being gifted with a steady hand, do nothing from beginning to end but **pull and diminish** <note>original: *tirare & diminuere*; referring to drawing out the notes and breaking them into smaller, faster divisions</note>, that is, making vain flourishes and **coloraturas** <term>Coloratura: elaborate melodic ornamentations or flourishes</term>, especially when they strike in at the same time as other instrumentalists. Such players are no better than the others and also wish to be seen and held as great masters and makers of fast coloraturas. From this, nothing is heard but an unpleasant **confusion** <note>original: *Confusion*</note> and a disagreeable conflict—a **mess** <note>original: *Zuppa*, literally "soup," used here to mean a miserable, lame thing</note>—entirely unpleasant and burdensome to the listeners.

Therefore, it is much better if the lutenist—**now with strokes and sweet repercussions; now with wide passages, and now narrow and doubled, then with some bass flourishes, with beautiful contests and persistent imitations, repeating and drawing the same fugal themes on different strings or locations; in short, with long groups, trills, and accents in their proper time, weaving the voices together to give beauty to the ensemble, and taste and delight to the listeners** <note>original: *Hora con botte, e ripercosse dolci; hor con passaggio largo, & hora stretto, e raddopiate, poi con qualche sbordonata, con belle gare e perfidie, repetendo, e cavando le medesime fughe in diverse corde, o lochi; in somma con lunghi gruppi e trilli, & accenti à suo tempo, intrecciare le voci, che dia vaghezza al conserto, e gusto, e diletto all’ uditori*</note>—sometimes plays with lovely down-strokes and up-strokes; now with far-reaching, now with short, drawn-in, and doubled **passages** <term>Passaggien: ornamental runs of fast notes</term>; now with a **bass-heavy flourish** <note>original: *sbordonata*</note> of strange harmony, as if one were about to go out of the key, with a pretty and beautiful manner of **contests and persistent imitations** <note>original: *gare & perfidie*; where the player persistently repeats a musical motif</note> in which he repeats and brings about the same fugues on different strings and in different places, repeating and reiterating them. In short, he should use and weave in the voices with long groups, trills, and accents at the right time, so that he gives the concert a sweetness and taste, and provides enjoyment for the listeners.

Alongside this, however, he should take great care and use **judgment** <note>original: *judicio*</note> to see that he does not **interfere with** <note>original: *offendiere*</note> the other instrumentalists or run along with them at the same time. Instead, he should take his time, especially when instruments of the same kind are near one another and are not tuned in different pitches or are not of different sizes. Whatever is necessary to **observe** <note>original: *Observiren*</note> regarding the lute as the foremost instrument must be taken into account in the same way with other similar instruments.

The **theorbo** <term>Theorba: a large, long-necked lute with additional bass strings</term> greatly enriches the melody with its full and lovely **harmonies** <note>original: *Consonantiis*</note> because one must properly strike the large, coarse, long-extended bass strings—**your drone strings** <note>original: *Ci uoi bordoni*</note>—with very fresh strokes and slow runs downward and upward—**striking and walking gracefully** <note>original: *ripercotendo & Passeggiando leggiadramente*</note>. This is a special **excellence** <note>original: *Excellentia*</note> in this instrument above others, using quiet and moderate **trills and accents** <note>original: *con Trilli & Accenti muti*</note> made with the hand very close to the bridge. 

The **double harp** <term>doppel Harffe: a harp with two or three rows of strings to allow for chromatic playing</term>, because it is as good in the bass as in the treble, must be handled everywhere with lovely **plucked** <note>original: *pizzicate*</note>, sharp strokes—**with vigorous placement** <note>original: *Con vis poste*</note>—so that both hands **respond** <note>original: *Respondiren*</note> well and finely to each other with trills, etc. In short, it requires someone who can play good **counterpoint** <term>Contrapunct: the art of combining different melodic lines to create a complex harmonic texture</term> upon it.

<summary>This page warns lutenists against over-ornamenting and clashing with other players, advising instead for a varied and tasteful style. It also provides specific performance instructions for the theorbo and double harp, emphasizing their unique harmonic and technical capabilities.</summary>
<keywords>lute, theorbo, double harp, coloratura, basso continuo, ornamentation, musical judgment, counterpoint, ensemble performance</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how specific "ornamenting instruments" should be played within an ensemble. While the previous page focused on the lute and harp, this page moves to the cittern, the lirone, the violin, and the bass violone, emphasizing the need for stylistic awareness and group coordination.</meta>

The large cittern <note>original: *Die grosse Cither*</note>, called the **Ceterone** in Italian, as well as the **common cittern** <note>original: *Cetera ordinaria*</note>, should be used like other instruments **playfully** <note>original: *Schertzando*</note> and **counterpointing over the part** <note>original: *Contraponteggiando sopra la parte*</note>. This means that one should produce all kinds of good and merry jests with little runs, jumps, and counterpoint upon it.

Since every instrument has its own **terms** <term>Terminos: the specific technical vocabulary and idiomatic playing styles unique to an instrument</term>, the one who plays it should use these and regulate himself accordingly, so that he may produce his own high-quality work.

Instruments for which a bow is used have a different character than those that are plucked with fingers or **quills** <note>original: *Fedderkeilen*</note>. Therefore, the one who plays the **Lirone** and the **large Lyra** <note>A large, many-stringed bowed instrument used for playing chords, also known as the *lira da gamba*</note> should make long, clear, and bright-sounding strokes and **drawn runs** <note>original: *Tiraten*</note> with the bow, so that he may bring out the middle parts or voices well, and pay diligent attention to the **major and minor thirds and sixths**. Although this is difficult on this instrument, a great deal depends on it.

The treble fiddle, called the Italian **Violin** <note>original: *Violino*</note>, requires beautiful **passages** <note>original: *Passaggien*; melodic runs</note>, various and long **playful figures** <note>original: *Schertzi*</note>, **little responses** <note>original: *rispostine*</note>, and fine fugues which are repeated and echoed in different places, as well as graceful **accents** <note>original: *Accentus*</note>, quiet long strokes, **groups** <term>Gruppi: a vocal or instrumental ornament similar to a trill, often used at cadences</term>, **trills** <note>original: *Trilli*</note>, and so on.

The large bass fiddle, the Italian **Violone**, proceeds quite solemnly, as is fitting for the deep voices. With its lovely **resonance**, it maintains the **harmony** of the other voices and stays as much as it possibly can on the thick strings; often also touching the **double bass** <note>original: *Contra Baß*</note>, which is to say, the octave on the coarsest strings.

And it is now highly necessary with all these aforementioned **ornamenting instruments** <term>OrnamentInstrumenten: instruments intended to add melodic decoration rather than just the basic harmonic foundation</term> that everything be used with good understanding and deliberation. For when an instrument is alone, it must perform everything and lead the music firmly and surely.

However, when a company and other instruments are present, they must look out for one another and give each other space and room. They should not, as it were, collide against one another; rather, when there are many of them, each should wait for his time until the turn comes to him to demonstrate his **playful figures, trills, and accents**. They should not chirp among themselves like a flock of sparrows, where whoever can shriek and crow the loudest and strongest is the **best cock in the basket** <note>An idiomatic expression meaning "the top dog" or the one in charge</note>. This is likewise to be **observed** with treble violins, cornetts, and the like.

**M.P.C.** <note>Abbreviation for Michael Praetorius Creutzburgensis</note> And this point is, above all things, in every **concert**...

<meta>catchword: most- [original: aller-]</meta>

<summary>Michael Praetorius details the specific playing styles for the cittern, lirone, violin, and violone, emphasizing that musicians must respect the unique character of their instrument while maintaining ensemble balance. He warns against "chirping" over one another, urging players to take turns with their ornamentations.</summary>
<keywords>cittern, ceterone, lirone, violin, violone, ornamentation, ensemble dynamics, Michael Praetorius, musical terminology, string techniques</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for instrumentalists and singers within an ensemble, moving from the specific techniques of bowed and plucked instruments to the broader issue of ensemble balance, volume control, and the pitch-shifting dangers of overblowing and shouting. It concludes by introducing the theoretical justifications for the General Bass as formulated by Agostino Agazzari.</meta>

...must be most diligently observed by all kinds of **instrumentalists**, as well as **vocalists** and singers: so that one does not overplay or outshout the other with his instrument or voice. This is currently a very common habit, through which many magnificent works of **music** are fundamentally ruined and destroyed. In such cases, everyone always wants to be heard above the others; thus, the **instrumentalists**, especially on the **cornetts** <note>original: *Cornetten*; a wooden wind instrument with a trumpet-style mouthpiece</note> by their overblowing, and also the singers with their **shouting** <note>original: *Vociferation*</note> and out-calling, eventually rise so high in pitch that the **organist**, when he plays along, must stop altogether and finds himself at the **end** <note>original: *Final*</note> because the entire **choir**, through their excessive overblowing and outshouting, has been pulled upward by a half, or often even a whole tone, or more.

**Agostino Agazzari** <note>original: *A. A.* / *A. Aggazarius*</note>. This is undoubtedly what **Agostino Agazzari** intended; for he desires that wind **instruments**—because of the change caused by human breath, and especially the **cornetts** <note>original: *Zincken*</note>—should not be mixed into or used in quiet, good, and lovely **music**, but only in great, resounding **music**.

Sometimes, however, one can also use the **trombone** <note>original: *Posaun*</note> in small-scale **music** for the bass, provided it is played well and sweetly, alongside the small **chamber organs** <note>original: *Positiflin*</note> or organ stops of 4-foot pitch. **Michael Praetorius** <note>original: *M. P. C.*, referring to Michael Praetorius Creutzbergensis</note>. However, he who can truly master and moderate his **cornett** and similar **instruments**, and is a master of his instrument, shall not be included in this restriction.

To conclude the discussion on **General Bass** <note>original: *General Baß*; also known as Basso Continuo</note> music, I include here **Agostino Agazzari’s** own words, translated from Italian into the German language; it is left to each individual how they wish to receive and understand them:

> “Since I know that the **General Bass** is despised by some—who either do not under-
> stand for what purpose it is intended, or else cannot play from it—
> it seems to me that it would not be misplaced to mention something of it here.
> This method of playing from the **Continuous Bass** <note>original: *Basso Continuo*</note> was
> invented and brought into use for three reasons:
> 1. Because of the current custom and **style** in singing,
> where one **composes** and sings just as if one were
> **reciting** an **oration**.
> 2. For the sake of great convenience.
> 3. Because of the great quantity, variety, and multitude of
> **works** and **parts** that are necessary for **music**.

->T ij<-
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page warns against the common practice of singers and wind players drowning each other out and sharpening the pitch, and begins Agazzari's defense of the Basso Continuo based on its suitability for the modern recitative style.</summary>
<keywords>ensemble balance, overblowing, cornett, trombone, Agostino Agazzari, General Bass, Basso Continuo, pitch drift, vocal style, Michael Praetorius</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Agostino Agazzari’s theories regarding the "General Bass" (Basso Continuo), shifting from the practicalities of instrumentation to the aesthetic and practical justifications for this "new" method of notation and performance.</meta>

150.

->I.<-

„ Concerning the first [reason], I say: Because the correct way to **express** <note>original: *exprimiren*</note> the words has recently been discovered, in which one sings as nearly as possible just as if one were otherwise speaking to someone else—which is best achieved with a single voice, or at least with few voices—as seen in the modern **melodies** <note>original: *Melodien*</note> of several excellent people currently available, and which is very much in use in Rome at present. Therefore, it is not necessary to transcribe them all or put them into **tablature** <note>original: *Tabulatur*; a complex system of notation for keyboard or lute</note>; rather, it is sufficient to have the bare **bass**, as long as the **signs** <note>original: *Signa*; the numbers and symbols used in figured bass</note> are marked above it. And if someone were to tell me that for the old **motets** <note>original: *Moteten*</note> and pieces which are full of **fugues** <note>original: *Fugen*</note> and **counterpoint** <note>original: *Contrapuncken*</note>, this bass is not enough, I would give this answer in return: that such songs and their like are no longer in use among us because of the confusion and mutilation of the text and the words, which comes from the long and intertwined **fugues**. Furthermore, it is because they have no true style of delight or pleasantness. For when all the voices are sung, one hears neither the **sentence** <note>original: *Periodum*</note> nor the **sense** <note>original: *sensum*</note>, because everything is interrupted by the **fugues**, which repeat so often; and every voice sings and pronounces specific and different words at the same time, which displeases intelligent people who pay attention to such things. Indeed, it nearly happened that **music** was entirely driven out of the **Church** by a **Pope** for these reasons, had not **Giovanni Palestrina** <note>original: *Iohan Palesttrino*</note> taken up the matter and proven that the fault lay with the **composers** and not in the art of **music** itself. To confirm this, he composed a **Mass** called the ***Mass of Pope Marcellus*** <note>original: *Missa Papae Marcelli*</note>. Therefore, although such compositions may be good according to the **rules of counterpoint** <note>original: *Regulen de Contrapuncta*</note>, they are nonetheless not good according to the rules of good and true **music**. They arose because people never understood the **duty** <note>original: *Officium*</note>, the **purpose** <note>original: *finem*</note>, and the correct **precepts** <note>original: *Praecepta*</note> of this art, but instead looked only at the **fugues** and notes, and not at the **emotions** <note>original: *affectus*</note> and the correspondence of the words. Similarly, many of them first created the composition of the notes, and only after that did they patch and piece together the words and the text underneath with great effort and difficulty. And let this be enough on that matter.

The second reason is the great convenience: that he who learns to play has no need to trouble himself with **tablature**. Considering that such a thing is a burdensome and tedious matter in which one easily goes astray, especially when one is required to play **music** **on the spot** <note>original: *ex improviso*; improvised or at sight</note>.

The third reason seems to me important enough on its own to introduce such a **General Bass** <note>original: *GeneralBaß*; Basso Continuo</note>: namely, the great quantity of **works** <note>original: *Operum*</note> and books that one...

<summary>This page explains why the "General Bass" (figured bass) is replacing complex notation, arguing that old polyphonic styles obscured the meaning of the text. It cites Palestrina’s famous Mass as the turning point that saved church music by balancing counterpoint with clear emotional expression.</summary>
<keywords>General Bass, Basso Continuo, Palestrina, Pope Marcellus Mass, counterpoint, fugue, musical expression, notation, church music reform</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the translation of Michael Praetorius's *Syntagma Musicum* (Volume III), specifically concluding the section where he quotes the Italian composer Agostino Agazzari on the benefits of the "General Bass" (Basso Continuo) before providing his own practical advice for organists.</meta>

151.

<meta>catchword: A</meta>

...is otherwise necessary for music-making: For if one were to transcribe and put into **tablature** <note>original: *Tabulatur*; a complex form of musical notation for keyboard or lute that shows finger placement rather than just pitches</note> alone all that is sung in a single year in one of the churches in Rome where music is practiced, the **organist** <note>original: *Organist*</note> would need a larger library than any Doctor of Laws. Therefore, the bass was rightly invented in this manner, because it is not necessary, when singing with the organ or **regal** <note>original: *Regal*; a small, portable pipe organ with reed stops</note>, for one to play all the voices or parts exactly as they are composed. But if one wishes to play all the voices as they are woven together in the manner of **counterpoint** <note>original: *Contrapuncktes*; the technique of combining different melodic lines</note>, that is another task, and does not belong here to our current intent and purpose. Thus far Agazzari <note>original: *Hactenus Agazzarius*</note>.

->**Note Well.**<-

In conclusion, however, I must here give a friendly word of advice to the organists: when a **concert** <note>original: *Concert*; here referring to a multi-part musical performance</note> with several choirs is arranged in the church or even at the table for a feast, they should act just like excellent **orators** <note>original: *Oratores*</note>. When orators treat important matters and wish to speak impressively, they commonly employ a useful and fitting **introduction** <note>original: *Exordij*</note>—even though it does not strictly belong to the main work—solely for the purpose of making the listeners **well-disposed, attentive, and ready to learn** <note>original: *benevolos attentos & dociles*; a standard goal of classical rhetoric</note>, and to encourage them. So also should the organists, at the beginning with their **preludes** <note>original: *Præludijs*</note>, as it were, call together and entice the listeners and the entire **consort** <note>original: *Consort*; the ensemble of musicians</note> to immediately look for their parts and to tune and **intonate** <note>original: *intoniren*; to find the starting pitch</note> their instruments purely and without error, and thus to prepare themselves for the start of a good and well-sounding piece of music.

Because the lutenists and the violists mostly begin tuning their lutes and viols in G, it is very necessary that [the organist] first fully strike the octaves in G with both hands, lingering and holding still there a little while; thereafter falling into D, then into A, following into E, further into C and F; and in each one—but with the left hand—holding still for two or three measures <note>original: *Tact*</note>, even if they bring in fine little runs and other **ornamentations** <note>original: *Diminutiones*</note> with the right hand, as is customary in **toccatas** <note>original: *Tocaten*</note>. This should last until the others have purely tightened and tuned their lutes, viols, and violins, etc. And then they can begin a small **fugue** <note>original: *Fugam*</note>, a lovely **fantasia** <note>original: *phantasiam*</note>, or a toccata; then break it off briefly and proceed neatly and gradually to the final note in the **key** <note>original: *Clave*</note> in which the concert begins, so that they may catch the tone of the concert again with good grace. Then the entire consort altogether, with the full group, may begin in God’s name to perform a good concert, **motet** <note>original: *Mutet*</note>, **madrigal** <note>original: *Madrigale*</note>, or even a **pavan** <note>original: *Patuanam*; a slow processional dance</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the argument for the efficiency of Basso Continuo over full scores and provides practical advice for organists on how to lead an ensemble's tuning and introduction using preludes and rhetorical principles.</summary>
<keywords>Basso Continuo, organist, tuning, rhetoric, prelude, Agostino Agazzari, ensemble performance, musical notation</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 151, where Praetorius was concluding a section of advice to organists, comparing their role to that of an orator who uses a fitting introduction to prepare the audience.</meta>

152.

A truly immense nuisance and unpleasant sound arises when, while the **organist** is playing the **prelude** <note>original: *præambuliret*; playing an introductory piece before the main music begins</note>, the instrumentalists meanwhile begin to tune their **bassoons** <note>original: *Fagotten*</note>, **trombones** <note>original: *Posaunnen*</note>, and **cornetts** <note>original: *Zincken*; wooden, leather-covered wind instruments played with a trumpet-like mouthpiece</note>, making so much whistling and commotion together that one’s ears ache from it, and one might even catch a cold chill. Indeed, it sounds so foul and makes such a din that one cannot tell what is what <note>original: *obs gestochen oder gehawen ist*; literally "whether it is stabbed or hewn," an idiom meaning "a complete mess"</note>. Therefore, it is only right that everyone—before they must appear for service in the church or elsewhere—should tune their cornetts and trombones nicely in their own **lodgings** <note>original: *Losament*; from the French *logement*</note>, and prepare a good **embouchure** <note>original: *Ansatz*; the way a player applies their lips to the mouthpiece</note>, so that such dissonance and bad sound may no longer offend the **ears and minds of the listeners** <note>original: *Auditorum aures vnd animos*</note>, but rather delight them.

---

## Chapter VII.
### How every concert and motet with few
### or many choirs can be arranged and distributed
### in haste and without special effort using all kinds of
### instruments and human voices.

<image-desc>decorative woodcut initial O with floral and foliate motifs</image-desc>
Although I could have recorded in greater detail how and in what way every **concert-piece** <note>original: *Concert-Gesang*</note> might be arranged in several specific manners by varying the instruments and other elements, I have nonetheless hesitated to do so for the time being. This is because music has now risen so very high that excellent musicians are now found even in **our fatherland of Germany** <note>original: *Germania nostra patria*</note>. These musicians not only publish through public printing most magnificent and lovely German and Latin **concerts and songs** <note>original: *Concert vnd Cantiones*</note>, but they themselves also know how to better arrange and direct such concerts **by choirs** <note>original: *per Choros*</note> than I, in my insignificance, could prescribe or bring to light.

To speak briefly of this, however, I have considered the following method, which I have not seen elsewhere before: namely, that for one **author** <note>original: *Autore*</note>, one should record the **clef signs** <note>original: *Claves signatas*; the specific clefs used for each part</note> of every voice of a concert-piece one after another. I have observed this in my own work *Polyhymnia* within the **General Bass** <note>original: *Basso generali*; the continuous bass line that provides the harmonic foundation</note> part, where I have recorded the clef signs of all the songs contained therein, organized for all voices in proper order.

<meta>catchword: Therefore (Do)</meta>

<summary>Praetorius critiques the unprofessional habit of musicians tuning loudly during an organ prelude and introduces a practical method for organizing complex multi-choir musical works by cataloging their clefs.</summary>
<keywords>tuning etiquette, cornett, trombone, bassoon, musical arrangement, German musicians, Polyhymnia, clef signs, General Bass</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 152, where Praetorius concluded his advice on the proper tuning of instruments before a service to avoid a "foul din" that might offend the listeners. This page begins Chapter VII, offering practical examples for arranging choirs.</meta>

In this way, as if in a mirror, the nature of the entire **concert-piece** <note>original: *Concerts*</note> can be clearly observed and noted: how high or low each voice may approximately **ascend** <note>original: *ascendiren*</note> or **descend** <note>original: *descendiren*</note> according to the **tone** or **musical mode** <note>original: *Tono oder Modo*</note> in which the song is set. From this, one can determine which wind or stringed instruments are suitable to use for each voice, and which **choir** <note>original: *Choro*</note> the **chapel choir** <note>original: *Capella*</note> and **singers** <note>original: *Cantores*</note> should be assigned. As an example, I will here include several **motets** <note>sacred polyphonic choral compositions</note> by **Orlando di Lasso** <note>original: *Orlandi*; a famous Franco-Flemish composer of the late Renaissance</note>, which are widely known <note>original: *quæ in omnium manibus sunt*; literally "which are in everyone's hands"</note>.

->1. Choir. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; 2. Choir.<-

<image-desc>Musical notation showing vocal and instrumental ranges for a seven-part setting of "Praise the Lord, ye children, for 7 voices" <note>original: *Laudate pueri Dominum à 7*</note>. The notation is divided into two choirs. Underneath the staves are numbers 1 through 7. The staves show different clefs—C-clefs and F-clefs—and the specific note ranges for each part. A fermata symbol appears above the final double bar line.</image-desc>

In this example, it is immediately visible that if **instrumentalists** <note>original: *Instrumentisten*</note> are available, in the **first choir**, the two **sopranos** <note>original: *Discant*</note> should be performed with two **transverse flutes** <note>original: *Querflöiten*</note>, or two **treble violins** <note>original: *Discantgeigen*</note>, or two **cornetts** <note>original: *Cornetten*</note>. The **alto**, however (acting as the **bass** <note>original: *Bassett*</note> in this specific choir), should be a **human voice** <note>original: *humana voce*</note>. In the **other choir**, the alto (acting as the **soprano** <note>original: *Cantus*</note> in this second choir) should also be a human voice, while the two **tenors** and the **bass** must be arranged and performed with three **trombones** <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>.

->1. Choir. &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; 2. Choir.<-

<image-desc>Musical notation showing vocal and instrumental ranges for an eight-part setting of "When the Lord turned back the captivity" <note>original: *Inconvertendo à 8*; a setting of Psalm 126</note>. The notation is divided into two choirs. Underneath the staves are numbers 1 through 8. The staves show various C-clefs and F-clefs and their respective note ranges. A fermata symbol appears above the final double bar line.</image-desc>

For the **first choir**, one can very suitably use three **transverse flutes**, or three **muted cornetts** <note>original: *stille Zincken*</note>, or three **violins** <note>original: *Violini*</note>. Or perhaps one violin, one cornett, and a transverse or **recorder** <note>original: *Blockflöit*</note> mixed together. For the **bass** <note>original: *Bassett*</note> of this choir, a **tenor singer** <note>original: *Tenoristen*</note> may be used, and alongside him, if desired, also a trombone. Even a trombone or **bassoon** <note>original: *Fagott*</note> **without a human voice** <note>original: *absque voce humana*</note> would work well. In that case, a boy should be assigned to one soprano part so that the words can be heard and understood. For the **other choir**, one can use **purely voices** <note>original: *eitel voces*</note>, or **violas da gamba**, or **violas da braccio**, or recorders, along with a bassoon or **quart-trombone** <note>original: *QuartPosaun*; a large trombone pitched a fourth lower than the standard tenor</note>. However, [ensure] that either the soprano or the tenor, or both...

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: mit</meta>

In such choirs, the **soprano** <note>original: *Cant*</note>, **tenor**, or **alto**, etc., must also be sung with the human voice simultaneously. Anyone can look up and adapt more examples of this kind themselves from the **clef signs** <note>original: *Claves Signatas*</note> as they are recorded in modern **music books** <note>original: *Musicorum libris*</note>.

Here, a very brief indication shall be given as to which instruments can be used for a specific voice or **part** <note>original: *partoyen*</note>, according to how it is **cleffed** <note>original: *claviret*</note> or marked with a clef sign.

### I.
### Cornetts: Violins.
->Choir of **Cornetts** <note>original: *Zincken*</note> and **Soprano Violins** <note>original: *Discant Geigen*</note>.<-

<image-desc>A musical illustration showing four musical staves with various clefs (G-clefs and C-clefs) and notes representing the typical pitch ranges for cornetts and violins in these registers.</image-desc>

page 155.
B ij
<meta>catchword: IL</meta>

If in a choir these four **clef signs** <note>original: *Claves signatæ*</note> are found, whether in **standard singing** <note>original: *Cantu regulari*; the natural scale</note> or **transposed** <note>original: *transposito*</note>—that is, in **major** <note>original: *♮ duro*; cantus durus</note> or **minor** <note>original: *binolli*; cantus mollis</note>—however they might be mixed together, they essentially form a **cornett** <note>a wooden, leather-wrapped wind instrument with a trumpet-like mouthpiece</note> or **violin ensemble**. However, in very high-pitched choirs it is almost better to use violins rather than cornetts, unless a skilled cornettist is available who knows how to control <note>original: *moderiren*</note> and master his instrument well, and thus can take the highest **soprano part** <note>original: *Cantum*</note> for himself. One should not always use nothing but cornetts, or nothing but violins, but occasionally mix them together; for example, using one violin and two cornetts; two violins and one cornett; or one violin, one cornett, and one **transverse** or **recorder** <note>original: *Quer- oder Blockflöit*</note>. One might also assign a **soprano singer** <note>original: *Discantisten*</note> to sing one part with the **human voice** <note>original: *humana voce*</note>. In such cases, the **basset** <note>the mid-low or "little bass" part</note> should not be sung, but can be played with a **trombone** <note>original: *Posaun*</note> or a similar instrument, as the situation requires. And in such choirs, a **basset** part is commonly found written in these clefs:

— <image-desc>A five-line musical staff with a tenor clef <note>C-clef on the fourth line</note></image-desc> — or — <image-desc>A five-line musical staff with a high bass clef <note>F-clef on the third line</note></image-desc> — or — <image-desc>A five-line musical staff with a standard bass clef <note>F-clef on the fourth line</note></image-desc> — 

which is then either sung, or played along with a trombone or **bassoon** <note>original: *Fagott*</note>.

But if, in addition to the basset, the — <image-desc>A five-line musical staff with an alto clef <note>C-clef on the third line</note></image-desc> — is found among the other clefs listed above at the beginning, it is better to use a trombone with the cornetts (provided an instrumentalist is available who can play a good **alto** on the trombone) and a **viola da braccio** <note>original: *Viol da bratio*; the violin family</note> or, as it is otherwise called, a **tenor violin** <note>viola</note> with the violins.

For cornetts have their lowest note at *a*, though some instrumentalists can manage to produce *g*, or even *f*, in **falsetto**. However, because at such a depth it has no **grace** <note>original: *gratiam*</note> but sounds almost like a cow-horn; and since the **soprano violins** <note>original: *Discantgeigen*</note> only go down to *g* and do not produce a proper **harmony** on their lowest strings; I therefore consider it much better and more pleasant, as just mentioned, to use a trombone or tenor violin for those parts.

**Vocabulary:** **Cornetts**, **Violin**, **Soprano**, **Basset**, **Trombone**, **Transverse Flute**, **Recorder**, **Soprano Singer**, **Bassoon**, **Viola da braccio**, **Tenor Violin**, **Clef Signs**.

## Transverse Flutes—
# Ensemble of Transverse Flutes or Fifes.

Example 1: <note>Four musical staves showing arrangements by Claudio Merulo: "Song for 7 voices" and "Song for 8 voices."</note>
**or**
Example 2: <note>Four musical staves showing "Blessed are all" (8 voices) by Giovanni Gabrieli; "Come let us rejoice" (8 voices) by Hans Leo Hassler; and "Great mystery of the inheritance."</note>
**or**
Example 3: <note>A single musical staff segment.</note>

<note>Additional musical notation showing various clef arrangements, including a bass clef.</note>
**or**
**sometimes also**

Choirs marked and designated in this manner are correctly arranged for three **transverse flutes** <note>original: *Querflöiten*</note> and one **bassoon** <note>original: *Fagott*</note>, or a **quiet shawm** <note>original: *stiller Pombard*; a low-pitched woodwind with a more mellow tone than a standard shawm</note>, or a **trombone**. For although transverse flutes are sometimes used in the **natural scale** <note>original: *Cantu ♮ duro*; the scale using B-natural rather than B-flat</note>, it does not work equally well in all **modes** <note>the historical system of scales used before modern major and minor</note> or keys. Therefore, one is accustomed to performing the tenth mode—the **Hypoaeolian**—one tone lower on the transverse flutes. And no modes are better suited for this than the **Dorian**, **Hypodorian**, and the **Hypoaeolian** transposed down a second. This is because the natural **range** <note>original: *ambitus*</note> of the transverse flute does not naturally go higher than G <note>g''</note>, though it can certainly be pushed up to A <note>a''</note>.

The first and second examples shown above are more convenient and better to use than the third. For the **tenor part** in the <note>C-clef on the fourth line</note> becomes far too quiet in its lower range on the transverse flute, so that one cannot clearly hear it against the **soprano** <note>original: *Cant*</note> and **alto**, which always remain in the upper octaves. Therefore, it is better that...

...use a **trombone** or a **tenor violin** <note>original: *Tenorgeig*</note> for this clef. Otherwise, such a tenor part can be performed and used very comfortably and correctly an **octave higher** <note>original: *in Octava superiore*</note> with a **transverse flute**—if no other flutes are available—alongside all kinds of other instruments.

### III.

**Chapel** <note>original: *Capella*</note>; **human voice** <note>original: *vox humana*</note> : Human voices.
**Flutes** <note>original: *Flauti*</note> : Flute Ensemble.
**Violas da Gamba** : Viol Ensemble.
**Violas da Braccio** : Violin Ensemble. <note>Instruments played "on the arm" like the modern violin or viola, as opposed to "on the leg" like the gamba.</note>

<image-desc>Musical notation showing five separate staves, each with different clefs (Bass, Tenor, Alto, Soprano, and Treble) and specific notes positioned to illustrate the ranges or assignments of the instrumental and vocal groups mentioned above. Below the staves is the text "All nations. 16 Voices. I. Gabrieli." <note>original Latin: "Omnes gentes. 16. Voc. I. Gabr." refers to a motet by Giovanni Gabrieli.</note></image-desc>

Such ensembles, in the **natural scale** <note>original: *Cantu ♮ duro*; the hexachord using B-natural</note> or the **flat scale** <note>original: *bmolli*; the hexachord using B-flat</note>, properly belong to the **chapel** <note>the full vocal-instrumental ensemble</note> and to human voices—or as they are also called, **vocal** or **concertato** voices <note>parts designed for soloists or specific vocal groups</note>—that is, for the **singers** and vocalists. For these **clefs** <note>original: *Claves*</note> remain within their proper and natural **limits** <note>original: *terminis*</note> for human voices, being neither too high nor too low.

These ensembles are also very well suited for **recorders** <note>original: *Blockflötten (Flauti)*</note>; as well as for the **violas da gamba**, or in their absence, for the **violas da braccio**. However, nowadays the ensemble for the violas da gamba is usually marked and **cleffed** <note>written with specific clefs</note> almost like the first trombone ensemble (as follows hereafter). This is because the highest string on the **treble viol** <note>original: *Discant Viol de Gamba*</note> is quite thin and is not heard with the same strength as the other, thicker strings on the tenor or bass viols. Therefore, it is better to take an **alto-tenor viol** instead of the treble viol, or otherwise mostly remain on the thicker strings when playing the treble part.

Since boys are also often found—and most of them could be trained so well, if only one would apply good diligence to them and not let the effort become wearisome—that they could sing a part marked thus— <image-desc>A single musical staff with a G-clef and a note on the second line (G4)</image-desc> —the G, also

<keywords>instrumentation, Giovanni Gabrieli, clefs, violas da gamba, recorders, vocal ranges, boys' voices, musical notation</keywords>

IV.

...can certainly have pure and correct [registers]; which then must necessarily be present especially in my first style, as in *In Dulci Jubilo*, *Now Praise, My Soul, the Lord*, and *Alone to God in the Highest*: whenever the trumpets and kettledrums are directed [to play].

Therefore, one can also sometimes use such high signatures—namely, the high *g*—in the aforementioned **chapels** <note>the full musical ensemble</note>: Or one may let the **Alto** <note>original: *Altum*</note> be sung an octave higher by a boy, which creates or gives off a pleasant harmony. Similarly, in some **songs** <note>original: *Cantionibus*</note>, letting the **Tenor** part be sung an octave above by a boy is not unpleasant to hear.

If one wishes to arrange a **flute choir** <note>original: *FlöttenChor*</note> among and alongside other distinct choirs equipped with other instruments: I consider it better that a **fourth-trombone** <note>original: *QuartPosaun*; a trombone pitched a fourth lower than the standard tenor</note> or, what is even more convenient, a **bassoon** <note>original: *Fagott*</note> be assigned to the Bass. Likewise, a trombone or **tenor violin** <note>original: *Tenorgeig*</note> should be used for the Tenor instead of the flutes. This is because the tenor and especially the **bass flutes** <note>original: *Baßflötten*</note> are far too soft in the low register, so that they cannot be heard well—indeed, very little—next to the small **descant** <note>soprano</note> and alto flutes, or against the other instruments in the accompanying choirs.

However, if one wishes to use the flutes entirely alone, without the addition of other instruments, in a **canzona**, **motet**, or also in a **concerto** arranged for choirs: then one can very well and fittingly use the whole set and range of flutes, especially the five types starting from the largest <note>literally: "coarsest"</note>, because the small ones shriek far too strongly and loudly. This produces a very pleasant, quiet, and lovely harmony, especially in rooms and chambers; since in a church, the large **basset** and **bass flutes** cannot be heard well. For this reason, the other choirs—which might be arranged with **violas da gamba** or human voices alongside them—must perform and intone their parts with a **subdued voice** <note>original: *submissa voce*</note>, quietly and softly, if indeed one choir and one voice is to be properly heard and observed alongside the other.

Item: If in a concerto there are two such marked **instrumental choirs** <note>original: *Chori Instrumentales*</note> (which are to be found distinctively in my third style): then one can arrange it according to the described manner, provided that the Alto in both choirs is performed in the **upper octave** <note>original: *octava superiore*</note>, that is, an octave higher.

IV. Trombones: Bassoons:

# Trombone / Bassoon Consort.

<image-desc>Eight numbered musical examples (1-8) displaying various configurations of C and F clefs on five-line staves. The examples illustrate different ways to notate parts for trombone and bassoon ensembles. The word "or" is printed between several of the examples to indicate alternative notation styles.</image-desc>

If these **clefs** <term>musical signs that fix the pitch of the notes on the staff</term> are found in the **consorts** <note>original: *Choren*; here referring to ensembles of instruments of the same family</note> as they are recorded here, or are set in many other various ways: they are all intended so that they must be arranged with pure **trombones** <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, or **bassoons** <note>original: *Fagotten*</note>, or **shawms** <term>original: *Pommern*; a family of powerful double-reed instruments, predecessors of the modern oboe and bassoon</term>, or even with bassoons and trombones mixed together.

And for the most part, one allows the **Alto**, which is marked and cleffed like this <image-desc>musical staff showing an alto C-clef</image-desc>—or sometimes also one of the **Tenors**, <image-desc>musical staff showing a tenor C-clef</image-desc> who carries the best **aria and melody**, to sing with a **human voice** <note>original: *humana voce*</note> among the trombones and bassoons;

<meta>Catchword: Where then</meta>

<summary>This page explains how to arrange instrumental consorts using trombones, bassoons, and shawms based on specific clef configurations. It also highlights the common practice of including a human alto or tenor voice to sing the primary melody within these instrumental groups.</summary>

<keywords>trombone, bassoon, shawm, consort, clefs, orchestration, vocal performance, Renaissance music notation</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for arranging instrumental ensembles, specifically focusing on the pitch ranges and practical limitations of woodwind and brass instruments like the bassoon and trombone when substituting for vocal parts.</meta>

In cases where the **Alto** is not sung, but must be played on an **alto recorder** or a **treble violin** in the octave above <note>original: *Epidiapason*; a term derived from Greek meaning an octave higher</note>. Just as frequently, a **Soprano** <image-desc>Musical staff snippet showing a C-clef on the bottom line, used for soprano parts.</image-desc> is also provided for singing, or for the **cornett**, or for the **treble violin**; as seen in the 3rd and 5th examples.

However, it is particularly important to note here, if one wishes to use only **bassoons** or **shawms** <term>original: *Pommern*; a family of loud, double-reed instruments</term> for such a consort, that special care must be taken so that in those parts marked with this **clef** <image-desc>Musical staff snippet showing a bass F-clef on the fourth line.</image-desc>, the melody or the notes do not rise higher than **b-flat** <note>original: *b*; in historical German notation, this refers specifically to B-flat</note>; for the **standard bassoons** <note>original: *Chorist Fagotten*; the most common size of the instrument at the time</note> cannot naturally be brought any higher. Although, as indicated in **Volume Two** <note>original: *Tomo Secundo*; referring to the second volume of Praetorius’s *Syntagma Musicum*, titled *De Organographia*</note>, some players have now progressed so far that on the **bassoons** or **dulcians** (as some call them), if the instruments are very good and particularly well-handled, they can purely intone and manage 4, 5, or more **notes** <term>original: *Claves*; literally "keys," but here referring to pitches</term> higher into the upper register. Or one can use a ***Zingelcorthol*** <term>a small, high-pitched variant of the dulcian</term> and a **treble bassoon**, although these are rarely found to be purely intoned and correctly tuned.

For the deep **basses**, however, one always takes a **double bassoon** or a **double shawm**, or a **fourth-trombone** <term>original: *Quart-Posaun*; a large trombone pitched a fourth below the standard tenor trombone</term>; just as for the true, common basses, where the **clef** <image-desc>Musical staff snippet showing an F-clef on the fourth line with three ledger lines below the staff, indicating an extremely low range.</image-desc> is marked on the fourth line in this way, a **fourth-trombone** must always be used. However, the **octave-trombone** <term>original: *OctavPosaun*; a double-bass trombone pitched an octave below the tenor</term> and the very large **sub-bass** or **contrabass viols** cannot easily play from these very deep bass parts, as it seems somewhat strange and unfamiliar to them. Therefore, one must transpose these same basses to the **octave above** <note>original: *in Epidiapason*</note> when copying them; that is, the **F-clef** <note>F-clef symbol</note> is removed from its high position on the sixth or fifth line and placed on the middle (third) or even the fourth line (counting from the bottom). In this way, it becomes quite right and usable for the instrumentalist.

->As for example:<-

This last part is intended for the very deep instruments to play at the proper pitch; however, it is actually an **octave lower**.

If an ensemble has four or even five voices, and the **Tenor** or **Fifth part** <term>original: *Quintus*</term> has the **C-clef** <term>original: *ɔl:*</term> on the fourth line, it often happens that the notes **G** <note>original: *Γ-ut*; the lowest note in the medieval Guidonian scale</note> or **A** <note>original: *A-re*</note> occur a fifth above the Bass. Such fifths in the low register can sound somewhat unpleasant or "muddy" if they are not carefully moderated. In such a case, one should not sing that part with a full, loud voice, but rather sing it an **octave higher** <note>original: *in Octava superiore*</note>, or perform it with **some kind of instrument** <note>original: *Instrumento quodam musiciren*</note>, whichever is most suitable.

This must also be carefully observed when performing with instruments alone (as will be shown shortly). For those instruments that play the lower part nearest to the Bass—otherwise called the **Baritone** <term>original: *Barytonus*</term>, **Wandering Part** <term>original: *Vagans*; a part that fills out the texture</term>, **Fifth** <term>original: *Quintus*</term>, or **Sixth** <term>original: *Sextus*</term>—one must use moderation. This ensures that their overly strong tone and the loudness of the fifths do not ruin the fundamental role of the Bass. One should also avoid holding the final note longer than the Bass player—a habit some Tenor singers have. What a foul sound this produces can be better understood by the ears than seen with the eyes.

However, one can also **apply** <note>original: *adhibiren*</note> and use **trombones** <term>original: *Posaunen*</term>, **bassoons** or **dulcians** <term>original: *Fagotten oder Dolcianen*</term>, and **shawms** or **bombards** <term>original: *Pommern oder Bombarden*</term> in other common **clefs** <term>original: *Clavibus*</term> that are otherwise intended for ensembles of viols, flutes, or human voices, in the following manner:

In this regard, it should be noted that for such large, deep bass instruments as <term>Shawms</term> <note>original: *Pommern*</note>, <term>Bassoons</term> <note>original: *Fagotten*</note> or <term>Dulcians</term> <note>original: *Dolcianen*</note>, and <term>Trombones</term> <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, no <term>compositions</term> <note>original: *Cantiones*</note> are better suited...

It emphasizes the necessary transpositions—such as playing a fourth or fifth lower—to accommodate the deep range of these instruments.</summary>

<sig>X 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Dieweil (Because)</meta>

...to adapt and accommodate <note>original: *accommodiren*</note> [them], as those which are set in the **Hypodorian mode** (which is for us the **Second Mode**) and also in the **Hypoionian mode** (which is for us the **Twelfth Mode**, otherwise called the **Fifth or Sixth Tone**).

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation on six-line staves, labeled 3 and 4. Each system contains six musical segments showing pitch ranges. The staves use various C and F clefs to indicate the appropriate register for different voices.</image-desc>

->A fourth or fifth lower. <note>original: *In Quarta vel Quinta inferiore*</note><-

And both of these, as well as the first two in the **Hypoionian mode**, can be performed with 6 **krummhorns** <term>krummhorns</term> <note>original: *Krumbhörnern*; wind instruments with a curved shape and a capped reed that produces a distinctive buzzing sound</note>, a second higher <note>original: *in Secunda superiore*; meaning the music is played one whole tone higher than written</note>. The following, however, is played with krummhorns at the written pitch <note>original: *im rechten Thon*</note>; but with **bassoons**, **shawms** <term>shawm</term> <note>original: *Pombarden* or *Pommern*; a powerful, double-reed woodwind instrument, the ancestor of the modern oboe</note>, and **trombones** <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, it is played a fifth lower.

<image-desc>Two systems of musical notation on six-line staves, labeled 5 and 6. Each system contains six segments labeled 1 through 6, representing parts from soprano to bass. Between the two systems is the word "or" <note>original: *vel*</note>.</image-desc>

These preceding examples can be performed with 6 **dulcians** <term>dulcian</term> <note>original: *Dolcianen*; a one-piece predecessor to the modern bassoon</note>, 6 **shawms**, or 6 **trombones**. 

Although one could indeed transpose such keyboard-based compositions <note>original: *Clavirte Cantiones*</note> an octave lower—since on the **Double-Quint Bassoon** and the **Double-Great Bass Shawm**, one can reach the lowest pitch FF <note>the F below the bass staff</note> at a 12-foot organ pipe length; and on the **octave trombones** one can even reach EE, DD, and if necessary CC at a 16-foot pipe length. However, because at such great depths it creates a very unpleasant and disagreeable sound—not only because of the rough-sounding thirds and fourths (as discussed in the **Second Volume** <note>Referring to the *De Organographia* volume of Praetorius’s *Syntagma Musicum*</note>), but also because it is not easy to perform rapid notes in the four lowest clefs of the great bass shawms and bassoons—it is therefore better and quite convenient to transpose motets, concerts, sonatas, and canzonas set in such clefs <term>clefs</term> <note>original: *Claves Signatas*</note> a fourth or fifth lower, as indicated under the clef markings. This can likewise be achieved on very large **double bass and bass viols**, where the **bass viola da gamba** is used for the soprano part <note>original: *Discant*</note>.

<vocab>Hypodorian, Hypoionian, krummhorns, bassoons, shawms, trombones, dulcians, Double-Quint bassoon, Double-Great Bass Shawm, clef markings, bass viola da gamba</vocab>

<summary>Praetorius provides technical instructions for transposing music to suit low-register wind and string ensembles, warning that playing in extremely low registers can sound "disagreeable" and hinder fast passages.</summary>
<keywords>transposition, musical modes, krummhorn, shawm, bassoon, trombone, dulcian, viola da gamba, instrument range, Syntagma Musicum</keywords>

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on instrumental transposition and range, specifically how to adapt compositions for lower-register ensembles such as krummhorns, dulcians, shawms, and trombones.</meta>

However, since not every player can reach the high $g'$ and $a'$ <note>original: *ḡ vnd ā lamire*; referring to specific pitches in the hexachord system</note> in the alto register on the common **tenor trombone** <term>Tenor-Posaun</term>, and even less so on the standard **bassoon** <term>Chorist-Fagot</term> <note>the 'chorist' bassoon or dulcian was the standard-sized bass instrument of the family</note>; and therefore cannot reach those notes in the first voice (the Soprano <note>original: *Cantu*</note>) when it is to be played a **fourth or fifth lower** <note>original: *in Quarta oder Quinta inferiore*</note> as an alto part; one must perform that same voice either with an **alto trombone** <term>Alt-Posaun</term> or a **small dulcian** <term>Zingel Corthol</term> <note>a *Zingel Corthol* is a small soprano version of the dulcian/bassoon family</note>, which is a small **soprano bassoon** <term>Cant-Fagot</term>, or else have it sung by an alto singer <note>original: *Voce humana von ein Altisten*</note>. 

In the composition, however, care must be taken above all else that the notes in each voice do not ascend much beyond an octave. For although some instrumentalists can go up to $g'$ on standard bassoons, and to $a'$ or even higher on tenor trombones: this ability is generally not granted to everyone. Instead, they must linger <note>original: *commoriren*</note> and remain at $b\flat$ for the bassoons, and at $a$ or at most $a'$ for the trombones. Likewise, the **baritones**, or "vagrant" voices (that is, the fifth part <note>original: *Quintus*</note>), whose clef <term>Clavis Signata</term>—the F-clef—stands on the third (the middle) line, must not be set lower than $c$.

For such clef settings, the **violas da gamba** <term>Violen de Gamba</term> are also very well suited, especially when the music is transposed a fourth or fifth lower.

# V. Muted Horns. Curved Horns. Krummhorn <term>Krumbhörner</term> Choir.
<note>The krummhorn is a "capped-reed" instrument with a distinctive J-shape that produces a buzzing, nasal tone. Praetorius uses the Latin *Corna muti* and Italian *Storti* to refer to the same family.</note>

<image-desc>Musical notation for a four-part krummhorn ensemble, labeled 1 through 4 above and Soprano <note>original: *Discant*</note>, Alto, Tenor, and Bass below the staves. It illustrates the standard clefs and ranges for these instruments.</image-desc>

This must be transposed on the krummhorns in the following way:

<image-desc>A continuation of musical notation showing transposed ranges. A label "At the fourth below" <note>original: *In Quarta inferiore*</note> appears below the first two staves. A separate lower staff is labeled "Under-Bass" <note>original: *Vnter Baſs*</note>.</image-desc>

The bass can reach an octave: however, the other voices—namely the Soprano <note>original: *Cant*</note>, Alto, and Tenor—can only do so with great difficulty on the krummhorns without the keys <note>referring to the physical keys on the instrument used to extend the range beyond the basic finger holes</note>. Otherwise, they naturally have only six notes <note>original: *Claves*; here referring to the basic pitches available within the standard fingering</note>. As described more fully in the Second Volume <note>original: *Tomo Secundo*; referring to Praetorius’s *De Organographia*, which provides detailed descriptions of musical instruments</note>.

If, however, the melody <note>original: *Cantus*</note> has a flat <note>original: *B mollis*</note>—for example, in the Hypo-Ionian mode—it must be transposed to the second above <note>original: *in Secundam Superiorem*</note>, which is one tone higher, and thus performed on the krummhorns.

<image-desc>A musical score for four voices, arranged in five staves. The voices are labeled with letters: "C." (Cantus/Soprano), "A." (Altus), "T." (Tenor), and "B." (Bassus). And for such krummhorn ensembles <note>original: *Choren*</note>, the Mixolydian mode <note>original: *MyxoLydius*</note> (which for us is the Seventh Mode) transposed to the fourth—not the fifth—is best suited. Likewise, the regular Hypo-Mixolydian <note>original: *HypoMyxoLydius Regularis*</note> (the Eighth Mode) is appropriate. The transposed Hypo-Ionian mode <note>original: *HypoIonicustranspositus*</note> also adapts itself to this, though not on its own <note>original: *per se*</note>; rather, when it is transposed to the second above <note>original: *in Secundam superiorem*</note> (as seen in the previous diagram <note>original: *Schemate*</note>), it becomes equivalent to the Mixolydian transposed to the fourth.

### VI. Shawm <term>Schalmeyen</term> Choir.
<note>The shawm is a loud, double-reed woodwind instrument with a conical bore, widely used in the Renaissance and a direct ancestor of the modern oboe.</note>

Just as with the krummhorns, not all pieces are suitable for the shawms. This is for the reasons cited in the second volume <note>original: *im Andern Tomo*</note>, on page <note>original: *fol.*</note> 37, where it is also indicated which modes <note>original: *Modi*</note> are best suited for them. All the difficulty and inconvenience arises from the fact that the various sizes <note>original: *Sorten*</note> in almost all instrumental consorts <note>original: *Accorten*</note> are tuned a fifth apart from each other. This could all be easily remedied if some instruments tuned a fourth apart were available.

<summary>This page details specific transposition rules for playing different musical modes on krummhorns and introduces the technical challenges of arranging music for shawm ensembles, specifically citing the difficulties caused by instruments being tuned in fifths.</summary>
<keywords>transposition, krummhorn, shawm, Mixolydian mode, Hypo-Ionian mode, instrument consorts, tuning intervals, Renaissance woodwinds</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of shawm ensembles, moving from the general introduction of the "Shawm Choir" to the specific acoustic challenges and tuning adjustments required for smaller instruments versus the larger members of the bombard family.</meta>

<page-num>167.</page-num>

...might be: just as was mentioned at greater length in the previously described section. The higher and smaller the **shawms** or similar **instruments** <note>original: *Instrumenta*</note> are, the more strangely and further they deviate from the correct pitch. For this reason, on the **Soprano Shawm** <term>Discant-Schalmey</term>, the F is not found at its proper pitch, but rather F♯; and thus not the *fa*, but the *mi* <note>In the Guidonian hexachord system used in the Renaissance, "fa" and "mi" indicated the positions of semitones. Praetorius is noting that the instrument's natural scale produces a major interval where a minor one might be expected.</note>. Consequently, a **ficta song** <term>Cantus fictus</term> <note>original: *Cantus fictus*; referring to music that uses accidentals (sharps or flats) to move outside the standard natural scales</note> must necessarily be used with it.

<image-desc>A musical staff showing an ascending and descending scale in diamond-shaped mensural notation. It is labeled "Soprano Shawm" [Discant-Schalmey] underneath and concludes with the abbreviation "etc." [&c.]</image-desc>

The most common and best method is to leave the standard **Soprano Shawm** aside and use only the 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 5th types and varieties of the **bombards** <term>Pommern</term> <note>The *Pommer* (bombard) is the powerful, double-reed ancestor of the bassoon and oboe, characterized by its long wooden body and bell.</note> counting from the bottom (as they are noted in **Volume Two** <note>original: *Tomo Secundo*</note> on page 22) for an **ensemble** <term>Accort</term> <note>An "Accort" or consort refers to a set of instruments of the same family tuned to play together.</note>, as recorded here: in this way, the pitch is lowered by exactly a fourth.

<image-desc>A 2x2 grid of musical staves showing instrument ranges. The top left staff is labeled "Small Alto Bombard" [Klein alt Pommer]. ## VII.
### Lute Choir.

I call it a **Lute Choir** <term>Lauten-Chor</term> when one arranges together **harpsichords** <term>Clavicymbel</term> or **spinets** <term>Spinetten</term>, **quilled instruments** <note>original: *Instrumenta pennata*; refers to keyboard instruments where the strings are plucked by quills</note> (otherwise commonly called "instruments"), **theorbos** <term>Theorben</term>, **lutes** <term>Lauten</term>, **bandoras** <term>Bandoren</term>, **orphoreons** <term>Orpheoreon</term> <note>A scalloped-edged plucked instrument similar to a cittern</note>, **citterns** <term>Cithern</term>, a large **bass lyre** <term>Bass-Lyra</term> <note>likely the *lirone*, a large bowed chordal instrument</note>, or as many of such and similar **fundamental instruments** <term>Fundament-Instrumenten</term> <note>instruments that provide the harmonic foundation or basso continuo</note> as one can manage to bring together. Along with these, a **bass violin** <term>Bass-Geig</term> is not ill-suited for the sake of the foundation. This choir was called an **English Consort** <term>Englisch Consort</term> above on folio 5; because of the striking of so many strings, it creates a truly beautiful **effect** <note>original: *effectum*</note> and gives off a magnificent, lovely **resonance** <note>original: *Resonantz*</note>. 

In this manner, I once had the magnificent and exceptionally beautiful **motet** <note>original: *Motetam*</note> of the excellent composer **Giaches de Wert** <note>original: *Iaches de V Werth*</note>, "*Jesus went forth*" <note>original: *Egressus Iesus*</note> for seven voices <note>original: *à 7. vocum*</note>, performed with 2 theorbos, 3 lutes, 2 citterns, 4 harpsichords and spinets, 7 **violas da gamba** <term>Violen de Gamba</term>, 2 **transverse flutes** <term>Quer-Flöitten</term>, 2 boys [sopranos], 1 altist, and one large **viol** <term>Violen</term> (bass violin) without organ or **regal** <term>Regal</term> <note>a small portable pipe organ with reed stops</note>. This gave off such an exceptionally magnificent, glorious resonance that, due to the sound of so many strings, it almost shimmered <note>original: *geknittert*; literally "crackled" or "rustled," describing the dense, sparkling acoustic texture of a large plucked ensemble</note> throughout the entire church.

Regarding the **Third Choir**, this should also be noted: it is quite pleasant to hear if one occasionally lets the **Soprano** <term>Cant</term> and **Tenor** sing alone together, while the **Alto** and **Bass** are left out or played by instruments. Because the Soprano and Tenor mostly proceed in intervals of sixths with one another, this creates a good effect. As may be heard at greater length in the **preface** <note>original: *præfation*</note> of the *Polyhymnia Panegyrica* regarding the **General Bass** <term>General-Bass</term> <note>basso continuo</note>.
