### The VIII. Chapter
# ADMONITION
## and Reminder,
#### In what manner, in my Polyhymnia as well as other Works, the Latin and German Sacred Church-Songs and Concert-Canticles can be arranged and set up.

Although it is indeed impossible to record and describe every single one of the various styles of composers at this time: I have nonetheless wished to notify and explain several here, especially those which I have used in my current new (though modest) Works, namely in the *Polyhymnia*.

Among others, however, there are primarily twelve types included therein.

### The I. Type.

<margin>The First Type.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' with ornate scrollwork and floral patterns.</image-desc> Of the First Type are the Latin and German Songs <term>Cantiones</term> in *Polyhymnia* titled: *Trumpets and Timpani-players* <note>original: "Tubicinia & Tympanistria"</note>. In these, one can, at one's pleasure <term>ad Placitum</term>, employ <note>original: "adhibiren"</note> and use the trumpeters and army-kettledrums in those churches where it can be justified <note>i.e., where such loud, secular-associated instruments were permitted in a sacred setting</note>.

If, however, one does not wish, cannot, or is not permitted to use the trumpeters or army-kettledrummers <term>Heer-Paucker</term>, these Songs can nonetheless be performed quite well in city churches without the help and contribution of the trumpeters; and their sonatas <term>Sonaden</term> <note>instrumental flourishes or interludes</note>, and whatever was composed for them therein, can then be entirely omitted. But where other instrumentalists are available, one can have the same performed with violins <term>Geygen</term>, cornetts <term>Zincken</term>, and trombones <term>Posaunen</term>.

The German songs were, for the most part, intended by me at the beginning so that the people and the entire congregation in the church [might] simultaneously with...

<summary>Michael Praetorius introduces the eighth chapter of his treatise, outlining twelve ways to arrange his sacred compositions, focusing first on the optional use of trumpets and timpani in the Polyhymnia collection.</summary>
<keywords>Polyhymnia, arrangement, trumpets, timpani, orchestration, sacred songs, city churches, cornetts, trombones, congregational singing</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "First Type" of concerted songs (from page 169), which include parts for trumpets and kettledrums. The catchword from the previous page, "Sinte-", connects to "sintemahl" (meaning "since" or "because") in the second paragraph of this page.</meta>

...could sing along with them. However, since this might seem all too plain and simple to some, I have changed them and, in various places, wished to include more *variations* <note>original: "Variationes"</note> with alternating choirs, and for the sake of the trumpeters, several *repetitions* and *digressions* <note>original: "Repetitiones vnd Digressiones"</note>.

And this is to be noted here: since trumpeters are usually accustomed to hurrying forward too much (since the trumpets *require* and demand a strong breath, which cannot be *continued* so slowly), one should hurry the *beat* <term>Tact</term> <note>The "tactus" or steady hand motion used to keep time</note> a little more at the places where the trumpeters join in. Otherwise, they always finish their *sonatas* <note>instrumental flourishes or interludes</note> too early. Afterward, however, the *beat* can be slowed down <note>original: "protrahiret"</note> again until the trumpeters begin once more.

These *concerted* songs are to be arranged in such a way that five, six, or seven trumpeters, with or without a kettledrummer <term>Heerpaucker</term>, are placed in a separate location near the church. This is so that when they stand in the church, the powerful blast and reverberation of the trumpets does not drown out or deafen the entire *music*. Instead, one part can be heard clearly alongside the other. Then the chapel master <term>Capellenmeister</term>, or someone else who is certain of the *beat*, must have the *figured bass* <term>General-Baß</term> before him and lead the *beat* so that the choir of musicians <note>original: "Chorus Musicorum"</note> in the church on one side, and the trumpeters on the other side—especially the one who leads the *Fifth* <term>Quint</term>, or as they mostly call it, the *Principal*—can see him and direct themselves accordingly.

I have also included with each such *concerted* song the parts as the trumpeters may blow them (though subject to the improvement of any sensible person).

And since in all things, *variety* <note>original: "Varietas"</note> is charming and pleasant, I have also introduced several *variations* into the German songs. In these, the trumpeters and kettledrummers do not always join in together with the full choir. Instead, sometimes the chorale <note>the main hymn melody</note> is led to the whole choir of musicians by the *clarino* <note>the highest trumpet register</note> alone; sometimes a duo <note>original: "Duum"</note> is made with two *clarinos*, or one *clarino* and a *principal*; or even a trio <note>original: "Trium"</note> with two *clarinos* and a *fifth* (that is, the *principal*).

And among the trumpeters, there must above all be at least two present—one who leads the *fifth* and the other who blows the second *clarino*—who understand music and can perform it from the notes just as I have composed and prescribed it.

<summary>This page provides practical instructions for performing concerted music with trumpets, emphasizing the need to adjust the tempo to the physical demands of the instruments and recommending spatial separation to balance the volume. Praetorius also specifies the need for musically literate trumpeters capable of playing specific parts like the clarino and principal.</summary>
<keywords>trumpets, kettledrums, tempo adjustment, spatial arrangement, clarino, principal, figured bass, conducting, music literacy</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for coordinating trumpeters and kettledrummers with a church choir. The previous page ended with the chapel master needing to lead the beat so both groups stay in sync despite being in different locations.</meta>

Since the first <term>Clarin</term> <note>Clarino: the highest trumpet part, capable of playing complex melodies</note> is directed solely at the <term>Choral</term> <note>the main hymn melody or plainchant</note> and can be easily played by anyone familiar with that <term>Choral</term>: It is also necessary that the one leading the <term>Alter-Bass</term> <note>a middle-range trumpet part acting as an alto or second tenor</note> should study his part from the provided notes so that the <term>consonances</term> and <term>chords</term> in the three <term>principal voices</term> meet together correctly. The others—such as the <term>Volgan</term>, <term>Grob</term>, <term>Fladdergrob</term> <note>German names for the lower, rhythmic trumpet parts: 'Volgan' is the third part, 'Grob' the fourth, and 'Fladdergrob' the lowest rhythmic drone</note>, and the <term>Kettledrummer</term> <term>Heerpaucker</term>—follow only the person leading the <term>Principal</term> part. They can find their <term>parts</term> on their own, so they have no need for written notes at all.

However, when trumpeters experienced in <term>Music</term> <note>meaning those who can read formal notation rather than just playing by ear</note> are not available, I have thought of an easier method: they may simply play their usual <term>Sonadas</term> <note>instrumental flourishes or fanfares</note> with proportions and <term>triples</term> <note>triple-time sections</note>, or even without them, according to how the <term>concerted song</term> is composed, playing for a full, half, or quarter <term>Post</term> <note>a specific unit of musical time or measures</note>, and thus join in the harmony. For guidance, I have included several such common <term>Sonadas</term> here.

At this point, I must explain several <term>terms</term> <note>original: "Terminos"</note> as they are commonly used among trumpeters.

An <term>Intrada</term> is like a <term>preamble</term> <note>original: "præambulum"</note> and a <term>finale</term> <note>original: "final"</note>; they use it at the beginning before they start their <term>Sonadas</term> when the signal is blown for the table <note>announcing a banquet</note>, and they also use it for the pause and the <term>finale</term>.

A <term>Sonada</term> or <term>Sonata</term> is what they use for blowing at the table, and also for dancing. I call the opening dance a <term>Sonada</term> without the <term>triple</term>; the following dance, however, is a <term>Sonata</term> with the <term>triple</term>. And because so much depends on the one who leads the <term>Quint</term> or <term>Principal</term> <note>the core rhythmic trumpet part that anchors the ensemble</note>, the <term>Clarino</term> player, the <term>Kettledrummer</term> <term>Heerpaucker</term>, and all the others must follow his lead.

->A <term>Post</term> contains 16 <term>measures</term> <term>Tact</term>:<-
->A half <term>Post</term> contains 8 <term>measures</term>:<-
->A quarter <term>Post</term> contains 4 <term>measures</term>:<-

Although some wish to calculate a <term>Post</term> as 4 <term>measures</term> and a quarter <term>Post</term> as 2 <term>measures</term>, this cannot be allowed to pass.

The <term>Principal</term>, <term>Quinta</term>, or as some call it, the <term>Sonata</term>, is the true <term>Tenor</term> <note>the stabilizing middle voice</note> that rules and leads the entire choir of trumpeters and kettledrummers.

The <term>Clarino</term> <note>original: "Clarien"</note> is the <term>Discant</term> <note>the highest voice</note>; it leads the <term>melody</term> or <term>Choral</term> and <term>embellishes</term> <note>original: "exorniret"</note> it with rising and falling <term>diminutions</term> <note>original: "Diminutionibus"; fast decorative notes that break down a long note into many short ones</note> or <term>coloraturas</term> <note>vocal-like ornamentation</note>, according to the player's pleasure and the very best of his skill and ability.

<summary>This page explains the hierarchy and roles within a trumpet and kettledrum ensemble, defining technical terms like 'Post' and 'Sonada' and emphasizing the importance of the Principal player in leading the group.</summary>
<keywords>trumpet ensemble, Clarino, Principal, Kettledrums, musical terminology, Sonada, Intrada, musical measures, ornamentation, liturgical music</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for trumpet ensembles and introduces a second style of liturgical performance involving spatially separated boy singers. It picks up from the previous page's discussion of standard trumpet flourishes.</meta>

The <term>Alter-Bass</term> <note>literally "Old Bass," a middle-range trumpet part</note> acts like an alto voice, which consistently harmonizes in thirds, fourths, and—less frequently—fifths with the <term>Sonada</term> <note>the melodic fanfare</note> or <term>Quinta</term> <note>the second-highest trumpet part</note> as it proceeds.

The <term>Volgan</term> <note>the third trumpet part</note> holds the <term>fifth</term> above the bass or <term>Grob</term> and always remains on a single tone, specifically G.

The <term>Grob</term> <note>the "Coarse" or fourth trumpet part</note> is the true bass and foundation; it also remains on a single tone, specifically C, at the <term>four-foot pitch</term>.

The <term>FladderGrob</term> <note>the "Fluttering-Coarse," the lowest rhythmic trumpet part</note> holds yet another <term>octave</term> below the bass or <term>Grob</term>, which is the C at the <term>eight-foot pitch</term>.

And because it is very common for the <term>Quinta</term> and the <term>Clarien</term> <note>the highest, melodic trumpet part, also called the Clarino</note>—especially when they lead a <term>Choral</term> <note>a hymn tune or plainchant</note> or other styles in their fanfares—to mostly move in octaves with one another, which appears strange to an experienced <term>Musico</term> <note>a trained, literate musician</note>: I have therefore chosen to compose and print the musical notation for the top three voices—the <term>Clarin</term>, <term>Quinta</term>, and <term>Alter-Bass</term>—for several of these <term>concerted songs</term>. This is so that those <term>Trommeter</term> <note>trumpeters</note> who understand <term>Music</term> <note>formal notation rather than playing by ear</note> may practice them and thus join in with the musical choir with better <term>gratia</term> <note>grace or elegance</note>. Nonetheless, it is difficult to compose these parts entirely without <term>dissonances</term> and other forbidden <term>intervals</term>.

And songs of this first type can be found in my first and second <term>Polyhymnia Heroica</term> <note>meaning "Heroic Polyhymnia," a collection of works for trumpets and drums; the original Latin subtitle translates to "or the art of Trumpeting and Kettledrumming"</note> and in the first and second appendices to the <term>Polyhymnia Panegyrica</term> <note>meaning "Festive Polyhymnia," collections of large-scale choral and instrumental works</note>.

---

### The Second Type.

<margin>
The Second
Type.
</margin>

In this second type, four boys must be placed in four separate locations in the church, opposite one another or wherever it is most suitable. The first boy, stationed by the organ, begins entirely alone; immediately after him, the second; then the third; and finally the fourth (who must be placed near the <term>plenum chorum Musicum</term> <note>the full musical choir</note>, specifically the <term>chorum pro Capella</term> <note>the core chapel choir or support ensemble</note>). Each should sing what is found in his part purely, freshly, clearly, and audibly, almost as if "speaking" the notes. To this, the entire <term>Chorus Vocalis & Instrumentalis</term> <note>the vocal and instrumental choir</note> and the organ then respond—a technique which the Italians, as [noted]

<meta>This page continues the instructions for performance practice, specifically focusing on the arrangement and accompaniment of "concerted" boy singers within a large-scale polychoral work. It transitions from the previous discussion of trumpet ensembles to the practicalities of spatial separation and "foundation" instruments.</meta>

mentioned above: <term>full ensemble concertos</term> <note>original: "concerti Ripieni"</note>, which is called <term>Choir</term> <note>original: "Chorus"</note> or <term>Full Ensemble</term> <note>original: "Concentus plenus"</note>, the full-voiced <term>Choir</term>; and is designated by others with the word <term>Everyone</term> <note>original: "Omnes"</note> or <term>All</term> <note>original: "Tutti"</note>.

However, because it might sound somewhat bare and loud if the boys were to sing and <term>intonate</term> <note>provide the opening notes</note> all alone while positioned far apart from one another, without <term>Foundations</term> <note>original: "Fundamenta," meaning harmonic accompaniment</note>: (although, if the boys have fine, pure voices, it is also not unpleasant to hear). Therefore, it is very good that—wherever they can be obtained—a <term>Regal</term> <note>a small, portable reed organ</note>, <term>Positive Organ</term>, <term>Harpsichord</term>, <term>Theorbo</term>, or Lutes be assigned to each boy. This is so that when the boy sings, the instrument is played along with him; and when he is silent, the playing is likewise stopped at that same location. Just as a separate <term>Bass</term> can be extracted from the <term>General-Bass</term> <note>Basso Continuo</note> or from the <term>String Ensemble</term> <note>original: "Capella Fidicinum"</note> to match what each boy sings, and can be used there on the <term>Foundation Instrument</term>. Indeed, I have included this as an example in the pieces *Who the shepherds* <note>original: "Quem pastores"</note> and *Where the King of Glory is* <note>original: "Vbi rex est gloriarum"</note>, which is to be found there in the <term>Thirteenth</term> and <term>Fourteenth</term> <note>original: "Decimotertio" and "Decimoquarto"</note>, that is, in the 13th and 14th voices or parts.

By following this, anyone can extract such <term>Basses</term> for the other parts in a similar fashion and use them in the arrangement.

It is necessary, however, that the <term>Organist</term>—when one boy is assigned to him—pulls the quietest and softest <term>Gedeckt register</term> <note>a "stopped" pipe organ stop with a sweet, muted tone</note> at <term>8-foot pitch</term>, in the <term>Ruck-positive</term> <note>the organ division located behind the player's back</note> or in the <term>Upper Work</term> <note>the highest division of the organ pipes</note>. He should <term>intonate</term> with the same boy at a very slow <term>Beat</term> <note>original: "Tact"</note>. But when the <term>Full Choir</term> <note>original: "Plenus Chorus"</note> enters, he can use a sharper <term>Register</term> in the <term>Main Organ</term> or in the <term>Positive</term>, yet still not the full <term>Organ</term> <note>original: "Werck"</note> (as some would wish, so that it does not outshout and drown out the other <term>Choirs</term> of <term>Vocalists</term> and <term>Instrumentalists</term>).

For the second boy, one can have a <term>Regal</term>; for the third, the loud <term>Choir</term> and <term>Harpsichord</term> (if <term>Organists</term> and such <term>Instruments</term> are available); and for the fourth, a <term>Positive</term> or <term>Regal</term> or <term>Harpsichord</term>. I am also accustomed to assigning an <term>Instrumentalist</term> to each boy: for example, to the 1st boy, someone with a <term>Soprano Violin</term> <note>original: "Discant-Geigen"</note>; to the 2nd, a <term>Cornett player</term>; to the 3rd, also a <term>Soprano Violin</term>; to the 4th, a <term>Recorder</term> or <term>Transverse Flute</term>, or even a fine <term>Little Flute</term>. This flute, especially in the <term>Full Choir</term> <note>original: "Pleno Choro"</note>, does not sound badly when used by a good <term>Master</term>. These instruments, however, should not join in until the word <term>Full Ensemble</term> <note>original: "Ripieni"</note>, <term>Everyone</term>, <term>All</term>, or <term>Full Choir or ensemble</term> is marked there. But where <term>Instrumentalists</term> are not available in abundance, it is better that they stay together; and then one can place them all together in a special place—

page 174

be placed; the <term>vocalists</term> <note>singers</note> should also be positioned separately (just as was arranged by me a few years ago at Naumburg). In such a case, the parts for the <term>full choir</term> <note>original: "pleno Choro"</note> must be copied out one or more times specifically for the instrumentalists.

However, since there are not enough organists or <term>foundation instruments</term> <note>instruments that provide the harmonic base, such as the organ or harpsichord</note>—namely <term>Regals</term> <note>a small, portable reed organ</note>, <term>Positives</term> <note>small pipe organs</note>, or <term>Harpsichords</term> <note>original: "Clavi Cympel"</note>—available in all places, I have therefore added a separate <term>String Ensemble</term> <note>original: "Capellam Fidicinium"</note>. This must be played quite briskly and sharply by four <term>string players</term> <note>original: "Geigern"</note> (who can perhaps be placed with the second or third boy, opposite the organ). This is because this ensemble, just like the organ, serves as the foundation for all four boys and continues throughout without pauses. Although, in some songs that are somewhat long, such as *How Brightly Shines* <note>original: "Wie schön leuchtet," the famous Lutheran hymn</note> and others, I occasionally let this <term>String Ensemble</term> pause; during those times, the <term>Lute Ensemble</term> or the organ with a soft stop must be used.

Alternatively, one can use the organ for two [groups], such as for the first and third boys, who must be placed closest to it; the <term>string players</term> <note>original: "Violisten"</note> are then used for the other two, namely the second and fourth boys. In that case, one must note and underline in the <term>String Ensemble</term> parts what those boys standing near them are singing. Whatever is left over—what the other two boys are singing with the organist—the organist can either transcribe from the string parts for himself or note and mark it in the <term>General Bass</term> <note>the basso continuo or through-bass part</note>. This is so that the variation and alternation may be better observed in all parts.

If it should happen that there is an organist for each boy—and thus four organists, which can certainly occur in very few places—one can leave out the <term>String Ensemble</term> in the first and third <term>verse for the boys</term> <note>original: "versu puerorum"</note>, since the organists alone can sufficiently provide the foundation and middle parts. Then, for the sake of variety and so that the viols do not always play at the same time, one can let them join in alone for the second and fourth verses, and then have them all play together when the <term>entire full choir</term> <note>original: "ganze volle Chorus pro Capella"</note> joins in.

It is also quite charming and makes the words of the text easier to understand if, at the beginning, the first verse is sung by the boys entirely alone, accompanied by a soft, lovely organ stop, so that the violins and lutes remain completely silent. However, if four good boys are not available, one can use two boys and two <term>Tenors</term>, or three boys and one <term>Tenor</term>, or even four <term>Tenors</term>. Alternatively, in place of the second and fourth boys, one could use two <term>Cornetts</term> <note>original: "Cornet," a wooden wind instrument with a trumpet-like mouthpiece</note>, two <term>Violins</term>, or one <term>Cornett</term> and one <term>Violin</term>, according to what is available.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the performance of polychoral works, specifically focusing on how to substitute voices with instruments and how to utilize organ stops like the cymbal-bells. The catchword "Die Erste" from the previous page likely refers to the "First Kind" of arrangement, leading into the "Second Kind" and eventually "The Third Kind" introduced here.</meta>

can have. For even if one or two **sopranos** <note>original: "Discant"</note> are heard not through the **living or human voice** <note>original: "viva oder humana voce"</note>, but rather through **instrumental wind** <note>original: "Instrumentali flatu"</note> without text, one can still easily guess and follow the words and the previously sung text of the first and third sopranos from the melody in the second and fourth sopranos, which responds to the first like an echo. In some organs, one has the **cymbal-bells** <note>original: "Cymbel-Glöcklein," a high-pitched organ stop featuring small bells</note>; when these are drawn for the **full choir**, they are heard as quite lovely, beautiful, and charming. They may also be used occasionally when the boys sing alone, provided they do not ring too strongly. Every **musician** and **organist** can see the opportunity for this in his own church and can think further and better upon the matter.

Since some of this **second kind of concert** also have many verses, one can use or leave out as many of them as one wishes, according to the needs of each church and the opportunity provided by the preacher.

The *He whom the shepherds adored* <note>original: "Quem Pastores"</note> and *Rejoice, dear Christians* etc. can be performed one tone higher; and in the **full choir** <note>original: "pleno Choro"</note>, especially during the "God's little Son is born" section, one or two **trumpets** can join in. However, because it is a fourth lower than the **trumpet's pitch**, one must attach two and a half **crooks** <note>original: "Krummbügel," detachable pieces of tubing used to change the instrument's pitch</note> from a **trombone** onto the trumpets; this provides the correct pitch in **G** <note>original: "G sol re ut," a pitch designation in the hexachord system</note>.

And **songs** <note>original: "Cantiones"</note> of this **second kind** will be found in *Polyhymnia IV*, **for the harmony of many boys' voices** <note>original: "telepaidophōnō seu Quatuor vel trium puerorum Concentu"</note>; some will also be found in *Polyhymnia III: Panegyrica*.

## The III. Kind.

<margin>The Third Kind.</margin>
The concert-songs and psalms adapted to this **Third Kind** are mostly directed toward a few **solo voices** <note>original: "Voces Concertatas"</note> according to the current Italian manner. Here, one, two, or more vocalists—who must not only be **secure** and certain, having a beautiful, natural, and pure voice, but also know how to moderate the same delicately and lovely, and to sing with a **pleasing grace** <note>original: "gratia mente"</note>—are heard singing with the organ or **regal** <note>a small, portable reed organ</note>. This is currently common in Italy and has been further described by Ludovico Viadana and many other Italian musicians in the sixth chapter on **thoroughbass** <note>original: "General-Baß," the system of harmonic shorthand for keyboard players</note> and in other places. However, because all sorts of **instruments** and **instrumental ensembles** <note>original: "Capellen"</note> can be arranged for such solo voices, and thus many **variations** can be established and used, I have observed six different manners in this kind and wished to make use of them.

<summary>This page describes performance practices for multi-part vocal music, including substituting voices with instruments, using organ bell stops, and the use of brass crooks to adjust trumpet pitch. It also introduces the "Third Kind" of concertato style influenced by Italian composers like Viadana, focusing on soloists with basso continuo.</summary>
<keywords>concertato style, Discant, organ stops, cymbal-bells, trumpet pitch, crooks, Polyhymnia, Ludovico Viadana, thoroughbass, solo voices, Italian manner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for performing polychoral and concertato works. The previous page discussed substituting voices with instruments and using specific organ stops like cymbal-bells. Here, the author begins a numbered list of "Styles" (Manieren) for arranging performers.</meta>

## The First Style.

The **First Style** is directed toward the current manner of *Lodovico Viadana, Giovanni Damasceni, Antonio Cifra, Giacomo Finetti, Serafino Patta*, and countless other **Italian Musicians** <note>original: "Italorum Musicorum"</note>. In this style, one arranges two, three, or four **concertato voices** <note>solo voices meant to be heard distinctly</note>—either placed together or separated from one another so that one voice may be heard clearly and distinctly from the others—alongside the organ or a <term>Regal<gloss>a small, portable reed organ with a buzzy tone</gloss></term>. The organist must strike and play along from the **thoroughbass** <note>original: "General-Bass"</note>. 

In this First Style, one can always, **for the sake of variety** <note>original: "pro variatione"</note>, use **tenors** in place of **sopranos** <note>original: "Discantisten"</note> if desired. In such a case, the bass—provided one is present—must either be left out or performed **at the lower octave, or at the diapason below** <note>original: "in Octava inferiore, seu sub Diapason"</note>, although this is not necessary in all instances. Conversely, one can use **sopranos** in place of **tenors**, provided no **alto** is present. In **three-part pieces** <note>original: "Triciniis"</note>, however, the bass can sometimes be performed not **with a human voice** <note>original: "humana voce"</note>, but perhaps with a **bass violin, trombone, or bassoon**. Sometimes the bass can even be left out entirely, letting only the two upper voices sing to the organ or **regal**. One can also reverse it and have the two upper voices played by two **cornetts** <note>original: "Cornet," a wooden wind instrument with a mouthpiece like a trumpet</note>, two **violins**, or two **flutes**, while the bass is sung **with a human voice**, according to whatever one or the other desires.

---

## The Second Style.

The **Second Style** is almost identical to the First Style, and should be used as was just mentioned regarding the **three-part pieces**. However, I find this particularly pleasing: that at the beginning (as I have also noted in the music), the boys sing alone, and a **quart-trombone** <note>a large trombone pitched a fourth lower than the standard size</note> or **bassoon** is used for the bass **at the lower octave**. When the **triple-meter section** <note>original: "proportion," referring to a change in time signature</note> begins, two **cornetts**, two **violins**, or two **flutes**—or perhaps one **cornett** and one **violin**, or one **viol** and one **flute**, or however else one wishes to mix them—should join in together with the boys. When the **triple-meter section** comes to an end, the instruments should remain silent until the next **triple-meter section**, alternating in this way through to the **final**.

In my opinion, an even better **grace** <note>original: "gratia"</note> and charm could be given to this style if the **triple-meter sections** were [performed] fully <note>the catchword "völlig" (fully/completely) indicates the sentence continues on the next page</note>

<summary>This page outlines two specific "styles" of musical performance: the first focuses on the Italian concertato method using small groups of soloists with basso continuo, while the second emphasizes alternating between vocal groups and instrumental ensembles during sections of triple meter.</summary>
<keywords>performance practice, Lodovico Viadana, concertato, basso continuo, instrumentation, regal, cornett, trombone, three-part pieces, triple meter</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of performance styles for polyphonic and concertato music. The previous page ended while describing how voices could be substituted or omitted, particularly in three-part pieces where the bass might be played by an instrument rather than sung.</meta>

...were fully set, and thus the entire **choir** <note>original: "Chorus"</note> would join in there: which would then almost agree with the 6th and 9th manner.

---

## The Third Style.

The **Third Style** is also similar to the First: except only that I have there **diminished** <note>added fast, decorative notes to a simple melody</note> the **soprano** <note>original: "Discants"</note> in the **Italian manner**, and—as some call it—**colored** and **broken** it. However, I have simultaneously included the **plain notes** <note>original: "schlechten Noten"</note> underneath and alongside them, so that those to whom this manner of singing is still currently unknown may nonetheless be able to **attain** <note>original: "assequirn"</note> and grasp these songs.

Alongside this, however, an **instrumental bass** <note>original: "Bassus Instrumentalis"</note> is set for each **vocal part** <note>original: "Cantu-Vocali"</note>. Thus, two basses are set for the two sopranos; these basses **fuge** into one another <note>interweave their melodies in imitation</note> in much the same way as the two soprano parts. One bass must then be performed with a **large trombone** <note>original: "Quart-Posaun"</note> or **bass violin**, and the other with a **bassoon** or a **mute shawm** <term>Pommer<gloss>a powerful woodwind instrument of the shawm family; the "mute" or "still" version was designed to be quieter</gloss></term>. Thus, they must be played with different instruments so that one may be more clearly distinguished and heard from the other.

And if one wishes, the voices—not only in this style but in all following styles of this third type—can be arranged quite conveniently **by choirs** <note>original: "per Choros"</note> and thus separated from one another, provided that a bass always remains and is kept with its assigned **vocal part** <note>original: "Cantu"</note>. Although, in the absence of instruments, one can certainly leave the basses out and have the sopranos sing alone to the **organ**, **positive organ**, **regal**, or other **foundation instruments** <note>original: "Fundament-Instrumenten"; the instruments providing the harmonic base, such as harpsichord or lute</note>.

For variety, one can also perform these two soprano parts solely with instruments—such as two **violins** and two **cornetts**, or two **recorders**, or one **cornett** and one **violin**—and leave out the **human voices** <note>original: "humanas voces"</note>. Or, use the **human voices** the first time; the **instruments** the second time; and use **both together** the third time. I have observed this style when practicing **madrigals** and other German secular songs for four, five, and six voices, and it pleased some quite well. Thus, **for the first time** <note>original: "pro prima vice"</note>, one has five or six singers; **the second time** <note>original: "Secunda vice"</note>, five instrumentalists with stringed or wind instruments; and **the third time** <note>original: "Tertia vice"</note>, let them all join in and perform together.

<summary>This page describes the "Third Style" of performance, characterized by the use of Italian-style ornamentation (diminutions) and the pairing of specific instrumental basses with vocal parts. It outlines flexible performance options involving choirs, various instruments like the trombone and shawm, and the alternation between voices and instruments.</summary>

<keywords>Italian style, diminutions, ornamentation, orchestration, trombone, bassoon, Pommer, madrigals, performance practice, basso continuo, polychoral</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s systematic explanation of different performance "manners" or styles for sacred music, specifically focusing on the use of instrumental ensembles to support vocalists and the organ.</meta>

## The Fourth Style.

The **Fourth Style** is similar to the First; except only that I have placed there, in stead of the basses, a **string ensemble** <note>original: *Capellam Fidicinium*; from the Latin *fides*, referring to stringed instruments</note>, which, alongside the organ or **regal** <note>a small, portable pipe organ that uses reed pipes to produce a buzzing sound</note>, helps to fill out and enrich the **harmony** in the middle voices even more; as shall be discussed further in Chapter 3 of this Third Part.

And this **ensemble** <note>original: *Capella*</note> is particularly good for inexperienced organists who cannot yet **perform** <note>original: *præstiren*</note> their part from a **figured bass** <note>original: *General-Bass*; a system of shorthand notation used by keyboard players to improvise accompaniment</note> right away: because the middle parts in this ensemble are found fully written out. And in this **string ensemble**, it is sufficient if these four voices are set cleanly and correctly on their own, even if, when **checked against** <note>original: *revidiren*</note> the other **vocal parts**, they might seem somewhat strange to some. And I could indeed have set these four voices for this string ensemble—and elsewhere—with very little effort so that they would correspond with the associated vocal and **soloist parts** <note>original: *Concertat-Stimmen*</note> in **unisons** or **octaves**. But because I intended for the **hymn melody** <note>original: *Choral*</note> to also be heard and noticed in the **viols** and other **instruments**, I have chosen to set it in this manner: not doubting—since it is very common among the most excellent **Italian musicians** <note>original: *Musicis Italis*</note> of the present time—that sensible and sincere **musicians** will be **content** <note>original: *contentiren*</note> with it, and will not be reluctant to **observe** the **reasons** <note>original: *rationes*</note> I have cited in the **third volume** <note>original: *Tertio Tomo*</note> regarding this.

However, it must be particularly noted here that in small churches, **chapels**, and rooms, when only one, two, or a few **vocal voices** are sung alone, if a regal or other **foundation instrument** <note>original: *Fundament-Instrument*; an instrument like an organ or lute that provides the harmonic basis for a piece</note> is present, the string ensemble should **make music** <note>original: *Musiciren*</note> very softly and quietly, or else be left out entirely: otherwise, one cannot properly hear the **human voices** <note>original: *Voces humanas*</note> **because of the sound of the instruments** <note>original: *propter sonum Instrumentorum*</note>. In large churches, however, where one can **separate** <note>original: *separiren*</note> this string ensemble somewhat further from the **voices** and place them apart, they must not be omitted: but rather, they are highly necessary to use **for a fuller harmony** <note>original: *propter pleniorem harmoniam*</note>.

---

## The Fifth Style.

The **Fifth Style** is similar to the Fourth, except that in addition to the string ensemble, there is a **choir for the ensemble** <note>original: *Chorus pro Capella*</note>, which occasionally in the mid-

<meta>catchword: dle /</meta>

<summary>Michael Praetorius describes the 'Fourth Style' of musical arrangement, emphasizing the use of a string ensemble (Capella Fidicinium) to support vocalists and assist organists who struggle with figured bass. He also provides practical advice on balancing instrument volume with human voices depending on the size of the performance space.</summary>

<keywords>Capella Fidicinium, string ensemble, figured bass, harmony, musical performance, church acoustics, Michael Praetorius, musical terminology, vocal music, instrumental accompaniment</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s instructions on the Fourth and Fifth styles of performance, specifically regarding the spatial arrangement of musicians. It then introduces the Sixth Style, which emphasizes the separation of instrumental and vocal groups to ensure the clarity of the text.</meta>

ten / sometimes at the end / entering together with **voices** and **instruments** <note>original: *Vocibus vnnd Inſtrumentis*</note> / it has been brought to that.

However, in these Fifth and Fourth Styles, this is primarily to be observed: that one should separate the boys and other **singers** <note>original: *Concentores*; those who sing the concerted or solo parts</note> (who lead the **soloist** and **vocal** parts) from one another, according to how they are divided into **choirs**. If possible, one should assign a **foundational instrument** <note>original: *Fundament-Inſtrument*; such as an organ, harpsichord, or lute that provides the bass and harmony</note> to each boy or **choir**; but where such are not available, it is all the same. The **string ensemble** <note>original: *Capellam Fidiciniam*</note>, however, must be placed off to the side in such a location that it can come to the aid of all the boys or **choirs**. Then (as I have occasionally marked between the **bar lines** <note>original: *Virgulis*</note> and strokes in the **figured bass** <note>original: *General-Baſs*</note>, part fifteen), one can change and vary **at will** <note>original: *pro libitu*</note> with all sorts of other stringed or wind **ornamental instruments** (as was also indicated in the aforementioned Chapter 3). Sometimes, in certain verses, one can leave these out entirely, and the **soloist parts** sing alone with the **foundational instrument**; but in the following verse, the **ornamental instruments** in the **string ensemble** can be allowed to join in again immediately.

---

## The VI. Style.

The Sixth Style is where, in addition to the **soloist parts**, there is not just one **string ensemble** which can play through the whole song like a **figured bass**, but rather two separate **instrumental choirs**. Each of these leads the middle parts and the entire **harmony** <note>original: *Concentum*</note> for the **soloist parts** in its own place and **choir**. In these **harmonies** and songs, one places the first **soprano** and **tenor** (or whatever the **soloist parts** may be) by the organ; then the second **soprano** and **tenor** opposite them by a **regal** <note>a small, portable organ</note>. The **instrumental choirs**, however, are each placed to the side of their respective **vocal choir** in a separate location. This is done so that the vocalists can be heard more properly before the instrumentalists, and each voice can be perceived and heard clearly and distinctly on its own. Otherwise, if they all stood near and atop one another, the character of the **soloist parts** and the words of the text could not be so well observed and understood over the instrumentalists.

<summary>Praetorius explains the practical arrangement of "choirs" of singers and instruments, emphasizing that soloists and their instrumental support should be physically separated in the church to ensure the audience can hear the sacred text clearly.</summary>
<keywords>performance practice, choir placement, string ensemble, soloist parts, figured bass, foundational instruments, musical clarity, Baroque acoustics</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Sixth Style of performance, focusing on the physical placement of musicians to maximize acoustic clarity and the various instrumental combinations that can be used to support vocal choirs.</meta>

Therefore, in this and similar styles, the arrangement can be set up almost crosswise <note>original: *Creutzweis*</note> for the sake of variety <note>original: *pro variatione*</note>. This is done so that the two **vocal choirs** <note>original: *Choros Vocales*</note> are placed directly opposite each other. Then, place the **first instrumental choir** <note>original: *primum Chorum Instrumental*</note> not far from the second vocal choir, and the **second instrumental choir** not far from the **first vocal choir**. In this way, the vocal parts can be heard even more distinctly and clearly, while the instruments can be heard and observed from a distance with better grace <note>original: *gratia*</note>. It then appears as if such a **concerto** <note>original: *Concert*</note> were arranged and set for four separate choirs.

The **first instrumental choir** can be performed with cornets <note>original: *Cornetten*; curved wooden trumpet-like instruments</note> and trombones <note>original: *Posaunen*</note>, or also with recorders and flutes <note>original: *Block-Querflöiten*</note> and bassoons <note>original: *Fagotten*</note>, though they should be played very quietly and softly. The **second instrumental choir** can be played with four bass viols <note>original: *Violen de Gamba*</note>, or four violas <note>original: *Violen de Braccio*</note>, or in the manner mentioned in the previous Chapter 7 regarding the recorder and viol choirs. One can also employ and use the theorbo <note>original: *Theorba*; a large, long-necked lute</note>, lutes, pandora <note>original: *Pandoer*; a wire-strung plucked instrument</note>, and citterns <note>original: *Cythern*</note> for this choir at the same time, if they are available.

Alternatively, if enough instruments are not available to supply both choirs, one could use the theorbos and lutes etc. entirely alone for one choir, while for the other, employ four **ornamental instruments** <note>original: *Ornament-Instrumenta*</note> consisting of violins or wind instruments. Or, one could leave out the lute choir and use the aforementioned instruments for one choir alone, while using no instruments at all for the other choir, but rather only the organ or regal <note>original: *Regal*; a small, portable reed organ with a buzzy sound</note>. Or one could extract and write out a single **string ensemble** <note>original: *Capellam Fidiciniam*</note> from both, playing continuously throughout. Or, in each instrumental choir, only the **treble** <note>original: *Cantus*</note> and **bass** <note>original: *Bassus*</note> could be used to accompany the vocal parts, as seen in the work of Antonio Burlini in his *Riviera Fiorita* <note>original: *Antonius Burlinus*; an Italian composer whose 1612 collection is cited here as a model</note>. Or the instrumental choirs could be left out entirely, and the **soloist parts** <note>original: *Concertat-Stimmen*</note> sung and performed with only the organ or regal, as shown in the first style.

---

## The VII. Style.

In the seventh style, the chorale is diminished <note>original: *diminuiret*; the practice of breaking long notes into fast, decorative passages</note> in two sopranos <note>original: *Discanten*</note> or tenors in the first **verse** <note>original: *Versu*</note>, and is handled and performed in a fugal style <note>original: *Fugenweis*</note>. Along with this, however, for every treble part there is an **instrumental bass** <note>original: *Bassus Instrumentalis*</note> <meta>catchword: ver-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the spatial arrangement and varied instrumentation of vocal and instrumental choirs to achieve acoustic balance. It concludes by introducing the Seventh Style, characterized by ornamental "diminutions" and fugal writing in the voices.</summary>
<keywords>choir arrangement, acoustics, instrumentation, cornets, trombones, viols, theorbo, Antonio Burlini, chorale diminution, fugal style</keywords>

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of vocal and instrumental arrangements, specifically moving from the variations of the Seventh Style into the Eighth and Ninth Styles. It maintains the focus on how to combine different choirs and "capellas" (reinforcement groups) to achieve a grand, "panegyric" or celebratory sound.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>
<sig>Z 3</sig>

is present, in much the same way as found in the third style: except that in the second and following verses and parts, in addition to the previous ones, a **third instrumental or vocal choir** <note>original: *Tertius Chorus Instrumentalis oder Vocalis*</note> (as in: *We Believe*) is sometimes added. Occasionally (as in: *Christ our Lord came to the Jordan*), the remaining and thus complete instrumental parts and choirs are joined to the first and second **instrumental bass** <note>original: *Basso Instrumentali*</note>. This style might perhaps also be quite pleasant to hear.

However, since this style can be arranged in several different ways and manners, I have chosen to indicate this more extensively for those who may have a desire and love for it in my work **Polyhymnia: The Herald, or Panegyric** <note>original: *Polyhymnia caduceatrice, seu Panegyrica*; a collection of large-scale festive works by Praetorius</note> under the section for the **basso continuo** <note>original: *General-Baß*; the foundational bass line played by the organ or other harmony instruments</note>. There, I also explain how it is to be understood when notations of 2, 4, 5, 7, & 8 voices, etc., are found above certain songs. I refer the **kind musician** <note>original: *benevolum Musicum*</note> to that work for this occasion.

---

## The VIII. Style.

The eighth style is almost like the sixth: except that in this one, there are not only the two **instrumental choirs** <note>original: *Choros Instrumentales*</note> and one or two **capellas** <note>original: *Capellen*; additional groups of singers or players used to reinforce the main parts and add volume</note> present (each of which usually proceeds alongside its own choir); but also the **soloistic voices** <note>original: *Concertat-Stimmen*; parts written for skilled soloists to "compete" or "concert" with the ensemble</note> are sometimes set by themselves against the **basso continuo**, without the assistance of the instrumental choirs and capellas. They carry their harmony with the **foundation instrument** <note>original: *Fundament-Instrument*</note>, namely the **organ** and **regal** <note>original: *Regal*; a small, portable pipe organ with a reed-like sound</note>. Afterwards, however, the choirs and capellas join in again and do not forget to contribute their part. An example of this is found in *Polyhymnia III. Panegyrica*, Number 31: *O God from Heaven*, etc.

---

## The IX. Style.

Regarding: *Now come, Savior of the Gentiles*; *Wake up*; *Behold how fine*; *Our Father*; *My soul magnifies the Lord*; *Christ, you who are day and light*; *In you, Lord, have I put my trust*; *Praised be you, Jesus Christ*; and several others.

(Which is the ninth style) all sorts of manners are included, namely the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, 5th, and 6th manners of the third type, as well as the 2nd, 5th, 6th, 7th, and 8th types besides. This can be easily recognized and understood by anyone who sees and hears them.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on complex polychoral arrangements, detailing the Eighth Style's use of soloists against a continuo foundation and the Ninth Style's synthesis of all previous techniques.</summary>
<keywords>polychoral, basso continuo, capella, soloists, organ, regal, Polyhymnia, Praetorius, musical styles</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the large-scale musical works in Praetorius's "Polyhymnia" collection. It transitions from discussing the specific styles of arrangement to practical advice for directors, particularly regarding how to handle the vocal parts and the use of instruments.</meta>

<page-num>182</page-num>

And indeed, many other concerted songs <note>original: *Concert-Gesänge*; musical works where different groups of voices or instruments "compete" or cooperate</note> contained in these *Polyhymnia* volumes—if one wishes to properly weigh <note>original: *ponderiren*</note> and consider them—can be referred <note>original: *referiret*</note> and drawn not only to the specific style under which they are listed, but to several other various styles and manners as well; as shall then be noted and indicated, **God** willing, for almost every song in its respective *Polyhymnia*.

However, in all these manners of the Third Style, the following must be observed <note>original: *obseruiren*</note>: that where no ornamental instruments <note>original: *Ornament-Instrumenta*; instruments like the violin, cornetto, or lute used to add decorative flourishes</note> are available—or even if they are at hand—one can sometimes, for the sake of variety <note>original: *pro variatione*</note>, leave them out entirely and allow only the soloistic <note>original: *Concertat*</note> parts to be sung by the organ or other foundation instruments <note>original: *Fundament-Instrumenten*; instruments like the organ or harpsichord that provide the harmonic ground</note>, clearly, gracefully, and as is fitting, with a pure and full voice: just like those pieces set in the First Manner of this Third Style.

Therefore, above each song in the thorough-bass <note>original: *General-Bass*; the continuous bass line with numbers indicating the harmony</note> parts, I have noted, numbered, and placed the soloistic <note>original: *Concertat*</note>—that is, the vocal and principal—parts. These parts carry and hold the essence of the entire song <note>original: *Essentia totius Cantionis*</note> and the whole body and main structure <note>original: *Corpus vnd Principal Werck*</note> of this style, and for that reason, they must necessarily be sung very clearly and well. I have done this so that one may immediately see, know, and recognize at first glance how many and which principal parts are actually required for the chosen song. The instrumental parts, however—which are added, as it were, incidentally, for the sake of greater fullness and ornament <note>original: *per accidens, majoris plenitudinis & ornatus gratia*</note>, and without which the song can still be performed <note>original: *Musiciret*</note> quite well—I have not marked above the bass; rather, they are to be found by the clef signatures <note>original: *Clavibus Signatis*</note> in the thorough-bass of each *Polyhymnia*.

In schools where plenty of boys are available, one can—especially in the Fourth Manner, in the songs **Praise be to Thee, Jesus Christ** and **From Heaven High I Come to Earth**, etc., in *Polyhymnia III*—place two boys at each vocal part <note>original: *Cantu*</note>. Thus, when they are separated into three different locations from one another (especially where no foundation instruments are present to guide them), they can help and support one another more boldly and surely.

However, because in several songs the soloistic parts found in the vocal choir <note>original: *Choro Vocali*</note> may initially seem very difficult to those—especially in schools—to whom the Italian manner of singing is unknown, and whose throats and voices are not yet disposed or trained <note>original: *Disponiret vnnd Abgerichtet*</note> for it; especially since these same divisions, runs, and trills <note>original: *Diminutiones, Tiratæ, Tremoli*</note> and other such ornaments...

<summary>Praetorius provides practical instructions for performing his "Polyhymnia" works, explaining that instrumental "ornament" parts are optional, while the "concertato" vocal parts are essential and must be clearly performed. He also offers tips for school choirs, such as doubling boy sopranos to ensure confidence during complex, multi-choir arrangements.</summary>
<keywords>Polyhymnia, performance practice, thorough-bass, concertato, vocal music, musical education, ornamentation, Michael Praetorius, Renaissance music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s technical performance instructions for the Third Style of composition, specifically addressing how to teach difficult ornamental parts to young singers and how to manage long multi-verse works. It then introduces the Fourth Style of arrangement.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

style cannot be named—by someone who has not previously heard or learned it—nor can it be grasped very quickly or brought forth with the voice and throat at the beginning. Therefore, in several of these concerted works <note>original: *Concerten*; pieces where different groups of voices or instruments interact or "compete"</note>, I have placed the plain, interrupted chorale <note>original: *schlechten vnterbrochenen Choral*; the basic hymn melody</note> directly underneath each voice; however, in some cases, I have placed it separately at the back of the fourteenth part <note>original: *Decimo quarto*; refers to the 14th volume or part-book in a set</note> because there was enough space remaining there. I have done this so that every musician can better find their way through it, inform his boys <note>the boy sopranos or trebles</note> accordingly, and be able to present and prescribe the simple song <note>original: *Simplicem Cantum*</note> to them from the diminished <note>original: *ex Diminuto*; a style of ornamentation where long notes are "divided" or broken down into many fast, small notes</note> version. Although a more extensive, detailed, and complete report on this can be found in the *Instruction for Instrumentalists and Singers* <note>original: *Instruction pro Symphoniacis & Concentoribus*</note>.

Finally, regarding this style, it is also to be observed <note>original: *observiren*</note> that in the concerted songs of the fourth, fifth, sixth, seventh, and eighth manners (in which all, or indeed most, verses of the Psalm and song are found composed one after another), one can take only the first verse and perform it <note>original: *figurirn*; to perform with musical "figures" or ornaments</note> beforehand with the added instruments, or else with just the soloistic voices <note>original: *Concertat-Stimmen*</note>. The following verses, however, can be sung completely through by the congregation in the plain chorale style <note>original: *Choraliter*</note>; and one can conclude with the last verse if there is enough time remaining. I have appended more detailed reminders about this in the thorough-bass <note>original: *General-Baß*; the continuous bass line providing the harmonic foundation</note> for each concerted piece.

And this Third Style—along with the songs <note>original: *Cantiones*</note> directed toward its various manners—will, God willing, mostly appear in *Polyhymnia* III, IV, and V, as well as in the *Panegyrica* and the *Thirty-Voice Exercises* <note>original: *Exercita tricenim*</note>.

## The IV. Style.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'I' within a square frame featuring ornate floral and foliate scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

<insert><margin>The Fourth Style.</margin></insert>
In this style, every musician will see for himself how he can arrange the choirs <note>original: *Choros*</note>, either positioned across from one another or however else it seems good to him. However, very diligent care must be taken with each choir as to how the first, second, third, and subsequent verses or settings follow one after another, so that no confusion <note>original: *Confusion*</note> may arise from it.

<summary>Michael Praetorius explains how to teach complex vocal ornaments to young singers by providing the underlying plain melody and offers advice on alternating soloists with the congregation during long psalms. He also introduces the Fourth Style, emphasizing the spatial arrangement of choirs.</summary>
<keywords>vocal ornamentation, diminution, pedagogy, choral performance, Polyhymnia, liturgical practice, spatial music, thorough-bass</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues Praetorius’s practical advice for church music directors on organizing ensembles and managing the flow of a service where both the professional choir and the congregation sing. It transitions from the Fourth Style into the Fifth Type of composition.</meta>

The second choir can be arranged like a **string ensemble** <note>original: *Capella Fidicinia*; an ensemble consisting primarily of bowed or plucked strings</note>, or perhaps with lutes, violins, and other pleasant **instruments**, just like the **English consort** (of which mention was made at the beginning of this **volume** <note>original: *Tomi*</note>). The **tenor** part can be sung by a **tenor** or a **treble singer** <note>original: *Discantisten*; a high-voiced singer, often a boy soprano</note> who has a pure voice.

If later the **chorale** <note>the plain, traditional hymn melody</note> is to be sung sometimes after or between the **polyphony** <note>original: *Figural*; elaborate, composed music with independent voice parts</note> (which should be noted in the first, second, third, and fourth manners of the third type), then as soon as the **third-to-last verse** <note>original: *antepenultimus versus*</note> has been sung **in the chorale style** <note>original: *choraliter*</note>, the **organist** must join in with a strong registration on the **organ**. After about three or four **beats** <note>original: *Tacten*</note>, he should play the **finale**, so that the people in the church remain silent. Then, the last verse should be started **in the ornate style** <note>original: *figuraliter*</note>, and the song concluded in that way. However, as I have observed and found in some places, the congregation in the church—especially before they are accustomed to it and truly understand the practice—does not easily let themselves be stopped. Therefore, it is almost better to let them sing the last verse completely to the end, and then immediately begin to **perform music** <note>original: *Musiciren*</note> on that same verse once more to conclude the service.

---

### The V. Type.

<margin>The Fifth Type.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the section, featuring white interlaced vine-like patterns on a black rectangular background.</image-desc>
In this style, a **Hallelujah** or **Gloria**, or another beautiful **phrase** <note>original: *Sententia*</note> worthy of attention, is **performed** <note>original: *Musiciret*</note> and **repeated** at the beginning, middle, and end, and before and after; this is not unpleasant to hear.

Such interspersed **Hallelujahs** and **phrases** <note>original: *Sententiæ*</note> cannot be better named than **reinforcement parts** <note>original: *Ripieni*; parts that fill out the texture of the full ensemble</note> and **refrains** <note>original: *Ritornello*; literally "little returns"</note>. For although (as shown above) the word **refrain** <note>original: *Ritornello*</note> is understood to mean the **repetitions** performed only with **instruments** without **vocal** parts, I am not displeased to also use the name **refrain** <note>original: *Ritornello*</note> for those which are set with both **vocal** and **instrumental** parts. Such **refrains** and **repetitions** were used by the royal prophet and supreme **music director** <note>original: *Capelmeister*</note> **David** in his 8th, 24th, 42nd, 103rd, 107th, 118th, 136th, 148th, 150th, and other **Psalms**.

<meta>catchword: As then</meta>

<summary>This page provides instructions for organizing instrumental consorts and coordinating the transition between congregational singing and ornate choral music. It also introduces the 'Fifth Type' of composition, characterized by the use of instrumental and vocal refrains known as ritornellos.</summary>
<keywords>instrumentation, English consort, chorale performance, organist instructions, congregational singing, ritornello, ripieni, King David, Psalm settings, performance practice</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical structure, moving from congregational participation to the use of repetitions and instrumental interludes, drawing historical parallels with ancient liturgical forms.</meta>

Just as the ancients were very diligent in their Latin choral songs with such repetitions <term>original: *repetitionum*; the repeating of specific musical or textual phrases</term> or ritornellos <term>original: *ritornellen*; short instrumental or vocal interludes that return throughout a piece</term>; as seen in the Invitatories <note>the opening chants of the Matins service that "invite" the congregation to worship</note>.

> Come, let us rejoice <note>original: "Venite exultemus"</note>) Christ is born for us, Come, let us adore him. Hallelujah, the Lord has risen Hallelujah: Come, let us: Hallelujah, the King ascending into heaven, Come, let us adore him. Hallelujah, the spirit of the Lord has filled the round world: Come, let us adore him, Hallelujah. True God, one in Trinity, and Trinity in unity: Come, let us adore him.
> (On the Birthday of the Lord) God was made man, God is now reconciled to us sinners.
> (A little child is born to us) Glory to the Trinity for eternal ages.
> (In the Hymn of Bishop Theodulph) Glory, praise, and honor be to you, O King Christ Redeemer.
> Likewise, Faithful cross, the one noble tree among all: Sweet wood, etc. Lord have mercy, Christ have mercy, Lord have mercy: Lord have pity, Christ hear us, Save us.
> Christ the Lord was made obedient, etc.
> (In the Hymn of Bishop Fortunatus) Hail, festive day, venerable through all time: On which God conquered Hell, and holds the stars.

To imitate this in some measure, I have not only arranged several ritornellos solely for instrumental parts between various Latin and German songs <note>original: *Cantionibus*</note>, but I have also accommodated suitable texts beneath some of them, which have a certain emphasis <note>original: *Emphasin*; a rhetorical weight or significance</note>.

### As in *Polyhymnia III* <note>The third volume of Praetorius’s large collection of Lutheran church music</note>.

In "Praised and Blessed" <note>original: "Gelobet vnd Gepreiset"</note>: There is the ritornello; "Amen, God the Father and Son be praise in heaven’s throne, his Spirit etc."

"A boy is born" <note>original: "Puer natus"</note>: Ritornello: "Sing, rejoice, triumph, thank the Lord." "Now come, Savior of the heathens" <note>original: "Nu kom der Heyden H."</note>: Ritornello: "Praise be to God the Father’s throne, Praise be..." "Come, Holy Spirit": Ritornello: "Come, Holy Spirit, fill the hearts of your faithful: Hallelujah."

"O Lamb of God" Ritornello: "So we will now praise and thank at all times, the Father and Son." These are all arranged according to the beat <note>original: *vff den Schlag*</note>, as are all those contained under the second style in *Polyhymnia IV*.

"Behold how fine" <note>original: "Sihe wie fein"</note>. Ritornello: "Praise the Lord, all heathens, and praise him all people."

"Christ is Risen." "Christ went to Heaven." Ritornello: "Hallelujah, Hallelujah, Hallelujah."

"Our Father in Heaven." Ritornello: "Amen, that is, may it be true, strengthen our faith forever, etc."

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical structure, specifically focusing on the implementation of ritornellos—recurring instrumental or vocal refrains—within various sacred compositions by Michael Praetorius. It provides concrete examples of how these interludes are used to frame or bridge different sections of hymns and liturgical settings.</meta>

**My soul magnifies the Lord.** <note>original: *Meine Seel erhebt den H.*</note> Ritornello. **My soul magnifies the Lord** and my spirit rejoices in God my Savior. Or: **As he spoke to our fathers, Abraham and his seed forever.**

**Christ, you who are day and light.** <note>original: *Christe der du bist Tag vnd Licht*</note> Ritornello. **Praise, honor, and glory be to God the Father, and likewise to his wise Son, and the Holy Spirit's grace.** <note>original: *GOtt Vater sey Lob/Ehr vnd Preiß /darzu auch seinem Sohne weiß/deß H. Geistes g.*</note>

**In you, Lord, have I put my trust.** <note>original: *In dich hab ich gehoffet HErr*</note> Ritornello. **Glory, praise, honor, and majesty be prepared for God the Father and the Son, and the Holy Spirit likewise.** <note>original: *Glory Lob/Ehr vnd Herrligkeit/ sey GOtt Vater vnd Sohne bereit/dem H. Geist m.*</note>

**Praised be you, Jesus Christ.** <note>original: *Gelobet seistu JEsU Christ*</note> Ritornello. **Praised be you, Jesus Christ, that you were born as a man.** Or: **All this he has done for us, to show his great love.**

->**Make the gates wide and the doors in the world high:** <note>original: *Machet die Thore weit vnd die Thüre in der Welt hoch*; a reference to Psalm 24</note><-

**As the gracious God.** <note>original: *Als der gütige GOtt*</note> Ritornello. **God, through your goodness, protect us always from the Devil; Christ, Savior of the world, stretch out your hand over us.**

**In the *Te Deum Laudamus*:** <note>The "Te Deum" is an ancient Latin hymn of praise; "We praise you, O God."</note> there are many different Ritornellos. For instance:
In the 1st Part: 1st and 2nd Ritornello with instrumental voices. In the 2nd Part: 1st, 2nd, and 3rd **Hallelujah** (where several symphonies <note>In this period, "symphony" referred to an instrumental interlude rather than a long orchestral work</note> are also mixed in at the same time). 1st, 2nd, and 3rd: **A hymn becomes you, praise becomes you, to you [is honor owed].** <note>original: *Te decet hymnus, te decent laudes, tibi d.*</note>
In the 3rd Part: 1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, and 5th: **You are the King of Glory, you are the King of Glory, O Christ.** <note>original: *Tu rex gloriæ, tu rex gloriæ Christe.*</note>
In the 4th Part: 1st and 2nd: **Hallelujah in another style.**
In the 5th Part: 1st and 2nd: **Glory to God in the highest.** <note>original: *Gloria in excelsis Deo.*</note> **Let us bless the Lord: Let us give thanks to God.** <note>original: *Benedicamus : Deo dicamus.*</note> In the German version, **Lord God, we praise you,** <note>original: *HErr GOtt dich loben wir*</note> similar examples are also to be found.

**In *Polyhymnia* VIII:** <note>The eighth volume of Michael Praetorius's massive collection of sacred music, published in 1619</note> three Masses or <term>Kyrie</term> <note>The "Lord have mercy" portion of the liturgy</note> are also arranged in this manner: Specifically, before and after, as well as between the <term>Kyrie/Christe/Kyrie/etc.</term> in the first <term>Missa</term> <note>Mass setting</note>; **Glory to God in the highest.** <note>original: *Gloria in excelsis Deo.*</note> In the second; **A hymn becomes you, to you [praise is owed].** <note>original: *Te decet hymnus, te d.*</note>
In the third, namely **Lord, source of goodness,** <note>original: *Kyrie fons bonitatis*; a specific traditional melody for the Kyrie</note> the Ritornellos are mostly arranged for instrumental voices.

**In the Song of the Three Young Men:** <note>original: *Cantico trium puerorum*; a biblical canticle from the Book of Daniel</note> Ritornello: **Bless the Lord, all works of the Lord.** <note>original: *Benedicite Domino omnia opera Domini.*</note>

**In the Invitatory:** <note>The opening chant of the Matins service</note> Ritornello: **Come, let us rejoice in the Lord, let us shout for joy to God our Savior.** <note>original: *venite exultemus Domino, jubilemus Deo salutari nostro.*</note>

**In the praises of the Paschal Victim:** <note>original: *victimæ paschali laudes*; a sequence hymn for Easter</note> Ritornello: **Christ has risen, Christ my hope has risen.** <note>original: *Surrexit Christus , Surrexit Christus spes mea.*</note>

**Praise God, the God of Gods.** <note>original: *Laudate Deum, Deum Deorum.*</note> **Praise the Lord, the King of Glory.**
**Come to the sanctuary of the Lord.** <note>original: *Venite ad sanctuarium Domini.*</note> Ritornello: **Come and let us return to the Lord, and we shall live in his sight.** <note>original: *Venite & revertamur ad Dominum, & vivemus in conspectu ejus.*</note> **Give thanks to the Lord, Ritornello: For his mercy endures forever.** <note>original: *Confitemini Domino... Quoniam in seculum misericordia ejus.*</note> for 4 or 5 choirs.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of specific scriptural and liturgical texts used for musical refrains. It transitions from German hymn examples on the previous page to Latin liturgical sequences and concludes with a technical justification for using "ripieni" refrains, citing the influence of the composer Gabriel Fattorini.</meta>

**Praise the Lord, O Jerusalem; praise your God, O Zion.** <note>original: *Lauda Hierusalem Dominum, lauda Deum tuum Sion*</note> **Give thanks to the Lord, for he is good, for [his mercy endures] forever, etc.** <note>original: *Confitemini Domino, quoniam bonus, quoniam in feculum &c.*</note> for 8 or 9 choirs. And anyone can seek out such passages according to their own pleasure from the Latin and German Psalms of David, as well as from other spiritual songs, or even invent and devise them themselves. Likewise from the Latin choral chants, especially for the high feast days. Such as: In **Advent**: On the **Feasts of the Nativity, Resurrection, Pentecost, Trinity,** etc. **Come, O Lord, and do not delay: release the sins of your people Israel.** <note>original: *Veni Domine, & noli tardare: relaxa facinora plebis tuae Israel.*</note> **Praise, honor, power, and glory to God the Father, together with the Son and the Holy Spirit, for eternal ages, Amen.** <note>original: *Laus, honor, virtus, gloria, Deo patri cum filio, sancto simul paracleto, In sempiterna secula, Amen.*</note>

**Come and deliver us, our God. Do not fear: tomorrow you shall go out, and the Lord will be with you, Emmanuel.** <note>original: *Veni & libera nos Deus noster. Nolite timere...*</note> **The Word was made flesh and dwelt among us.** <note>original: *Verbum caro factum est, & habitavit in nobis.*</note> **A child is born to us, and a son is given to us.** <note>original: *Puer natus est nobis, & filius datus est nobis.*</note> **Glory to God in the highest, and on earth peace to men of good will: Hallelujah.** <note>original: *Gloria in excelsis Deo, & in terra pax hominibus bonae voluntatis: Halelujah.*</note> **Glory to you, O Lord, who were born of a Virgin** (who appeared today; who rose from the dead; who rose today) **with the Father and the Holy Spirit, for eternal ages.** <note>original: *Gloria tibi Domine, Qui natus es de virgine...*</note>

**Come, O nations, and adore the Lord.** <note>original: *Venite gentes & adorate Dominum.*</note> **In this Christmas (Easter) joy, let us bless the Lord.** <note>original: *In hoc natali (paschali) gaudio, benedicamus Domino.*</note> **Help us, O God of our salvation, and deliver us, O Lord.** <note>original: *Adjuva nos Deus salutaris noster & libera nos Domine.*</note> **This is the day the Lord has made; let us rejoice and be glad in it.** <note>original: *Haec est dies quam fecit Dominus, exultemus & laetemur in ea.*</note>

**Let Christians offer praises to the Paschal victim.** <note>original: *Victimae paschali laudes immolent Christiani.*</note>

**May the grace of the Holy Spirit be present with us.** <note>original: *Sancti Spiritus adsit nobis gratia.*</note> **Grant us the merit of virtue, grant us the outcome of salvation, grant us eternal joy.** <note>original: *Da virtutis, meritum, da salutis exitum, da perenne gaudium.*</note>

**We invoke you, we adore you, we praise you, O blessed Trinity.** <note>original: *Te invocamus, te adoramus, te laudamus ô beata Trinitas.*</note> **Praise be to the unbegotten Father, praise to his only-begotten [Son], praise be to the Holy Spirit, the triune and simple God, Amen.** <note>original: *Laus Patri fit ingenito, Laus ejus unigenito, Laus sit sancto spiritui, trino Deo & simplici, Amen.*</note>

**Let us bless the Father and the Son with the Holy Spirit. Blessed are you, Lord God, and worthy of praise forever, Hallelujah.** <note>original: *Benedicamus Patrem & Filium cum sancto spiritu...*</note>

**Praise becomes you, a hymn becomes you, to you is owed all honor and glory, forever.** <note>original: *Te decent laudes, te decet hymnus...*</note>

**Praise, O Zion, your Savior; praise your leader and shepherd with hymns and songs.** <note>original: *Lauda Sion Salvatorem, lauda ducem & pastorem, in hymnis & canticis.*</note> However, another reason for this <term>invention: a creative musical technique or structural idea</term> was that I saw the newly added <term>ripieni: supplementary choir parts used to reinforce the texture in louder sections</term> in Gabriel Fattorini’s two-voice concertos. In those, an emphatic Latin sentence or a charming, beautiful verse is always sung in between, mostly in the same rhythmic proportion, by the full choir three or four different times. This matches quite well with the style I have included in my other works, except that there the full choir does not begin the piece, but only begins to sing and sound in the middle and at the end.

<summary>This page provides a collection of Latin liturgical texts for various feast days (Christmas, Easter, Pentecost, Trinity) to be used as musical refrains. Praetorius also credits the composer Gabriel Fattorini for inspiring his use of the 'ripieno'—a full choir reinforcement that repeats a short, powerful sentence throughout a piece.</summary>
