<meta>This page continues the transition into practical vocal instruction, moving from the general duties of a musician to a specific critique of modern singers who over-ornament their music. It introduces the upcoming section based on the groundbreaking Italian methods of the early 17th century.</meta>

...the voice, and are also gifted with a supple throat for <term>diminution</term> <note>original: "diminuiren." This refers to the practice of breaking long notes into many fast, short notes to decorate a melody.</note>, yet do not allow themselves to be bound by the <term>laws of the musicians</term> <note>original: "Musicorum leges," the fundamental rules of composition and harmony.</note>. Instead, they go on and on with their excessive <term>coloring</term> <note>original: "colorirn," the addition of decorative runs and flourishes.</note>, overstepping the <term>limits</term> prescribed in the song, and ruining and obscuring it to such a degree that one does not know what they are singing. One can neither hear nor even less understand the text or the notes (which the composer set, and which give the song its best decoration and <term>grace</term>).

Which bad manner (to which several instrumentalists have also particularly accustomed themselves) hardly <term>affects</term> <note>Moves or touches the emotions.</note> or delights the <term>listeners</term>, especially those who have some knowledge of the art, but rather makes them annoyed and sleepy. Therefore, so that the <term>natural power</term> and <term>grace</term> given to the song by the master [composer] is not taken away by such <term>deformity</term> of <term>diminution</term>, but rather that every word and <term>meaning</term> <note>original: "Sententia."</note> may be properly understood by everyone: It is highly necessary that all <term>cantors</term> or singers practice diligently from their youth <term>in the voice and articulate pronunciation</term>, and make themselves familiar with the same.

How, however, and in what form this happens, and how one may accustom oneself to a good way of singing according to the current, New Italian Manner—to <term>express</term> the <term>accents</term> and <term>emotions</term> <note>original: "affectus exprimirn." The Baroque goal of music was to represent and trigger specific emotional states in the listener.</note>, and how one can most properly and conveniently <term>apply</term> the <term>trills</term>, <term>groups</term> <note>original: "Gruppen" or *gruppi*, a specific type of rapid ornament alternating between two adjacent notes.</note>, and other <term>coloraturas</term>—this shall soon come forth, with God's help, in a separate little treatise. For this work, Giulio Romano (otherwise called Giulio Caccini of Rome) in his *The New Music* <note>original: "Le nuove Musiche" (1602), the most influential book of the early Baroque, which introduced the solo singing style.</note>, and Giovanni Battista Bovicelli have been especially useful to me.

For a lovely, correct, and beautiful way of singing, three things are required, as in all other arts: namely, <term>Nature, Art or Instruction, and Practice</term> <note>original Latin: "Natura, Ars seu Doctrina, & Exercitatio."</note>.

<sig>1. NATU-</sig> <note>Catchword for the next page: "NATURE"</note>

<summary>The author critiques singers who obscure the composer's intent with excessive ornamentation and introduces a new instructional guide based on the Italian styles of Caccini and Bovicelli. He identifies Nature, Art, and Practice as the three pillars of good singing.</summary>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from criticizing poor singing habits to providing positive instruction on the natural requirements of a singer and the technical basics of the new Italian-influenced style. The previous page ended with the necessity of diligent practice from youth.</meta>

# I. NATURE.

First, a singer must naturally possess a voice, in which three **requirements** <note>original: "Requisita."</note> and three **faults** <note>original: "vitia."</note> are to be noted.

The **requirements** are these: first, that a singer has a beautiful, lovely, trembling and vibrating voice (yet not in the way some are accustomed to in schools, but with a particular **restraint** <note>original: "moderation."</note>) and a smooth, flexible throat for **diminution** <note>original: "diminuiren." The technique of breaking long notes into many fast, short notes to decorate a melody.</note>. Second: to be able to hold a steady, long breath without frequent **breathing** <note>original: "respiriren."</note>. Third: to choose a voice part such as **Soprano** <note>original: "Cantum."</note>, **Alto**, or **Tenor**, etc., which he can maintain with a full and clear sound without **falsetto** (that is, a half-voice or forced voice).

And in this regard, **intonation** <note>original: "Intonatio."</note> and **exclamation** <note>original: "Exclamatio."</note> are to be noted.

## INTONATION.

**Intonation** is the manner in which a song is begun. There are different opinions regarding this. Some want it to begin on the correct pitch; some on the **second** <note>original: "Secunda."</note> below the correct pitch, provided that the voice gradually climbs and raises itself; some on the **third** <note>original: "Tertia."</note>; some on the **fourth** <note>original: "Quarta."</note>. Some say one should begin with a charming and dampened voice. These different styles are for the most part included under the name of **accent** <note>original: "Accentus."</note>.

## EXCLAMATION.

**Exclamation** is the proper means to **move** <note>original: "moviren."</note> the **emotions** <note>original: "affectus."</note>, which must be done by raising the voice. It can be applied and used on all **half notes** <note>original: "Minimis."</note> and **quarter notes** <note>original: "Semiminimis."</note> with a dot when **descending** <note>original: "Descendendo."</note>. And the following note, which moves somewhat quickly, **moves** more **emotion** than the **whole note** <note>original: "Semibrevis."</note>, which is better suited for the raising and lowering of the voice without **exclamation** and possesses better **grace** <note>original: "gratiam."</note>. This shall be declared in detail and with examples in the aforementioned **treatise** <note>original: "Tractat."</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the natural physical requirements for a singer, including breath control and vocal quality, while introducing the technical concepts of "Intonation" (how to start a note) and "Exclamation" (a technique for expressive singing).</summary>
<keywords>vocal requirements, Intonation, Exclamation, breath control, falsetto, musical affect, baroque singing techniques</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical requirements and potential faults of a singer to the technical "Instruction" (Doctrina) section. It begins defining the various types of musical ornamentation, specifically "diminutions" or "coloratura," which were essential to the expressive style of the early 17th century.</meta>

The **faults** <note>original: "vitia."</note> in the voice are: that some **breathe** <note>original: "respiriren."</note> and take breath with great frequency; some sing through the nose and by holding the voice back in the throat; some sing with their teeth clenched together. All of which is not to be praised, but rather **disfigures** <note>original: "deformiret."</note> the harmony and makes it unpleasant <note>The source text reads "anmutig" (charming), but given the context of "disfigures," it is likely a misprint or archaic inversion for "un-anmutig."</note>.

And thus far concerning **nature** <note>original: "Natura."</note>: now follows **instruction** <note>original: "Doctrina."</note>.

## 2. INSTRUCTION.

Secondly, a singer must have the proper knowledge to form **diminutions** <note>original: "Diminutiones." The technique of "diminishing" or breaking down long notes into many fast, short notes to decorate a melody.</note> (otherwise commonly called **coloratura** <note>original: "Coloraturen."</note>) in a lovely and **appropriate** <note>original: "Apposité."</note> manner.

**Diminution** <note>original: "Diminutio."</note>, however, is when a larger note is **resolved** <note>original: "resolviret."</note> and broken into many other fast and smaller notes. Of these there are now different kinds and **modes** <note>original: "Modi."</note>: some of which occur **stepwise** <note>original: "Gradatim." Moving by adjacent steps of the scale.</note>, following one after another: such as **accents** <note>original: "Accentus."</note>, **tremolo** <note>original: "Tremulo."</note>, **groups** <note>original: "Gruppi."</note> and **tirata** <note>original: "Tirata." Meaning a "shot" or "pull," referring to a rapid scale passage connecting two distant notes.</note>.

->**The Accent is:**<-

When the notes are drawn in the throat in the following manner.

<margin>Note Well</margin> The double-flagged note <note>A sixteenth note.</note>, under which a 3 is marked, signifies that it should be triple-flagged <note>A thirty-second note.</note>, of which 32 belong to one **beat** <note>original: "Tact." Referring to a full measure or rhythmic unit.</note>.

## Tremolo: Is nothing other than a trembling of the voice over a single note: organists call it <term>mordents</term> <note>From the Italian *mordere* ("to bite"). A rapid alternation between the written note and the note above or below it.</note> or moderants,

### Groups: Are used in <term>cadences</term> <note>original: "cadentiis"</note> and <term>formal conclusions</term> <note>original: "clausulis formalibus"</note> / and must be struck more sharply than <term>tremolos</term> <note>original: "tremoli"</note>.

-> Tirades : <-

### Are long, fast <term>little runs</term> <note>original: "Läufflin"</note> / which are made <term>stepwise</term> <note>original: "gradatim"</note> / and run up or down across the keyboard.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ornamentation, transitioning from stepwise figures like the tirata to ornaments that use repeated notes or leaps, specifically focusing on the early Baroque "trillo."</meta>

## Diminutions <note>the practice of breaking long notes into many shorter, decorative notes</note> that do not proceed stepwise / are the Trill and Passages <note>virtuoso melodic runs</note>.

### The Trill:

It is of two types: The first occurs in **unison** <note>on the same pitch</note>, either on a line or in a space [of the musical staff]; when many fast notes are repeated one after another.

<image-desc>Three lines of musical notation demonstrating the "Trillo" as a series of repeated notes on a single pitch (G). The notes are written in white mensural notation with flags. The first two lines include numbers (3 3 3) and the syllable "ve-ni" <note>Latin for "come"</note> underneath to indicate rhythm or grouping. The third line is a shorter fragment.</image-desc>

And examples of this type are to be found in Claudio Monteverdi <note>original: "Claudio de Monte verde"</note>.

The other kind of Trill is performed in various different ways. And although it is impossible to learn how to form a trill correctly from written instructions / unless it happens **by the living voice and help of a teacher** <note>original Latin: "viva Præceptoris voce & ope"</note> / and it is sung and demonstrated to the student / so that one learns it from the other / just as one bird learns from another. Therefore, apart from the aforementioned **Giulio Caccini**, I have not yet found this kind of trill described by any Italian author / but find only a "t:", "tr:", or "tri:" placed above the notes that are meant to be formed with a trill. Nevertheless, I have deemed it necessary to add several types here in passing / so that the currently ignorant **beginners** <note>original Latin: "Tyrones"</note> may see and know at least a little of what is roughly called a Trill.

## Diminutions <note>the practice of breaking long notes into many shorter, decorative notes</note> that do not proceed stepwise / are the Trill and Passages <note>virtuoso melodic runs</note>.

### The Trill:

It is of two types: The first occurs in **unison** <note>on the same pitch</note>, either on a line or in a space of the musical staff; when many fast notes are repeated one after another.

And examples of this type are to be found in the works of **Claudio Monteverdi** <note>original: "Claudium de Monte verde"</note>.

The other kind of Trill is performed in various different ways. And although it is impossible to learn how to form a trill correctly from written instructions / unless it happens **by the living voice and help of a teacher** <note>original Latin: "viva Præceptoris voce & ope"</note> / and it is sung and demonstrated to the student / so that one learns it from the other / just as one bird learns from another. Therefore, apart from the aforementioned **Giulio Caccini**, I have not yet found this kind of trill described by any Italian author / but find only a "t:", "tr:", or "tri:" placed above the notes that are meant to be formed with a trill. Nevertheless, I have deemed it necessary to add several types here in passing / so that the currently ignorant **beginners** <note>original Latin: "Tyrones"</note> may see and know at least a little of what is roughly called a...

<meta>catchword: Grup. [short for Gruppo, another type of ornament]</meta>

<summary>This page illustrates the "Trillo," an early Baroque ornament involving the rapid repetition of a single pitch, and emphasizes that such vocal techniques are best learned through direct imitation of a teacher.</summary>
<keywords>Trillo, Claudio Monteverdi, Giulio Caccini, vocal ornamentation, musical notation, pedagogy, unison ornaments</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific ornaments of the "trill" and "group" discussed on page 239 to a broader discussion of "passages"—melodic runs that connect notes. It concludes the current section of the volume with a promise of a future, more detailed publication on the subject.</meta>

240.

# Passages. 
<note>original: "Passaggi"; fast, virtuosic melodic runs used to bridge the gap between two main notes of a melody</note>

They are fast runs / which are performed both **stepwise** <note>original: "Gradatim"</note> and also **by leaps** <note>original: "Saltuatim"</note> through all **intervals**, as much **ascending** as **descending**, placed and created over notes that have some time value.

And they are of two kinds: Some are simple / which are formed with **minims** <note>half notes</note> or **semiminims** <note>quarter notes</note>, or **minims** and **semiminims** together: Some are "broken" / which are made from **fusae** <note>eighth notes</note> or **semifusae** <note>sixteenth notes</note>, or **fusae** and **semifusae** at the same time.

(The **semiminims** are called **chromata** <note>Greek/Latin for "colored notes"</note> by the Italians; the **fusae** however are called **semichromata**: The **semifusae** are called **bischromata** <note>double-colored</note>.)

Beginning students of this art, however, should first take their start with the simple and basic passages / and afterwards diligently exercise and practice themselves gradually in the "broken" ones / studded with **eighth notes** / until they finally arrive at those with **sixteenth notes** / and are able to manage them.

### 3. EXERCISE.

However, so that one can better understand what has been briefly touched upon so far, it must be **demonstrated** with all kinds and many examples **diminished** <term>Diminution</term> <note>the process of "breaking" a long note into many smaller, faster notes to create an ornament</note> in various ways (for the **method of Diminution** is drawn above them so that one can see from it in what manner these and those types of notes, and also these and those intervals, are to be **diminished** and **colored**). Because this is too extensive, however, and cannot be contained in this **volume**: May the well-meaning Musician and Cantor be satisfied with this for now, until the specific, detailed treatise in **Rules** and **Examples** is published by me shortly with God's help. To that work, I wish to refer and direct the kindhearted musician who is eager for such a new way of singing, etc. **Meanwhile, may the benevolent and sincere musician thrive and live, and continue to cherish and love me; whom I, as long as I live, study and desire to serve faithfully with all my strength.** <note>original Latin: "Intereà valeat & vivat, meq; fovere & amare pergat benevolus ac sincerus Musicus; cui ego pro viribus fideliter in-servire studeo & aveo, dum vivo:"</note>

-> Micaël Prætorius of Creutzberg <-

<summary>Michael Praetorius defines "passages" as melodic runs and categorizes them by their rhythmic speed, while advising students on a progressive path of practice from slow to fast notes. He concludes by promising a more comprehensive future work dedicated specifically to the rules and examples of these musical diminutions.</summary>
<keywords>passages, passaggi, diminution, vocal pedagogy, music theory, Michael Praetorius, rhythmic notation, early Baroque singing</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions from the previous discussion on musical ornamentation, shifting focus to the practical organization of musical parts (part-books) and responding to potential critics of the "new Italian style" featured in the author's work.</meta>

241

Finally, I have deemed it necessary to include and append this here.

-> 1. Regarding Chapter X, folio 87 and folio 90. <-

Because of the many different variations of the **concertante voices** <term>Concertat-Stimmen</term> <note>soloistic vocal or instrumental parts that "compete" or play against one another in a concerto style</note> in concert-style songs, it was not always suitable for the **alto** <note>Altus</note> to be placed in the alto part, the **tenor** <note>Tenor</note> in the tenor, or the **bass** <note>Bassus</note> in the bass. Therefore, I had to consider another method. And because one must anyway designate the other voices—those that follow after the **soprano** <note>Cantus</note>, **alto**, **tenor**, and **bass**—with numbers and names such as the **fifth voice** <term>Quinta voce</term>, **sixth part** <term>Sexta parte</term>, or **fifth**, **sixth**, etc., I thought it not inappropriate to use such numbering right from the beginning, in the following manner:

-> First. } { Soprano. <-
-> Second. } { Alto. <-
-> Third. } meaning { Tenor. <-
-> Fourth. } { Bass. <-
-> Fifth. } { Fifth voice. <-

-> And so forth. <-

I am certain that sensible **musicians** <term>Musici</term>, if they reflect properly on the matter, will agree with me in this case; since **choir directors** <term>Cantores</term> and **organists** <term>Organisten</term> can find particular **convenience** <term>Commoditet</term> and ease in arranging and transcribing music through this method—especially if such a **table** <term>Tabella</term> or **Harmonic Mirror** <term>Speculum Harmonicum</term> <note>a reference chart for part-writing</note> is available, just as I have always included in the **General Bass** <term>Basso Generali</term> <note>the basso continuo or accompaniment part</note> of my ***Polyhymnia*** <term>Polyhymniarum</term> <note>one of Praetorius’s major collections of Lutheran church music</note>.

-> 2. Regarding Chapter XII, folios 91 and 92. <-

Because I am also more than certain that some **musicians** (who have not yet **observed** <term>observiret</term> this new Italian style of composition, and at first perhaps might not quickly **grasp** <term>affequiren</term> my actual meaning, or might interpret it differently than I intended) will perhaps **judge** my **work** <term>Opere</term> **unfavorably and thus less skillfully** <note>original: "sinistrè atque ita minùs dextrè judiciren"; literally "left-handedly and thus less right-handedly"</note>. Therefore, I can well endure—and would even request—that such **scruples** <term>scrupel</term> or doubts be revealed to me either in writing or in person, so that I might give everyone my more thorough and specific opinion on the matter.

Otherwise, I also have the words of Giovanni Francesco Capello, the Venetian:

<sig>Hh</sig>

<summary>The author explains his standardized system for numbering musical parts to avoid confusion in complex arrangements and invites constructive criticism from those unfamiliar with the modern Italian style of composition.</summary>
<keywords>part-books, voice numbering, concertante, Italian style, basso continuo, music theory criticism, Michael Praetorius, Polyhymnia</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical arrangement and performance practice, specifically focusing on the doubling of parts in large-scale choral works and the practical requirements for instrumental accompaniment in multi-choir settings.</meta>

242

I wanted to include here what recently came into my hands in one of his prefaces, translated from Italian into German, where he says:

I assure the critics and "clever-clogs" <note>original: "Klüglinge" — those who think themselves experts but lack practical wisdom</note> of the music used today: that when one doubles the choir parts in the **ripienos** <term>Ripieno</term> <note>the full ensemble parts that reinforce the texture, as opposed to the soloists</note> (that is, in the **full choir** <term>Pleno choro</term>), and lets one or two perform together in **unison** <term>Unisonis</term>, and the second and third in **octaves** at the same time—as can be done everywhere—the singing sounds much more joyful and full in this manner. And it produces a very beautiful **effect** <term>effectum</term>: *Listen, test it, and be satisfied.* <note>original Latin: "Audite, probate, acquieſcite."</note> *So he says.* <note>original Latin: "Hæc ille."</note>

Experience now proves that this can indeed be observed in 3, 4, or 5 choirs. For it sounds good to the ear, especially in large churches, even if it might not otherwise be permitted on the page <note>literally "on the card," referring to the written musical score</note> according to the rules of composition. In **motets** <term>Moteten</term> <note>polyphonic choral compositions based on sacred texts</note> with 6, 7, and 8 voices, however, such doubling can easily be avoided and the parts written purely. As for why I handled the **string ensemble** <term>Capella Fidicinia</term> <note>an ensemble composed of bowed string instruments</note> in several places in the ***Polyhymnia Panegyrica***—as indicated on pages 100 and 118—and set the instrumental parts in that ensemble "E" to be pure and correct within themselves, and also set the **concertante voices** <term>Concertat-Stimmen</term> <note>the soloistic vocal or instrumental parts that "compete" or play against the larger group</note> by themselves: I chose to set and arrange them this way with special diligence and careful consideration. I did this so that I might test and learn how the **harmony** <term>Harmonia</term> would prove itself and sound in such a style.

When performing with multiple choirs and appointing specific organists or lutenists for various distinct choirs: I have no doubt that everyone will know, without my reminding them, that the **thorough-bass** <term>General-Baß</term> <note>also called basso continuo; a continuous bass line that provides the harmonic foundation</note> must be copied out as many times as there are choirs, and within it, for each choir, the parts they are to play for their specific choir must be underlined in red or another ink.

But more on this, God willing, in the separate treatise on the **thorough-bass**.

In the **thorough-bass** part of the ***Polyhymnia III. Panegyrica***, at the very beginning as well as in the notes for each concert, one can find all sorts of necessary **admonitions** <term>Admonitiones</term> and **observations** <term>Obſervationes</term> that can be referred back to the Third Part of this **Third Volume** <term>Tomi Tertij</term>.

<summary>Michael Praetorius discusses the practical benefits of doubling choir parts in unisons and octaves for a fuller sound in large churches, citing Italian influences. He also explains the necessity of providing separate, color-coded thorough-bass parts for organists and lutenists in multi-choir performances.</summary>
<keywords>ripieno, doubling parts, unisons, octaves, motets, Polyhymnia Panegyrica, string ensemble, concertante, thorough-bass, basso continuo, music performance practice</keywords>

# ERRATA in VOLUME III.

->To the Reader, Greetings.<- <note>original: "Lectori S."</note>

**K**ind and dear reader: Although I had truly hoped that this third volume <note>original: "Tomus Tertius"</note> of my *Syntagma Musicum* would have been published and brought to light somewhat better, according to my wishes and desires: unfortunately, the opposite has occurred. In my absence, through such exceedingly great negligence and lack of diligence, so many errors <note>original: "Errata"</note> were left in that I have had to have several entire sheets <note>original: "Bogen"</note> reprinted elsewhere.

And 1. above all things, the numbers at the top of the pages must be changed for the sake of the Index. For instance:
G iij. <note>A signature mark indicating the third leaf of the 'G' gathering.</note> is page 21. Thus, on the following pages it must be 22, 23, 24, until gathering D, where the number is again correctly noted as 25.
On the last page of N, it should be folio 104; and on the first page of O, 105; and so on through P and Q, until R, where it correctly returns to the proper numbering at 129.

->T, face 2, should be folio 146.<-

->2.<-

Page 2, line 3: should be called / Canzonette <note>Short, light vocal pieces.</note>.
p. 4, l. 10. 11. For any harmonic C <note>original: "Pro quavis C. Harmonica"</note>: As then a principal:
l. 14. that He entirely f.
p. 5, l. 7. that She the words.
p. 8, l. 22. in several Two.
l. 30. that She the Motets <note>Polyphonic choral compositions.</note>.
p. 7, l. 12. 13. 14. 15. 16. are many letters in the Proper Names <note>original: "Propriis Nominibus"</note> and Italian words <note>original: "Italicis vocabulis"</note> incorrect/ though not much depends on that.
p. 9, l. 2. Since—in the [work] of John:
l. 30. one after another in one voice in Unison <note>original: "Vnisono"</note>,
p. 10. the tables should be as follows.

p. 113. after lin. 9. this should also be included: Which by the ancients was called the *Wanderer*. <note>original Latin: "Vagans." In Renaissance music, this was often the name given to a fifth or sixth voice part that did not have a standard range or role.</note>

p. 128. l. 20. 21. Marchian Donkeys <note>original German: "Marchianische Esel." A derogatory term likely referring to people or musicians from the Margraviate of Brandenburg.</note>; these are not my words / but those of *Bernardo Strozzi* <note>An Italian composer and writer (c. 1560–1630). Praetorius is clarifying that he is quoting an insult, not originating it.</note>: Therefore, I hope one will not interpret this poorly of me.

<summary>This final page of the errata list corrects various typos and musical notation errors in the latter half of the book, including corrections for page numbering, clef placement, and liturgical references. It marks the formal conclusion of the third volume of Praetorius’s encyclopedic work on music.</summary>
